Preface

I recently retired from a 40-year career in the semiconductor industry, working at
Micron and Philips-Signetics (now NXP). This book is intended to address a particularly troublesome subject that tended to pop up a few times a year during the
development of CMOS technology. Plasma and process damage is an often overlooked subject that can wreak havoc with development schedules, timelines, yield,
reliability, etc.
This subject has been studied since the early 1980s when high-energy processing
started to dominate chip manufacturing. High-energy processes have the propensity
to damage CMOS chips. This book covers this topic and several related topics.
Plasma and process damage affects many engineering disciplines during CMOS
development. This book is intended to be a practical working guide for engineers of
all disciplines. Process integration engineers must provide design and electrical
rules that minimize the risk of plasma and process damage. Chip designers and
layout engineers must be aware of the subject and provide practical mitigation with
layout and circuit optimization. Parametric test structures need to be designed to
detect and characterize process and plasma damage. Parametric and functional test
engineers need to understand damage mechanisms and optimize testing procedures
for detection. Reliability engineers must have product and parametric test procedures in place to detect plasma damage as part of reliability testing. Equipment
manufacturers need to understand the problem and deliver problem-free fab equipment to chip makers. Process engineers must develop and optimize recipes that
minimize damage. Manufacturing engineers must maintain and monitor the chip
fabrication line to avoid excursions.
Chapter 1 introduces the subject and provides some background information.
The common defects in CMOS manufacturing are reviewed. The topics covered in
this book are selected from a defect list, focusing on the stranger and complex
defects.
Chapter 2 introduces the antenna effect that commonly occurs in plasma and
process damage. The antenna effect amplifies the damage and exacerbates plasma
damage problems. Several types of antennas are reviewed, including direct and

v

vi

Preface

indirect antennas, source–drain antennas, well antennas, and damage across power
domains.
Chapter 3 covers practical damage protection circuits, including diodes and transistors. Protection of specific chip components, including gate dielectrics, metal–
insulator–metal capacitors, wells, triple wells, and memory wordlines using
protection circuits can help mitigate damage. The use of protection circuits to optimize a CMOS design for damage mitigation is discussed.
Chapter 4 discusses the statistical nature of plasma and process damage electrical results, showing multiple types of distributions. The damage observed during
product qualification is covered. The cumulative damage caused by repeated damage throughout the process flow is reviewed. The damage caused by processing and
tool excursions is covered.
Chapter 5 covers the large spectrum of time-zero electrical problems that occur
due to plasma and process damage, ranging from catastrophic structural damage to
subtle parametric shifts. The similarity of process and plasma damage to radiation
damage and electrical stress is discussed. Catastrophic damage, including electrical
arcing and electrostatic damage during processing, is covered, along with gate
dielectric breakdown mechanisms. Transistor degradation mechanisms caused by
damage are described, including threshold voltage shift, interface state generation,
trapping, and multiple leakage mechanisms. Subtle electrical effects such as transistor mismatch, ring oscillator speed degradation, and noise are described. The failure
of the CMOS isolation due to trapping-related threshold voltage shifts is reviewed.
The degradation of various CMOS parameters, such as contact resistance, high-k
capacitance, and radiation hardness, is discussed.
Chapter 6 discusses the latent damage after process and plasma damage, which
can create reliability risks for CMOS chips operating in the field. Most CMOS reliability mechanisms can be degraded by damage, including hot electron effects, bias
instabilities, and dielectric lifetimes. The reliability of CMOS metallization can be
degraded by process damage, including damage to intermetal dielectrics, corrosion,
electrical migration, and stress migration. The physics of latent damage recovery is
covered.
Chapter 7 covers the electrical behavior of single-atom defects and their effects
on CMOS, including stress-induced leakage currents and giant random telegraph
signal (RTS). These defects can be aggravated by process damage. The types of
defects in this category are reviewed.
Chapter 8 covers process damage in specific types of CMOS technology that are
very susceptible to process and plasma damage. These sensitive technologies
include nonvolatile memories, CMOS imagers, DRAM memories, metal–insulator–metal (MIM) capacitors, low-k dielectrics, finfets, and silicon on insulator
(SOI) technology.
Chapter 9 covers the specific process steps that can cause plasma and process
damage. There are a large number of processing techniques that can induce damage,
ranging from simple steps, such as deionized water rinse to complex high-density
plasma systems. Many different processing tools are covered, including ion implantation, plasma deposition, plasma etching, through-silicon vias, and bond pad

Preface

vii

processing. The general concepts of plasma process relevant to damage are covered.
Non-plasma processes known to cause damage, such as wet processes, spin processes, and resist processing, are covered.
Chapter 10 describes techniques to measure process and plasma damage inline
on uncompleted product wafers, short process flows, and simple insulator on silicon
test wafers. These techniques can greatly accelerate learning and problem solving.
Chapter 11 provides a comprehensive survey of relevant parametric test structures necessary to analyze plasma damage problems thoroughly. Methods and uses
of the test structures are reviewed.
Chapter 12 provides recommendations for design rules to minimize damage
through practical design and layout methodologies.
Chapter 13 describes parametric testing methodologies for the detection and
analysis of damage, including time-zero damage detection and quick techniques to
detect latent reliability damage.
Chapter 14 covers the complex subject of hydrogen in CMOS process technology. Hydrogen can hurt or help, depending on the situation. Hydrogen interacts with
plasma and process damage, complicating the analysis of damage. The physics and
mechanisms of hydrogen in a CMOS wafer consisting of 16 different phenomena
are described. These hydrogen mechanisms include drift and diffusion of hydrogen,
mobile hydrogen during device operation, passivation and depassivation of interface states, acceptor and donor depassivation, interaction with silicon and oxygen
vacancies, interaction with thermal donors, interaction with contaminants, bias temperature instability, hydrogen donors and acceptors, hydrogen-related physical
defects, hydrogen emission from films, hydrogen interaction with metals, deuterium
behavior, and enhanced low-dose rate radiation damage.
Chapter 15 provides a review of metal contaminants in CMOS technology. The
common sources of metal contaminants are examined, along with likely culprits
among fabrication equipment and consumable supplies. The relevant important
properties of metals that impact electrical behavior are reviewed. The worst-case
contaminants, copper and iron, are examined in detail, along with their specific
behavior in silicon. Techniques for distinguishing between iron and copper are
described. Case studies of metal contamination in DRAMs and CMOS imagers are
presented.
Chapter 16 covers mobile ion contamination in CMOS technology. The physics
of mobile behavior in dielectrics is described, covering drift and diffusion. The electric effects of mobile ion contamination in CMOS are surveyed, including threshold
voltage shifts, MOS capacitor degradation, diode degradation, gate dielectric degradation, and intermetal dielectric degradation. Product reliability failures are
reviewed. Electrical techniques for measuring mobile ion contamination are taught,
including transistor measurements, isolation transistor measurements, triangular
voltage sweep (TVS), thermally stimulated ionic current (TSIC), and capacitance–
voltage (C-V) methods. A review of the effects of mobile ions on very sensitive
nonvolatile memories is presented. Die seal structures inside the product CMOS
that can block mobile ions are covered. The analytical chemical techniques used to
detect and quantify mobile contamination are described. Modern and historical

viii

Preface

techniques to mitigate mobile ion contamination, along with mobile ion barriers, are
reviewed.
Chapter 17 provides a survey of fixed charge in CMOS devices. A fixed charge
is a mechanism by which a charge is permanently trapped in dielectrics in a wafer.
The charge shifts the active and isolation threshold voltages, degrading the circuit
operation. The electrical symptoms of fixed charge are described with common
causes in silicon dioxide. The dependence of fixed charge on the silicon crystalline
orientation is reviewed. Carbon as a source of fixed charge is described. The fixed
charge interaction with hydrogen and diffusion barriers is examined. Fixed charge
in spin-on dielectrics, silane-based dielectrics, and nitrided dielectrics is described.
The fixed charge polarity as a function of the film properties is described, along with
the use of aluminum oxide as an engineered film to target the polarity and quantity
of charge. Fixed charge in through-silicon vias is described as a case study. Finally,
techniques to mitigate fixed charge are reviewed.
I hope you like this book.
McCall, ID, USA  Kirk Prall
June 2025

Chapter 1

Background

1.1 Introduction
Modern CMOS chip fabrication requires processing the wafer through dozens of
distinct types of processing equipment (tools) and hundreds or thousands of potentially unique process steps that can potentially damage the wafer. This damage can
take many forms, ranging from catastrophic physical damage to the wafer to latent
reliability problems potentially causing chip failure in the field. Process damage has
different mechanisms that are specific to equipment and processing conditions. This
damage is known by several names, including process-induced damage (PID),
plasma damage (PD), plasma processing-induced damage (PPID), etc. Plasma damage is sometimes used as a generic term for process damage, even if the damage is
caused by a process that uses no plasma, such as deionized water processing. This
book defines a plasma process as any processing step using ionized species.
Therefore, this book attempts to use only the terms “process damage” and “plasma
damage,” where “process damage” is used as a generic term, and “plasma damage”
is used for processes using plasmas (Cheung, 1999).
Plasma damage became an increasingly important topic in the 1970s and 1980s
as the industry transitioned from mostly thermal and wet chemical processes to
high-energy plasma processes. An excellent book on plasma damage was published
by Cheung in 1999 (Cheung, 1999). This is the only book in existence known to the
author. A dedicated conference on this subject called the International Symposium
on Plasma and Process-Induced Damage was held from 1996 to 2003. A few good
dissertations on this subject have been published by Zhong (2001) and Park (1998).
This book attempts to provide an updated and comprehensive view of the subject.
Process damage can cause havoc with yield, reliability, performance, etc. Process
damage can be subtle and easily mistaken for poor chip design, lack of process
margin, variability, testability problems, etc. Process damage can remain hidden in
a mature process, degrading a semiconductor company’s economic success. The
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_1

1

2

1

Background

process damage can be dependent on multiple factors, including electrical charging
of the wafer, high-energy light (UV or X-ray), temperature, etc.
Process damage results in device degradation, which is remarkably similar to the
effects of electrical overstress caused by the voltage or current stress of the devices.
Therefore, electrical overstress is commonly used to simulate the effects of process
damage on device characteristics. Process damage characteristics are also similar to
those of nuclear radiation damage.
Plasma damage is caused by a combination of the plasma processing system, the
process recipe, the chip design, and the CMOS wafer. The complexity of this phenomenon is partly due to the interaction of the wafer controlled by the CMOS manufacturing company and the processing tool controlled by the tool vendor.
Combining all these factors in modeling or simulation is difficult and not often
achieved (Lee, 2023). Most process and plasma damage research tends to be empirical due to the complexity of attempting to model the processing tool, plasma conditions, plasma interaction with the wafer, the structures on the wafer surface,
damage-related device physics, and reliability physics. As a result, this book uses
only a limited amount of mathematics.
There is typically a trade-off between avoiding process damage and manufacturing efficiency. High-power and high-energy processes have high throughput rates
and lower costs but are more likely to cause process damage by providing rapid
energy transfer to the wafer. Equipment manufacturers optimize their tools and processes to minimize process damage, but there is a strong interaction with the chip
maker’s technology requiring coordination with the equipment manufacturer.
Process damage problems are often discovered late in the technology development
cycle, potentially causing significant delays in the time-to-market for a CMOS technology. A CMOS manufacturing fab must carefully qualify manufacturing equipment to control process damage.

1.2 Spectrum of Process Damage
Process and plasma damage can create a spectrum of different damage mechanisms.
Figure 1.1 shows multiple categories of process damage roughly sorted by the
energy required to create the category from the highest to lowest energy necessary
to create the defect. High-energy damage events can structurally damage the wafer
surface through electrostatic discharge, arcing, and structural melting. These high-­
energy events usually result in chip failure yield loss and chip failure. These types
of damage are easily observed as physical damage in failure analysis. Slightly less
energetic events can cause dielectric degradation or breakdown. Parametric shifts in
the device parameters require even less energy. Low-energy events can cause transistor mismatch or noise. Nevertheless, lower energy events can cause latent,
challenging-­to-detect reliability hazards.

1.3 Commonly Damaged CMOS Structures

1.3 Commonly Damaged CMOS Structures
The structures on the CMOS wafer that are commonly damaged by processing are
typically the structures that can withstand the least amount of voltage or current
stress. Additionally, the sensitivity of structures to damage plays a significant role in
the severity and extent of the damage. Table 1.1 shows a partial list of CMOS structures commonly degraded or destroyed by process damage.

4

1

Background

The gate dielectric is extremely sensitive to process damage. Gate dielectric
damage can be observed in dozens of different parametric measurements. Dielectric
damage can result in a spectrum of different effects on the CMOS transistor, ranging
from slight parametric shifts to catastrophic time zero failures with a variety of
problems in between. Damage can create hidden, latent reliability problems that are
not observed with routine test procedures. The gate dielectric is typically optimized
to achieve the minimum thickness possible to increase transistor performance, making it one of the most susceptible elements in a CMOS process.
A metal-insulator-metal (MIM, also called metal oxide metal, MOM) capacitor
is commonly added to a CMOS process to provide a chip design with a high-­
performance capacitor. The MIM capacitor is also optimized for minimum thickness to achieve a high capacitance, making it susceptible to process damage.
The dielectrics used in CMOS isolation to separate transistors, such as shallow
trench isolation (STI), can trap charge, shifting the electrical behavior of the isolation and leading to parametric shifts or electrical failure of the isolation. The isolation tends to fail first as a function of the damage due to its greater sensitivity.
Low-k dielectrics are commonly used in the metallization layers of CMOS processes. This dielectric is a porous, low-density film necessary to achieve a low
dielectric constant, enabling increased performance and reduced power consumption. However, exposure to diverse types of processing can degrade the dielectric
constant and change the material properties.
Parametric test structures tend to be very susceptible to damage because they are
generally unprotected by electrostatic discharge protection circuits on the bond
pads. As a result, process damage to the parametric structures can create misleading
data that may not match the actual product performance. In addition, the bond pads
can collect a large amount of charge, damaging the devices connected to the pads.
Therefore, careful experimentation and analysis are necessary to segregate the parametric structure process damage from the undamaged product-level electrical
performance.
Nonvolatile memory transistors such as floating gate transistors, silicon oxide
nitride oxide silicon (SONOS), and other related structures are sensitive to process
damage. These devices are designed to trap charge to achieve nonvolatility and
retain data without power. A process-induced current passing through these transistors adds or removes charge from these structures’ charge-trapping regions. This
trapping creates threshold shifts identical to storing the memory state, resulting in
an abnormal state. The abnormal state may remain during further wafer processing
and may be observable electrically after the wafer has completed processing. The
abnormal state may be neutralized or reduced by thermal or light exposure during
subsequent wafer processing following the damaging process step. Process damage
can also damage sensitive dielectrics in the nonvolatile cell, causing yield or reliability problems.

1.4

Survey of Process Damage Defects

5

Dynamic RAM (DRAM) operates by storing minimal amounts of charge on
extremely small capacitors. As a result, DRAMs are sensitive to atomic-level defects
that can be caused by process damage. These defects can cause leakage and charge
loss, destroying the data stored in the DRAM cell.
CMOS imagers and charge-coupled devices (CCDs) collect light in a diode, creating a small amount of charge, which is amplified, creating an image. Unfortunately,
the created charge is sensitive to atomic-level leakage, making imagers susceptible
to damage.
The susceptibility of a CMOS structure to process damage depends on the size
of the structures. Small structures can be more easily damaged by the electrical and
thermal energy created during the process damage. For example, Finfets and silicon
on insulator transistors have extremely small silicon areas and can be easily damaged. In addition, the continuous aggressive scaling of CMOS technologies has
resulted in increased sensitivity to process damage, requiring more engineering
diligence.

1.4 Survey of Process Damage Defects
Manufacturing CMOS chips requires extreme cleanliness and defect control as a
large spectrum of defect mechanisms must be mitigated for successful production.
Table 1.2 shows a partial list of common CMOS technology defects grouped by
general category. This book focuses on selected topics with strange electrical signatures rather than more physically oriented defects. The defects marked with a* are
covered in this book, while ordinary physical defects are excluded. References are
given in the table to direct the reader to information on topics not covered in
this book.

Chapter 2

The Antenna Effect

2.1 Introduction
Process or plasma damage occurs when electrical charge accumulates on structures
on the wafer surface during a process step or when electromagnetic energy couples
into the wafer. The charge builds up, increasing the total energy on the surface of the
wafer as the increasing charge raises the voltage on the structure. The accumulated
energy can damage the wafer structures as the voltage and current move through the
wafer. The dissipated energy causes electrical damage through multiple mechanisms. A small amount of damage breaks atomic bonds, and creates electron and
hole traps, and interface states, shifting the electrical characteristics of the devices.
A large amount of damage may destroy the structures and melt the structures on the
wafer’s surface, causing physical damage. Certain structures on the wafer may
amplify these phenomena, increasing the severity of the problem. This problem is
called the antenna effect, and it has nothing to do with traditional transmitting and
receiving electromagnetic antennas. The word antenna in this book refers to conducting structures on the wafer that collect charge. The name antenna was adopted
to describe this phenomenon.
Many different CMOS structures can create antennas, worsening damage. The
antenna can be a rectangular conductor, a conductor with a high perimeter length, or
an array of contacts etched into a dielectric on top of a conductor. The conductor can
be metallization or gate conductors, junctions, or wells. Structures that can absorb
charge (electrets), such as resist or masking layers and leaky dielectrics, can also
cause plasma damage. Plasma damage can occur without an antenna, but it is usually much worse with an antenna.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_2

9

10

2

The Antenna Effect

2.2 Direct Antenna Effect
The amount of charge and energy accumulated on the structures is proportional to
the ratio of the structure collecting charge to the structure being damaged, with
larger ratio structures receiving more damage. This phenomenon is called the
antenna effect. Figure 2.1 shows a sketch of a CMOS antenna structure with a small
transistor on an active area connected to a large antenna on the shallow trench isolation (STI). The charge collected by the antenna is dissipated into the transistor during the plasma damage event. However, the charge cannot dissipate from the
structure on the isolation and flows into the leakier transistor gate oxide, worsening
the damage because of the presence of the antenna.
The amount of charge collected on an antenna is proportional to the capacitance
and area of the antenna. A larger antenna collects more charge than a small antenna,
resulting in a higher current flowing through the small-area gate dielectric, increasing the gate dielectric damage. An extreme structure with a large antenna area and a
small gate dielectric area creates the worst-case conditions for damage to the dielectric. The ratio of the antenna area to the gate dielectric is used as a figure of merit
for the likelihood of damage given by Eq. (2.1).
AR =

Area of antenna
Area of gate dielectric

(2.1)

The antenna essentially amplifies the potential for damage by increased accumulated charge. For example, an antenna ratio of 1000:1 can increase the effective
current per unit area of the receiving structure from milliamp levels to amperes of
current, causing havoc on the wafer. The sensitivity of a specific process to

Fig. 2.1 Example of a simple gate antenna

2.3

Indirect Antenna Effect

11

Fig. 2.2 Example comparing failure rates for structures with and without a metal finger antenna

antenna-enhanced damage can be determined by comparing the dielectric damage
of multiple structures with different antenna areas to that of a structure with no
antenna. Figure 2.2 compares an arbitrary dielectric failure criterion plotted against
the antenna ratio for a spectrum of structures with increasing antenna area (Wang,
2004). The plot shows increasing dielectric damage as the antenna ratio increases.
A reference device without an antenna is also shown to demonstrate the baseline
performance with no antenna. The absence of an antenna does not guarantee that
process damage will not occur, but it just makes damage less likely.

2.3 Indirect Antenna Effect
Direct process damage can occur when a structure susceptible to damage is directly
exposed to CMOS processing of the structure. Process damage can also occur indirectly, caused by an unrelated process step occurring much later in the CMOS process flow. Figure 2.3 shows an example of indirect process damage (Choi et al.,
2013). The plasma exposure of a metal line separated from the damaged transistor
by many metal and dielectric layers can cause process damage. For example, exposure of the metal 4 (M4) layer to a plasma causes charge to flow through all the
underlying layers of metals (M1–3) and damage the underlying transistors. This
indirect damage needs only a low resistance path from the antenna to the damaged
structure. Indirect damage can also occur without a conducting path through the

transistor junction,

transistor for process steps
after metal 4 formation

metal. For example, wafer exposure to high-energy light (UV or X-ray) late in the
process can damage underlying structures if the light can reach the structure.
However, high-energy light damage does not show an antenna effect and does not
correlate with antenna ratios.
The left transistor in Fig. 2.3 is protected after the metal 4 process steps when the
right transistor junction diode is electrically connected to the gate of the left transistor through metallization. The junction connection of the right transistor provides an
alternative leakage path, reducing the current through the gate dielectric and lessening the damage.

2.4 Source-Drain Antenna Effects
The most common plasma damage source is damage to transistors from antennas
connected to the gate terminal. The source or drain of a CMOS transistor can also
create a path for plasma damage through either the source-drain active area or
through metallization connected to the source-drain. However, design rules generally forbid the formation of large active area antennas, as large source-drain regions
usually have no circuit advantages. Charge injected into the source and drain terminals can damage the transistor by conduction through the gate dielectric in the
source and drain overlap region (lightly doped drain), as shown in Fig. 2.4 (Yang
et al., 2018). The transistor parasitic capacitance components shown in the figure
can cause charging energy on the source and drain regions to be coupled to the gate
conductor, creating a propensity for plasma damage. Increasing the source-drain

2.4

Source-Drain Antenna Effects

13

Fig. 2.4 Cross-section sketch of a CMOS transistor showing transistor related parasitic capacitance components. The spacer-lightly doped drain overlap capacitance is shown as CGD(LDD) and
CGS(LDD). The spacer capacitance is CGD(spacer) and CGS(spacer). The source-drain to substrate capacitance is CBD and CBS. The source-drain capacitance is shown as CDS(channel)

Fig. 2.5 Sketch showing a MOS transistor with antennas connected to the source, gate, and drain.
(a) Cross-section sketch of transistors with antennas. (b) Equivalent circuit schematic of the transistor during plasma damage stress. See the reference for details on the circuit model

area creates a conducting antenna that can collect charge. The junction to substrate
capacitance CBD and CBS increases with the source-drain area, storing increased energy.
This source-drain to gate overlap region tends to be sensitive to damage as it can
easily modulate the transistor’s behavior when damaged (see Chap. 5). Figure 2.5
shows a cross section, with antennas connected to the three terminals of the transistor (Lin & Sery, 2003). The plasma charging creates a current source flowing into
the three terminals, damaging the transistor. Details on the damage are covered in
the following chapters. Silicon-on-insulator (SOI) technology tends to be very susceptible to damage from the source and drain. This topic is covered in Chap. 8.

14

2

The Antenna Effect

2.4.1 Differential Antennas
A transistor with antennas on both the gate and diffusion regions may have different
amounts of damage depending on the size and difference in sizes of the antennas. A
large antenna on one terminal, along with a small antenna on another terminal, can
cause more extensive plasma damage due to the difference in charging conditions.
This phenomenon is called differential antenna damage, and it has complex behaviors covered in Chap. 8. The differential antenna effect can be severe in silicon-on-­
insulator (SOI) technology, as described in Chap. 8.
The drain, source, and substrate connections can also impact plasma damage
protection in addition to gate connections. Bond pads or antennas connected to
these terminals can impact plasma damage. Figure 2.6 shows a sketch of the terminals of a MOS transistor undergoing plasma damage with antennas on all nodes
(Lin, 2016a, b). The experiment performed in this figure connects various terminals
to a diode with a temporary connection that can be removed with a fuse. The fuse is
intact during process damage during the backend of line processing and is removed
before electrical measurements. The plot in Fig. 2.6b shows a probability plot of
threshold variation for different bond pad conditions during plasma damage. The
configuration with all pads grounded through a diode has a very tight distribution,
indicating that the grounded pads have successfully drained charge from the pad.
Structures with floating pads that function as antennas have a degraded distribution,
indicating increased variation due to plasma damage. It is helpful to have duplicate
transistor layouts with standard bond pads and a minimum pad area that can be

factor in the damage behavior. (b) Probability plot showing the threshold voltage variation with
different bondpad connection configurations

probed. Comparing these two different structures can determine the impact of
plasma damage on all the probe pads.
The source and drain areas can significantly impact the process damage. As the
source and drain areas increase, the leakage increases, creating a high bias across
the gate dielectric that increases damage. This effect typically dominates when the
source and drain junctions are floating, making the junction less leaky as the area
increases. Figure 2.7 shows an example plot comparing the gate dielectric leakage
with different source-drain areas ranging from the design rule minimum to 100
times the minimum source-drain area (Lin & Sery, 2001). At the 100× area, more
than 50% of the gate dielectrics tested leak, while there were no defective gate
dielectrics at the design rule 1×.
Worst-case damage conditions for differential antenna plasma damage often
occur when one antenna is much larger than the other, even if the overall antenna
ratio calculated by the simple ratio of the antenna area to the gate area is lower. The
likely cause of this phenomenon is that differential antennas with a slight difference
charge the gate and drain to similar potentials. Conversely, significant differences in

Fig. 2.7 Probability plot of gate leakage as a function of the source drain areas from a 1×, 10×,
and 100× multiplier of the minimum design rules

16

2

The Antenna Effect

antenna sizes cause large differences in charging from each terminal, resulting in
considerable stress across the gate dielectric and increasing plasma damage. For
example, Fig. 2.8 shows the results of an experiment varying the difference between
the gate and drain antennas (Akbal et al., 2015b). The plot shows a high dielectric
breakdown failure rate for a major difference (46,000) between a large gate antenna
and a minimal drain antenna. The failure rate drops as the drain antenna increases,
reaching zero as the difference reaches 100 and lower. For this case, the antenna
ratio equations require subtraction of the antenna ratios.
The literature also shows cases where the differential antenna damage worsens
when the gate and drain antenna areas are the same. For example, Fig. 2.9 shows a
scatter plot of gate dielectric leakage for three different differential experiments
(Lai et al., 2006). The number of extrinsic data points increases as the drain antenna
area is increased until it matches the gate antenna ratio. The worst-case results occur
for large antennas on the gate, drain, and source. This result is opposite to the results
shown in Fig. 2.8.

antenna ratio

2.5 Capacitive-Coupled Antenna Effect
The direct and indirect antenna effects occur as a metal or doped semiconductor
collects charge, and it flows to the weakest leakiest structure, causing damage.
Process and plasma damage can also occur by capacitive coupling through dielectrics, where a voltage on one capacitor plate tries to increase the voltage of the other
plate. Structures on the wafer can form intentional or unintentional capacitors.
Charge collects on one plate, and the generated voltage is coupled onto the second
plate, creating enough voltage to damage sensitive structures and devices.
Metal-insulator-metal (MIM) capacitors (also called metal oxide metal capacitors (MOM)) are commonly added to a CMOS process embedded into the backend
of line metallization process. MIM capacitors provide an essential circuit element to
the CMOS chip designer, increasing the circuit performance and capability by
enabling high capacitance per unit chip area. MIM dielectrics can suffer from process damage, as described in Chap. 8, but they can also be a conduit to damage
underlying devices such as CMOS transistors.
Figure 2.10 shows several possible MIM capacitor circuit configurations (Takeda
et al., 1995; Weng et al., 2009). Figure 2.10a shows a MIM capacitor with the upper
terminal connected to a metal antenna in the metal layer above the capacitor. The
lower terminal is connected to another metal-routing line. Figure 2.10b shows a
MIM capacitor with the upper terminal connected to a metal antenna in the metal
layer above the capacitor. The lower terminal is connected to the substrate.

18

2

The Antenna Effect

Fig. 2.10 Sketch and schematic of a metal insulator metal (MIM) capacitor in various configurations. (a) MIM is connected to a metal antenna and another metal line. (b) MIM capacitor is connected to an antenna and a ground in the semiconductor substrate. (c) MIM capacitor connected to
an antenna and a CMOS transistor gate. MTM is defined as capacitor top metal. MBM is defined
as capacitor bottom metal

Figure 2.10c shows a MIM capacitor with the upper terminal connected to a metal
antenna in the metal layer above the capacitor. The lower terminal is connected to
the gate of a CMOS transistor. The first two configurations only have the potential
process damage to the MIM capacitor because there are no sensitive gate structures
in the circuit.
The circuit connected to the CMOS gate shown in Fig. 2.10c can cause process
damage. The charging current supplied to the antenna increases the voltage on the
upper plate of the MIM capacitor. The voltage is split between the MIM capacitor
and the gate capacitance. The voltage coupled to the gate may reach a high-enough
voltage that can damage the gate dielectric and transistor. The MIM capacitor is also
susceptible to damage during a plasma damage event.
The exact nature of the damage occurring in these configurations strongly
depends on the electric fields created by the capacitive coupling. A large MIM
capacitor connected to a small area gate creates the maximum potential for damage
to the transistor because of the high gate dielectric electric field caused by these
conditions. A high electric field occurs across the MIM capacitor for a large gate
area and a small MIM capacitor area. The MIM capacitor is usually designed to
withstand the operating voltages of the chip, which can be exceeded by plasma
damage. The gate’s dielectric thickness and reliability capability and MIM dielectric are critical factors in this case.

2.6 Virtual Antenna Effects
A coupling capacitor precipitating process damage does not necessarily have to be
constructed from structures designed into a CMOS circuit, as in the MIM case.
Instead, coupling capacitors can be formed from the plasma elements that occur

2.7

Well Coupling-Related Damage

19

Fig. 2.11 Sketch of a dielectric on a metal feature on the surface of a wafer forming a capacitor
with one capacitor terminal being the plasma and the metal being the second terminal by coupling
to the plasma. (a) Equivalent circuit. (b) Cross-section process sketch showing the formation of a
capacitor during plasma dielectric deposition

during processing in a fabrication tool. The wafer is separated from the plasma by a
region with very few charge carriers called the sheath. The sheath can behave as a
capacitor, coupling the wafer to the plasma. The plasma can serve as one terminal
of the capacitor coupling to underlying conductors through a dielectric on the surface of the wafer. The capacitor consists of a plasma terminal and a conductor structure on the wafer surface separated by a dielectric that can couple enough voltage
into the sensitive devices to damage them. Figure 2.11 shows a sketch showing a
path for process damage through a capacitor formed by the plasma and a conducting
feature on the surface of the wafer (Cheung, 1999).

2.7 Well Coupling-Related Damage
2.7.1 Bulk CMOS Well Coupling-Related Damage
The previous section discussed process damage caused by coupling through MIM
capacitors to sensitive circuits. Other possibilities exist, including coupling through
unintentional parasitic capacitances. Coupling through CMOS wells is an additional
coupling mechanism that can cause process damage. CMOS wells are used to form
doped regions to configure and separate NMOS and PMOS transistors for proper
electrical operation. The wells also form junction diodes performing a critical isolation function.
The wells also form a junction capacitance at well interfaces and well-substrate
interfaces. This junction capacitance can also couple voltages caused by plasma
charging. The well capacitances can couple excess voltages, charging, damaging,
and similarly destroying sensitive devices similar to the MIM capacitor problem

20

2

The Antenna Effect

Fig. 2.12 Cross-section of a CMOS process showing transistors and wells demonstrating how
plasma charging can couple from one well region to another well region

described previously. Figure 2.12 shows an example cross-section sketch of a
CMOS circuit (Martin et al., 2011). Antennas connected to junctions in one well can
change well potentials during processing to levels not seen during regular operation.
The charged wells can couple voltages to electrically connected wells through metal
lines or adjacent wells. CMOS devices in the connected well can undergo stress and
damage, degrading circuit operation and reliability.
Due to large design rules at well boundaries, multiple transistors are placed in a
well for layout efficiency for a typical CMOS layout. As a result, multiple connections to the wells from the source, drain, and well contacts create more propensity
for process damage with the increasing number of well connections. However, wells
also serve as diodes to protect dielectrics.
The coupling ratios for well-related coupling are more difficult to estimate than
those of a simple metal or gate area antenna. This is because many factors impact
process damage created by well coupling, including the following shown in
Table 2.1 (Martin & Kamp, 2020). Complex computer analysis such as SPICE or
technology computer-assisted design (TCAD) can provide the insight necessary to
optimize design and design rules to minimize well coupling-related damage.
The well size, guard rings, and related rules change the resistance between the
circuit elements and the substrate, impacting charge dissipation from the antenna.
The CMOS well may float during plasma processing depending on the electrical
connections of the well, or it may be shorted to other terminals. Figure 2.13 shows
a plot of the gate leakage of a PMOS device from plasma damage for three different
well configurations (Krishnan et al., 1998). Figure 2.13a shows the best results for
a simple floating well connection with a small well. Figure 2.13b shows worse
results with the well shorted to the substrate, creating a higher stress by bypassing
the well diode. Figure 2.13c has a significant defect tail with a larger n-well, likely

due to the increased well antenna area. The junction and well resistances can significantly influence well-related plasma damage.
The well coupling-related damage tends to be a function of both the antenna ratio
and the well area, as shown in Fig. 2.14 (Martin & Kamp, 2020). The damage
increases as the metal antenna area increases, coupling more charge into the well.
The damage increases as the well area increases, coupling more voltage and energy
into the gate dielectric. The upper right region of the plot has greater damage due to
a large well antenna area and a large metal-to-well antenna ratio. The lower left
region of the plot has no damage due to a small well and metal antenna.

2.7.2 Triple Wells
Basic CMOS technology consists of PMOS transistors placed in an n-well and
NMOS transistors placed in a p-well. In addition, a third well is often added, typically consisting of a p-well surrounded by n-type regions. This structure is called
triple-well technology, and the third well is called a deep n-well or triple well.
NMOS transistors are formed in the p-well surrounded by the deep n-well. A CMOS

22

2

The Antenna Effect

Fig. 2.14 Plot showing the two-dimensional behavior of well coupling related damage as a function of the metal antenna to well area ratio and the well size. The lower left region of the plot shows
no damage, and the upper right region shows damage. The plot is assembled from various papers
referenced by the author of the reference

triple well structure has several advantages: improved isolation, improved soft error
rate, noise reduction, and circuit advantages. The circuit advantages include biasing
the transistor body differently in the triple well compared to devices in the bulk
p-well. The advantages for memories include improved refresh for DRAMs and
optimization of the program and erase operations for nonvolatile memories.
Triple-well CMOS has disadvantages for plasma damage. An n+/p/n structure is
formed by the source and drains, the p-triple well, and the deep n-well. Electrically,
the n+/p/n structure forms two back-to-back diodes with better isolation and leakage properties than a single diode formed in a p-well in a p-substrate. The improved
isolation reduces leakage, which can increase the degradation from process damage
because the charge drained from the antennas is reduced.
There are different ways for damage to occur in triple-well structures, as shown
in Fig. 2.15 (Martin & Kamp, 2020). First, the p-triple well can be charged from the
antennas, and the voltage stress can propagate to an additional well (remote well),
damaging the transistors in that well, as shown in Fig. 2.15a. Second, the isolated
p-well can be charged from the plasma stress, and the remote well can increase the
voltage stress on a transistor in the triple well (home well) if the remote p-well is
near the ground, as shown in Fig. 2.15b.
As the well size increases, higher voltages can be coupled from the well as a
function of the well capacitance ratios into the separate remote well, causing
increasing damage to the transistors in the remote well. Figure 2.16 shows a Weibull

2.7

Across Power Domains’ Damage

23

Fig. 2.15 Process cross-section sketches showing different possibilities for well damage. (a)
Damage from a triple well (home well) with antennas into a transistor in a remote well. (b) Damage
from a remote well into a triple well

probability plot of the gate leakage voltage after a current revealing stress for different well sizes (Martin, 2013). The reference curve has the minimum well area
allowed by the design rules. Well sizes of 10 times and 100 times the minimum
design rules are shown. The gate leakage significantly increases for the large
well sizes.

2.8 Across Power Domains’ Damage
Modern CMOS chips tend to operate with different supply source voltages connected to the chip externally or generated internally through voltage regulation or
charge pumping. As a result, the chips have different power domains that separate

24

2

The Antenna Effect

Fig. 2.16 Probability plot showing the impact of well coupling on gate dielectric damage for different well areas as a multiple of the minimum design rule. The x-axis voltage is the final voltage
at the end of a constant current stress after plasma damage. The minimum well area is the reference
group. Two large well sizes (10× and 100× design rule) are compared

circuits operating at different voltages. Chip design methodology carefully manages
different on-chip voltages to prevent higher voltages from damaging more sensitive
lower voltage circuits. The designed substrate bias also commonly varies between
circuits for various reasons, including noise reduction, memory array operation,
dynamic substrate biases, etc. Some CMOS circuits are typically placed in a triple
well to isolate substrate voltages for transistors inside the triple well. Figure 2.17
shows an example schematic with two power domains (Vdd1 and Vdd2) (Chu et al.,
2018). However, plasma damage can cause havoc across power domains exceeding
designed conditions, with antenna damage propagating from different supply
regions. Plasma damage can cross the power domains in either direction depending
on multiple factors, including well configuration, gate dielectric thickness, supply
voltages, etc. Damage can occur during processing when the current and voltage
conditions exceed the design parameters.
Figure 2.18 shows a cross section of CMOS transistors in a circuit with different
power domains. Antennas in the wells for one domain can collect process damage
charge that transfers through transistors and metallization, damaging transistors in a
different domain (Chu et al., 2017).

2.9 The Reverse Antenna Effect

Vss1 and Vss2 are the two grounds for the different power domains

arrows from the antennas in a triple well to transistors in different well with a different supply voltage

2.9 The Reverse Antenna Effect
Process damage normally increases as the antenna ratio increases, as shown in
Fig. 2.2. Occasionally, different behaviors can be observed. Gate dielectric defects
increase as the area of the capacitor increases. A large capacitor has a higher probability of being defective when compared to a small capacitor due to the statistical
probability of defect density, which typically is constant per unit area. The process
damage antenna behavior competes with increasing gate dielectric defect density,
resulting in a peak failure rate at a specific gate dielectric area. The failure rate initially increases as the gate area increases, followed by a decreasing failure rate as
the antenna ratio decreases. An example of this behavior is shown in Fig. 2.19
(Krishnan et al., 2002). A peak in the gate dielectric defect density is shown because
of the two competing mechanisms. The right side of the bell-shaped curve shows

26

2

The Antenna Effect

Fig. 2.19 Plot demonstrating the reverse antenna effect by showing the percentage failure rate for
abnormal gate leakage plotted against gate area

decreasing defects with increased gate area due to a decreasing antenna ratio, causing less plasma damage. The left side of the curve shows increasing failures with
increasing gate area due to a large area having more dielectric defects. The result of
the two competing mechanisms is a worse-case condition with an intermediate
gate area.

2.10 Remote Antennas
Antennas connected to a CMOS transistor can occur anywhere on the chip, even on
the opposite side of the chip. Multiple antennas connected to the same transistor can
also be on opposite sides of the chip. Separated antennas may be the worst-case situation for damage if there is any across chip or wafer variation in the plasma or processing conditions (Akbal et al., 2015a, b). The worst-case conditions occur when
the plasma charges one antenna in a positive direction and another in a negative
direction, creating a severe bias across the transistor (Martin, 2021). Figure 2.20
shows an antenna separation experiment (Lai et al., 2006). The gate antenna distance to the transistor is varied, and the gate dielectric damage is monitored. The
source-drain antenna is placed close to the transistor in this experiment. Only the
closely placed gate antenna (<100 μm) does not show evidence of gate damage,

References

27

Fig. 2.20 Gate leakage for a remote antenna experiment with the distance to the gate antenna
varied with the source-drain antenna distance held constant next to the transistor

while remote antennas have a significant extrinsic tail, indicating gate dielectric
degradation.

Chapter 3

Diode and Transistor Protection

3.1 Introduction
Gate dielectrics are typically the most sensitive structures in a CMOS chip. However,
other circuit elements such as diodes and transistors can protect gate dielectrics by
draining charge away from the gate, reducing stress during process damage. Usually,
for most CMOS circuits, every gate dielectric is naturally connected to a source or
drain junction of the transistors driving the gate after processing is completed. The
connected junctions partially protect the gate dielectrics from process damage from
the point in the process flow where they are electrically connected to the end of the
process flow. CMOS design practices commonly prevent the connection of transistor gate terminals without a gate connection to a drain or source. Design rules may
prohibit connecting a gate terminal directly to a power supply to avoid unprotected
transistor gates, avoiding worst-case circuits for process damage.
Transistors can be added to CMOS circuits solely to protect sensitive locations
from plasma damage, placing junctions between the sensitive locations and the
antennas. This strategy obviously can impact the chip area if used extensively.
However, it can be useful for targeted specific critical circuits such as analog blocks
where the protection transistors can degrade without affecting the circuit operation
while protecting critical transistors from degradation. Figure 3.1 shows several
example circuits (Martin et al., 2021; Zhao et al., 1996). Figure 3.1a shows an
example where a CMOS transmission gate (pass gate) consisting of an NMOS and
PMOS in parallel is placed between an antenna and a protected gate to protect transistors tied to the power supply and ground. Figure 3.1b shows a sacrifical transistor
placed between the antenna and a protected transistor with the gate and drain tied to
the antenna. The sacrifical transistor characteristics can degrade slightly without
affecting the operation of the protected transistor. Careful analysis is needed to
ensure that the added transistors improve process damage and not worsen the damage.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_3

3

Diode and Transistor Protection

Fig. 3.1 Schematic sketches showing the use of CMOS transistors to protect sensitive locations.
(a) Transmission (pass) gates to protect devices. (b) A sacrificial transistor (MOS2) is placed
between the antenna and the gate of a protected transistor (MOS1)

Fig. 3.2 Schematic showing a transistor-diode protection circuit to prevent plasma damage by
placing the diode between the gate and substrate of the protected device

Circuits can be configured to protect devices from process damage by connecting
a diode between the antenna and the substrate in parallel with the protected device.
The diode creates a leakage path, draining the charge from the antenna, directing the
voltage away, and limiting the stress from the protected device to minimize process
damage. Diode leakage reduces the accumulation of charge on structures, limiting
process damage. The diode must leak significantly more than the dielectrics, which
is usually the case, enabling reduced dielectric stress by enabling the charging current to bypass the sensitive dielectric. Figure 3.2 shows a sketch of a schematic of a
diode protection circuit (Hiura et al., 2020). The diode must be oriented so that it is
reverse-biased during regular chip operation to avoid a forward bias condition
where a large current may affect circuit operation.
Figure 3.3 shows a process cross-section sketch of a diode protecting a transistor
gate from plasma damage. The diode must be connected to the gate as early in the
process flow as possible. Typically, the diode is connected at the first metal level, as
shown in the figure. A diode connected at the first metal level protects the gate during all subsequent processing steps, including the backend of line steps such as

3.1

Introduction

31

Fig. 3.3 Cross-section sketch of a gate protection diode

metal, vias, dielectric, bond pads, etc. However, the diode does not protect the
CMOS gate dielectric during process steps before it is connected to the gate earlier
in the process flow, such as the gate etch, implants, etc.
The addition of diode protection circuits to a CMOS design may create a significant layout and routing penalty, potentially increasing the chip area. Generally, it is
not practical to add diodes to protect the gate of every transistor on a chip. Instead,
specific sensitive circuits, such as matched transistors, or worse-case antennas can
be protected as a part of the design process.
Parametric test structures isolate individual CMOS components, allowing
detailed measurements and characterization of the isolated devices. Parametric
structures commonly lack diodes on the gates to avoid measurement limitations.
The protection diodes can interfere with some parametric measurements such as the
gate current, gate-to-drain capacitances, etc.
Diodes can be added to parametric test structures to protect the parametric
devices from process damage. Parametric structures are directly connected to bond
pads, creating an antenna on most devices. Some structures should be repeated with
and without a diode protecting the gate to allow easy diagnosis of plasma damage.
Diodes conduct current very asymmetrically, with the forward bias conducting a
large amount of current and the reverse bias condition conducting a low current,
which is many orders of magnitude lower. Therefore, the diode current during
plasma damage conditions must be high enough for plasma charging in both directions to mitigate process damage effectively.
Plasma damage diode protection can significantly improve plasma damage compared to an unprotected device. Diode protection does not necessarily eliminate
plasma damage but can significantly reduce the problem. Figure 3.4 shows some
examples of successful plasma damage mitigation (Matsunaga et al., 2007; Sommer
et al., 2011). Figure 3.4a shows improved gate leakage and reduced dielectric damage with diode protection for dielectrics damaged by multiple metal etches.
Figure 3.4b shows a dramatic Vt shift and variability mitigated by diode protection
during film deposition.

32

3

Diode and Transistor Protection

Fig. 3.4 Examples of the beneficial results of diode protection against plasma damage. (a)
Probability plot of gate leakage for nmos and pmos transistors exposed to multiple metal etches
with and without diode protection showing reduced gate leakage degradation with diodes. (b)
Probability plot showing a pmos shifted threshold voltage and variability caused by a SiCN film
deposition and a tight distribution with diode protection

Fig. 3.5 Sketch showing different types of protection diodes in a CMOS process. (a) n+ to substrate diode. (b) p+ diode in an n-well. (c) n-well to substrate diode. (d) n+ diode in a triple well

A CMOS process has two complementary transistors: a NMOS transistor, usually placed in a p-type substrate, and a PMOS transistor in an n-well, requiring at
least two types of diodes to protect all the transistors from plasma damage. Figure 3.5
shows a sketch of several types of ordinary diodes in a CMOS process. The characteristics of the different diodes affect the diodes’ protection efficacy. Therefore, the
protection diodes are selected based on the type of transistor to be protected and the
electrical characteristics of the diode.

3.3 Transistor Clamp Protection

33

3.2 Diodes for Protection from Well Damage
Diodes can also protect from potential coupling-related well damage by preventing
the well from charging during process damage or by draining the well charge, preventing damage to connected transistors. Figure 3.6 shows an example of methods
to prevent damage from well coupling (Martin & Kamp, 2020). Figure 3.6a shows
diode placement between the triple well and a downstream transistor, draining
charge from the triple well and preventing damage to the downstream transistors.
Figure 3.6b shows the placement of the diodes in the triple well, providing a path to
drain the charge from the triple well, preventing damage. This protection strategy is
more efficient as it uses one diode per well instead of one diode per transistor.

3.3 Transistor Clamp Protection
In addition to using a two-terminal diode as a clamp, CMOS transistors can be used
as a process damage protection device, turning on during a plasma damage event,
clamping the current, and protecting the circuit. These structures protect against
well-related damage as they can shunt the damaging current to the ground, protecting circuits across power domains and outside the well. For example, Fig. 3.7a
shows a NMOS transistor clamp used as a protection device for damage related to
well charging for a CMOS process with a buried n-layer (Kuo et al., 2023). The
clamp transistors are placed outside the wells and shunt the charging current to
ground. Figure 3.7b shows a gate leakage probability plot of an input–output circuit
connected to the damaging well. The gate leakage is substantially reduced with the
addition of the NMOS clamp.

Fig. 3.6 Sketch showing diode protection strategies for protecting against well damage. (a)
Diodes placed between the well and the downstream transistors. (b) Diodes placed in the charging
well to drain charge from a triple well

34

3

Diode and Transistor Protection

Fig. 3.7 Use of a CMOS transistor as a plasma damage protection device. (a) Circuit sketch showing nmos clamp transistors outside of a well with a n-type buried layer protecting circuits outside
of the well. (b) Probability plot of the gate leakage current of an input-output circuit with and
without the transistor clamp

3.4 Diode Operating Under Processing Conditions
CMOS processing temperatures can range from near room temperature to greater
than 1000 °C for rapid thermal processing. Processing times can range from nanoseconds to milliseconds for rapid processing to hours for furnace processes. The
protection diode characteristics change drastically throughout the range of CMOS
processing conditions. During processing, the diode operating characteristics usually shift to a more favorable operating regime for protecting against plasma damage for processes with temperatures above room temperature.
The protection diodes during plasma damage operate under processing conditions that are vastly different from the normal chip operation. Plasma process conditions commonly reach temperatures of 400 °C during processing. In addition,
wafers under processing conditions are often exposed to high-intensity, high-energy
light (UV) that creates electron–hole pairs in the junction’s depletion region, further
increasing the current. The UV light may be blocked if the wafer region is protected
by metal or UV-blocking films. The elevated temperature and light exposure cause
the protection diode to become more conductive, enhancing plasma damage
protection.
Figure 3.8 shows a simulated plot of diode leakage for a reverse-biased diode as
a function of temperature (Wang, 2004, p. 79). The reverse-biased diode leakage
increases by nearly 10 orders of magnitude between room temperature and 400 °C,
greatly improving plasma damage protection. The forward bias current also
increases at elevated temperatures (not shown).
Protection diodes exposed to light have increased electrical conduction if the
photon energy is greater than the semiconductor band gap energy. For silicon, photoconduction begins at an energy of 1.1 eV or 1127 nm and varies as a function of
wavelength. Plasma processes emit a large spectrum of wavelength as the ions and
electrons recombine in the high-energy plasma, as described in Chap. 9, which provides an example of emitted wavelengths during plasma processing. The diode

3.4 Diode Operating Under Processing Conditions

35

Fig. 3.8 Simulation of reverse diode leakage vs. temperature

photoconduction discharges the accumulating charge on the antenna, decreasing
plasma damage.
Unfortunately, damage to dielectrics by high-energy photons can offset the beneficial photoinduced diode leakage. Photons with energy greater than the barrier
energy of the metal–dielectric or dielectric–semiconductor barrier interfaces can
inject photocurrents into the dielectric, causing electron damage and trapping (Snow
et al., 1967). For the Si–SiO2 barrier, this energy is about 3.2 eV. In addition, photons with energy greater than the band gap of the dielectric can create electron–hole
pairs, creating severe damage to the dielectric. Figure 3.9 shows an example of a
diode and gated diode with and without illumination, showing about three orders of
magnitude increase in leakage with illumination (Krishnan & Amerasekera, 1998).
The left side of the plot (0–2 V) shows gate-induced diode leakage due to band-to-­
band tunneling for the gated diode that is not affected by illumination, while leakage
on the right side of the plot is dominated by junction leakage and is light-sensitive.
All the CMOS transistors leak at high rates when exposed to plasma processing,
adding to the junction leakage. This leakage provides additional paths to drain
charge from the antennas. Figure 3.10 shows an in situ transistor measurement during a downstream oxygen plasma resist strip process (Flounders et al., 1993). The
wafer reaches about 300 °C, and there is a spike in the drain-to-source current.

36

3

Diode and Transistor Protection

Fig. 3.9 Reverse-biased I-V plot of a diode and gated diode with and without illumination. The
diode and gated diode currents are similar in the overlap region (left of the arrow). At higher biases,
the leakage is higher due to gate-induced diode leakage. The diode area is 250 μm2

3.5 Diode Protection for Silicon-on-Insulator Technology
Silicon-on-insulator (SOI) technology does not have the capability to add a diode in
a straightforward manner like bulk CMOS (Fig. 3.3) because there is no substrate
region accessible without a unique contact. Figure 3.11 shows a sketch of a lateral
diode formed in an SOI process (Martin et al., 2021). As shown in the figure, two
silicon regions are doped with opposite polarities, forming n+ and p+ regions.
Additional contacts and interconnects are required, as shown in the figure. The lateral SOI diode requires increased area and interconnect, impacting the chip size if
diodes are used frequently.
The special contacts shown in Fig. 3.12 can be added to an SOI chip, enabling a
connection directly to the substrate (Pelella et al., 2007). The special contact is
formed by etching a contact hole through the dielectric on top of the silicon regions
(ILD), the isolation dielectric (STI), and the buried oxide. The contact etch is followed by implanting the p+ region and forming metallization. This p+ contact can
drain the plasma charge from the local circuit after it is formed, reducing the

3.5 Diode Protection for Silicon-on-Insulator Technology

37

Fig. 3.10 Plot showing drain current during exposure to a plasma correlating to the wafer
temperature

Fig. 3.11 Cross-section sketch of a silicon on insulator (SOI) CMOS wafer showing a lateral
diode. The connection in red shows the conduction path of the diode

38

3

Diode and Transistor Protection

Fig. 3.12 SOI CMOS cross section showing a process damage protection device formed by a
special p+ contact to the substrate through the buried oxide (BOX)

severity of the process damage. Alternatively, a n+ diode could be used. The reference claims these special contacts can be placed throughout the chip, serving as
lighting rods and draining away plasma charge. This technique has the penalty of
added process complexity and chip area.
The special p+ contact to the substrate or top-connected n+/p+ diode can actually
increase process damage by providing a hard ground to the substrate for the floating
body, increasing the stress across the gate dielectric during process damage.
Figure 3.13 compares different antenna configurations for a sensitive SOI PMOS
transistor (Martin et al., 2021). There is little difference between the reference and
gate and drain antennas. However, adding a special substrate contact to the drain
causes a significant time-zero threshold shift indicative of increased plasma damage. The transistors with the special contacts also have an increased reliability
degradation.

3.6 Diode Protection of MIM Capacitors
Protection diodes can also be used with MIM capacitors in addition to other mitigation techniques, such as limiting antenna ratios. Figure 3.14 shows a process cross-­
section sketch of a MIM capacitor protected by a diode (Wang, 2004). The diode is
connected to the MIM capacitor as early in the flow as possible, protecting the MIM
structure from process damage at subsequent process steps. During operation, the
diodes must be correctly biased to avoid forward biasing the diode and sinking large
amounts of current into the substrate. Practically, diode protection of MIM capacitors in a logic chip may require increased area and interconnect. However, the
diodes may be placed under the MIM capacitor, minimizing the area penalty.

3.7

Protection of Memory Wordlines

special substrate contact

3.7 Protection of Memory Wordlines
Wordlines in a memory chip are common gates extended across a memory array,
connecting the gates of many individual memory cells to reduce the array area. As
a result, the wordlines do not have any leakage path to drain the injected charge

40

3

Diode and Transistor Protection

Fig. 3.15 Process cross-section sketch of a row driver to flash wordline connection. (a) Direct
connection. (b) Diode protection by routing through a n+ jumper

from process damage. In addition, for nonvolatile memories, there may be two
dielectrics in series consisting of the tunnel oxide and blocking or interpoly dielectric, further reducing the leakage and allowing increased voltage that can overstress
the array transistors connected to the wordline.
The wordline connects the control gates of many NVM cells running across a
memory array. NVM cells are very susceptible to plasma damage, as described in
Chap. 8. A diode can protect the cells along a wordline by placing a diode at the
location where the wordline connects to the row driver circuit. Figure 3.15 shows a
process cross-section sketch for a wordline connection (Takebuchi et al., 1996).
Figure 3.15a shows a direct connection without a diode. Figure 3.15b shows a wordline connection through a protection diode. In this example, the unprotected wordline had multiple defective NVM cells on each wordline. The protected wordline
had no failures. Wordlines that are biased with both positive and negative voltages
cannot use a simple diode and require a back-to-back diode structure.
However, adding wordline diodes to a high-density NVM chip may be impractical. Figure 3.16 shows a transmission electron microscopy (TEM) photograph of
the wordline contacts for a high-density 3D NAND device (Choe, 2018). The wordline contacts are labeled 3 in TEM. Adding diodes to a very dense array of connections would significantly increase the chip size. Therefore, the process must be
highly optimized for high-density arrays because diode protection is not viable. The
contact depth increases from left to right in the TEM image. The leftmost contact
receives more plasma damage during the time necessary to clear the dielectric from
the rightmost contact during the contact etch. Additionally, 3D NAND wordline
structures are susceptible to damage, as the varying dielectric distance to the wordline shown in Fig. 3.16 results in a large overetch for the higher wordlines. Process
damage to wordline structures typically manifests as excess electrical damage that
varies with the wordline location.
Other memories, including DRAMs, can also experience process damage
through the wordline interconnects. For DRAMs, the most common symptoms of
wordline-related damage are single-atom defects, as described in Chap. 8.

Chapter 4

Signatures of Process Damage

4.1 Introduction
Plasma and process damage can have many different signatures that are observable
in multiple ways in the massive data taken during a CMOS manufacturing process.
Some signals may be observed in global signals, such as chip yield, burn-in yield,
and qualification failure. Parametric data is often the primary means of detecting
damage problems. The damage parametric data can take multiple statistical forms,
including a shift in the mean value, the development of a defect subpopulation
showing as kurtosis, creating a defect tail, or a significant increase in the variation
of a parameter. Plasma and process damage can show up in statistical process control data, such as parametric trend charts correlating with tool excursions, preventative maintenance, etc. Damage may be cumulative as a wafer is processed through
the CMOS process flow, with multiple steps damaging the chip, each damaging step
adding to the degradation of the chip. This chapter covers these topics, showing
examples of actual data.

4.2 Signatures of Process Damage
Plasma damage can be challenging to diagnose, with varying symptoms and many
different electrical effects. Plasma damage can show random or systematic patterns
across a chip, region, or wafer. Virtually, any electrical chip problem may be caused
or aggravated by process damage. Plasma damage can worsen preexisting electrical
problems and may worsen electrical problems due to unrelated causes such as nonuniformity. The electrical symptoms of plasma damage can be consistent or widely
vary depending on the specific electrical parameters affecting the chip operation.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_4

43

44

4

Signatures of Process Damage

Some common symptoms of plasma damage occur across the full spectrum of
potential plasma damage problems. Table 4.1 shows a list of these common symptoms. These mostly unique signatures allow plasma damage to be diagnosed correctly, separating plasma damage from other sources of process problems.
The defining signature for plasma damage is the correlation with antenna structures, which is not observed for other causes of process damage. Circuits and layouts with antennas typically fail first. This signature is unique for plasma damage,
isolating the cause of a process problem. The improvement in plasma damage with
a diode connected to the gate is also a unique signature of plasma damage.
Plasma damage can cause a specific spatial signature ranging from everything
being dead to only a single device on every chip being dead. Unfortunately, baffling
spatial signatures are a common theme for plasma damage, where the damage
occurs on specific devices, leaving similar devices undamaged as if the defect can
differentiate between wafer locations. As a result, the ability of plasma damage to
appear intelligent can be befuddling.
Plasma damage usually correlates with a specific process tool or process step,
showing a good correlation with the tool or recipe parameters that change the charge
fluence or energy of charge carriers reaching the wafer.
The final thermal processes and alloys can partially recover plasma damage,
resulting in partial healing with time after damage. High-energy UV light can also
partially reverse plasma damage.

structures are not affected. It may affect only one of several identical structures on the chip
Damage varies with process conditions, especially parameters related to power, bias, throughput,
voltage, etc.
The problem correlates with specific processing tools
Sensitive to final alloy conditions with more time, temperature, and hydrogen typically
improving symptoms
Recovery with time at room and elevated temperatures may occur
It may improve with post-testing thermal bakes (200–500 °C)
It may improve with high-temperature processing (500+ °C)
Exposure to UV light may improve symptoms
Parametric shifts between inline and final testing may be observed

structures depending on design rules. Parametric data can be bad, but product results good

Damage is detected with a diagnostic stress. See Chap. 13

antennas are better

4.4

Cumulative Damage

45

Plasma damage is often first detected in parametric testing, measured on test
structures placed in the scribeline, showing poor electrical behavior. Poor parametric electrical results can sometimes occur on a wafer with reasonable product chip
results due to the increased sensitivity of parametric structures.
Finally, plasma damage can degrade the chip’s reliability, creating latent defects
that are difficult to detect without extensive reliability testing. In addition, different
designs fabricated in the same process can have different reliability characteristics
due to the interaction of the design and layout with process damage. The reliability
and qualification of different designs in the same process can be affected by design-­
specific plasma damage.

4.3 Statistical Signature of Process Damage
Plasma damage has a myriad number of potential symptoms that are manifested in
many different statistical distributions of electrical or physical parameters. Process
damage can modify a normal electrical distribution by causing a shift in the mean or
widening the distribution, increasing the standard deviation, and degrading the process control capability.
Process damage can also create non-normal distributions where the parameter
distribution is a mixture of intrinsic and defective extrinsic data. Additionally, the
distribution can become bimodal, as some devices are degraded or destroyed while
others are undamaged.
Figure 4.1 shows some examples of finfet parameter distributions created by
plasma damage (Hiblot et al., 2019). Figure 4.1a shows that plasma damage created
an additional unique distribution with reduced gate leakage current (due to trapping). Figure 4.1b shows a plasma damage distribution with a long extrinsic tail of
high gate leakage. Figure 4.1c shows plasma damage, shifting 30% of the measurements, leaving the rest of the data unmodified. Figure 4.1d shows that the mean and
standard deviation are increased by plasma damage, while the distribution
remains normal.

4.4 Cumulative Damage
Process damage can occur at many steps in a CMOS process. Similar or identical
processes are often repeated many times in a CMOS process flow, creating the
potential for repeated process damage. Each time a wafer is processed through a
repeated step, process damage can repeatedly occur, with device degradation worsening with each additional exposure to the degrading process step. For example, a
CMOS process usually has many layers of metal. The processing for each layer of
metal is typically identical or very similar. Figure 4.2 shows a sketch of example

46

4

Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 4.1 Examples of different finfet gate leakage statistical distributions caused by plasma damage with an antenna compared to distributions without an antenna and without plasma damage. (a)
Bimodal distribution after damage. (b) Defect tail (kurtosis) after damage. (c) Defect tail widening
distribution after damage. (d) Large increase in mean and variation after damage

structures constructed with repeated process steps and can be subjected to repeated
process damage (Martin, 2020).
Accumulated damage can occur as a summation of processing damage that a
CMOS wafer incurs through multiple process steps. The accumulation of process
damage can occur as the wafer is processed through many different equipment types
and process recipes. The following chapters describe the most common steps of the
process that often cause plasma damage. The accumulated damage can be quantified by antenna test structures that combine antennas on different layers into one
structure, as shown in Fig. 4.2. The damage is measured inline after each antenna
has completed processing throughout the process flow. Performing parametric measurements inline after specific process steps is crucial to isolating the exact process
step causing damage. Figure 4.3 shows an example of the cumulative damage
caused by processing through multiple process modules, including a conductive
gate metal and five interconnect layers (Simon et al., 2000).
Process damage from multiple damaging steps can repeatedly damage CMOS
structures, causing increasing damage as the wafer progresses through the entire

process flow. Some regular process steps, such as a thermal treatment, may partially
recover the damage, but the subsequent process damage steps degrade the device
further. The damage tends to be additive throughout the process flow. Process steps
tend to be repeated throughout the process flow, creating an environment for
repeated damage. Steps such as resist ashing, etches, film depositions, etc., are often
repeated.
Figure 4.4 shows another example of cumulative process damage causing gate
leakage (Wang et al., 2001). Identical structures are compared with one antenna at
each process module, and multiple combined antennas for all modules are monitored. This comparison allows the examination of cumulative damage effects. The
structures with a single antenna isolate the process damage at one process region,
with the individual failure rate ranging from 0% to 20%. The failure rate for structures with multiple antennas on multiple layers ranges from 18% to 85%. The
cumulative antenna structures suffer from process damage at multiple steps, resulting in ever-increasing damage as the wafer is processed.

structure is degraded by multiple process steps, causing process damage. Vt in this
