on different wafer regions collect electrons and ions or holes, respectively. The positive and negative differences change as a function of the metal layer, with less ion
charging at higher metal layers.

4.5 Process Damage by Excursions
Semiconductor manufacturing is heavily controlled by statistical process control,
electrical monitoring of tool performance, and other techniques. Process excursions
are typically quickly detected and remedied with a limited loss of wafers inline in
the manufacturing process. Process damage can be subtle as it may not be detected
inline during processing or after the wafer is completed during electrical testing at

50

4

Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 4.6 Probability plot showing Vt distribution for a finfet flash process damage test structure
showing cumulative damage throughout the metallization section of a CMOS process from metal
2 through metal 6. The damage is measured at metal 2 and again at metal 6. Both positive and negative threshold voltage shifts indicate that the plasma is not uniform across the wafer. The cumulative damage shifts the Vt more negative as the wafer processing progresses

time zero. Latent damage can be undetected without reliability testing. Process
damage excursions can create an elevated risk of shipping unreliable products when
an excursion occurs. Figure 4.7 shows an example of a process damage reliability
excursion caused by a deviation of a dielectric deposition chamber (Martin et al.,
2009). The excursion was undetectable by parametric and electrical testing. The
excursion was detected by performing a two second revealing gate current stress of
2 C/cm2 (see Chap. 13). The excursion was detected by measuring the threshold
voltage before and after the current stress was applied and analyzing the difference.
This example highlights the need for fast production reliability monitoring of a
production process.
Also, plasma damage can vary throughout the maintenance cycles of fabrication
equipment as the system undergoes periodic cleaning and part replacement. For
example, Fig. 4.8 shows a factor of 2 variation in plasma damage before and after
periodic maintenance of a high-current ion implanter as measured by a specialized
test wafer using nonvolatile memory (CHARM) (Gonzalez et al., 1988; Siu
et al., 2002).

Chapter 5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

5.1 Introduction
Process damage can wreak havoc with CMOS chips, causing a wide range of electrical symptoms and damage. The damage can create time-zero electrical problems,
which can be observed immediately after wafer processing, resulting in probe and
parametric electrical changes that cause multiple problems, including yield loss,
margin loss, functionality, etc. Process damage can also cause chip reliability problems by partially wounding the CMOS devices, resulting in reduced chip lifetime
and failure in the field (see Chaps. 4 and 6).

5.2 Similarity of Process Damage and Electrical Stress
The electrical effects of process damage tend to be remarkably similar to damage
caused by electrical gate stress by applying voltage or current stress to a CMOS
structure. This similarity provides some insights into the actual process damage,
which similarly stresses the devices. Electrical stress results can be used as a proxy
for modeling process damage in simulations. Figure 5.1 compares the interface state
density Dit caused by plasma exposure to a constant current electrical stress (Wang,
2004). The constant current stress conditions are selected to match the plasma damage conditions. This comparison provides some insights into the harmful conditions
caused by plasma damage during processing. The concept shown here is that the
process damage creates similar voltage and current conditions on the surface of the
wafer as the electrical stress does, allowing an estimation of the processing damage
conditions that cannot be directly measured in situ during processing.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_5

55

56

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.1 Example plot showing similar degradation of plasma damage and a constant current
electrical stress

5.3 Similarity of Process Damage and Radiation Damage
Some of the process damage electrical characteristics described in this chapter are
very similar to those caused by exposing CMOS chips to ionizing radiation such as
cosmic rays, neutrons, X-rays, etc. Radiation hardness is an essential property of
CMOS devices, particularly in space and nuclear environments. Additionally, commercial CMOS chips are assessed for radiation hardness to ensure problem-free
operation in everyday environments. The following sections describe common process damage electrical effects caused by process damage. Some of the process damage electrical effects described in Table 5.1 and Chap. 6 can be caused by exposure
to radiation, with the possible exception of structural damage caused by electrostatic discharge, metallization degradation, etc. The electrical symptoms of process
damage and radiation damage are very similar because the fundamental damage
mechanisms are similar, caused by the creation of traps, interface states, etc.
Additionally, process damage can degrade the radiation hardness of CMOS technology, as described in Sect. 5.9.6.

5.4

Time-Zero Process Damage Electrical Effects

59

5.4 Time-Zero Process Damage Electrical Effects
Process damage can be observed through the degradation of many electrical characteristics. The various structures commonly damaged by process damage can have
slightly different electrical signatures, as shown in Table 5.1. Any device can simultaneously have multiple damage signatures consistent with the typical device abnormalities possible for that device. The potentially affected devices are shown in the
second column. This table does not include higher-order metrics such as yield, chip
failure rate, etc., and shows only degradation modes that can be measured parametrically on test structures. Latent reliability process damage problems are
described in Chap. 6.
The process damage correlation with the structure layout consisting of different
antenna ratios is considered the definitive proof of plasma damage. This antenna
correlation with electrical effects is often used to isolate plasma damage from other
types of process damage.

5.4.1 Electrical Static Discharge Process Damage (ESD)
CMOS processes and equipment have the potential to generate an electrical arc or
electrostatic discharge, which can leave visible physical damage on the wafer, as
shown in Fig. 5.2 (Cantin et al., 1999; Lee et al., 2008; Ma, 2004). The amount of
damage is proportional to the energy discharged in the damaging process. The
energy discharged in the damaging process typically causes local heating sufficient
to melt the materials on the surface of the wafer. The discharge often results in
lightning-like marks on the surface of the wafer. An arc typically destroys an
affected CMOS chip but can possibly wound nearby chips, creating latent damage.
The defects can be random across the chip and wafer or have a specific pattern
occurring on only certain locations and structures based on the chip and the local
layout of the wafer.

Fig. 5.2 Example of electrostatic or arcing damage to a wafer. (a) Arc mark from a chip to the
edge of a wafer. (b) Cross-section TEM of metal and silicon melted together due to electrostatic
discharge in a scrubber clean process. (c) Wafer map of defects from defect in (d). (d) Defect
showing melted silicon active area due to ion implantation damage

60

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Electrostatic discharge can leave unique damage signatures in the wafer, giving
convincing evidence that an electrostatic discharge occurred. Commonly, tendrils
can be observed in the electrostatically damaged region, such as the defect shown in
Fig. 5.2a. This tendril type is commonly observed even in lighting damage, as
shown in Fig. 5.3.
Additionally, structural damage can occur during an electrostatic event, as shown
in the more extreme examples in Fig. 5.2. For small amounts of damage, it is often
difficult to differentiate between ESD damage and other origins of damage, such as
dielectric breakdown. The physical destruction is a signature of ESD damage, while
dielectric breakdown can have many causes.

5.4.2 Dielectric Damage Caused by Process Damage
The process damage charge flowing through gate oxide and other dielectrics can
damage the dielectric’s atomic structure, creating broken bonds and atomic-level
locations that trap electrons or holes. These atomic-level defects can affect many
CMOS transistor device parameters described in this section. Numerous methods
exist to characterize and quantify these defects, including capacitance-voltage techniques, charge pumping, etc. (Schroder, 2006). Parametric structures with antennas
can be characterized with these techniques to measure these defects with and without process damage.

Fig. 5.3 Photograph of a lightning strike on a sidewalk showing tendrils. (Source unknown)

5.4

Time-Zero Process Damage Electrical Effects

61

5.4.3 Dielectric Breakdown
Dielectric breakdown is the catastrophic failure of a dielectric caused by excessive
voltage or current, exacerbated by thermal stress. Dielectric breakdown is commonly observed in process damage situations. Dielectric breakdown can leave catastrophic physical damage similar to arcing, or it can leave subtle minor damage that
is difficult to detect or no observable defect at all (Selvarajoo et al., 2005; Tang
et al., 2004). The dielectric damage is also proportional to the amount of energy
dissipated in the damage event. Dielectric damage and physical arcing damage can
be closely related or caused by the same process damage event. Figure 5.4 shows
TEM images of various types of structural damage due to dielectric breakdown
(Lombardo et al., 2005; Raghavan, 2009; Rider, 2021; Shubhakar et al., 2015; Tang
et al., 2004). The temperature increases as more energy is dissipated during the
breakdown event, creating more damage. The rough temperature of the event can be
estimated depending on what materials are melted or mixed. Low-energy dielectric
breakdown can result in minute amounts of damage.
Figure 5.4a shows a TEM image showing slight damage at the gate dielectric
interface forming subnanometer-scale defects formed during a low energy damage
event. Figure 5.4b shows a hard dielectric breakdown (HBD) with dielectric
breakdown-­induced epitaxy (DBIE), where the silicon regrows from the damaged
region, forming a quasi-single crystal region grown from the substrate. Figure 5.4c
shows a TEM image, with the gate silicide and polysilicon intermixed because the
high temperature was above the melting point. Figure 5.4d shows damage to the
underlying silicon and tungsten plug contact heated to around the melting point of
tungsten.
Dielectric damage can vary from a slight increase in leakage through stress-­
induced leakage, higher leakage with soft breakdown, and a high level of leakage
with a hard short. Soft dielectric breakdown may result in variable leakage through
time or multiple measurements. Soft breakdown typically occurs in low-energy

Fig. 5.4 TEM Photographs showing different amounts of structural damage due to dielectric
breakdown as a function of local temperature during the breakdown event. (a) Low energy breakdown forming hillocks. (b) Dielectric breakdown and breakdown-induced epitaxy (DBIE) of silicon forming a silicon bump. (c) High energy breakdown event forming a melted gate (burnt)
region. (d) High energy event resulting in the melting of tungsten plug contact

62

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

breakdown events. The electrical resistance of a defect can vary with time and partially recover toward its pre-breakdown value. As a result, soft breakdown can
behave erratically, resulting in sporadic confusing electrical results. Figure 5.5
shows a plot of a high-k dielectric breakdown for a constant voltage stress progressing from soft breakdown to a lower resistance hard breakdown state (Okada
et al., 1999).
Dielectric damage can occur in any dielectric used in a CMOS process, including
gate dielectrics, intermetal dielectrics, isolation dielectrics, etc. Dielectric breakdown destroys the material’s extremely high electrical resistance, creating a leaky
material or an electrical short. Figure 5.6 shows some examples of various dielectric
breakdown and leakage levels compared to the initial undamaged I-V curve dominated by direct or Fowler-Nordheim tunneling. A dielectric may leak by stressinduced leakage current (SILC) for low amounts of damage. SILC leakage is a
conduction mechanism caused by charge carriers moving through the dielectric
hopping between the damage-created defect traps (Ielmini et al., 2004). Figure 5.6a
shows an I-V curve where SILC leakage overwhelms the undamaged Fowler-­
Nordheim or direct tunneling current (Okada et al., 1999). As the damage increases,
the leakage transitions from SILC to soft breakdown, finally ending up in the low-­
resistance hard breakdown state. Figure 5.6b shows this progression, transitioning
from the right side of the plot to the left side of the plot (Strong et al., 2009, p. 300).
Dielectrics subjected to process damage are degraded by the charge flowing
through them. The charge damages the dielectric by breaking the atomic bonds in
the dielectric and at the dielectric-semiconductor interface, creating interface states

Fig. 5.5 Plot of gate current versus time showing high-k dielectric breakdown for various size
transistors progressing from the initial soft breakdown (red arrow) to hard breakdown (orange arrow)

5.4

Time-Zero Process Damage Electrical Effects

63

Fig. 5.6 Dielectric breakdown I-V curves showing various amounts of damage. (a) A 2.4 nm
oxide dominated by direct tunneling showing initial leakage and stress-induced leakage current
and breakdown. The unstressed intial curve is shown, along with various amounts of increasing
stress induced leakage current after current stress. (b) A thicker 4.35 nm dielectric dominated by
Fowler-Nordheim tunneling showing initial Fowler-Nordheim leakage and various levels of damage, including stress induced-leakage current (SILC), soft breakdown (SBD), and hard breakdown (HBD)

and carrier traps. This degradation creates electrical changes or makes the dielectric
more susceptible to further damage. Characterization techniques such as charge
pumping (Barbottin & Vapaille, 1999), capacitance-voltage (C-V) analysis, trapping measurements, etc., allow for direct or indirect measurements of the traps and
interface states (Barbottin & Vapaille, 1989; Nicollian & Brews, 1982). Interface
states and traps are the primary causes of the parametric shifts in many of the following sections.

5.4.4 Enhanced Breakdown Due to Radius of Curvature
The electric field across a dielectric is increased at the corners of conductors or
semiconductors, enhancing conduction. The corner field enhancement increases
conduction by field emission and increases degradation caused by plasma damage
in structures such as the finfet gate corners, metal interconnect corners, gate edges,
and shallow trench isolation edges (Yamabe & Imai, 1987). Figure 5.7a shows a
sketch of a conductor corner covered by a dielectric. The corner radius is denoted
by r, and the dielectric thickness is denoted by tox. The location of the enhanced field
is denoted by E. Figure 5.7b shows the electric field enhancement as a ratio of the
corner field divided by the field in a flat region as a function of the radius of curvature divided by the dielectric thickness.

64

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.7 Sketch and plot showing increased electric field at a structural corner. (a) Sketch of corner showing radius r and dielectric thickness tox. (b) Plot showing the ratio of the increased electric
field at a corner to the average electric field in a flat region as a function of the ratio of the radius
to the dielectric thickness for a silicon dioxide dielectric. The peak electric field is at the corner
shown with the arrow labeled E

5.5 Interface States
Interface states are electrically active defects at the semiconductor surface-­dielectric
interface that can interact with charge carriers flowing in the transistor inversion
region at the interface. Charge carriers in the inversion region provide most of the
current flowing through a MOS transistor. Interface states interact with the inversion
charge, temporarily trapping the channel current carriers and lowering the gain and
drive of the transistors. Interface states are generally attributed to dangling bonds
between the gate dielectric and semiconductor at the interface. Figure 5.8 shows a
quasistatic C-V plot of a MOS capacitor with various metal antennas (Shin & Hu,
1993). The flat band and inversion region of the plot are distorted due to the formation of interface states. This distortion can degrade transistors and capacitors.
Interface states can also create reliability hazards for the CMOS chip. Figure 5.9
shows a plot of the interface state density due to back-end-of-line (BEOL) plasma
damage with and without an antenna (Hiblot et al., 2019).

5.5.1 Gain (gm) Degradation
The gain (increase in signal strength) or transconductance (gm) is a CMOS transistor
metric used to measure a transistor’s ability to amplify a gate signal into a drain-to-­
source current. The transistor gm is defined as the ratio of the partial derivative of the

Fig. 5.9 Probability plot of interface state density with and without a backend of line antenna

The electron trapping, trapped charge, and interface states generated by process

Figure 5.10 shows an example of gm degradation for a finfet technology due to
back-end-of-line process damage during interconnect processing with and without
an antenna (Hiblot et al., 2019). Degradation of the gm characteristics can shift the
circuit-operating parameters or cause circuit failure.
Interface states are a primary cause of conductance generation, as the states interfere with the movement of charge carriers at the gate dielectric-silicon surface.
Interface states can be degraded by plasma and process damage, as shown in
Fig. 5.11. The exposure of gate dielectrics to an argon plasma is shown to increase
the number of interface states as a function of the energy level in the silicon bandgap.

5.6 Trapping
Traps can degrade devices by capturing charge carriers for short time periods,
extended periods, or permanently. Trapping can shift several device parameters,
including the threshold voltage, conductance, and dielectric conduction, and create
reliability hazards. Traps can be located in the dielectric bulk or at the interface. In
addition, process damage can create traps as current flows through dielectrics or
through high-energy radiation damage. The traps can be created without trapping a
carrier, remaining neutral and unobservable. However, after process damage, transistor operation or stress can populate the traps, causing electrical shifts and reliability degradation. The gate dielectric trapping behavior is a strong function of the
dielectric thickness, with thicker dielectrics having a high propensity for trapping.
Traps can be detected by forcing current through a dielectric and monitoring the
voltage across the capacitor through time. As the traps fill with charge carriers, the
Fowler-Nordheim or direct tunneling current flowing through the dielectric increases
or decreases as the trapped carriers modify the electric field across the dielectric by
changing the shape of the quantum mechanical barrier. Figure 5.12 shows an example of a trapping curve where the voltage across the capacitor is monitored as a
function of time while the current is being forced (Cheung, 1996). As shown in the
figure, both hole and electron trapping can occur and shift the voltage across the
capacitor in opposite directions. The green line shows a curve where the electron
and hole current can be observed through time as the shape of the barrier changes,

68

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.12 Voltage versus time sketch of the voltage measured across a gate dielectric for a forced
constant current. (a) Electron and hole trapping curves are shown along with the combined curve.
A no trapping curve is shown as a reference. (b) Band diagram sketch showing the change in the
barrier thickness and shape with electron and hold trapping

as shown in Fig. 5.12b. As the holes are trapped in the oxide, the effective E-field
increases at the emitting interface, leading to increased current. As electrons are
trapped, the effective E-field decreases at the emitting interface, thus decreasing the
current. The trapping characteristics may vary widely, depending on the dielectric,
damage, and measurement conditions. The location of the traps in the dielectric is
also important in the behavior of the trapping curve, modifying the electric field and
changing the conduction.
The shape of the combined trapping curve also depends on the current level
being forced. High current levels can prevent the observation of either hole or electron trapping, as the signal from the opposite carrier type of trapping overwhelms
the smaller signal. Figure 5.13 shows the capacitor gate voltage versus time plot for
a constant current stress with different antenna ratios (Brozek et al., 1996). The
control curve in Fig. 5.13a with no antenna shows a slight observable hole trapping
and slow electron trapping. Figure 5.13b, c show rapid electron trapping with no
observable hole trapping at the stress conditions. Figure 5.13d shows a curve for the
largest antenna. The curve shows initial hole trapping and a large amount of electron
trapping.

5.6.1 Threshold Voltage Shifts
Process damage can shift the threshold voltage (Vt) of the transistor, causing havoc
with the circuit operation, resulting in loss of yield, loss of electrical margin, etc.
The threshold voltage may shift positively or negatively during process damage
depending on the current direction and the trapping rates of holes and electrons. The

5.6

Trapping

69

Fig. 5.13 Process damage creating dielectric traps observed by Fowler-Nordheim current stress
on PMOS transistors versus time with back end of line antennas. Different antenna sizes are
shown: (a) control transistor without antenna, (b) small, (c) medium, (d) large. The forced current
is 0.1 A/cm2. The transistor size is 0.5 ×5 .0 μm with a 9 nm dielectric

threshold voltage can also shift in one direction and then change, shifting in the
opposite direction as damage progresses, following a similar behavior to the gate
voltage shown in Fig. 5.13. For an NMOS transistor, positive trapped charge
decreases Vt, and negative trapped charge raises Vt. For a PMOS transistor, the
results are the opposite.
Figure 5.14a shows a plot of the threshold voltage for a finfet transistor array
with and without an antenna, showing a negative shift caused by process damage in
the metal and interconnect process module (Hiblot et al., 2019). Figure 5.14b shows
a plot of the threshold voltage shift with and without damage due to electron beam
exposure (Pyo & Kim, 2013; Vici et al., 2020).
The amount of threshold shift can be a function of the design rules in addition to
the antenna area or perimeter ratios to the gate area. Figure 5.15 shows the threshold
voltage plotted against the antenna area for different antenna structures and design
rules (Matsunaga et al., 2007). There is a variation in Vt that is dependent on the
finger spacing, indicating a dependence on the space design rule, as described in
Chap. 9.
The threshold voltage due to process damage can also shift due to the active area
and gate design rules. Figure 5.16 shows a plot of the PMOS threshold voltage as a
function of the gate and active area layout (Li et al., 2007). Subtle changes such as
the number and position of dummy gates and the active area layout can cause significant changes in the threshold voltage shift due to plasma damage. The figure

Plasma damage can vary widely depending on the transistor channel length.
Figure 5.17 shows a plot of the threshold voltage shift versus the gate length, with
the largest channel length showing a negligible shift and the shortest channel length
showing a significant shift. This phenomenon may be due to spacer or channel edge
plasma damage. It also may be due to charge trapping in the nitride composite
spacer (Li et al., 2007). There is a slight change in the antenna ratio as the channel
length changes but not enough to explain the significant shift in the threshold
voltage.
Contact and via processing can also cause a Vt shift. Figure 5.18 shows a plot of
threshold voltage variation as a function of the number of contacts connected to the
transistor gate metallization (Tsui, 2000).

5.7 Transistor Leakage Mechanisms
5.7.1 Introduction
A CMOS transistor’s ability to prevent unwanted leakage between all its terminals
is an essential characteristic of the device, enabling chip operation and low power
capability. Figure 5.19 shows a sketch of the MOS transistor leakage mechanisms

72

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.17 Plot showing the threshold voltage shift due to plasma damage plotted against channel length

Fig. 5.18 Probability plot showing the threshold voltage shift as a function of the number of contacts connected to the gate of the transistor

5.7

Transistor Leakage Mechanisms

73

Fig. 5.19 Cross-section sketch of a MOS transistor showing different leakage mechanisms. Ijd and
Ijs are the diode leakage mechanism from the drain and source junctions to the substrate. Isubvt is the
subthreshold gate-modulated leakage current between drain and source that occurs when the transistor is off. Ipun is a leakage between the source drain junctions that occurs at high drain biases
when the gate is off. Gate dielectric leakage is denoted by Igb for leakage through the channel. Igd
and Isd is the dielectric leakage from the gate to the source and drain in the source or drain to the
gate overlap region. The gate induced diode leakage caused by band to band tunneling is labeled
Igidl and Igisl. Also, leakage can occur at the STI edge (Isti). For thin wafers, generation from the
backside can reach the active transistor (Ibck)

(Calimera et al., 2012). Process damage can potentially worsen most of these leakage mechanisms. This section briefly describes the leakage mechanisms and degradation caused by process damage.
Gate dielectric leakage from the gate to channel (Igb) or source and drain (Igd and
Igs) intrinsically occurs through Fowler-Nordheim tunneling or direct tunneling, as
described in Sect. 5.9.3. Plasma damage can degrade the insulating properties of the
gate dielectric, causing dielectric breakdown discussed previously or stress-induced
leakage current (SILC) (see Chap. 7).
Leakage can occur from the drain and source to the substrate by junction leakage
(Ijd and Ijs) or gate-induced diode leakage (Igidl and Igisl). Drain-to-substrate diode
leakage usually requires severe process damage (e.g., arcing) or other structural
damage, such as the creation dislocations (Yamaguchi et al., 1996). However, drain-­
to-­substate leakage can occur for other reasons besides plasma damage, including
salicide processing, metal contamination, structural defects, contact leakage, etch
damage, etc. (Claeys & Simoen, 2018). In addition, process damage to the gate
dielectric can degrade the edge damage as shown in Sect. 5.7.3.
Leakage can occur from drain to source due to punch-through (Ipun) or subthreshold conduction (Isubvt). Drain-to-source leakage is usually caused by the process

74

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

damage-induced transistor parameter shifts (e.g., Vt shift, subthreshold degradation)
described in this chapter. The punch-through path shown by Ipun in Fig. 5.19 is less
sensitive to plasma damage as the deep channel doping dominates the bulk leakage
path. Additionally, other mechanisms can occur, such as STI edge leakage or carrier
generation from the back of the wafer for thinned wafers.

5.7.2 Subthreshold Conduction Degradation
Subthreshold conduction is an essential property of CMOS devices. A MOS transistor in the off-state still conducts a small amount of current that can severely degrade
the power dissipation of a CMOS chip (Hu, 2010). Figure 5.20 shows a sketch of the
log of the drain current plotted against the gate voltage. The log plot allows the
subthreshold conduction to be easily observed as a straight line at gate voltages
below the on-state of the transistor in this example. Two points on the plot are used
to calculate the subthreshold slope S from the plot in units of mV per decade of current. The subthreshold slope is given by Eq. (5.2) and is a strong function of temperature (T). The parameter η is a technology-dependent parameter specific to an
individual technology and transistor.

An ideal transistor would have a perfect vertical infinite slope in the subthreshold
plot, turning on instantly at the threshold voltage, eliminating the leakage for digital
circuits. Fundamental physics limits the subthreshold slope to 60 mV/decade at
room temperature (300 K). The theoretical limit is for η = 1 and is given by Eq.
(5.3), where k is Boltzmann’s constant (in eV), and q is the electron charge. The
minimum theoretical limit is a function of temperature and fundamental constants
and contains no parameters that can be optimized.

Process damage can also degrade the subthreshold conduction region of transistor operation (Kamei et al., 2007). For example, a Vt shift described previously can
degrade the threshold region by shifting the NMOS transistor threshold voltage left
in Fig. 5.20a toward zero, increasing the off-state leakage. Additionally, the subthreshold slope can be degraded by process damage due to carrier traps, interface
states, and fixed charge by creating a path for charge carriers across the channel.
Figure 5.21 shows an example plot showing degradation in the subthreshold regions
of CMOS transistors with high-k and silicon dioxide dielectrics. Vt is shifted toward
zero in the lower-left plot. The flat curves near zero Vgs show drain-to-source leakage where the gate control of the transistor is lost. The transistor leaks severely as Vt
approaches zero voltage as the channel is always on. Junction leakage and subthreshold conduction can be differentiated by determining if the drain current flows
to the substrate or the source.
Figure 5.22 shows an example of subthreshold slope degradation caused by a
metal etch as a function of gate oxide thickness with and without diode protection
(Park & Hu, 1998). Diode-protected transistors show no degradation as a function
of antenna length, while unprotected transistors show degradation as a function of
dielectric thickness. Process damage strongly depends on the gate oxide thickness,
with the thicker dielectrics experiencing a more significant threshold shift, indicative of increased trapping and interface state generation.

5.7.3 Gate-Induced Diode Leakage Degradation
Gate-induced diode leakage (GIDL) is one of the leakage mechanisms shown in
Fig. 5.19 that can be degraded by process damage (Chen et al., 1987). GIDL is a
band-to-band tunneling phenomenon that occurs in the transistor region where the
gate overlaps the source and drain junctions, as shown in Fig. 5.23. A high electric
field across the gate dielectric in this region is generated by the difference in the gate
and source or drain voltages, increasing with increasing difference in source/drain
to gate potentials. The high gate electric field bends the energy bands in the source-­
drain region to the point where the diode depletion region barrier width is thin
enough that carriers can tunnel through the junction barrier. Both electrons and

76

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.21 Example showing subthreshold degradation due to Ar/O2 plasma damage for high-k and
SiO2 NMOS and PMOS transistors measured at a drain voltage of 0.05 V. (a) NMOS high-k dielectric. (b) NMOS SiO2 dielectric. (c) PMOS high-k dielectric. (d) PMOS SiO2. Three levels of damage are shown: undamaged (solid line), moderate damage (green dotted line), heavy damage (blue
dashed line)

holes are involved in the GIDL mechanism. For an NMOS device, the tunneling
electrons are collected by the source and drain. Holes created through the GIDL
process are injected into the substrate and flow toward the substrate contacts for
bulk CMOS. The direction of electron and hole flow is shown in the figure. Plasma-­
damage-­created defects such as traps and interface states may cause an increase in
the GIDL leakage current (Kim, 2015).
GIDL can be degraded by process damage, creating traps and interface states,
increasing the GIDL current (Chen et al., 1989). Figure 5.24 shows an example of
GIDL degradation caused by plasma damage from a metal antenna. GIDL has a
unique electrical signature that eliminates ambiguity. The GIDL current increases as
the off-state gate voltage is biased further away from the threshold voltage, as shown
in the figure. The degradation shown correlates with an increase in the interface

5.7

Transistor Leakage Mechanisms

77

Fig. 5.22 Plot showing the subthreshold slope as a function of metal antenna perimeter for several
different dielectric thicknesses with and without diode protection

state density. The GIDL current is sensitive to source-drain damage in the gate overlap region, as described in Sect. 5.7.4. Process-damage-related defects, such as
traps, etc., can increase the GIDL through trap-assisted SILC by adding a dielectric
leakage component, as shown in Chap. 7 (Kim, 2015; Ma et al., 2001).

5.7.4 Lightly Doped Drain (LDD) Region Damage
The region where the gate overlaps the doped source or drain junction is called the
source-drain overlap region. Charge flowing into the gate dielectric can reach the
substrate through conduction over the channel or over the source-drain regions. This
overlap region is sensitive to process damage. There can be many process sources
for damage to the source-drain region. For example, direct physical damage to the
gate edge and spacer edge can occur from exposure to the plasma, causing chemical
contamination, kinetic damage, etc. Direct exposure to the plasma can generate
electrically active defects, such as traps, etc.
The source-drain regions may have higher current flow depending on several factors, including floating potentials of the well, plasma charging from the source or

78

5

The depletion regions are shown by the dashed lines and show a depletion region created in the
heavily doped n+ source drain region due to the high electric field between the gate and the drain.
The depletion region is not to scale and is exaggerated under the gate

Fig. 5.24 Subthreshold plot of log Ids versus Vgs showing gate induced diode leakage (GIDL) on

Fig. 5.25 Sketch comparing process damage in the channel with process damage over the source-­
drain regions

drain, potentials of the source-drain regions, etc. In addition, the source-drain region
usually has a very tiny area relative to the channel area. Therefore, the current density in the source-drain overlap region during plasma damage can be much higher
than the current density in the channel, creating a propensity for more damage in a
small region. The source-drain overlap region can be susceptible to trapped charge,
interface states, or dielectric leakage. The lightly doped drain (LDD) region resistance can be modulated, changing the source-drain resistance and impacting the
transistor electrical parameters. The modulation of the LDD region can change the
transistor’s channel length and mobility, impacting the transistor’s current drive.
This region of the transistor is critical to its reliability performance. For example,
the hot-carrier lifetime, etc., is controlled by this region and can be degraded by
damage. Figure 5.25 shows a sketch highlighting the differences between damage
in the channel and the source-drain overlap region (Brozek et al., 1995).
Modulation of the source-drain resistance with damage can easily occur.
Figure 5.26 shows the source-drain resistance plotted against the gate voltage for
two different etchers under two overetch conditions (Brozek et al., 1995). In this
example, the resistance shifts by a factor of 2, creating a significant shift in the electrical device parameters.
The effective electrical channel length is a metric describing the distance between
the source and drain in an MOS transistor measured from the junction edges. Many
CMOS parameters are a strong function of the channel length, affecting the drive
current and many other parameters. The resistance modulation of the source-drain
resistance can create a significant shift in the effective electrical channel length,
measured across several devices with different channel lengths. Figure 5.27 shows
a plot of the change in the effective channel length (Leff) plotted against the gate

Damage to the source-drain region can have other effects than parametric and
reliability shifts. Damage to the gate edge and dielectric can cause excess noise
through the creation of interface states, traps, and leakage. Figure 5.28 shows transistor current-voltage currents with significant noise caused by source-drain edge
damage for a high-k dielectric transistor (Cho et al., 2011).

5.7.5 Punch-Through Degradation
Punch-through is another CMOS transistor leakage mechanism shown in Fig. 5.19.
In this case, the leakage current can flow between the source and the drain. A CMOS
transistor is designed to have a strong electrical barrier between the source and
drain, created by tailoring the doping in the channel and source-drain edges. Plasma
damage can inject charge in various channel locations, including an asymmetric
charge distribution that can weaken the punch-through resistance or cause punch-­
through-­related reliability problems. For example, contact etch damage was shown
to degrade a punch-through-related PMOS reliability problem called hot-electron-­
induced punch-through (HEIP) (Koyangi et al., 1987).

82

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

5.8 Transistor Degradation Mechanisms
5.8.1 Process Damage-Induced Transistor Mismatch
Process damage commonly degrades the variability of many transistor parameters,
increasing the mismatch of matched transistors in critical circuits in CMOS chips.
CMOS wafers exposed to excessive process damage can be expected to have
increased variation in the affected device parameters in this section. Degraded mismatch of critical circuits can cause yield, margin, performance, and reliability problems. Process damage can often be mistaken for process variability, poor circuit
design, testing problems, etc. Design rules and layout methodology (see Chap. 12)
are critical to controlling process damage for matched transistors. All antenna structures connected to matched transistors of similar critical circuits should have an
identical layout matching any potential antenna structures to achieve identical damage. Design modifications to protect the critical circuits with diodes, pass transistors, etc., can also mitigate process damage (Zhao et al., 1996).
Figure 5.29 shows an example of process damage creating matching issues (Shin
et al., 1993). The circuit diagram shown in the figure is for a differential pair amplifier (Baker, 2019, p. 735). This amplifier detects low-level signals and magnifies
them, providing a useful signal. It is commonly used in critical applications such as
sense amplifiers for memory arrays. The transistors in the circuit must be carefully
matched for the amplifier to work correctly. This type of circuit is sensitive to
plasma damage. Different antenna sizes are connected to the inputs A1 and A2 by
using different pad sizes. The output V0 can be monitored to determine the offset
voltage error caused by the damage. Figure 5.29b shows the offset voltage error
caused by plasma ashing. The error shown as tens of millivolts is significant because

Fig. 5.29 Example of degradation in transistor matching with plasma damage with a metal
antenna. (a) Schematic of a differential pair amplifier with different antenna sizes on transistors
M1 and M2. (b) Offset voltage between the transistors M1 and M2 with different antenna sizes on
transistor M2. The area of the small pad is 6400 μm2

5.8

Transistor Degradation Mechanisms

83

Fig. 5.30 Transistor Vt mismatch for different ratios of gate antennas due to damage from
metal etching

it is of the same order of magnitude as signals typically amplified by the circuit and
would cause a severe malfunction. The control line in the figure uses a wet metal
etch to avoid plasma damage, proving the cause of the mismatch. An additional
example is shown in Fig. 5.30, where metal patterning causes a significant Vt mismatch, highlighting the need for an identical layout (Zhao et al., 1996).

5.8.2 Process Damage Noise Degradation
Noise is ever present in electronic systems and is universally generated by thermal
processes at temperatures above absolute zero. Additionally, electronic and defect
processes in semiconductors generate noise that can be significantly increased by
process damage. Some sources of noise are fundamental and not related to process
damage. These sources include thermal noise caused by the Brownian motion of
electrons or shot noise caused by individual electrons moving across a junction.
Other noise sources are less fundamental and can be modulated or degraded by
process damage. These sources include 1/f noise that dominates at low frequencies
caused by mobility variation, fluctuations in carrier numbers, and random thermal
signal (RTS) noise caused by carrier capture and emission. Process damage typically modulates 1/f and RTS noise. These two noise sources tend to be confounded
and interact.

84

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Figure 5.31 shows an example of this interaction observed in CMOS imagers
(Wang, 2008). Figure 5.31a is a histogram of the digital output of a CMOS imager
pixel as a function of frequency, showing a 1/f noise characteristic. Figure 5.31b–d
shows the pixel noise for three pixels taken at different points on the 1/f histogram
with various levels of noise. Multimodal noise can be observed in some pixels
(Wang, 2008).
RTS noise in CMOS transistors strongly depends on the transistor area, with the
noise increasing for smaller transistors with a 1/WL (length and width) dependence.
Scaling makes the noise worse, and process damage degrades the noise in a scaled
transistor more severely. The RTS noise for a CMOS transistor can be roughly
approximated as a voltage source in series with the gate by Eq. (5.4), where K is a
process-dependent constant, Cox is the gate capacitance, W and L are the transistor
width and length, f is the frequency of the noise, and C is a constant between 0.7 and
1.2 (Tsividis & McAndrew, 2011, p. 453).

Cox WL f C

Fig. 5.31 Example of 1/f and RTS noise in a CMOS imager across 15,000 frames (pictures). (a)
1/f noise histogram with arbitrary units. (b) Pixel A, low noise pixel output. (c) Pixel B, medium

5.8

Transistor Degradation Mechanisms

85

RTS problems can cause severe problems in highly scaled devices such as NVM,
DRAM, imagers, finfets, etc., with process damage degrading the devices further.
The damaged dielectrics and semiconductor interfaces caused by process damage can increase the transistor noise. The increased noise is caused by the increased
number of traps and interface states caused by the damage. Charge carriers populate
and depopulate these defects, creating noise as the carriers are added or subtracted
from the operating transistor channel current. In addition, damage to the gate dielectric can create a noise interaction between the gate and drain caused by the defects
modulating the tunneling current between the gate and drain. Damage to the source-­
drain overlap region is described in Sect. 5.7.4.
Transistor noise can degrade the chip operating margin (speed, voltage, etc.),
causing a loss in operation robustness, manifested as a testing problem, yield problem, design problem, process variation, etc. Figure 5.32 shows an example of
increased noise with plasma damage with variations in antenna sizes. First, the transistor drain current, etc., is measured continuously as rapidly as possible to obtain
many measurements under the same conditions. Then, the current is plotted against
the elapsed time of the measurements to generate plots of the variation.
Figure 5.33a shows a noise plot for two different antenna sizes for transistors
damaged by an inductively coupled plasma (ICP) metal etch. The larger antenna
size increases noise with the noise spikes caused by the traps filling and detrapping,
yielding current conduction at different levels. The spikes have a time constant associated with the time required for the trap to emit or absorb a charge carrier.

antenna sizes

Noise behavior in CMOS devices is a complex topic (Bonani & Ghione, 2001).
Several different metrics are commonly used to express noise behavior. For example, Fig. 5.34 shows the noise metrics drain current noise (A2/Hz) and the noise
power spectrum (dB/Hz) demonstrating noise degradation with plasma damage
(Shin et al., 1993; Tzeng et al., 2002).
Noise can sometimes be observed during routine I-V measurements. Figure 5.28
(shown previously) shows plots of the drain, gate, and substrate currents for a high­k gate transistor damaged by the plasma gate etch. The noise can be observed as
abnormal noisy I-V curves, as shown in the figure.
The RTS noise signatures in the previous figures oscillate between two or more
metastable states. The oscillation frequency between states depends on the type of
defect trapping and detrapping the charge carrier. The energy level of the trap determines the difficulty of trapping or ejecting a charge carrier, determining the characteristic frequency of the RTS defect. Figure 5.35 shows a plot of the characteristic
frequency for a few RTS defects as a function of temperature (Janesick, 2001). The
time constant is a strong function of temperature, changing by many orders of

5.8

Transistor Degradation Mechanisms

87

Fig. 5.34 Noise plots showing degradation due to plasma damage. (a) Drain current noise as a
function of metal antenna ratio damaged during metal etch and resist strip and correlation with
interface state density. (b) Noise power spectrum versus frequency for different antenna sizes during metal etch

Fig. 5.35 Emission time constant for various types of RTS defects as a function of temperature
with the trap energy

magnitude. Traps with low energy levels have a low time constant, while traps with
high energy levels have a high time constant. The extracted energy level is somewhat helpful in identifying the exact type of defect, as shown in the figure. Most
RTS traps tend to have a characteristic energy near half the bandgap (0.55 eV for
silicon) because most defects are Schockley-Read-Hall (SRH) defects that dominate the carrier lifetime in silicon.

88

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Long-time constant SRH defects are challenging to screen while testing the chip,
requiring extensive testing and burn-in at different temperatures, adding significant
cost. The defects described here can easily escape testing, reaching the customer in
the field and degrading quality and reliability. Customer-specific processes, such as
mounting chips on boards, expose the chip to a low-temperature thermal cycle,
which can uncover latent defects such as RTS noise sites. As shipped from the factory, a good chip can become defective through customer processes, requiring some
mitigation to replace or recover the chip. For example, DRAM datasheets contain
methods for built-in self-testing (BIST) and repair techniques, replacing defective
cells with redundant cells (Park et al., 2021). This allows for repair after the DRAM
is mounted to a circuit board.

5.8.3 Ring Oscillator Degradation
Ring oscillators consisting of an odd-numbered series of inverters connected serially are used to provide a relatively simple circuit to measure the performance of
CMOS technology, providing relevant metrics for actual circuit operation. As
expected, ring oscillators can degrade with process damage, given that many transistor parameters are affected by process damage. Therefore, the complex circuits in
a CMOS chip can be expected to have degradation similar to that of ring oscillators.
Figure 5.36 shows an example of ring oscillator degradation with gate etch processing and hot electron damage (Li et al., 1995). Both hot-electron stress and plasma

Fig. 5.36 Probability plot showing the plasma damage degradation in ring oscillator performance
after hot electron stress with differences in gate etch

5.9 Other Signatures

89

damage to the same transistors are needed to observe the damage shown. This plot
is an excellent example of latent damage that manifests degradation after reliability
testing.

5.9 Other Signatures
5.9.1 Contact Resistance Degradation
Contact etch processes remove a dielectric, creating a hole for a metal contact to
junctions and ohmic contacts in the substrate. The etch must remove the dielectric
and stop on the surface of the wafer without significant damage to the substrate,
salicide, etc. Contact etches are high-energy processes forming a vertical anisotropic contact hole. The energetic etch can damage the bottom of the contact hole,
creating a noncrystalline damaged region or an interfacial layer that can degrade the
contact resistance or cause nonohmic behavior. CMOS processes are heavily optimized to clean and remove damaged layers at the bottom of the contact to minimize
the electrical resistance. High-energy ions from the etch plasma can be implanted
into the substrate beneath the contact. Often, a dielectric layer is placed over the silicon substrate to allow the contact etch to stop or partially stop in the film, allowing
a transition to a less damaging etch. Figure 5.37 compares three different contact
etch process options, showing an extreme variation in contact diode electrical characteristics (Yang, 1998).

5.9.2 Degradation in MOS
Capacitance-Voltage Characteristics
The capacitance-voltage (C-V) characteristics of a MOS capacitor provide essential
information about a CMOS process. Also, as MOS capacitors are used as a circuit
element, it is crucial that the C-V characteristics are well-controlled and match the
design models. Plasma and process damage can significantly modify a MOS capacitor’s characteristics. As a worst-case example, Fig. 5.38 shows C-V plots of the
native oxide dielectric after direct exposure to an argon plasma (Nakakubo, 2015).
The undamaged reference C-V is abnormal due to the low-quality and thin native
oxide. Different argon plasma treatments change the C-V characteristics toward a
more normal behavior as the native oxide becomes thicker than the reference. In less
severe cases, plasma damage can shift the threshold voltage due to fixed charge,
change the shape due to interface states, etc.

90

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.37 Contact diode I-V characteristics of a contact formed with three different etch processes. (a) A tetraethylorthosilicate (TEOS) glass is used as a bottom layer above the substrate
etched with a standard low power contact etch process. (b) A nitride layer was used as the bottom
layer etched with the same low power etch as (a). (c) The same etch process as (1) with an added
damage removal soft etch

5.9.3 Impact of the Gate Dielectric on Process Damage
The gate dielectric is the material most commonly damaged by process damage.
The entire wafer surface is covered with transistors containing gate dielectric, creating many structures susceptible to process damage. In addition, the dielectric properties can determine how sensitive the dielectric is to process damage. Figure 5.39
shows an example gate leakage plot of process damage as a function of the gate
oxide thickness for different damaging process steps (Krishnan et al., 1998). Note
that the worst-case damage occurs at around 3–4 nm dielectric thickness. The reference does not explain why the metal etch experiment in Fig. 5.39 increases monotonically instead of showing a peak at 3–4 nm.
The gate dielectric thickness dependence is a complicated mixture of electrical
and physical parameters and may differ for different CMOS technologies and processes. Some insights can be gained by performing analytical or technology
computer-­aided design of the antenna damage circuit. Figure 5.40 shows a plot of
the peak electrical field during plasma damage for a range of gate oxide thicknesses
and antenna ratios, indicating the peak plasma damage occurs in the 35–50 Å range
(Weng, 2007).
The gate dielectric thickness can determine the severity of process damage. Thin
dielectrics less than about 3 nm leak more than thicker dielectrics due to direct

5.9 Other Signatures

91

Fig. 5.38 Capacitance-voltage plots of a mos capacitor damaged by the exposure of a silicon
surface exposed to an argon plasma with three different plasma conditions, power, and a reference.
The C-V characteristics are measured on the surface native oxide with a mercury probe. The abbreviations are as follows: ICP inductively coupled plasma, CCP capacitively coupled plasma

Fig. 5.39 Process damage gate dielectric leakage plots showing the 90% or 95% leakage current

Fig. 5.40 Calculated peak electric field as a function of gate dielectric thickness and antenna ratio

tunneling conduction, making them more resistant to damage due to more charge
being drained from the gate during the damage event. Thicker dielectrics conduct
less because the thicker dielectric prevents direct tunneling. Thicker dielectrics conduct by Fowler-Nordheim (F-N) tunneling and leak less than thin dielectrics, making them more susceptible to damage. Figure 5.41 shows the gate dielectric leakage
for different dielectric thicknesses. Direct tunneling causes thin dielectrics to leak at
low voltages, which improves process damage by draining the charge that accumulates on antennas, reducing the severity of process damage. Thin dielectrics may
perform better because the charge to break down is higher as electrons can pass
through the dielectric with a low probability of damage. Thicker dielectrics have
less leakage and more propensity for damage by carrier conduction. The worst
dielectric thickness for plasma damage may be for an intermediate thickness, as
shown in Fig. 5.41 (Ranuárez et al., 2006). Thicker dielectrics also trap more tunneling charge carriers, resulting in increased parametric shifts.
In addition to the different tunneling mechanisms shown in Fig. 5.41, slight variations in the gate dielectric thickness in the different tunneling regions can significantly impact the tunneling. Figure 5.42 shows a similar plot with smaller increments
in gate dielectric leakage, clearly distinguishing between the direct tunneling and
Fowler-Nordheim tunneling regions (Strong et al., 2009).
Process damage is a strong function of the dielectric thickness, as shown in
Fig. 5.39. Slight changes in the dielectric thickness of a few angstroms can shift the

gate leakage orders of magnitude, easily changing the delicate balance between the
charge flowing onto the antenna from the processing conditions and charge flowing
off the antenna through dielectric leakage. A modification of this balance can create
a significant change in the process damage. Figure 5.43 shows the gate dielectric
leakage plotted against the voltage and thickness (Strong et al., 2009). A change in
the thickness of 1 Å can change the dielectric leakage by an order of magnitude. The
process control capability for a gate dielectric is usually a few angstroms, requiring
process damage characterization across the possible thickness ranges in
manufacturing.
In addition to gate leakage, other process damage-related parametric shifts are
strong functions of the dielectric thickness. The severity of damage varies with the
dielectric thickness, ranging from no shift to a significant shift across a range of gate
dielectric thicknesses. Figure 5.44 shows an example of a Vt shift across a wafer due
to metal etch damage (Park & Hu, 1998). The thicker dielectrics show a significant
shift, while the shift for thin dielectrics is negligible. Thicker dielectrics have a
higher propensity for charge trapping because a carrier transiting a thicker dielectric
has a higher probability of damaging the dielectric, creating a defect, and trapping

5.9 Other Signatures

95

Fig. 5.44 Plot of a NMOS threshold voltage shift across a wafer for several different dielectric
thicknesses damaged by a metal etch of antenna. The antenna area is 640 × 640 μm2, and the transistor size is 10 × 1.5 μm2

a carrier. Also, a charge trapped in a thicker dielectric has a lower probability of
escaping the trap due to a longer tunneling distance. The stress-induced leakage
current (SILC) is reduced in thick dielectrics as the probability of trap-to-trap tunneling across the thick dielectric is less likely. Conversely, thinner dielectrics have a
low propensity for trapping, having a low probability of encountering an atom in the
material and a high probability of a carrier escaping the trap through tunneling.

5.9.4 Isolation Edge Leakage
The edge of the channel where the edge of the active transistor meets the shallow
trench isolation (STI) region can be susceptible to process damage. Process damage
to this region can create a parasitic channel parallel to the active transistor channel.
The parasitic channel can create a leakage path, significantly deviating from the
designed transistor behavior, impacting chip performance and leakage. Figure 5.45
shows a sketch of a transistor with the field edge leakage path highlighted (Lee &
Lee, 2013). The leakage path occurs where the channel meets the STI edge.
The susceptibility is caused by the lack of gate control in this region and the difficulty in doping the edge. Fixed charge or interface states created in the parasitic
region by process damage can lower the threshold of the parasitic transistor forming
the leakage path. Fixed charge can cause a much larger threshold voltage shift in the

96

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.45 Sketch showing a CMOS transistor with a field or STI edge leakage path. (a) Planar
leakage path. (b) Finfet leakage path

Fig. 5.46 Sketch showing enhanced electric field enhancement at the STI-channel edge where the
top of the shallow trench isolation is recessed below the silicon surface, enhancing the electric field

parasitic leakage region because of the thick isolation dielectric compared to the
active CMOS fet. The threshold shift Vt as a function of the fixed oxide charge Qf is
given by Eq. (5.5), where tox is the dielectric thickness, and ε is the dielectric permittivity. This relationship indicates that the thick field edge region with a large tox has
the propensity to leak with a smaller amount of fixed charge than the active transistors. Thus, it is not unusual for the field edge to fail first for smaller amounts of
process damage.

Q f tox



(5.5)

The geometry of the field edge affects the electrical field lines. The electric field
enhancement occurs as a function of the radius of curvature at the corners, as
described in Sect. 5.4.4. Figure 5.46 shows a sketch showing the enhanced field
lines at the edge and corners of the isolation, making leakage worse (McLain et al.,
2017). The STI dielectric in the sketch is recessed below the silicon surface, enhancing the corner electric field. The shallow trench isolation in a CMOS process is
optimized to lower the corner electric field.
The parasitic transistor at the isolation field edge normally never conducts over
the normal operating voltage range of the active CMOS transistors. However,

5.9 Other Signatures

97

Fig. 5.47 Example plot of a sub-threshold kink showing the NMOS field (STI) edge parasitic
transistor turning on at a different gate voltage than the mosfet. The undamaged parasitic STI
transistor leakage on the right shifts to the left after plasma damage (red lines). The blue lines
shows the I-V active NMOS transistor I-V curve before and after damage. The black curve shows
the combined active NMOS FET and the damaged parasitic isolation transistor current

process damage can shift the parasitic device’s operation into the transistor’s operating range, creating two devices in parallel and causing havoc on the chip operation.
The parasitic transistor current-voltage characteristics can be observed in the characteristics of the active transistors, as shown in Fig. 5.47 (Schwank et al., 2008;
Teng et al., 2014). Typically, the threshold voltage of the undamaged parasitic isolation transistor is far higher than that of the CMOS transistors, as shown on the right
side of the plot, and never affects the I-V characteristics of the active CMOS devices.
As the isolation edge is damaged, the threshold voltage of the parasitic transistor
drops. As a result, it can conduct at the same voltages as the CMOS transistor,
severely distorting the I-V characteristics. Both conduction paths can be observed in
the combined I-V curve. The ordinary transistor NMOS I-V characteristic shifts to
the left with plasma damage. The damaged parasitic transistor is shown on the left
side of the figure with a lower Vt and low current due to the limited width of the STI
edge parasitic. The total transistor current is the sum of the two transistors, typically
causing catastrophic failure of the CMOS chip. The NMOS or PMOS isolation may
fail depending on the polarity of the charge, with NMOS failing with positive charge
and PMOS failing with negative charge. The parametric properties, such as diode
characteristics, etc., can also be modified by trapped charge.
Modifications to the gate layout to reduce field isolation failure are possible in
some CMOS processes. The channel length at the edge of the shallow trench can be
extended, as shown in Fig. 5.48a (Esqueda et al., 2005). This dog-bone layout can
provide experimental evidence of enhanced leakage at the field edge due to process

98

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.48 Dog-bone transistor layout and electrical simulation showing symptoms of leakage
along the shallow trench isolation. (a) Top down layout showing extended gate length along the
shallow trench isolation to vary STI edge leakage. The center gate length is 0.18 μm. The sketch is
not to scale (b) simulated gate leakage as a function as dog-bone length showing reduced leakage
with an increased dog-bone length

damage. Extending the channel length at the STI edge can reduce damage-induced
leakage. Figure 5.48b shows a simulation of the off-state leakage as a function of
dog-bone length for a fixed amount of STI edge charge density. This technique is
useful for longer-channel CMOS devices such as planar devices and may not be
viable for finfet devices. Other layout variations shown in Chap. 11, such as donut
devices, can form a transistor, eliminating the STI edge leakage and providing proof
of STI leakage (Lacoe, 2008). These options are likely impractical in advanced
CMOS technologies.
The isolation process can impact the susceptibility to isolation failure. For example, a thin nitride liner is sometimes used in shallow trench isolation (STI) to protect
the silicon during oxidation. The nitride liner can trap electrons or holes, changing
the electrical behavior along the vertical regions near the surface. Figure 5.49 shows
an STI cross-section sketch where the holes can become trapped, affecting the leakage in the field edge regions in red (Teng et al., 2014). The trapped holes lower the
threshold voltage locally at the shallow trench isolation (STI) edge, creating a leakage path at the boundary of the STI edge and the gate dielectric.

5.9.5 High-k Dielectric Dependence
CMOS technology transitioned to high-k dielectrics, moving away from silicon
dioxide and silicon nitride-based dielectrics to reduce dielectric leakage and enable
continued scaling. High-k dielectrics behave differently under process damage conditions. Significant differences in the band structure, such as reduced bandgap and
band offsets, may cause increased dielectric leakage under process damage

5.9 Other Signatures

99

Fig. 5.49 Sketch showing hole trapping in the nitride liner in shallow trench isolation

Fig. 5.50 Dielectric breakdown field versus dielectric constant

conditions, creating the propensity for increased or reduced damage due to draining
the damaging charge. High-k dielectrics tend to have lower bandgaps and offsets
when compared to the traditional dielectric SiO2 (McPherson et al., 2002).
Differences in bond strength change how resistant high-k dielectrics are to damage.
High-k dielectrics can be fundamentally weaker than silicon dioxide and silicon
nitride. Figure 5.50 shows the breakdown field plotted against the dielectric

100

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

constant, indicating that higher dielectric constant materials with weaker breakdown fields may be more susceptible to process damage (McPherson et al., 2003).
The ionic bonds in the high-k dielectric shift much farther than SiO2 under the presence of an electric field, making the bonds easier to break. This is a fundamental
property of high-k dielectrics.
High-k dielectrics, typically consisting of transition-metal oxides, are vastly differ from traditional SiO2-based dielectrics, with a large spectrum of process options.
Many fundamental material properties are quite different for high-k dielectrics,
including bandgap, band offsets, bond strength, etc. This spectrum of differences
can cause high-k dielectrics to occasionally be significantly better than SiO2. For
example, the trapping characteristics can cause the dielectric to be more resilient to
stress. Figure 5.51 compares constant current stress measurements for a high-k and
silicon dioxide dielectric with the same electrical thickness (Kamei et al., 2014).
The high-k results without process damage survive approximately an order of magnitude longer than the SiO2 dielectric, while the results are similar to SiO2 with
plasma damage. The difference in the lifetime is attributed to trapping characteristic
differences in this experiment.

Fig. 5.51 Probability plot comparison of SiO2 and high-k dielectric constant current (QBD) stress
failure times with and without plasma damage

5.9 Other Signatures

101

5.9.6 Degradation of Radiation Hardness
The radiation hardness of CMOS chips is vital for space and military applications,
as well as for commercial applications. The electrical signatures of process damage
and radiation damage can be remarkably similar, if not indistinguishable. Plasma
damage can significantly degrade the radiation hardness of CMOS transistors.
Figure 5.52 shows an example of radiation hardness degradation by plasma damage
where the transistors are exposed to X-rays after process damage (Yue & Sinha,
1998). The time-zero differences (before radiation exposure) in Vt and subthreshold
slope with and without a gate antenna are minor. After exposure to X-ray radiation,
the latent damage from process damage can be seen, increasing the shifts in the
transistor characteristics.

5.9.7 Increased Low-Radiation Dose Rate Sensitivity
There is a common phenomenon in radiation hardness testing called enhanced low
dose rate sensitivity (ELDRS), where radiating a device with a slower amount of
radiation per second actually causes more radiation damage (Fleetwood et al., 2024;
Tsetseris et al., 2005). This phenomenon may interact with plasma damage, changing the behavior and possibly resulting in radiation hardness degradation. The
enhancement can be factors of 5 or more. The cause of this phenomenon was attributed to increased H2 molecule formation at high dose rates, which affected

Fig. 5.52 Example of degradation of radiation hardness degradation by process damage demonstrated by comparing a gate antenna and no antenna before and after X-ray exposure. The plot
shows threshold voltage shift and subthreshold slope degradation (ΔS)

102

5

Electrical Signatures of Process Damage

Fig. 5.53 Plot showing enhanced low dose rate sensitivity (ELDRS) of interface state generation
for a constant total dose radiation exposure (10 Kev X-rays) at different dose rates

hydrogen kinetics as the higher dose rates saturated the dielectric with hydrogen. As
an example, Fig. 5.53 shows the interface state density as a function of the dose rate
for a capacitor irradiated to 200 krad (SiO2). The interface state density is higher as
the dose rate decreases (Fleetwood, 2013). Process damage can potentially degrade
ELDRS (Cellere et al., 2002) and the radiation hardness of CMOS chips.

5.9.8 Degradation of Electrostatic Discharge Circuits
Electrostatic discharge (ESD) circuits protect the chip’s bond pads from electrostatic discharge during chip and package handling or during the product life. These
circuits are designed to meet specific discharge models, such as the human body
model or machine models (Liou, 2016). Although these circuits are not designed to
protect against plasma damage, they serve this function. Plasma damage conditions
operate far outside the designed conditions for the common ESD models. Plasma
damage can degrade or damage the ESD circuits if the plasma damage conditions
cause damage in electrical operation regions where the ESD circuits fail.

5.9 Other Signatures

103

5.9.9 Diode Leakage
Diode leakage can be degraded by plasma and process damage through many mechanisms. The most common mechanisms are listed in Table 5.2. Degradation can
occur through metal-related mechanisms such as contamination in the junction and
metal filaments or other features spiking (shorting) the junction, which may occur
in salicide and other silicon surface metallization processes. The silicon surface can
be damaged through kinetic ion damage, removal of the surface, and contamination
by plasma components. Charge injected through plasma damage into the dielectrics
surrounding the diode can change the electrical fields, affecting the junction diode
behavior. Diodes are stressed through plasma damage and can be physically
degraded by excessive currents, resulting in thermal damage. Ion implantation can
create several types of defects, including defects caused by local amorphization of
the substrate and slip and dislocations during post-implant crystal regrowth (see
Chap. 9).
The investigation of diode leakage usually involves determining if the leakage is
area or STI edge-dependent, which can reveal if the defect is located at the STI
edge. If the leakage tracks the area of the diode, then the leakage is presumed to be
uniform. If the leakage does not track the area of the diode, then the leakage is presumed to be a point defect, such as a spiked junction.
For example, Fig. 5.54 shows a plot of diode reverse current leakage as a function of polysilicon etch time on the surface of a wafer (Miyawaki et al., 1991). The
reverse bias current leakage increases with increasing etch time until the surface is
exposed. The damage slope increases at this point, showing a higher damage rate
per unit time as the silicon surface is directly exposed to the plasma. The problem
was solved by reducing the plasma ion energy to minimize the damage.

Table 5.2 Common process and plasma-damage-induced mechanisms for diode leakage
Leakage mechanism
Metal contamination
Junction spiking by metallization
Surface damage
Excessive silicon loss
Trapped charge in surrounding dielectrics
Knocked on contaminants
Excessive electrical stress during damage
End-of-range ion implantation defects
Slip and dislocations after high-dose
implantation

Chapter 6

Latent Damage and Reliability
Degradation

6.1 Introduction
Process damage can cause latent damage in CMOS chips where the chip’s long-­
term reliability is degraded, while time-zero degradation is minimal or undetectable. Latent damage can be exceedingly difficult or impossible to detect at the end
of the process flow, where the chips are tested and screened before shipping to the
customer. Latent damage can be a severe field reliability hazard if it escapes into the
field. Process damage can be detected electrically by monitoring parametric and
product-level performance after the completion of processing. If the spectrum of
parametric testing described in Chap. 5 does not detect plasma damage, latent damage may exist, creating a reliability hazard. Common latent damage reliability problems are described in the next section.
Figure 6.1 shows an example of latent damage (Kim et al., 1999). There is no
correlation between the transistor parameters and the antenna ratios at time zero,
with all antenna sizes overlayed on the same line as the reference on the left side of
the plot. However, after a dielectric reliability stress test or revealing stress, the
parameters shift as a function of the antenna ratio. This result indicates that the gate
dielectric and interface have been degraded with increased traps and other defects.
This defect can cause severe reliability problems as the chip performance shifts with
time. Defects and traps created during plasma damage can create electrically neutral
traps that are not populated with trapped carriers. These electrically neutral traps are
not detectable with routine time-zero testing. Additional testing with carefully
designed methods is needed to detect latent damage.
Figure 6.2 shows an example of hot electron lifetime degradation caused by a
plasma-enhanced intermetal dielectric deposition process (Cheung, 1995). The
dielectric deposition damaged the gate dielectric, creating oxide defects through
trapping, as described in Chap. 5. The initial trapping rate is measured from the
slope of the curve obtained by forcing a current in a capacitor and measuring the
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
