hot-electron lifetime.

6.2 Damage Recovery
Following a process damage step, process steps later in the manufacturing flow may
partially recover the damage, healing the damaged dielectrics by detrapping trapped
charge, rebonding broken bonds, etc. Typically, thermal process steps can provide
enough thermal energy to rebond broken bonds by thermal excitation of the atoms
in the material. This process is called thermal annealing. Alloying processes treat
the wafer in a hydrogen (or deuterium) ambient (see Chap. 14). An alloy step repairs
damage by hydrogen bonding to dielectric defects, neutralizing charge, and replacing broken bonds. Annealing in a non-hydrogen environment such as N2 or Ar can
recover damage primarily through thermal energy without the reactive hydrogen.
Healing steps can partially recover parametric shifts caused by process damage,
reduce latent damage, and improve reliability degradation. Healing is a process that
makes latent damage challenging to detect by recovering observable parametric
shifts, leaving difficult to detect hidden damage.
Thermal annealing results improve with increasing time and temperature.
Figure 6.3 shows the partial process damage recovery of interface state defects

Fig. 6.3 Interface state density recovery after voltage stress with an anneal. (a) 400 °C in H2/N2
(b) 800 °C in N2. (c) 900 °C in N2. Polysilicon capacitors with an 8.5 nm SiO2 dielectric are
stressed at 9 V for 0, 2, 5, and 30 s. After the stress, the capacitors are annealed at 400 °C 20 min
in N2/H2, 800 °C 20 min in N2, and 900 °C in N2. After the anneal, the capacitors are again stressed
at 9 V, with the interface state density measured by high frequency and quasistatic capacitancevoltage measurements at various times

112

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

caused by a DC gate dielectric stress that simulates process damage (King & Hu,
1994). The experiment consists of a gate stress, followed by different thermal
anneals to partially repair the damage and hide the degradation. Next, a second
electric stress is used to determine whether the thermal anneal healed or hid the
damage. The unstressed control is the curve labeled 0 s. The stressed curves deviate
from the unstressed curve, generating increased interface states. The high-­
temperature anneal (900 °C) experiments show all the stressed groups merging to
the unstressed curve, indicating that the damage was mostly healed with the high-­
temperature anneal. The DC stress is followed by thermal annealing and a second
DC dielectric stress simulating a reliability stress measurement. The second stress
causes an increasing interface state density that saturates at different levels depending on the first stress step time. The latent damage from the first step can be observed
with the second stress. The latent damage is not observable in the time-zero interface state density. Annealing at increasing temperatures is more effective at eliminating the latent damage. The damage appears to be recovered entirely with an
anneal at 900 °C, while residual latent damage remains at lower temperatures.
However, the damage remains after the 900 °C anneal but is not detected in this
measurement.
Partial recovery occurs at lower temperatures. Figure 6.4 shows the shift in the
gate voltage for a constant current stress on a gate dielectric simulating dielectric
damage (King & Hu, 1994). The gate voltage increases to maintain a constant

Fig. 6.4 Gate voltage shift as a function of electrical constant voltage stress. An anneal at different
temperatures is inserted at about 81 s and the stress restarted after the anneal. Note that the anneal
slightly improves the trapping

6.2 Damage Recovery

113

current during the stress as charge is trapped opposing the voltage. Midway through
the stress, the wafers are annealed at various temperatures. The gate voltage shift
decreases after the thermal annealing, indicating that charge was removed or compensated for during the anneal. Temperatures from 400 to 900 °C show this effect,
partially healing the simulated process damage. The 900 °C anneal appears to
mostly remove the damage, with lower temperatures showing partial recovery.
Recovery can occur slowly at temperatures less than 400 °C, even at room temperature or below over hours, days, or weeks.
Recovering CMOS device damage is partially effective in alleviating device
damage, but the residual degradation remains, resulting in significantly inferior
devices compared to undamaged devices. In addition, the compromised devices
usually trap charge faster and degrade faster when subjected to additional damage.
After the 900 °C anneal, it appears that the damage is partially annealed, but it takes
less time to obtain the same gate voltage shift compared to the initial stress results.
This finding indicates that even the 900 °C anneal is insufficient to heal the damage
completely.
Another example of plasma damage recovery is shown in Fig. 6.5, where the silicon surface is exposed to a polysilicon etch that damages the silicon surface of a
diode (Miyawaki et al., 1991). The plasma degraded the reverse diode leakage, as
shown in Chap. 5. Different anneals were used to recover the damage partially. A
1000 °C 30-min high-temperature anneal recovers the plasma damage. Also, a
400 °C 30-min hydrogen alloy recovers the damage.
Dark current noise in a CMOS imager is the background noise observed when a
pixel is in the dark (Wang, 2008), as described in Chap. 8. Recovery of noise from
process damage can also occur in CMOS imagers. Figure 6.6 shows a plot of damage recovery of the dark current in a CCD imager after exposure to deep UV light
simulating a deep UV scanner (Li et al., 2004). The dark current partial recovery is

Fig. 6.5 Recovery of reverse biased diode leakage with annealing after exposure to a polysilicon
plasma etch at two different pressures at 100 W over the exposed silicon substrate

114

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

Fig. 6.6 Damage recovery in dark current to a charge-coupled device (CCD) image sensor
exposed to a UV laser stored at room temperature

observed over several weeks as defects disappear at a low rate from the room-­
temperature thermal energy. The room-temperature thermal energy causes the repair
of broken bonds, detrapping of trapped carriers, etc.
Each defect type has different annealing characteristics. For example, Fig. 6.7
shows a plot of defect annealing versus approximate temperature for vacancy and
dopant-related defects based on measurements and first-order modeling (Hull,
1999, p. 646). Each defect anneals at different temperatures, transitioning from a
specific concentration to a negligible concentration. This plot shows the annealing
temperature for a 15–30 min anneal. Defects can be annealed at lower temperatures
than the points shown in the plot for longer times (hours and days). Defects that
anneal near the maximum temperatures where CMOS chips operate or are tested
can result in a change in circuit behavior with time. The defects shown to anneal in
the 100–300° region may be a threat to the stability of CMOS chips.

6.3 Reliability Degradation by Process Damage
6.3.1 Introduction
The presence of antennas on a CMOS chip may greatly increase plasma and process
damage, causing havoc with yield, reliability, and the economic success of a semiconductor company. For example, Fig. 6.8 shows the chip burn-in failure rate

6.3 Reliability Degradation by Process Damage

115

Fig. 6.7 Plot showing defect annealing as a function of temperature for common vacancy, interstitial, and impurity interaction related silicon defects. The annealing time was 15–30 min. The
vacancy related defects are denoted with a V. The dopants are denoted by their atomic abbreviation

plotted against the yield of a parametric antenna test structure across different fabs
(Mason et al., 2000). The plot strongly correlates increased burn-in failures with
low antenna structure yield. The root cause of this problem was determined to be
caused by a variation in the plasma power, temperature, and chamber impedance for
a radio-frequency argon sputter process used as a back-sputtering via cleaning process. Additionally, product yield also correlated with the antenna test structure yield.
Process damage can degrade chip reliability, worsening many common CMOS
reliability mechanisms. Many common CMOS potential failure mechanisms can be
modulated by process damage. A partial list of reliability failure modes or degradation mechanisms caused or worsened by process damage is shown in Table 6.1. The
degradation of time-zero parameters is often followed by reliability degradation
related to the initial damage. For example, time-zero degradation in oxide parameters, such as Vt shifts, SILC, etc., correlates with degraded dielectric reliability.

116

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

Fig. 6.8 Plot showing the burn-in failure rate (arbitrary units) plotted against the yield of a parametric test antenna structure for multiple fabrication facilities

6.3.2 CMOS Transistor Failure Modes
CMOS transistors fail through multiple mechanisms, all of which can be degraded
and accelerated by process damage. Figure 6.9 shows transistor sketches with the
different operating conditions for each common failure mode. p-channel and
n-channel transistors can behave quite differently for different stress conditions due
to differences in mobility, etc. All these failure modes interact with hydrogen, as
described in Chap. 14.
Hot carriers are generated by high electric fields during transistor operation,
causing the carriers to have sufficient energy to damage the interface or become
trapped in the dielectrics shifting the parameters and causing device failure. In addition, hot carriers can be generated in the transistor channel during on-state stress,
particularly when biased in the saturation region of operation near the peak substrate current. The two primary modes of channel hot carrier degradation, as shown
in Fig. 6.9a, b, are channel hot carrier (CHE) and channel-initiated secondary electron (CHISEL) modes. For CHE stress, both electrons and holes can be trapped
depending on the bias, with a high gate voltage favoring electron injection and a low
gate voltage favoring hole injection in NMOS transistors. CHISEL is a mode caused
by substrate bias accelerating carriers, creating secondary carriers that can be
injected into the dielectrics (Takeda, 1995).
High-energy carriers can be generated during off-state stress (OSS) with a high
drain bias. This mechanism is also called nonconducting stress (NCS) or

hot-­electron-­induced punch-through (HEIP). The mechanism is shown in Fig. 6.9c.
The high drain bias can generate carriers in the drain depletion region that are accelerated into the dielectric, becoming trapped. HEIP is caused when carriers are
trapped in the channel, causing a decreased electrical channel length and reduced
punch-through resistance. The carriers can also become trapped at the isolation
edge, creating a parasitic leakage path.
Additionally, uniform gate stress can also cause parametric shifts, as shown in
Fig. 6.9d, e. This stress is called negative bias instability (NBTI) or positive bias
instability (PBTI). The physics of NBTI and PBTI is complicated, including traps,
dissociated bonds with a hydrogen interaction, etc. (Mahapatra, 2016).
Finally, the time-dependent dielectric breakdown (TDDB) described in Chap. 5
and shown in Fig. 6.9f is a failure mechanism.

conditions. The voltage conditions are reversed for a PMOS transistor. (a) Channel hot carrier
stress (CHE). (b) Channel initiated secondary electron (CHISEL). (c) Off-state stress (OSS), non-­
conducting stress (NCS), hot electron induced punch-through (HEIP). (d) Negative bias temperature instability (NBTI). (e) Positive bias temperature instability (PBTI). (f) Time dependent
dielectric breakdown (TDDB) and charge trapping

6.3.3 Degradation in Dielectric Lifetime
A dielectric may survive the initial process damage event with no noticeable shift in
dielectric leakage and no impact on product yield or function. However, the dielectric must be capable of surviving the required chip lifetime without failure or degradation in function. The process damage may degrade the dielectric’s useful lifetime,
resulting in a reliability hazard for chip failure in the field during its operating

6.3 Reliability Degradation by Process Damage

119

lifetime. This effect is called time-dependent dielectric breakdown (TDDB).
Dielectrics are characterized by test structures to predict the dielectric and product
lifetimes (Strong et al., 2009). Test structures matching the antenna ratio required
by the design rules specified for the CMOS process can be compared to test structures without antennas to determine the degradation due to the antenna effect.
Dielectrics are typically characterized by accelerated voltage, current, and temperature stress beyond normal operating conditions. The lifetimes under accelerated
conditions are extrapolated to the operating conditions to predict the lifetime under
the chip operating conditions. Lifetime-accelerated testing commonly uses two
methods: constant voltage or constant current stress. A constant voltage test forces
a constant voltage while monitoring the current flowing through the dielectric. A
current criterion determines the time of failure when the increasing leakage reaches
a specified point, and the resulting voltage is recorded (VBD test). The second commonly used test forces a constant current on a dielectric test structure while monitoring the voltage. This test is also called charge to breakdown (QBD). A voltage
criterion is established where the measured voltage is monitored over time.
Dielectric failure is defined when the voltage across the dielectric crosses the criterion. Figure 6.10 shows examples of VBD and QBD dielectric breakdown tests
(Strong et al., 2009, pp. 297, 300).
Figure 6.11 shows an example of charge to breakdown and time to breakdown
degradation caused by process damage during a plasma etch process versus the
antenna ratio (gate metal area on isolation divided by the gate metal area on the
active area) for a 7 nm gate dielectric (Kosako et al., 1998).

Fig. 6.10 Example plots of common dielectric lifetime tests. (a) Charge to breakdown (QBD)
with a constant forced current while monitoring the voltage for a 4.2 nm silicon dioxide stressed at
1 μA. The different labels refer to stress induced leakage (SILC), first and second soft breakdown
(SB), followed by hard breakdown (HB). (b) Voltage to breakdown (VBD) with a forced voltage
while measuring a current. The step function increase in current is due to the dielectric breakdown.
The upper curve is the forced voltage at 2.4 V across a 1 nm thick dielectric. The lower curve is a
periodic measurement at 1 V

120

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

Fig. 6.11 (a) Example plot of charge to breakdown versus antenna ratio measured with both gate
and substrate injection. (b) Degradation in time to breakdown with increasing antenna ratio

6.3.4 Hot Carrier Lifetime Degradation
CMOS transistors are designed to resist damage from high-energy hot carriers generated during operation using techniques such as the lightly doped drain. Constant
voltage scaling of the transistor increases the electrical field in the channel caused
by aggressively scaling transistors with limited voltage scaling, increasing the
energy of channel carriers. Process damage can degrade hot carrier resistance, creating potential reliability hazards for CMOS chips operating in the field. Transistors
are tested with accelerated voltages and temperatures (usually lower temperatures
where the channel mobility is higher) to obtain hot carrier data quickly. This data is
extrapolated to normal operating conditions. Process damage degrading hot carrier
lifetime may be mistaken for inadequate transistor design, requiring added process
cost to recover the reliability.
Figure 6.12 shows some examples of hot carrier lifetime degradation caused by
process damage (Rangan et al., 1999; Sachdeva et al., 2006; Tzeng et al., 2002; Vici
et al., 2020). Figure 6.12a shows the degradation of conductance or gain (gm) with
different antenna lengths for two different dielectric thicknesses. Figure 6.12b
shows a plot of drain current reduction as a function of antenna length for two different dielectric thicknesses. The thinner dielectric (2.5 nm) has little or no degradation, while a 4 nm dielectric shows significant degradation. Figure 6.12c shows a
PMOS hot carrier lifetime metric consisting of a function of the substrate current
plotted against the antenna ratio. Figure 6.12d shows the same metric for an NMOS
transistor.

6.3 Reliability Degradation by Process Damage

121

Fig. 6.12 Examples of hot carrier reliability degradation by process damage. (a) NMOS transistor
gain (gm) degradation for two different oxide thicknesses plotted as a function of antenna length
for a gate metal antenna. (b) Change in NMOS drain current with process damage versus antenna
length for a gate metal antenna. (c) A hot carrier reliability metric plotted against antenna ratio for
a PMOS transistor damaged during metal 1 (M1) processing. (d) A hot carrier reliability metric
plotted versus antenna ratio for a NMOS transistor damaged during metal 1 (M1) processing. The
metric τ/(Isub)n is a metric for estimating the transistor lifetime

6.3.5 Negative Bias Temperature Instability
(NBTI) Degradation
Negative bias temperature instability (NBTI) is a reliability failure mechanism
affecting mainly PMOS devices due to boron–hydrogen effects, etc. (Fleetwood
et al., 2009; Strong et al., 2009). A PMOS transistor biased in inversion (Vgs <0)
operated at higher temperatures (30–200 °C) accumulates positively charged defects
and interface states. Process damage also creates the same defects and can accelerate NBTI degradation, shortening the transistor’s lifetime. The NBTI lifetime of a

122

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

transistor can be reduced by orders of magnitude by process damage. Figure 6.13
shows an example of PMOS NBTI lifetime reduction caused by process damage
(Krishnan et al., 2001). NMOS transistors can suffer from a similar reliability problem called positive bias temperature instability (PBTI). Also, see Chap. 8 for an
additional example of NBTI degradation caused by dielectric deposition on top of a
MIM capacitor.

6.4 Metallization Reliability Degradation
6.4.1 Introduction
The chip specification in the datasheet limits the energy delivered to the chip by the
system to prevent damage. Additionally, CMOS chips are designed to carefully
manage power and energy during operation to prevent damage to the chip. For
example, the maximum current density for metallization is limited to avoid electromigration, which destroys the metal lines by slow migration of metal atoms, resulting in an open circuit. Under plasma or process damage conditions, the voltages and
current flowing through the metal interconnect are uncontrolled, possibly exceeding
the capability of metallization. Excessive current can result in time-zero metallization failure or latent degradation in the long-term reliability that can fail in the field
during the lifetime of the chip.

Fig. 6.13 PMOS with a 4 nm gate dielectric transistor NBTI lifetime (arbitrary units) versus the
perimeter ratio for process damage during Al metal 1 processing

6.4 Metallization Reliability Degradation

123

Fig. 6.14 Cross-section sketch showing a via chain with initial damage and plasma damage
caused voiding that degrades abnormally fast under electromigration testing

Furthermore, the intermetal dielectrics may be damaged or degraded, causing
reliability problems. In addition, excessive chip current can accelerate chip corrosion, stress migration, etc., during processing (Tsai et al., 2018a, b). Worst-case chip
locations containing large antennas, etc., tend to fail first for this problem. Low-k
dielectrics are covered separately in Chap. 8. Figure 6.14 shows a cross-section
sketch of a via chain damaged by plasma damage and subjected to electromigration
reliability testing (Tsai et al., 2018a, b). The damaged chain may fail abnormally
early because of the damage, as the electromigration lifetime is partially used up by
high current density during plasma damage. Any voids that grow during the plasma
damage stress increase the metallization resistance. The voids tend to occur at interfaces between metals.

6.4.2 Intermetal Dielectric Degradation
The large amounts of intermetal dielectrics that separate the metal layers in a CMOS
chip are potential sources of chip yield or reliability failure. The intermetal dielectric may be excessively stressed during a process damage event, resulting in damage
very similar to the process damage to gate dielectrics covered in Chap. 5. Figure 6.15
shows a sketch of a CMOS wafer cross section, highlighting potential locations for
dielectric process damage at the gate dielectric and the intermetal dielectric (Su
et al., 2020). Depending on the plasma, electrical, and structural conditions, the
damage may occur at either location or both.
The electric field at the corners in metal features is enhanced due to the radius of
curvature effect, as described in Chap. 5, resulting in a higher electric field compared to a simple parallel plate capacitor. The electric field is increased proportionally to the inverse of the radius of curvature squared. Due to this effect, process
damage can be expected to worsen at the corners of metal lines. Tighter design rules
with constant voltage scaling result in higher fields, increasing the effect.
The stress on intermetal dielectrics and the resultant damage depends on the
metal lines’ electrical connections. Figure 6.16 shows an example with two different connections to the lower metal lines (m1) (Su et al., 2020). The charging event

124

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

Fig. 6.15 CMOS process cross-section sketch showing multiple locations of dielectric damage to
the gate dielectric and the intermetal dielectric

Fig. 6.16 CMOS cross-section sketch showing different configurations for intermetal dielectric
damage between metal 1 (M1) and metal 2 (M2). (a) Metal 1 (M1) is isolated from the substrate
by the np junction. (b) Metal 1 is directly connected to the p-type substrate through a p+ region

from the metal 2 (m2) antenna stresses the dielectric between metal 1 (m1) and
metal 2 (m2). If the metal 1 line is completely floating (no connection to the substrate), reduced damage to the dielectric occurs because the metal 1 line capacitively
couples to metal 2, raising the metal 1 potential. However, if the metal 1 line is connected to a diode, as shown in Fig. 6.16a, the metal 1 line is partially isolated from
the substrate by the reverse-biased junction, resulting in less damage to the intermetal dielectric compared to Fig. 6.16b. Finally, if the metal 1 line is hard-wired to
the substrate, as shown in Fig. 6.16b, the substrate connection serves as a

6.4 Metallization Reliability Degradation

125

Fig. 6.17 SILC damage in intermetal dielectrics. (a) Sketch of an intermetal dielectric test structure consisting of an interdigitated comb of even and odd metal lines. (b) Plot of intermetal dielectric SILC leakage caused by plasma damage showing bimodal leakage

pseudo-­ground, creating the worst-case damage conditions by allowing bidirectional stress with no field-sharing.
In addition to the dielectric breakdown of the intermetal dielectric, process damage can cause stress-induced leakage (SILC) in an identical manner to SILC in gate
dielectrics, as described in Chap. 7. Process damage degrades the leakage properties
of the intermetal dielectric, causing a reliability hazard and potentially affecting
time-zero circuit operation. Figure 6.17a shows an intermetal comb process damage
test structure sketch (Tsai et al., 2018a, b). An antenna (not shown) may be connected to one terminal of the comb. A distribution plot of the SILC leakage caused
by process damage is shown in Fig. 6.17b, where 60% of the finger capacitors leak
after the damage.

6.4.3 Corrosion
Figure 6.18 shows an example of this phenomenon where increased plasma intensity across the wafer accelerates wafer corrosion (Tsai et al., 2018a, b). The plasma
intensity varies across the wafer and is measured by nonvolatile memory cells, creating a plasma damage intensity metric. Figure 6.19 shows an example TEM image
of a voided via connection to a metal line interface (Irrera et al., 2010; Tsai et al.,
2018a, b). The plasma damage electrical stress can accelerate corrosion, stress, and
electromigration degrading reliability (Bothra et al., 1998).
One factor associated with corrosion is the residual byproducts left on the wafer
after processing, particularly after plasma etch. Etch byproducts left on the wafer,
including reactive species such as fluorine or chlorine, can continue etching after the

126

6

Latent Damage and Reliability Degradation

Fig. 6.18 Probability showing metal corrosion correlation with regions of the wafer near the center that have increased plasma damage. Plasma intensity was measured with a nonvolatile memory
test structure. The corrosion was confirmed with physical failure analysis on sites with
increased damage

etch is completed, attacking metal structures such as tungsten vias. In addition,
exposure to moisture in room air can accelerate reactions. Postprocessing strips and
cleans are heavily optimized to eliminate byproducts and corrosion. An in situ strip
in the etch chamber is commonly used to minimize corrosion (Fatkhotdinov et al.,
2002). Across wafer damage variation can be related to across wafer residual
variation.

6.4.4 Electromigration and Stress Migration
Electromigration and stress migration are critical reliability risks for CMOS metallization. Backend of line processes are heavily optimized to ensure electromigration, and stress migration does not cause product failures over the product life.
Process and plasma damage can potentially degrade these reliability risks. For
example, Fig. 6.20 shows a plot of differences in electromigration lifetime with different plasma treatments before low-k dielectric deposition (Wang et al., 2002).

Chapter 7

Atomic-Level Defects and Electrical
Effects

7.1 Introduction
CMOS technology’s relentless scaling and advancement have resulted in ever-­
smaller devices and dimensions. Scaled small devices have become more susceptible to smaller and smaller defects reaching the scale where a single atomic-level
defect can cause device failure. Additionally, a few atomic defects can link electrically, creating potential chip failure mechanisms. The large spectrum of possible
atomic defects is challenging to summarize. This chapter attempts to describe the
atomic defects in advance of a detailed description of the technology-specific electrical behavior caused by these defects (Chap. 8).
Atomic-level defects frequently impact CMOS devices in various degrees,
depending on the type of device and the device dimensions. In addition, some technologies are inherently more susceptible because of the underlying physics of operation, allowing the detection of atomic-level defects. Table 7.1 shows a partial list of
single-atom electrical defects by the technology type. Details on the electrical
behavior are described in Chaps. 5 and 8. Atomic-level defects can be caused by
process damage. These defects may occur at minimal levels of damage and can be
observed before other types of damage occur. Typically, these defects can only be
observed in devices like memory arrays, imager arrays, etc. Large-area devices such
as parametric capacitors usually fail to observe these effects because the atomic-­
level defects are swamped by large-area effects such as tunneling. This section
introduces some of the single defect mechanisms.

Switzerland AG 2025

mechanisms

CCD and imagers, including dark current and white pixels

7.2 SiO2-Silicon Atomic-Level Defects
At the atomic level, the surface of a nearly perfect silicon wafer contains atomic-­
level imperfections that can be electrically active, resulting in the defects described
in this section. An atomic force microscope image of a silicon surface is shown in
Fig. 7.1, highlighting the atomic-level irregularity of the silicon surface (Bowler,
1997). The silicon surface can contain a step in the surface caused by the end of an
atomic layer crystalline plane, as shown in the lower right region of the image.
Missing or irregularly placed atoms in the silicon can be observed in the image and
may cause electrically active defects, dimers, etc. (Werner, 2006). Single-atom
defects and dimers consisting of pairs of electrically linked atoms of the silicon
defects can be observed on the silicon surface. Common silicon defects are sketched
in the figure.
CMOS process technologists carefully optimize the processing conditions to create the best possible silicon-dielectric interface and eliminate as many silicon interface defects as possible. Typical processes optimized include the dielectric
deposition preclean, dielectric deposition, and post-deposition annealing.
Additionally, plasma and process damage must be mitigated.
One of the key advantages of using silicon for integrated circuits is the use of silicon dioxide as a dielectric and its electrical properties in conjunction with silicon,
forming an electrically and chemically stable interface (Huff & Gilmer, 2005). The
discovery of this system was a breakthrough that enabled the exponential growth of
the semiconductor industry for decades. The advantages of this system include electrical and thermal stability, reliability, control of surface electrical defects, dopant
control, junction leakage, etc. These beneficial properties are due to the strong covalent bonding of the SiO2 dielectric, resulting in a higher melting point of 1740 °C,
which is higher than silicon’s (1410 °C). As a result, silicon dioxide is resistant to
damage, requiring high kinetic energy to damage the dielectric. However, much
weaker defects exist in the dielectrics, causing many reliability and electrical
problems.
The Si-SiO2 system has been one of the most heavily studied material systems
for many years, and numerous defect types have been discovered and researched.
Figure 7.2 shows a diagram of common defects in this system (Fleetwood et al.,
2009). The defects are shown roughly positioned where they exist in the system.

7.2 SiO2-Silicon Atomic-Level Defects

133

Fig. 7.1 Scanning tunneling microscope image of a silicon <001> surface shown on the left side.
Sketch of different defect types. Schematic of different defect types shown on the right side, i.e.,
(a) vacancy, (b) di-vacancy, (c) self-interstitial, (d) contaminant interstitial, (e) dopant interstitial,
(f) substitutional impurity, (g) impurity vacancy pair, (h) impurity self-interstitial pair

Fig. 7.2 Diagram showing common silicon dioxide-silicon defects with their rough position in the
MOS system along with high-k defects

Defects deep in the silicon, such as recombination centers and bulk silicon defects,
affect bulk and surface properties, degrading electrical properties such as minority
carrier lifetime and junction leakage. Si-SiO2 surface defects such as fast states and
interface traps affect surface electrical properties such as transistor mobility and
subthreshold leakage. Near-surface defects, called border traps, slow states, and
fixed charges, impact surface electric and dielectric properties. Bulk dielectric oxide
traps such as electron and hole traps can shift critical transistor parameters such as
the threshold voltage. Finally, defects at the top of the SiO2-gate metal interface can
modify electrical properties by pinning the work function and shifting the threshold
voltage.

134

7

Atomic-Level Defects and Electrical Effects

7.3 High-k Dielectric Single-Atom Defects
The dielectric SiO2, with minor variations, has served the semiconductor industry
for decades. High-dielectric (high-k) constant materials have recently replaced silicon dioxide in some applications, such as logic. High-k dielectrics have numerous
advantages, including reduced gate leakage, better electrical control of the MOS
channel, etc. (Wong, 2012).
High-k dielectrics commonly use transition-metal oxides, including HfO, HfSiO,
ZrO, etc., because of their compatibility with silicon and electrical properties. These
types of dielectrics have a strong ionic bond characteristic with the metal cation and
oxide anion bonding, which is much different from the covalent silicon dioxide
bonding. As a result, transition-metal oxides are quite different from silicon dioxide
dielectrics and have different dominant defects affecting the electrical behavior,
failure, and degradation. The dominant defects in transition-metal oxides tend to be
vacancy and interstitial defects. Vacancy defects are defects where an anion or cation atom is missing from the dielectric. Interstitial defects are defects where an extra
atom exists in the dielectric. Both the metal (cation) and oxide (anion) atoms can
occur in vacancy and interstitial defects. In addition, the defects can trap an electron
or hole, creating a charged defect. The defects can also trap two electrons or two
holes, creating a doubly charged defect. Table 7.2 lists these types of defects in
transition-metal oxides. High-k dielectrics can also have the defects shown in
Fig. 7.2, creating a complex spectrum of potential defects (Maier, 2004; Wong,
2012, p. 88).
A crucial factor in high-k dielectrics is their compatibility with silicon. A metal
oxide gate dielectric can interact with the silicon substrate with desirable or undesirable results. The hafnia-related material systems form a stable oxide with silicon,
resulting in a viable CMOS technology. Undesirable effects include intermixing,
causing silicate formation, formation of an unstable oxide, or the reduction of the
dielectric by the silicon. The undesirable effects can result in a nonviable dielectric.
Therefore, the interface between the high-k dielectric and the silicon substrate is
critical to obtain a reliable dielectric with the capabilities for advanced CMOS

in transition-metal oxides

Anion vacancy
Anion on interstitial site
Anion on regular lattice site
Cation vacancy
Cation on interstitial site
Cation on regular lattice site
Charged anion vacancy

Charged interstitial cation

7.3

High-k Dielectric Single-Atom Defects

135

technology. An extremely thin SiO2 layer often exists under the high-k dielectric
layer, serving an essential function at the silicon interface and improving atomic
layer deposition and the electrical quality of the interface. Figure 7.3 shows an
example TEM image of a high-k dielectric on top of silicon dioxide at the silicon
interface (Robertson, 2004).
The presence of the SiO2 dielectric interfacial layer can have electrical effects,
and it is heavily optimized in a CMOS process. Figure 7.4 shows a sketch of a
dielectric-silicon interface showing the impact on the dielectric defects (Fleetwood
et al., 2009, p. 128). The diagram shows that the oxide charge and fixed interface
states occur in two distinct locations and materials, affecting the electrical characteristics of the film and the transistor. The electrical characteristics of the two
regions are typically different.
High-k dielectrics tend to be more nanocrystalline and less amorphous than silicon dioxide, creating the propensity for defects at the grain boundaries that can
cause electrical defects. Figure 7.5 shows a plane-view TEM image showing an
example of a nanocrystalline structure for a hafnia and silicon dioxide mixture
(Robertson, 2004).

7.4 Stress-Induced Leakage Current
Stress-induced leakage current (SILC) is a common problem in MOS dielectrics
after electric stress or process damage, providing an excellent example of atomic-­
level defects (Fleetwood et al., 2009). SILC is the degradation of the insulating
properties of the dielectric caused by atomic-level defects created by charge carriers
flowing through the dielectric during process damage. The charge carriers have
enough kinetic energy to break the bonds in the dielectric, creating traps and interface states. The created defects serve as tunneling sites, allowing charge carriers to
tunnel between defects, creating an abnormally high current between the gate and
substrate. A single atomic-level defect can cause a significant increase in the dielectric current in dielectrics less than 90 Å. Thicker dielectrics can leak through multiple defects that are aligned physically and by energy levels in the band structure.
Figure 7.6 shows a sketch of SILC conduction through a dielectric (Ielmini et al.,
2004; Okuyama et al., 1998). A single or double trap can cause significant current
to flow through the dielectric, affecting sensitive devices.

7.4

Stress-Induced Leakage Current

137

Fig. 7.5 Plan view TEM showing the nanocrystalline structure of the HfO2/SiO2 alloy system

Fig. 7.6 Sketch showing stress induced leakage current (SILC) flowing through multiple trap
defects in a dielectric

138

7

Atomic-Level Defects and Electrical Effects

Multiple conduction mechanisms can occur with SILC (Fleetwood et al., 2009,
p. 500). Transient leakage can occur as a positive trapped charge or trap is annihilated by an electron, as shown in Fig. 7.7a. Additionally, the trapped charge can
contribute to the SILC current as carriers flow into or out of the interface states, as
shown in Fig. 7.7b. The trap-assisted SILC current can tunnel through traps, as
shown in Fig. 7.7c, d. Due to the transient nature of some of the SILC mechanisms,
some of the SILC current is temporary, changing with time.
SILC current is a strong function of the dielectric thickness, as more trapping
sites are needed for thicker dielectrics, requiring traps to be aligned spatially
between the silicon and the gate, allowing carriers to tunnel trap-to-trap across the
dielectric.
Nonvolatile memories are very sensitive to SILC currents because of the very
small numbers of electrons involved in the cell operation, as described in Chap. 8.
Figure 7.8 shows a probability plot of tunnel oxide leakage for a flash memory array
with different dielectric thicknesses after 104 cycles (Ielmini et al., 2002). Single
atomic-level defects can play a significant role in the reliability of sensitive devices
such as flash memories and other devices, limiting the scaling of the tunnel oxides.
Thicker tunnel dielectrics are required to limit SILC conduction by statistically
reducing the impact of tunneling through a few defects. Two bits in the distribution

Fig. 7.7 Band energy sketch of various SILC conduction mechanisms in a dielectric. (a)
Irreproducible component due to annihilating positive trapped charge with an electron. (b)
Transient component due to charging and/or discharging of interface traps. (c) Component, attributed to elastic trap assisted tunneling. (d) Component, attributed to inelastic trap assisted tunneling
(energy loss)

Giant RTS

a flash memory array showing dramatically reduced leakage with increasing dielectric thickness.
Two cells are highlighted, one with the leakage caused by a single atomic level trap and another
with two atomic level traps

were determined to be caused by a single trap and a double trap. The higher leakage
cell was determined to be caused by SILC leakage through two traps, and the lower
level leakage was determined to be caused by leakage through one trap, explaining
the higher leakage in the two-trap bit.

7.5 Giant RTS
Single-atomic defects can have an outsized impact on the electrical behavior of a
CMOS transistor when the defect occurs in an unlucky location in a small device.
Figure 7.9 shows a sketch representing a three-dimensional simulation of an NMOS
transistor, including traps and acceptor dopants in the channel (Bukhori et al., 2010).
For an advanced CMOS transistor, the presence of dopant atoms can locally reduce
the channel current by repelling the channel carriers, resulting in a filamentary conducting path (in blue) around the dopants. Traps in the channel can interact with the
dopant atoms, modulating the current in the filamentary conduction. A single trap
can modulate the remaining filament, creating an anomalously giant random telegraph signal as the trap is filled or emptied.
Figure 7.10 shows a plot of the drain current versus the gate voltage for a flash
memory cell with a giant RTS defect (Fantini et al., 2007). As the gate voltage is

140

7

Atomic-Level Defects and Electrical Effects

Fig. 7.9 Sketch showing filamentary conduction based on a 3D computer simulation of a NMOS
transistor with traps and dopant atoms modulating the current conduction path in blue

Fig. 7.10 Giant RTS in a flash cell. (a) Lot showing giant RTS in a flash memory cell as the control gate is ramped. (b) The current variation as a function of time

ramped, the drain current oscillates between two levels as the trap fills, blocking the
current or empties, removing the current block. The binary current levels are shown
in the figure. The change in drain current is the same order of magnitude as the typical drain current without RTS, which can have a significant impact on the NVM cell
reliability.

Chapter 8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

8.1 Introduction
Some types of CMOS technology are more sensitive to process and plasma damage.
As CMOS devices are scaled, the transistors become more sensitive to defects.
Planar nonvolatile memory cells had the smallest CMOS dimensions for a while,
making them very susceptible to atomic-level defects. Additionally, flash memory
cells typically store more than one bit per cell, requiring finer discernment of the
threshold voltage (or current), substantially adding to the sensitivity.
Some CMOS devices operate with very few charge carriers, which decrease with
each generation of scaling. These devices include nonvolatile memories, DRAMs,
and CMOS imagers. Planar nonvolatile memories were scaled until just a few electrons were used for each bit (Prall, 2007). DRAMs have been scaled down to the
fundamental limits of the storage capacitor, reaching around less than 10,000 electrons per bit. CMOS imagers have been optimized to detect a few photons, sometimes even a single photon, making them very sensitive to single-atom defects.
These technologies tend to be the most sensitive to low levels of defects.
This chapter includes other types of devices sensitive to process damage. Metal-­
insulator-­metal capacitors are sensitive to damage because they use thin dielectrics
in the plasma discharge path. Low-k dielectric films are sensitive because the materials used are easily damaged.
Advanced logic devices, such as silicon-on-insulators (SOIs) and finfets, are sensitive to damage due to their very small dimensions, which can be damaged with
low energy levels.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_8

144

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells
Nonvolatile memory (NVM) cells are particularly susceptible to process damage as
they are designed to collect charge to store the memory state. These transistors are
optimized to collect charge and, therefore, are unintentionally optimized for maximum process damage. NVM transistors contain a region to trap charge. For a floating gate cell, a small polysilicon gate collects the charge. A silicon oxide nitride
oxide silicon (SONOS) cell and related technologies contain a charge-trapping
dielectric layer. The charge flowing due to process damage flows through the NVM
cell, causing damage to the dielectrics and resulting in degraded function and reliability problems.
NVM cells are constructed with the trapping layer sandwiched between a tunneling dielectric and a blocking dielectric with a gate on one side and a semiconductor
on the other, as shown in Fig. 8.1. The NVM cell’s gate, source, or drain collects
charge during process damage situations, increasing the voltage on the terminals
until a current starts to flow through the transistor. For example, the current can flow
through the blocking dielectric into the trapping layer and then through the tunnel
oxide into the semiconductor. Alternately, the current could flow in the opposite
direction. The current flowing through the cell causes dielectric damage to the tunnel and blocking dielectrics in a similar manner as described in the previous chapters. An NVM cell manifests process damage in additional ways due to the functional
requirements of the memories beyond the mechanisms observed in a simple CMOS
transistor.
A sketch of an NVM cell is shown in Fig. 8.1. During plasma damage, charge
collects in the trapping layer, and the transistor Vt shifts proportionally to the charge
in the trapping layer. The trapping layer gains or loses charge through tunneling
driven by the electric field until the current through the blocking oxide is equal to
the current through the tunnel oxide. At this point, the Vt stops shifting unless the
conditions change. The charge in the trapping layer may remain in the trapping
layer during subsequent processing. Some process steps may cause the charge to
discharge from the trapping layer, shifting the cell’s threshold voltage toward its

Fig. 8.1 Sketch showing a nonvolatile memory cell

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

145

native uncharged Vt. The steps that can shift the NVM Vt require imparting enough
energy to the trapped charge to eject it from the trapping layer. High-energy exposure to thermal energy (temperatures greater than 200–400 °C) during processing or
high-­frequency light (UV or X-ray) can provide sufficient energy to detrap the carriers, erasing the cell. This exposure can eliminate the observable Vt shift, but latent
damage may remain.
Figure 8.2 shows a plot of NVM cell behavior where positive and negative voltage stresses (simulating plasma damage) are applied to the gate for 1 min, and the
resulting threshold voltage caused by the stress is plotted on the y-axis (Cheung,
1999). Vt does not shift at low voltages because there is not enough field to inject or
remove the charge from the trapping layer, resulting in zero threshold voltage shifts
for these conditions. At high-stress voltages, Vt shifts positively or negatively
depending on the polarity of the stress. Process damage similarly shifts Vt like the
voltage stress. Observation of cells with threshold voltages shifted from the native
Vt (no trapped charge) clearly indicates that the cell has been exposed to process
damage. Parametric measurements of NVM transistors during or after processing
can be used to diagnose process damage. Specially designed wafers with NVM cells
have been used to analyze process damage, but these are limited to process conditions that do not change or damage the wafer. Special finfet structures have been
used to analyze plasma damage (Wu et al., 2015).
The threshold voltage of an NVM cell can be calculated from a simple capacitance model of the transistor and trapping layer. Figure 8.3 shows a sketch of a

from adjacent memory cells (Prall, 2007).

These equations allow the calculation of the floating gate or trapping layer voltages Vfg. The current flowing on or off the floating gate can be calculated from the

The exposure of NVM cells to a plasma can shift Vt positively or negatively during a process damage event as charge is added or removed from the trapping layer,
with Vt controlled by the net charge on the common node. Figure 8.4 shows an
example of threshold voltage shifts caused by exposure to an Ar plasma (Alba et al.,
1997). The shift increases with increasing plasma exposure time. In addition, the
shift has an across-the-wafer variation, with the wafer center increasing in a positive
direction and the wafer edge decreasing in a negative direction caused by plasma
nonuniformity across the wafer.
Process and plasma damage can shift the initial threshold voltage measured
when the wafer is tested after the completion of processing, giving erratic and confusing parametric information. Knowing the actual uncharged threshold voltage is
essential for process control and engineering reasons. The completed wafer NVM
threshold voltage shift from the native Vt indicates the amount of plasma damage the

Fig. 8.4 Change in a flash cell Vt with exposure to an Ar plasma. Cells on the edge of the wafer
lose electrons, shifting the Vt negative. Cells in the center of the wafer gain electrons, increasing
the Vt in the positive direction

148

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

cell received during wafer fabrication, with more significant shifts indicating more
damage. The direction of the Vt shift indicates the polarity of the damage.
Exposing the wafer to UV light to remove the charge from the NVM cell is
sometimes performed before electrical testing to obtain the native threshold voltage.
Charge injected onto a NVM cell during process damage can be neutralized later in
the process by thermal process steps or steps exposing the wafer to high-energy
light, masking the severity of the process damage. Figure 8.5 shows an example of
process damage affecting an NVM array after processing (Roizin et al., 2003). The
cells with protection diodes on the gates (see Chap. 3) show a tight distribution at
the native Vt, while unprotected cells show a Vt shift of more than 1 V caused by
plasma damage with a large amount of variation. This example highlights the sensitivity of NVM chips to process damage.
The diodes connected to the gates of nonvolatile memories affect the behavior
during UV exposure by modifying the electric fields across the dielectrics, changing
the ejection and injection behaviors of carriers operating in the trapping region.
Nonvolatile memories are highly susceptible to process damage due to their very
small cell sizes and small cell capacitances, resulting in cell operation with very few
charge carriers (Prall, 2007). Some NVM cells operate with as few as ten electrons
per bit. Therefore, single electrons can be observed in the operation of an NVM cell.

Fig. 8.5 Plot of the Vt of NVM SONOS NROM cells exposed to a plasma, plotted against cell
number with and without diode protection. Forward and reverse denote forward and reverse directions for the two bits in the cell

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

149

Figure 8.6 shows a plot demonstrating observations of individual electrons in a
NAND memory cell’s operation, indicating the extreme sensitivity to atomic-level
defects (Nicosia et al., 2015).
The sensitivity of NVM cells to single charge carriers and single atomic-level
defects in dielectrics causes a host of operational and reliability problems requiring
extensive optimization to achieve a successful memory. Process damage can worsen
these problems. Process damage can be manifested either as an intrinsic defect
affecting all cells or an extrinsic defect creating a few defective bits in a memory
array with abnormal behavior. NVM suffers from technology-specific problems in
addition to the CMOS parametric problems described previously (Chap. 5).
Table 8.1 shows some NVM problems that can be caused or aggravated by process
damage. The following sections present examples of some of these issues.

Fig. 8.6 Observation of single electrons in the operation of NAND flash memory cells. Vt measurements are averaged by 20 measurements. Vs is the stepped gate voltage applied to the cell
during programming

Technology-Specific Process Damage

8.2.1 Stress-Induced Leakage in Nonvolatile Memories
The stress-induced leakage (SILC) problem described previously in Chap. 7 plays a
dominant role in nonvolatile memories (NVMs), causing or contributing to many
reliability problems observed in NVMs. The problems caused by SILC are described
in the following sections. NVMs have unique operational physics requiring electrons or holes to intentionally transit through dielectrics by tunneling, hot electron
injection, etc., to and from the trapping layer or floating gate. For every carrier
transiting the dielectric, there is a finite probability that the carrier can damage the
dielectric at the atomic level. The damage probability depends on the carrier energy,
position, dielectric thickness, etc. Therefore, NVMs are heavily optimized to minimize SILC damage.
The atomic-level defects caused by SILC damage create a local electrically
active defect that can serve as a leakage path, allowing carriers to tunnel or hop
between defects, causing dielectric leakage. SILC contributes to all the mechanisms
shown in Table 8.1. NVMs are designed so that a single atomic-level defect in the
dielectric does not cause bit failure. Mitigating SILC requires that the tunnel dielectrics are thick enough that multiple defects are required for the cell to fail. In addition, the defects must align physically within a small distance, allowing tunneling or
hopping. Defects outside of this small range do not contribute to the leakage current. The energy levels of the defects in the bandgap must also align to allow leakage. Figure 8.7 shows a sketch of the local leakage path in the dielectric caused by
aligned defects (Okuyama et al., 1998).
Critical optimization and targeting of the tunnel oxide process and thickness are
necessary to mitigate SILC-related reliability problems in nonvolatile memories.
Figure 8.8 shows an example highlighting the importance of tunnel oxide thickness

Fig. 8.7 Sketch showing a stress induced local leakage conduction path caused by defects aligned
spatially and energetically

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

151

Fig. 8.8 Plot of tunnel oxide current density versus electric field for multiple tunnel oxide thicknesses after current stress. The prestress curves are shown as a solid line and the post-stress curves
are shown as a dashed line

with a plot of tunnel oxide leakage plotted against the electric field for a range of
tunnel oxide thicknesses after a current stress (Brewer & Gill, 2008). The stress-­
induced leakage current increases with decreasing dielectric thickness. The initial
unstressed SILC leakage current is identical for all thicknesses except for the thinnest 51 Å group. The thicker dielectric groups show the lowest increase with the
current stress, partially mitigating the effects of SILC.
Silicon oxide nitride oxide silicon (SONOS)-based nonvolatile memory cells are
the basis for 3D NAND technology. The SONOS cell is optimized quite differently
from the SILC examples shown here. The tunnel dielectric is very thin, around
20 Å. This mitigates SILC damage as the tunneling electrons are less likely to cause
damage as they pass through the thin dielectric. SONOS technology has the advantage of the insulating trapping layer (the nitride), which reduces SILC as the electrons in the trapping layer cannot flow easily to the SILC defect locations like they
can in a conducting floating gate.

8.2.2 Data Retention
Data retention is the critical parameter that makes a nonvolatile memory nonvolatile. The data must stay in the NVM cell semipermanently, as specified by the data
sheet. Ideally, all charge carriers stay precisely where they are placed, providing a
permanent data storage mechanism. The data retention capabilities in the real world
allow an NVM data retention specification of typically 1–10 years uncycled or as
low as a few months cycled (Cai et al., 2015).
Data retention is measured by programming a data pattern into an NVM chip,
waiting for a specific time at a specific temperature, and rereading the chip to

152

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

determine if the pattern remains. First-order data retention is determined by counting the number of bits in the array that fail after a specific time. NVM chips are
designed with test modes that measure the cells’ Vt in the product array. Sometimes,
varying the supply or internal voltages can be used to estimate Vt. This capability
allows all or part of the memory array to be scanned. As a result, detailed data can
be collected to accurately determine the data retention capability of every bit in the
array. In addition, the shift in threshold voltage with time can be used to predict the
lifetime of bits under specific data storage conditions.
Data retention must be guaranteed across the NVMs’ operating and storage temperature range. High-temperature retention tests are typically performed and extrapolated to lower temperatures. However, the retention behavior can be complex due
to multiple data retention mechanisms that can be degraded by process damage. The
multiple mechanisms include thermionic emission, trap-to-trap tunneling, stress-­
induced leakage current, etc. The loss of charge from the trapping layer at elevated
temperatures can be due to thermionic emission, where the charge obtains sufficient
thermal energy to be ejected from the trapping layer. This mechanism is not generally degraded by plasma damage. The retention loss at lower temperatures
(25–100 °C) can be caused by stress-induced leakage. Multiple competing
mechanisms for charge loss yield a complex behavior for NVM retention. Figure 8.9

Fig. 8.9 NVM data retention plotted against temperature showing complex behavior with multiple mechanisms without cycling and through various cycling counts. At high temperatures, thermionic emission (TE) from the trapping or floating gate layer dominates. At lower temperatures,
stress-induced leakage current (SILC) dominates. At temperatures around 200 °C, retention
improves as the SILC damage is partially annealed

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

153

Fig. 8.10 Vt distribution of a chip-level 1 Mbit memory array damaged by a via etch, measured by
varying Vcc. (a) After programming is shown in red. (b) After 24 h storage at room temperature is
shown in blue. The Vcc provides an estimate of the threshold voltage

shows an example plot of the retention lifetime plotted against the temperature
(Kameyama et al., 2000). A mechanism-based model consisting of two mechanisms, thermionic emission (TE) and stress-induced leakage (SILC), is shown. The
low-temperature retention loss can occur at room temperature (Arai et al., 1998). In
addition, thermal annealing may occur, as shown in Chap. 6. The worst-case retention is at around 85 °C in this example. The thermal energy can anneal the SILC
leakage at higher temperatures, reducing the current.
Additionally, 3D NAND memories generally use silicon oxide nitride oxide silicon (SONOS)-based cells that have specific additional failure modes. These modes
include charge migration and charge redistribution in the nitride storage layer (Fan
et al., 2022). These data retention loss modes can be degraded by plasma damage.
Process damage causing stress-induced leakage current typically degrades retention at lower temperatures. An NVM cell that loses charge carriers from its trapping
region may also lose the data state if sufficient carriers are lost. Figure 8.10 shows
an extrinsic data retention problem caused by plasma damage for a room-­temperature
data retention test caused by a via etch process (Takebuchi et al., 1996). A few
defective bits rapidly lose charge and become visible at low threshold voltages,
dropping out of the main intrinsic distribution due to abnormal dielectric leakage.
The rest of the bits are undamaged and behave normally.

154

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.11 Charge gain from an erased state for a gate stress in a 1 Mbit flash array with two different via etch processes. Vcc variation is used to indirectly measure the threshold voltage in the array

8.2.3 Charge Gain
Dielectrics degraded by plasma damage can inadvertently cause charge gain in
addition to charge loss, causing erased state retention failures, with charge flowing
into the trapping layer by the same defects causing charge loss. Charge gain can
cause the failure of the NVM chip by changing the logical state of the memory cell.
The charge gain from an erased state due to process damage is shown in Fig. 8.11
with a gate stress (Takebuchi et al., 1996). One via etch recipe caused defects in the
NVM cell, creating leakage in the tunnel dielectric. This defect allows charge to
flow onto the trapping layer or floating gate, increasing the threshold voltage. A
second via etch recipe is free from this defect.

8.2.4 Over-Erase and Over-Programming
For NVM, it is crucial for the program and erase behaviors of all cells in a memory
chip to behave uniformly through time and the required number of cycles. Deviant
bits that program and erase differently from the majority bits can cause chip failure
by shifting the bit to a different logical state.
NVM chips typically use algorithms to force reduced variation in the Vt distribution to ensure that all bits in the array reach the correct logical state. The algorithms

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

155

can partially compensate for the deviant bits, but there are limits to the algorithm’s
ability to force deviant bits into the main distribution.
Figure 8.12 shows an example of over-erase in a NOR flash memory for two different stacked gate etch steps (Lee et al., 2002b). One recipe (split B) yields a normal distribution, while a second recipe (Split A) has an abnormal distribution caused
by defective bits erasing abnormally fast, creating a defect kurtosis tail at lower
threshold voltages (over-erase). Process damage has degraded the tunnel dielectric,
causing excessive leakage from the floating gate.
Figure 8.13 shows the variation in erase speed between four different NOR flash
cells caused by damage to the source-drain regions by the polysilicon floating gate
etch and ion implantation (Lee et al., 2002a). Careful measurements of a large number of bits are needed to determine if the erase speed change is due to an intrinsic
shift or a defect mechanism causing erratic behavior.
Defective bits created by plasma damage can over-program as well as over-erase.
An abnormal current through the tunnel or blocking dielectric injects or depletes
charge from the trapping layer, causing the abnormal functioning of the defective
bit. Figure 8.14 shows the distributions of 16 Mbit NAND chips before and after
1 M write-erase cycles (Park et al., 1995). The deposition of the final NAND passivation layer degrades the variation in the programming speed for a fraction of the
bits, increasing the deviation of the overall distribution. The three plots show the
increased deviation after cycling caused by adding passivation to the wafer.

Fig. 8.12 NOR flash erase distribution comparing two different stacked gate etch processes and
tools showing over erase with one of the etches. Split A is a magnetically enhanced reactive ion
etch tool using HBr/Cl2/He-O2 process. Split B is based on a transfer coupled plasma tool using
Cl2 and O2
