wafer distribution shown in Fig. 8.14a. Figure 8.14c shows further variation degradation caused by increased plasma power during the deposition. After cycling, the
highest power deposition group has a fast programming subset of bits with a high
threshold voltage. The PECVD deposition process created latent defects in the tunnel oxide, which degraded with cycling.

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

157

8.2.5 Endurance, Cycling Window Shift, or Closure
The ideal behavior of an array of NVM memory cells through the cycling life of the
chip needs to be consistent with the program and erase Vt reaching the same values
through cycling. Unfortunately, actual NVM cells change their behavior through
cycling as charge becomes trapped in the dielectrics. In addition to a shift in behavior, cells may catastrophically fail, no longer functioning, programming, erasing, or
retaining data.
Figure 8.15 shows a plot of the program and erase threshold voltages as a function of the number of cycles for an array of NAND flash memory cells (Park et al.,
1995). Ideally, the plot would be perfectly flat throughout cycling instead of turning
up at a high cycle count. However, the absolute value of the threshold voltages, in
this case, increases during cycling, and the difference between the program and
erase states decreases, closing the operating window of the array. The erase slowdown and program speed increase degrades the overall chip performance. The NVM
chip typically manages this shift by modifying the program and erase waveforms to
force the threshold voltage to be consistent through cycling. Figure 8.15 shows
degradation in the window behavior caused by process damage from the nitride
plasma deposition at the end of the process. The passivation process step shifts the
program-erase window as the recipe power changes with improved results for

Fig. 8.15 16Mbit NAND Flash single cell program-erase window shift and closure as a function
of plasma power during silicon nitride deposition. An unpassivated control group shows better
performance without plasma damage

158

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.16 Program-erase window closure and shift characteristics for a polysilicon silicon-oxide-­
nitride-oxide-silicon (SONOS) after exposure to extreme ultraviolet light (EUV) simulating EUV
lithography. The thermal anneal partially recovers the cycling window

reduced power. The unpassivated experimental group has much better behavior,
indicating that the deposition caused the damage.
Figure 8.16 shows the program-erase windows for a control process and a cell
exposed to extreme ultraviolet light (EUV) to simulate the use of EUV lithography
(Tsui et al., 2011). Exposure to EUV light shifts the entire program-erase window
in a positive direction. This experiment is a worse-case test of damage by EUV light
as the wafer is exposed to EUV light without resist and other layers that would partially or fully block the EUV light.

8.2.6 Erratic Programming and Erasing
NVM cells should behave consistently throughout the cycling life of the product, as
described previously. The gradual, well-behaved shifts shown previously constitute
one nonideal cycling behavior of an NVM memory. A second nonideal behavior is
an erratic program and erase phenomenon. Erratic behavior occurs when an NVM
cell behavior varies from cycle to cycle instead of gradually over time. Figure 8.17
shows some example plots of erratic programming (Chimenton et al., 2011). The
program or erase Vt undergoes a quantum shift at random cycles, shifting from one
value to another. The threshold can remain at the new value for a few or many
cycles, sometimes reverting to its original value. This erratic behavior can cause bits
to fail intermittently throughout the life of the NVM product.
The cause of this type of erratic behavior is attributed to erratic electron or hole
tunneling modulated by trapped charge in the dielectrics. The trapped charge can
enhance or inhibit tunneling locally through the dielectric (see Chap. 7). The trap

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

159

Fig. 8.17 Examples of erratic programming behavior through cycling in four different flash memory cells

can be occupied or unoccupied by a charge carrier, and the occupation can change
through time, voltage stress, or temperature. The occupation of the trap or lack of
occupation modulates the tunneling current, resulting in the characteristic quantum
jumps in the threshold voltage shown in Fig. 8.17. In addition, process damage can
create traps in the dielectrics, increasing the propensity for erratic behavior.

8.2.7 Electrical Disturbance Mechanisms (Read Disturb,
Erase Disturb, Program Disturb)
NVM cells are operated in a memory array consisting of cells connected in rows and
columns. Operating voltages are applied to the rows and columns to make the array
function. Unfortunately, the operating voltages are also applied to cells that are not
intended to be read, programmed, or erased along the same row or column as the
modified cell, creating disturb conditions. These unintended (unselected) cells may
receive a lower voltage than those being modified (selected). As a result, process
damage can increase the probability of defective cells that have disturb issues.
The unselected cells must be able to withstand many disturb cycles consistent
with the datasheet. Typically, disturb measurements are performed by stressing cells
for different amounts of time with different voltages and temperatures to develop a
mathematical model of the disturb kinetics.

160

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.18 Sketches of NOR and NAND flash memory bit lines. (a) NOR bitline showing overerase where the bitline column is shorted to ground, causing failure of the entire bitline by overwhelming the cell current of cells being read. (b) NAND array showing a passing wordline cell that
is over programmed, causing read failure of a different cell by blocking current in the string

The memory chip is designed to be operated without disturbing or modifying the
state of the unselected cells. The unselected cells must withstand the repeated operation of the other cells on the same row or column consistent with the product datasheet. The array must survive disturb conditions for the worst-case conditions,
where all other cells on the same wordline or bitline are programmed and erased.
The stress of the unselected cells is called disturb and is typically named by which
array operation occurs on the selected cells.
Figure 8.18 shows some examples of disturb in a flash memory array (Chimenton
et al., 2011). Figure 8.18a shows a NOR NVM architecture bitline schematic with
two connected cells. The NOR architecture connects the drain of every cell on the
bitline, forming a common node with all the transistors on the bitline connected in
parallel. The selected cell on the bitline is read by applying a voltage (Vread) to the

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

161

gate (wordline) of the selected cell and sensing the current on the bitline. The gates
(wordlines) of unselected cells on the bitline are biased to ensure that the unselected
transistors are off, preventing any leakage through the unselected transistors. If Vt of
the unselected transistor is sufficiently negative, current leaks through the transistor,
causing the bitline to appear shorted to ground, resulting in the failure of the entire
column. Over-erase or erase-disturb can cause Vt to be negative. Process damage
can increase the probability of defective cells that cause erase-disturb.
A similar effect can occur if the NVM cell is over-programmed in a NAND array.
If Vt of the programmed cell in Fig. 8.18b is too high, Vread is not sufficiently high to
turn on a programmed cell, and no current flows in the NAND string. As a result, the
sensing circuit cannot detect any NAND cell in the serially connected string, causing a bit-level chip failure. This problem can degrade multilevel chip performance
in particular.

8.2.8 NOR Read Disturbance
Figure 8.19 shows a plot of the read disturb for a NOR transistor as a function of the
drain voltage and time for various drain voltages (Lee et al., 2008). As the drain
voltage increases, an infinitesimal fraction of electrons in the channel becomes
more energetic, gaining enough energy to overcome the quantum barrier formed
with the tunnel oxide. As a result, electrons accumulate on the floating gate or trapping layer, shifting the threshold voltage higher and disturbing the transistor Vt to an
unintended value. The existence of read disturbance requires that NOR cells be
sensed with a very low drain voltage (~100 mV) to avoid slowly programming the
cell, causing read disturb.

Fig. 8.19 NOR read disturb plotted against time (arbitrary units) for different drain voltages

162

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

8.2.9 NAND Read Disturb
A NAND flash array is configured with multiple cell transistors connected in series,
as shown in Fig. 8.18b. All transistors in the NAND string have to be biased in order
to read any cell. Read disturb can affect either the one cell being read or the rest of
the cells biased to enable reading the sensed cell. The cell being read slowly programs through many read cycles, eventually failing by being read as an incorrect state.
A single cell in the NAND transistor string is read at a time by applying a read
voltage to the wordline while applying a higher pass voltage to all the other wordlines to ensure that the current flows through the string. The pass voltages supply
sufficient voltage to enable a sensing current to read the flash cell. However, the
passing transistors undergo a gate disturb stress while other cells are read on the
NAND string. As a result, the passing transistors slowly gain charge during many
reads, causing the cell’s threshold voltage to increase. Eventually, the Vt increase
causes the disturbed cell to fail to pass the current necessary for reading the string,
causing the entire cell string to fail by blocking the current flow. Figure 8.20 shows
an example of read disturb in a 3D NAND string (Du et al., 2019). The threshold
voltage of the disturbed cells increases as a function of time, as indicated by the
number of reads. An erased cell will slowly program until it reaches the threshold

Fig. 8.20 Vt shift plotted against the number of read disturb stress cycles for a 3D NAND array for
various Vpass voltages

8.2 Process Damage to Nonvolatile Memory Cells

163

voltage of a programmed cell, causing the bit to fail. A programmed cell will slowly
program further until the pass voltage fails to turn on the cell to read the string current, causing the entire string to fail to read. The Vt shift increases as the gate voltage
increases, resulting in a reduced time to failure. Tunnel dielectric defects induced by
process damage can degrade NAND read disturb performance, causing reduced disturb immunity. This problem sets a limit on the maximum number of read cycles
without rewriting the data.

8.2.10 Giant Random Telegraph Signal
Nonvolatile memories are susceptible to giant RTS, as described in Chap. 7. In addition, multilevel cells have minimal state separation between the logical bits, and
noise can cause the cell to erroneously change states during cell operations. Finally,
NVM cells generally have a small area with minimum transistor length and width
that is more susceptible to a small number of traps causing filamentary current conduction behavior (see Chap. 7) in the channel as the current avoids the traps, dopant
atoms, etc. Figure 8.21 shows an example of giant RTS in a floating gate flash
memory cell (Fantini et al., 2007). The cell drain current oscillates between two
metastable states, varying by a factor of 2 due to giant RTS.

Fig. 8.21 Giant RTS impact on the cell current of an NVM cell. (a) Cell current transition as a
defect captures an electron in a flash memory cell observable as two different curves in the drain
current versus gate voltage. (b) Static measurement showing a cell current transitioning between
two levels as a function of time

164

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.22 Sketch of a NAND floating gate cell with parasitic trapping locations labeled in the
interpoly dielectric, tunnel dielectric, and over the source and drain. The table shows the number
of electrons trapped in the parasitic labeled locations based on technology computer aided design
simulations necessary to shift the cell threshold voltage by 100 mV

8.2.11 Parasitic Charge Trapping
Planar nonvolatile memories were aggressively scaled until scaling was no longer
possible. The amount of charge stored on the NVM cell continuously decreased
until the number of electrons stored in the trapping region per state decreased to less
than 100 (Prall, 2007). Planar scaling of NAND stopped, and NAND scaling transitioned to three dimensions, increasing the number of electrons stored per cell
(Micheloni, 2016). Cells with just tens of electrons per state are extremely sensitive
to charge trapped in unintended locations outside the trapping layer. A small amount
of charge trapped in unintended locations can significantly affect the state (Vt) of the
cell and the data stored in it. Figure 8.22 shows an example of sensitivity to trapped
parasitic charge for advanced planar NAND flash memory cells (Prall & Parat,
2010). Technology computer-aided simulations are performed for three different
technology nodes for trapped charge in parasitic locations. The number of trapped
electrons necessary to shift the threshold voltage by 100 mV is shown. The number
of electrons needed to shift the threshold voltage decreases as the cell is scaled,
showing an increased sensitivity. Process damage can easily result in a few trapped
electrons in the flash cell. Other advanced technologies can be similarly sensitive to
the trapped charge. For instance, this effect can occur in a small finfet or SOI device.

8.3 Process Damage in CMOS Imagers and CCDS
8.3.1 Introduction
CMOS imagers are extremely sensitive to process damage because they are designed
to be very sensitive to light for low light detection reasons. This sensitivity makes
low-level process damage detectable in imagers beyond what is detectable in CMOS

8.3

Process Damage in CMOS Imagers and CCDS

165

Fig. 8.23 Sketch of a CMOS imager pixel cell diagram and schematic

logic. The pixel sensitivity can reach single-photon levels of detection (Janesick,
2001). A similar older technology, charge-coupled device (CCD), is also susceptible
to process damage, with nearly identical results to the degradation in imagers.
CMOS imagers consist of a unit cell called a pixel arranged in an orthogonal array.
The pixel consists of a light-detecting element, such as a photodiode, connected to
a circuit that amplifies and stores the light signal. The signal is read from the array,
transferring the image to the external world. The light from an optical image strikes
a diode detector in a CMOS imager cell, creating a pocket of charge that converts
the light to an electronic charge stored in the diode. An atomic-level defect in the
imager cell can drain the diode’s charge, causing a loss of the image data, resulting
in reduced sensitivity or a defective light or dark pixel.
Figure 8.23 shows an example sketch of a CMOS imager circuit (Wang, 2008).
The light is collected by a photodiode, creating a charge in the pn junction. The
transfer gate TG transfers the charge to the source follower transistor SF, amplifying
the charge. The amplified signal is transferred to the column outside the pixel by the
RS transistor. The RST is used to reset the pixel before the light is collected.
CMOS imagers consist of two primary types of imagers based on the location of
where the imager collects the light, as shown in Fig. 8.24 (Vici et al., 2020). The two
types are frontside and backside imagers. For frontside imagers, the light enters the
wafer frontside and passes through the metal, intermetal, and passivation layers
before reaching the wafer surface, where a photodiode collects the light.
Unfortunately, the complicated stack of metal and dielectric layers degrades the
amount of light reaching the wafer surface, reducing the scalability of the frontside
imager pixel. Backside imagers are formed with the photodiodes on the backside of
the wafer, eliminating the metallization layers in the light path. As a result, backside
imagers have the technical advantages of better scaling and performance. However,
the process requirements for backside imagers are more costly and complex, requiring wafer-to-wafer bonding, wafer thinning, etc. (Durini, 2014).

166

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.24 Process cross-section showing different imager configurations. (a) Frontside imager, (b)
backside imager

8.3.2 Atomic-Level Defects in CMOS Imagers
CMOS imagers are extremely sensitive to atomic-level defects, making them susceptible to process damage. Imagers are designed to generate as many electron-hole
pairs as possible per photon. As a result, the multicomponent pixel can have different electrical signatures based on where the atomic-level defect occurs. For example, Fig. 8.25 shows a pixel performance metric called temporal read noise (TRN)
measured by repeated pixel reads (Boukhayma, 2018). In this example, this metric
is exceptionally well controlled, reaching a noise level of less than one electron.
CMOS imagers suffer from random telegraph signal (RTS) noise, as described in
Chap. 7, which can severely affect the cell operation similar to the noise problems
observed in nonvolatile memories described in Sect. 8.2.10. RTS noise results in a
variable noise floor in the pixels as a single-atomic-level defect traps or discharges
a charge carrier, resulting in varying results with time. As a result, pixels may flicker
between light and dark, as the RTS leakage transitions between low and high values,
in addition to a high noise floor. The operation of the CMOS imager can be optimized algorithmically to improve the image results. For example, the signal from a
pixel may be averaged by reading the pixel multiple times and processing the result.
A correlated double-sampling technique (CDS) reads the pixel twice to average out
the noise, subtracting two consecutive readings to eliminate the noise (Wang, 2008).
This technique is ineffective for a pixel with RTS noise because the random transitions are not averaged. Figure 8.26 shows an example result of a CDS algorithm on
a pixel with RTS (Wang, 2008). The resultant pixel signal is trimodal based on the
time correlation between the RTS transitions and the sampling.

8.3

Process Damage in CMOS Imagers and CCDS

167

Fig. 8.25 An example of imager sensitivity to process damage showing the average for a noise
metric averages about half of an electron

8.3.3 Process Damage in CMOS Imagers
CMOS imagers and CCDs are sensitive to all of the electrical defects described in
Chap. 5. All of the process damage phenomena described previously can occur in a
CMOS imager or CCD. Process damage in imagers can cause multiple problems,
including gate leakage, junction leakage, STI leakage, and various types of noise
(Wang, 2008). Defects at a silicon-dielectric interface at the wafer surface or shallow trench isolation interface can be electrically active, degrading the performance
of the pixel. Defects in the bulk of the silicon can reduce the efficiency of the pixels
by generating carriers in the junction depletion region or diffusing from outside the
junction.
Imagers and CCDs are extremely sensitive to process damage. Common process
technologies can degrade the imager performance. The steps include gate etching,
contact etching, metal etching, sputtering, implantation, etc. (Janesick, 2001). All of
the sources of process damage described in Chap. 9 can be expected to affect CMOS
imagers. Additionally, CMOS imager fabrication has unique process steps and fabrication methods that can also be expected to cause process damage. These steps
include cavity etching, backside films, etc. The architecture of the imager pixel,
design, and process are heavily optimized to reduce the effects of process damage.

168

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.26 Example of random telegraph noise in a CMOS imager operated in a double sampling
mode (CDS). The pixel output is expressed as a digital number from an analog to digital converter.
S1 and S2 are the locations in time where the pixel output is read, creating a trimodal noise signal
based on when the pixel is sampled

8.3.4 Dark Current
The current generated when a pixel is in the dark is called dark current. Dark current
is a critical metric for imagers. The dark current sets the noise floor for the imager.
Advanced imagers can obtain noise floors at the single-digit electrons/s level (Kim
et al., 2006). Therefore, reducing the dark current results in improved sensitivity and
performance. A few examples of process damage degrading dark current in imagers follow.
A single-atomic-level defect in the dielectric-silicon interface can cause leakage
through an interface trap under the gate oxide or the shallow trench isolation, draining the photodiode’s collected charge. These defects occur at the semiconductor-­
dielectric interface or in the dielectric bulk. Figure 8.27 shows a CMOS imager cell
sketch, with the surface generation regions in the dielectrics marked with a red X.

8.3

Process Damage in CMOS Imagers and CCDS

169

Fig. 8.27 Sketch of a CMOS imager cell showing potential locations for defects caused by process damage

A single-metal atom forming a bulk defect can damage the junction integrity by
draining the charge from the diode, causing data loss (see Chap. 15). This type of
leakage mechanism is created when a defect exists in the junction’s depletion
region, creating an additional leakage path beyond the intrinsic generation and diffusion mechanisms, as shown in Fig. 8.27 (Tan, 2013). Defects at a silicon-­dielectric
interface at the wafer surface or shallow trench isolation interface can be electrically
active, degrading the performance of the pixel. Defects in the bulk of the silicon can
reduce the efficiency of the pixels by generating carriers in the junction depletion
region or diffusing from outside the junction.
CMOS imager pixels are optimized to mitigate the electrical effects of the surface defects shown in the figure. For example, a surface implant is used to optimize
the photodiode, minimizing the surface electrical problems. This structure is called
a pinned photodiode (Boukhayma, 2018).
Dark current distributions typically consist of intrinsic and extrinsic distributions, with the extrinsic distribution (kurtosis) consisting of defective pixels and
intrinsic pixels without defects. Figure 8.28 shows a sketch of an example of a dark
current distribution (Carrere et al., 2014). As described in Chap. 4, process damage
can affect both distributions by creating defective pixels or shifting the intrinsic
distribution.
Figure 8.29 shows a CMOS imager experiment (Sacchettini et al., 2019). A cavity is etched in a dielectric over the photodiode for optical reasons. The dielectric
etch process increases the dark current, adding noise and reducing the pixel sensitivity. A metal plate is placed atop a nonfunctional pixel as an experiment. The metal

170

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.28 Sketch showing an example distribution of a CMOS imager dark current measured by
the pixels. The small peaks labeled blemish pixel peaks are attributed to molybdenum (Mo)
contamination

plate pixel has no increase in dark current due to the cavity etch, proving that the
process damage is likely UV light emission from the plasma.
Figure 8.30 shows an experiment where the resist ashing process was performed
in two different chambers (Sacchettini et al., 2019). A chamber where the plasma is
created in the same chamber as the wafer shows a degraded dark current. A remote
plasma chamber where the plasma is created in a separate chamber from the wafer
has a much reduced dark current because the wafer is not exposed to UV light and
the plasma.
The dark current is extremely sensitive to the dielectric film on the silicon surface over the photodiode. Traps and interface states at the silicon-dielectric interface
can degrade the dark current. Process or plasma damage in this film can degrade the
dark current. Figure 8.31 compares different antireflective coatings on the silicon
surface of a backside CMOS imager (Sacchettini, 2019). The ONO film is susceptible to process damage, as charge can be trapped in the nitride layer, forming a
nonvolatile memory-like structure, as described in Sect. 8.2, with the surface properties varying with the charge trapped in the nitride layer.
As an example of the importance of trapping in a nitride layer, decreasing the
electrical resistance of the nitride layer can reduce the propensity for trapping and
reduce the dark current impact from trapping in the nitride. Lower resistance films
over the pixel have been shown to reduce the dark current.

8.3.5 High-Energy Light Damage in CMOS Imagers
CMOS imagers are sensitive to high-energy photons that can damage films and
devices, as described in Chap. 9. Dielectric films adjacent to the photodiode are
susceptible to photon damage, as shown in the following examples.
An experiment was performed in Fig. 8.32, where a CCD pixel is exposed to UV
laser, degrading the dark current as a function of the laser energy, showing increasing dark current with increasing energy (Li et al., 2004). However, this experiment
is not entirely realistic, as the wafer is exposed directly to light rather than through
a scanner with a resist on the wafer. Virtually all photons should be trapped in the
resist layers, making lithography less likely to be a source of process damage.

172

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.31 Dark current dependence on the backside anti-reflective coating applied to the backside
surface of a backside CMOS imager

Fig. 8.32 Plot of charge couple device (CCD) dark current as a function of exposure to a 157 nm
UV laser simulating UV lithography showing increasing dark current with increasing laser energy.
This degradation is a worst case condition as the wafer is not covered with resist or other layers
which will partially or fully block the UV light. Note that this wavelength has not been used for
production lithography

8.3

Process Damage in CMOS Imagers and CCDS

173

Fig. 8.33 Dark current recovery with time at room temperature after exposure to a 157 nm light

Photon damage from plasma processes, as described in Chap. 9, can degrade many
CMOS devices.
The dark current can recover with time as the broken bonds from the damage can
rebond, decreasing the dark current. Figure 8.33 shows an example of dark current
recovery after exposure to a 157 nm laser at room temperature (Li et al., 2004).
More than half of the damage can be recovered after waiting a few months at room
temperature, requiring careful record-keeping during experiments and analysis to
avoid incorrect conclusions.

8.3.6 White Pixel Process Damage
White pixel defects in CMOS imagers are caused by excessive leakage that electrically behaves as a pixel exposed to intense light with or without light exposure.
White pixels are caused by common CMOS defects such as particles, metal contamination, etc. Additionally, plasma damage can also cause white pixel defects
through the mechanisms described in this book. Figure 8.34 shows an example of
white pixel defects caused by damage due to a nitride spacer etch process during the
formation of the transfer gate transistor (Tokashiki et al., 2007). This type of damage is described in Chap. 9. The white pixel damage defect density increases when
a magnetic field is applied during the spacer etch (see Chap. 9 for a magnetic field
discussion).

174

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.34 Example of a white pixel defect caused by a nitride spacer etch process with and without
a magnetic field. The plot shows the defective white pixel count as a function of the analog to digital converter output code

8.4 Process Damage in DRAM Memories
Dynamic random access memory (DRAM) cells consist of a single transistor and
capacitor. A DRAM cell is also a device with high sensitivity to atomic-level defects.
The binary data is stored as a charge in a DRAM cell temporarily retained on the
cell capacitor and the junction. The data is volatile and is lost in milliseconds to
seconds unless the data is rewritten, refreshing the data. Any leakage mechanism
causing a loss of charge through the dielectric, isolation, junction, or transistor can
cause the data to be lost or cause performance degradation. Figure 8.35 shows a
cross section of a DRAM cell with common sources of leakage labeled (Lee et al.,
2012). Most of the mechanisms shown have been previously described as being
susceptible to process damage (see Chap. 5). For example, charge can leak through
the DRAM capacitor dielectric, degrading retention through a stress-induced leakage mechanism caused by process stress. The junction can leak excessively if damaged by process damage, causing data loss. A DRAM cell transistor subject to
process damage can leak through multiple mechanisms, including subthreshold
conduction, gate-induced diode leakage, shifted Vt, etc. In addition, parametric

8.4

Process Damage in DRAM Memories

175

Fig. 8.35 Cross-section of a DRAM cell showing leakage sources

shifts in the cell transistor can change the dynamic performance of the cell, affecting
the access speed.

8.4.1 DRAM Refresh
The data retention of a DRAM is determined by the loss of charge from the capacitor through all leakage mechanisms that drain the charge, destroying the binary
data. Most DRAM leakage mechanisms can be degraded by plasma and process
damage, reducing the refresh time. The mechanisms are described in this book,
mostly in Chap. 5. The access transistor can leak through subthreshold leakage at
the surface or in the bulk silicon or gate-induced diode leakage. The isolation can
leak, causing cell-to-cell leakage. The diode junction can leak through reverse leakage current, with a minority current diffusing from the wafer bulk or a minority
current generated from the backside surface of the wafer. The gate dielectric can
leak (Chap. 5). Also, the cell capacitor dielectric can leak by tunneling, similar to
the MIM capacitor (Sect. 8.5).
Each refresh degradation mechanism can have both an intrinsic component limited by primary device physics and an extrinsic component limited by fundamental
physics combined with a defect mechanism that can be degraded by plasma and
process damage. For example, the gate-induced diode leakage mechanism shown in
Fig. 8.35 can be degraded by plasma damage inducing SILC and degrading the
retention (Kim, 2015).

176

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

8.4.2 Variable Retention Time in DRAMs
A DRAM memory chip only retains its data for a short amount of time. The DRAM
manufacturer must guarantee that the DRAM chip can meet this minimum time
requirement called the refresh time. The manufacturer tests the DRAM chip for
extended times and temperatures to ensure that every bit in the array can meet the
refresh time requirement.
A DRAM cell’s refresh or retention time can be highly variable, ranging from the
bare minimum specification of around 64 ms to several seconds, depending on the
quality of the cell. In addition, the refresh time is strongly temperature-dependent,
with higher temperatures being worse due to increased leakage from most mechanisms. A near-perfect DRAM cell can retain data for a few seconds. A less-than-­
perfect DRAM cell has a lower retention time depending on the severity of the
defects in the cell. DRAM manufacturers replace low refresh cells with redundant
cells, eliminating them from the chip operation, as observed by the customer.
Figure 8.36 shows an example of a retention probability distribution plot of a
DRAM array (Hamamoto et al., 1998). The nearly perfect bits form the intrinsic
part of the distribution, and the less-than-perfect bits form an extrinsic low retention
tail. Process damage can degrade the retention characteristics of a DRAM chip,

Fig. 8.36 Probability plot showing an example of DRAM retention time for three different temperatures. The black dashed line (around −3σ from the mean at around 0.13% of the bits are defective) shows the boundary between the intrinsic distribution above the dashed line and the defective
extrinsic distribution below the dashed line. This distribution is worse than typical production
quality DRAMs

8.4

Process Damage in DRAM Memories

177

Fig. 8.37 Example of DRAM variable retention time (VRT). (a) Two-state VRT caused by a single atomic level defect. (b) Multi-state VRT showing six states likely caused by three atomic
level defects

increasing the extrinsic part of the distribution or causing cells to fail the retention
specifications. Single or a few atomic-level defects in a DRAM cell are sufficient to
cause refresh failures.
In addition, single-atom defects can cause a cell to have an erratic refresh time.
This phenomenon is called variable retention time (VRT). Figure 8.37 shows two
example plots of the variable retention time in a DRAM memory array (Bacchini
et al., 2014). Figure 8.37a shows a refresh time versus elapsed time plot of VRT
caused by a single-atomic-level defect, which causes the retention time to vary
between two different values depending on the state of the defect. The two states are
caused by the defect trapping or emitting charge. Figure 8.37b shows a refresh time
versus time plot for a DRAM cell with three defects. Each of the three defects can
trap or emit charge, resulting in six different retention or refresh times.
Variable retention time is strongly temperature-dependent with increased leakage at a higher temperature. Typically, the worst-case refresh conditions are at elevated temperatures. The VRT defects trap and detrap charge carriers more rapidly,
increasing the oscillation frequency at high temperatures. Figure 8.38 shows an
example of variable retention time at three different temperatures (Ohyu et al.,
2006). The refresh time was monitored through time at three different temperatures.
The two-state oscillations can be seen to increase with temperature. The degradation in the refresh time with temperature is evident. VRT problems create testing
difficulties for the DRAM manufacturer, as observed in the plot. The long time
constant for the refresh time transition on the order of hundreds of minutes makes
detecting variable retention time during chip testing difficult and expensive. A variable retention time defective bit can alternatively pass and fail the refresh specification through time, creating a propensity for a defective chip to escape the functional
test without repair. A defective VRT bit can escape testing by remaining in a good

178

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.38 DRAM data retention time vs. elapsed time for a defective VRT DRAM cell with VRT
for three different temperatures

state during chip testing, only failing in the customer’s possession, posing a severe
DRAM quality and reliability problem.
The behavior of defective VRT bits can vary with time with changing refresh
time and oscillation frequency. A DRAM cell that is good at time zero during testing
can become defective later in time, creating an untestable defect. Also, a defective
DRAM cell can become good over time, with the varying refresh time and bi-stable
states disappearing and reappearing. VRT is essentially an unstable phenomenon.
After DRAM chips have completed testing, they are mounted on a board such as
a dual inline memory module (DIMM) for insertion into a computer. The DRAM is
attached to the board with a conducting solder that connects the DRAM chip’s electrical pins to traces on the board. The solder must be melted to form this connection.
An infrared thermal heat step of about 250 °C for a few minutes melts the solder
physically and electrically bonds the chip to the board. This short, low-temperature
heat step provides enough thermal energy to disturb or create defective VRT bits in
a DRAM chip. Figure 8.39 shows a plot of the change in the refresh time of the
weakest bit in a DRAM chip for 10,000 packaged chips (Chang et al., 2005). The
chips are exposed to IR-mounting thermal process three times to simulate a worst-­
case condition. The refresh time of the worst bit can be observed to shift in a positive or negative direction for a portion of the chips tested, indicating that the weak
VRT bits are unstable. This result demonstrates that the board-mounting process
can shift or create VRT bits since these chips passed the refresh time specification
during testing. DRAMs commonly have post-package repair capability, allowing

8.4

Process Damage in DRAM Memories

179

Fig. 8.39 Plot showing the shift in the weakest bit refresh time for 10,000 (256 Mb) DRAM chips
to 3 cycles of infrared (IR) reflow

the replacement of defective cells after a package has been mounted to a board
(Takahasi et al., 2001).
The single-atom defect mechanism causing the variable retention time problem
has been heavily researched over the years. Understanding the mechanism is complex as the DRAM cell itself is needed to investigate VRT. Parametric structures
cannot be directly used because they are not sensitive to the necessary leakage levels. However, parametric structures can indirectly be used to correlate product VRT
problems with related parametric degradation. The underlying physics has been partially understood, yielding insights into solutions to mitigate the problem. Variable
retention time in DRAMs has very similar characteristics to a common noise problem in CMOS devices called random telegraph signal (RTS). RTS typically has
time-variant two-state behavior like VRT behavior. Figure 8.40 shows an example
of RTS in the drain current of a MOS device with similar behavior as DRAM RTS
(Chen, 2010).
Several possible atomic-level defects can potentially cause RTS and VRT, as
shown in Fig. 8.41. A bulk VRT defect (C) in the depletion region of the DRAM
storage node junction can cause intermittent variable junction leakage from the storage node to the substrate, killing the refresh time of the cell. This bulk defect may
be a metal ion, lattice defect, etc.
An interface trap at the isolation edge (B) or the gate-to-source/drain overlap
region (D) can create a bistable leakage location (Chang et al., 2005). The type of
defect needs to exist in an unfavorable location instead of anywhere in the device.
The channel leakage can also be modulated when a bistable defect exists in the gate

180

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.40 Example of random telegraph signal noise in a mos device

Fig. 8.41 Cross-section sketch of CMOS DRAM access transistor showing potential causes of
VRT in DRAMs and RTS in MOS transistors. Interface defects are located at the isolation edge
and the gate drain overlap region. Bulk defects are located in the depletion regions of the cell node
junction

dielectric or the surface (A). The electric field from a charge carrier trapped in the
defect can interact with the carriers flowing in the channel, modifying the current
depending on the occupation of the trap (Chen, 2010).
This assortment of potential VRT defects can be caused by process damage at
minute levels, far below levels visible through other types of damage (Goiffon et al.,
2020). Additionally, hydrogen can play a role in VRT, as described in Chap. 14.

8.4

Process Damage in DRAM Memories

181

8.4.3 DRAM Row Hammer Problem
The DRAM row hammer problem appeared in the last few decades as DRAMs
aggressively scaled to ever-smaller dimensions (Kim, 2017). As a result, the DRAM
storage node diode junction is closely adjacent to an unrelated passing wordline
used to access an unrelated cell. The unrelated passing wordline can toggle millions
of times during the data retention period depending on the address data pattern used
to select wordlines.
Rarely, a worst-case condition can occur during operation when a single rowline
(wordline) is toggled continuously during regular operation. The DRAM datasheet
allows continuous access to one row during a refresh cycle. Practically, there is
seldom a good reason for continuous row access. However, row hammer is a security risk that can be used to attack a computer. Row hammer can cause a change in
the data state of bits adjacent to the hammering row that can be used to access unauthorized regions of the memory space for nefarious reasons (Gruss et al., 2018).
Figure 8.42 shows an example of data on a row hammer test from three different
DRAM manufacturers (Park et al., 2014). The percentage of failing bits and rows is
plotted against the cumulative number of hammers for an entire 4 Gbit chip. The

Fig. 8.42 Example of the percentage of failing cells and rows for millions of row hammer events
in 3Xnm 4Gb DRAM chips for three different DRAM manufacturers (V1, V2, V3)

182

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.43 Sketch of a DRAM array showing the location of a single atomic defect trap X in the
passing wordline. The ejected current flows from trap X near the edge of the deeper passing wordline towards adjacent storage node junctions (J1 and J2). The ejected carrier is collected by a
nearby storage node junction, causing a refresh failure. The ejected carrier can also travel across
multiple cells to be collected by nearby nonadjacent storage nodes

failure percentage increases as the number of hammers increases. The number of
possible hammers in a typical DRAM for a refresh cycle can reach a few million, as
specified in the DRAM datasheet. The data shown here is for chips with no row
hammer mitigation applied.
The underlying physics of row hammer has been heavily researched and is partially understood. The passing wordline adjacent to the storage node can electrically
impact the surrounding region, essentially hammering this region. Figure 8.43
shows a cross-section sketch of a DRAM cell. Two DRAM cells share a single bitline contact, as labeled in the sketch. Two DRAM wordlines are positioned on either
side of the contact, forming the recessed cell transistor’s gate. The cell capacitor is
connected to the opposite side of the cell transistor gate (labeled Ccell). There are
n-type diode junctions underneath the bitline and cell capacitor contacts. The passing wordlines are adjacent to the cell capacitor contact partially embedded in the
shallow trench isolation.
As the passing wordline toggles high and low, the silicon region adjacent to the
passing wordline transitions between accumulation, depletion, and inversion. If a
trap exists along the silicon surface of the passing WL, such as in the location
labeled X, the trap can fill with electrons and eject electrons as the passing wordline
is cycled. An electron can fill the trap when the passing wordline goes positive,
attracted by the positive electric field. Additionally, the electron can be ejected when
the passing wordline goes negative, repelled by the negative electric field.
The ejected electron must travel somewhere driven by the electric field. It may
travel deep into the substrate, recombining and disappearing in a recombination
center with no impact on the electrical state of the DRAM. The electron ejected

8.5

Metal-Insulator-Metal Capacitor Damage

183

from point X can also follow the two paths labeled A and B in the sketch, attracted
by the positive electric field of the junctions when a positive voltage is stored in the
capacitor. The carrier may travel path A toward the silicon surface, where it can
compensate the charge on the capacitor, degrading the retention time. The electron
can also travel path B, repelled by a negative voltage and attracted to the positive
potential in the cell capacitor junction. Ejected electrons that collect in the path B
junction compensate for the positive charge stored in this junction, degrading the
refresh, potentially causing a bit failure in the DRAM chip.
The ejected electrons can also travel to junctions further away from the hammering row in either direction, where they are also collected, degrading refresh. Path C
in the figure shows an example of a junction farther away from the passing wordline
collecting the injection charge.
About 10,000 electrons are stored in a DRAM capacitor for a modern DRAM,
enabling digital data storage. This value can be calculated from Q = CV with a 7 fF
capacitor storing a 0.5 V signal. However, if a single electron is ejected for each of
a million hammers, the 10,000 stored electrons are easily compensated, causing loss
of the stored data. In this example, only one ejected electron in a thousand hammers
collected by the junction is necessary to cause data loss. This rare event is an example of a single-atom defect causing chip failure.
Customers and users in the field found the DRAM row hammer problem.
Unfortunately, DRAM manufacturers missed the problem, creating a reliability and
security risk. Many solutions to the DRAM row hammer problem have been developed and implemented. As a result, the row hammer problem is currently not a
significant issue, as solutions have mitigated the problem. Some of the solutions
were brute force solutions, allowing fixes in the field.
One example is increasing the refresh frequency, which reduces the time available for data loss, with performance and power penalties (Kim et al., 2020). Other
solutions include adding intelligence to the DRAM chip to detect hammer situations
and mitigate the problem by refreshing the victim rows. Plasma and process damage
can potentially degrade the DRAM row hammer performance by creating single-­
atom defects that are the root cause of the problem. The process modules with an
elevated risk of row hammer degradation include steps associated with the DRAM
cell construction such as the wordline, contacts, capacitor, cell plate, etc.

8.5 Metal-Insulator-Metal Capacitor Damage
8.5.1 Introduction
Metal-insulator-metal (MIM) capacitors (also called metal oxide metal (MOM)
capacitors ) are commonly added to a CMOS process to increase circuit performance and capability by enabling high-quality, high-capacitance capacitors. MIM
dielectrics can suffer from process damage similar to that of gate dielectrics (Wang,

184

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.44 Diagram of example structures for metal-insulator-metal related plasma damage. (a)
Floating MIM capacitor connected in the metallization layers. (b) MIM capacitor connected to an
antenna and ground. (c) MIM capacitor connected to an antenna and the gate of a transistor

2004). Damage mechanisms, including stress-induced leakage, dielectric breakdown, degradation in dielectric breakdown, etc., can occur in MIM capacitors. MIM
capacitors commonly use high-k dielectrics to provide high capacitance, which can
be more susceptible to process damage than silicon dioxide. The presence of MIM
capacitors can also cause damage by capacitively coupling charge and voltage into
sensitive transistors electrically connected to the capacitor. Figure 8.44 shows
sketches of common MIM configurations related to plasma damage (Weng et al.,
2009). Figure 8.44a, b shows configurations where the MIM capacitor itself can be
damaged. Figure 8.44c shows a sketch where the MIM capacitor can damage underlying CMOS transistors by coupling the antenna energy from a metal line connected
to the capacitor into the transistor.

8.5.2 Process Damage to MIM Capacitors
An example study of plasma damage and MIM capacitors is described in the following paragraphs (Weng et al., 2009). This work studied plasma damage caused by
high-density plasma dielectric deposition (HDP) on top of a metal antenna. The
metal antenna area is varied with the MIM capacitor area held constant at 5 μm2
with a 30 nm SiO2 dielectric thickness. Modern high-k MIM capacitors behave differently, but the lessons learned here apply. Figure 8.45 shows a probability plot of
MIM leakage with three different top metal antenna-to-MIM ratios for a MIM
capacitor connected directly to the ground. The antenna ratio used here is the ratio
of the metal antenna area to the MIM area. MIM leakage at ~1 nA indicates that
dielectric breakdown has occurred (right side of the plot). A small 100 antenna ratio
(AR) has no dielectric breakdown, while a 1000 antenna ratio has a failure rate of
30% of the MIM capacitors with degraded leakage or breakdown.

8.5

Metal-Insulator-Metal Capacitor Damage

185

Fig. 8.45 Probability plot of MIM capacitor leakage as a function of antenna ratio of upper metal
on top of the MIM capacitor during dielectric deposition on top of the capacitor

8.5.3 Process Damage Induced by MIM Capacitors
In addition to the direct MIM damage described in this study, when one terminal is
grounded, MIM and other high-capacitance structures in a CMOS process can interact with damage-sensitive structures or act as a conduit to damage other sensitive
structures. For example, charge and voltage collected by antennas above or below a
MIM capacitor can couple voltage through the MIM capacitor, increasing MIM
damage or damaging underlying structures. Figure 8.46 shows a sketch illustrating
this problem structure (Weng et al., 2009). Plasma current collected by the metal
antenna above the MIM capacitor charges the MIM upper plate. As a result, the
upper plate electrically couples to the lower plate, inadvertently raising the voltage
on the CMOS transistor gate. The voltage stress can damage or destroy the transistors through the degradation mechanisms described in this book.
Damage in this type of structure can occur in one of the two devices (MIM or
MOS) or both, depending on the resistance to damage of the dielectric and many
other factors. The antenna ratio calculation for this problem is complicated, and
several different methods can be used to estimate the antenna. One factor is the
capacitive coupling ratio between the two capacitors. The voltage stress applied to
the MIM capacitor is described by Eq. (8.4), where Vp is the stress voltage on the top
electrode of the MIM capacitor (Weng et al., 2009). Increasing the gate capacitance

increases the stress across the MIM dielectric. More analysis is needed in addition
to using a simple antenna ratio of the metal area divided by the gate area, as described
in Chap. 2.
The antenna ratio based on the coupling capacitance ratios of the MIM capacitor
to the gate capacitor can be used to estimate the stress voltage across the gate dielectric. The capacitance ratio depends on the capacitor area, dielectric constants, and
dielectric thicknesses for simple two capacitors in a series circuit, as shown in

The damage to the MIM dielectrics in this study also occurred when the MIM
capacitor was connected to a MOS transistor. The MIM breakdown correlates with
the metal antenna-to-gate area ratio. The results are very similar to the condition
where a terminal of the MIM capacitor is connected to ground. This correlation
occurs for both NMOS and PMOS transistors.
Additionally, the transistors were damaged when the antenna above the MIM
capacitor was exposed to a HDP dielectric deposition on top of the MIM. Figure 8.48
shows the gate dielectric breakdown degradation due to this damage (Weng et al.,
2009). The breakdown distribution of the thicker 3.5 nm dielectric shows a fraction
of the distribution with a reduced breakdown voltage and an increasing defect density for increasing antenna ratios. The plot shows the results for a thinner 1.5 nm
dielectric. The 1.5 nm dielectric shows no evidence of increasing breakdown with

Fig. 8.49 Plot of NBTI lifetime (arbitrary lifetime) as a function of MIM to MOS area antenna

The transistors surviving without dielectric breakdown in this study have no
time-zero degradation but have degraded reliability, creating a risk of product failure. Figure 8.49 shows a plot of the PMOS negative-bias temperature instability
(NBTI) as a function of the MIM/MOS antenna ratio for two different dielectric
thicknesses. The thicker 3.5 nm dielectric data shows that the NBTI lifetime is
degraded with the antenna ratio, indicating that the plasma damage also creates
latent damage. The 1.5 nm group may show a slight degradation with an increasing
antenna ratio (Weng et al., 2009).
CMOS processes commonly have multiple types of transistors with multiple
dielectric thicknesses to support the different voltages needed in a modern chip.
Design verification practices must ensure that the correct voltages are applied to the
CMOS circuits. These verification techniques should also include preventative measures to minimize process damage. Plasma design rules controlling the configuration of the MIM circuits can minimize the propensity for damage by eliminating the
worst possible process damage conditions.

Process Damage in Low-k Dielectric Films

8.6 Process Damage in Low-k Dielectric Films
8.6.1 Introduction
Low-dielectric constant (low-k) films are commonly used in the CMOS interconnect metallization layers to reduce the metal capacitance, improving speed and
reducing power dissipation (Baklanov et al., 2007). Low-k films achieve a low
dielectric constant by choosing specific materials, including silica-based compounds, silsesquioxane (SSQ), polymers, amorphous carbon, porous films, etc.
Low-k dielectrics tend to be of low density, partially replacing the material with tiny
pockets of air to reduce the dielectric constant. Low-density films such as these tend
to be unstable and easily degraded by process damage. Low-k films can be degraded
or compromised by process damage, causing multiple changes to the film. Process
damage may cause a change in density, dielectric thickness, dielectric constant, wet
and dry etch rates, electrical leakage, feature angle, reduction in the time-dependent
dielectric breakdown, etc. Ion bombardment from plasmas can modify the dielectric
near the surface, densify it, or change the chemical composition. Chemical and radical reaction mechanisms can modify the film near the surface, changing its molecular composition. High-energy photons (UV, etc.) can break atomic bonds, modifying
the material.
Porous low-k dielectrics achieve low permittivity by achieving a high porosity
level in the films. The porosity achieves a low dielectric constant because the dielectric constant of the air contained in the pore is the minimum possible at a value of 1,
significantly reducing the overall dielectric constant of the film by compensating for
the higher dielectric constant of the rest of the film. The porous films have a large
surface area exposed to the chemicals and plasmas during processing. The film may
shrink due to pore collapse during processing. In addition, the dielectric constant
may change as a function of the dimension, complicating the modeling of electrical
performance.
Many crucial factors are necessary for successfully implementing a low dielectric constant dielectric technology, as shown in Table 8.2 (Ho et al., 2003; Murarka
et al., 2003). Low-k materials are among the most sensitive materials used in

semiconductor processing. Therefore, most processes must be optimized for minimum damage due to the sensitivity of the material because of its density and

Plasma damage and other process damage can modify an exposed low-k surface,
modifying the properties described in Table 8.2. Figure 8.50 shows a TEM cross-­
section image of a low-k dielectric film exposed to an O2 plasma that modified the
surface properties observable as a dark layer on the film’s surface (Cheng et al., 2019).

8.6.2 Low-k Dielectric Damage Examples
A few examples of process damage follow. Figure 8.51 shows the dielectric leakage
and degradation in the breakdown voltage for a low-k dielectric with and without an
O2 plasma treatment commonly used in resist ashing (Cheng et al., 2019).
The dielectric constant of low-k materials can be degraded by process damage,
reducing the resistance-capacitance (RC) speed advantage of low-k materials.
Figure 8.52 shows a plot demonstrating the increase in the dielectric constant of a
low-k dielectric by exposure to a plasma containing N2, NH3, and O2 at various
ratios (Cheng et al., 2019). The y-axis shows the absolute value of the increase in the
dielectric constant. Low-k materials typically have a dielectric constant of 2–3. The
plot shows an increase of ~25%, which is a substantial shift. The degradation in the
dielectric constant may be anisotropic, favoring a specific direction in the material.
The directionality may be caused by the film being degraded in the same direction
as the plasma exposure.

The dielectric degradation may also be a function of design rules such as the
metal pitch. An increasing surface area exposed to the plasma causes rule-­dependent
degradation as the pitch is reduced. Figure 8.53 shows an example of the dielectric
constant changing as a function of the pitch for two different plasma treatments,
expressed as a change from the initial value (Ikeda et al., 2015). This phenomenon
must be carefully understood and characterized to enable accurate design models.
Typically, surface degradation in damascene channels causes a space-dependent
dielectric constant shift, resulting in a change in the dielectric constant with space.
Individual recipe parameters in a single step can cause a drastic shift in low-k
dielectric parameters. For example, Fig. 8.54 shows the change in the dielectric
constant for a porous low-k dielectric exposed to an oxygen plasma (Cheng et al.,
2019). Increasing time or power increases the damage depth, resulting in an
increased or decreased dielectric constant.
Plasma degradation of low-k dielectric films occurs for most common gases in
plasma processing. Figure 8.55 shows the density change for a low-k dielectric
exposed to different typical process gases in a plasma (Bao et al., 2007). All process
gases in this experiment densified the film, with a likely degradation in electrical
properties and film thickness shrinkage.

8.7 Damage in Finfet Technology
Finfet CMOS transistors are natural successors to planar CMOS technology. The
transistor has a common gate on three sides of a silicon pillar, significantly improving the gate density due to its three-dimensional structure. In addition, the three-­
sided gate provides increased control of the silicon channel in the pillar, improving
the transistor’s electrical characteristics. Finfets have been relentlessly scaled since
their introduction (Park et al., 2006). Finfet dimensions are approaching atomic
dimensions, with fin widths of the order of tens of atoms, resulting in increased
sensitivity to process damage. Scaling causes finfets to enter the realm where additional erratic behaviors occur, similar to those observed in nonvolatile memories
described in this chapter. The minute channel volume in a scaled finfet reduces the
ability to dissipate energy, making the transistor more susceptible to plasma damage
due to the minute fin width. Figure 8.56 shows an example TEM of a finfet transistor
showing a small-dimension fin (Gelsinger, 2022; James & Current, 2018).
The CMOS process necessary to fabricate a finfet exposes the channel of the
three-sided fin to damage from multiple process steps, including the silicon fin etch,
isolation formation, isolation recess to form the fin, implantation, contact formation,
etc. Figure 8.57 shows a sketch highlighting etch damage during the fin formation
step (Eriguchi et al., 2014). The ions in the etch impact the sidewalls, where they
can knock atoms into the silicon. In addition, ions can be sputtered off the horizontal
silicon surface and knocked into the fin sidewalls. The surface of the etch front
breaks bonds, creating a damaged surface. Careful optimization of the finfet processing conditions is required to minimize process damage. Post-etch processes to
remove the damaged surfaces are used to mitigate the damage from the etch.

8.7 Damage in Finfet Technology

195

Fig. 8.57 Cross-section sketch showing etch damage where lateral damage on the sidewall is a
significant fraction of the channel. (a) Backsputter of silicon atoms. (b) Backsputter of etchant
ions. (c) Knock on of silicon atoms forming an interstitial. (d) Knock on of etchant ions forming
an interstitial. (e) Lattice damage formed by broken bonds. (f) Vertical sidewall knock on of etchant
ions forming an interstatial. (g) Vertical sidewall knock on of silicon atoms forming an interstitial.
(h) Vertical sidewall lattice damage formed by broken bonds

The industry is transitioning from finfets to more advanced CMOS structures,
such as gate all around transistors (GAA), as finfet scaling has approached its limits.
These devices are also susceptible to process and plasma damage, requiring extensive optimization.
Finfets are susceptible to all the problems described in this book and additional
problems like the fin etch described above. The minute dimensions of finfet

196

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

Fig. 8.58 Plot showing 7 nm finfet TDDB after plasma damage as a function of the antenna ratio
for back end of line damage

transistors limit the dissipation of heat and energy, reducing the overall energy
required to damage the transistor. As an example, Fig. 8.58 shows a plot highlighting time-­dependent dielectric breakdown due to plasma damage in a 7 nm finfet
technology (Tsai et al., 2018). The TDDB lifetime decreases with increasing backend antenna ratio.

8.8 Silicon-on-Insulator Technology
8.8.1 Introduction
Silicon-on-insulator (SOI) technology can be very sensitive to plasma and process
damage. For an SOI wafer, the semiconductor is formed on top of a wafer with an
insulating dielectric, forming a silicon region on an insulator layer (Kononchuk &
Nguyen, 2014). This technology has many technical benefits, but it also has the
disadvantage of being an expensive wafer. However, an SOI transistor can behave
differently due to the structural and electrical differences compared with bulk silicon technology. Plasma and process damage behavior is different for SOI technology, requiring consideration of the following items: more severe damage from the
source and drains, damage from differential antennas, difficulty in diode protection,
and channel conduction during plasma damage.

8.8

Silicon-on-Insulator Technology

197

Dielectrics on all sides surround the SOI transistors, and as a result, the small
silicon region lacks the thermal conduction capability of bulk silicon technology,
where the silicon wafer acts as a heat sink (Martin, 2021). The thermal conductivity
of silicon is about 100 times higher than that of silicon dioxide. As a result, heat
tends to be trapped in the SOI silicon region, resulting in channel temperature
increases of more than 100 °C during operation. As a result, the transistor behavior
can change as a function of operating parameters such as frequency and voltage
(Tenbroek et al., 1996). Process damage may be worse on SOI structures due to
their higher sensitivity.

8.8.2 Effects of Buried Oxide
The underlying dielectric layer beneath the transistor, called a buried oxide (BOX)
layer, can be degraded by process damage, trapping charge, and forming interface
states that can cause leakage by forming a parasitic channel underneath the normal
silicon channel. This trapping region does not exist in bulk silicon, where charge is
typically trapped in the gate dielectric and isolation edges. Figure 8.59 shows a
sketch showing the location of the parasitic channel in the BOX layer. Damage to
the BOX layer can also cause more subtle transistor degradation, such as threshold
voltage variation. The impact of BOX damage is strongly dependent on the SOI silicon thickness.
The floating body prevents the formation of a protective diode connection from
the gate to the substrate. Therefore, diode protection in SOI technology requires

Fig. 8.59 Sketch showing a cross-section of an SOI transistor with the buried oxide (BOX) layer
under it showing the location of trapped charge. The parasitic transistor with a backside gate
is shown

198

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

either a two-terminal lateral surface diode or a special connection to the substrate
through the buried oxide layer (Martin, 2021). More details are covered in the SOI
diode section (see Chap. 3).
Unlike bulk silicon, the SOI body silicon is isolated from the underlying substrate, creating a floating body that is not electrically connected. The floating body
can be capacitively coupled and electrically biased by voltages applied to the gate,
source, and drain, changing the electrical behavior of the transistor by charging until
equilibrium is reached. This phenomenon is called the floating body effect in SOI
technology. From the plasma damage perspective, the floating body changes the
electrical degradation. The floating body charges up toward the gate potential during plasma damage from the gate, reducing the dielectric current. The charge flowing through the gate dielectric collects in the isolated silicon region and is unable to
flow into the bulk. The damage from the gate current is reduced as the bottom oxide
(BOX) insulator inhibits the gate current relative to a bulk wafer. Therefore, the
charged body can potentially reduce the amount of damage to the dielectric.
Plasma damage from the drain or source can charge up the floating body, increasing the dielectric current and potentially worsening plasma damage. The charge
flowing into the source/drain region also collects in the isolated silicon region. An
SOI device is more sensitive to source/drain charge as the charge cannot dissipate
into the wafer’s bulk.
Figure 8.60 shows a cross-section sketch showing damage locations for CMOS
formed in SOI wafers compared to a bulk silicon wafer (Yamamoto et al., 2019).
Figure 8.61 shows a plot comparing devices with source/drain antennas and gate
antennas, resulting in nearly identical shifts since both charge sources accumulate
in the isolated semiconductor region (Yamamoto et al., 2019). The figure shows that
the threshold voltage shift is worse for a 15 nm silicon layer than for a 10 nm layer.
Experiments varying the gate dielectric and BOX thicknesses can determine the
roles of damage to the gate dielectric or buried oxide in transistor degradation.

Fig. 8.60 Cartoon comparing process damage between a bulk wafer and an SOI wafer. (a) Drain
damage discharges to gate and bottom oxide in SOI CMOS. (b) Drain damage discharges to the
substrate for bulk CMOS

8.8

Silicon-on-Insulator Technology

region (floating body)

8.8.3 Propagation Between Floating Bodies
Floating body effects can cause plasma damage to propagate between different transistors, hopping from floating body to floating body, and extending the damage to
connected transistors as multiple floating bodies charge up. For example, Fig. 8.62
shows a sketch of two transistors illustrating transistor-to-transistor damage (Martin,
2021). A metal antenna is connected to the first transistor drain. The source of the

200

8

Technology-Specific Process Damage

first transistor is connected to the drain of a second transistor in a separate buried
layer. The charge flowing from the antenna into the first floating body flows through
the channel and into the second transistor drain, charging the body of the second
transistor and damaging the gate dielectric of the second transistor. In SOI technology, damage can propagate from transistor to transistor, complicating process damage and reliability.

8.8.4 SOI Antenna Ratios
Silicon-on-insulator complicates the calculation of an antenna ratio used to minimize plasma damage. Bulk silicon ratios are typically calculated based on the collecting antenna area (or edge) ratios to the gate or well area. SOI calculations should
additionally include the ratio of the charging antenna area (or edge) to the floating
body area, downstream connected floating body areas, etc.
Additionally, differential antenna ratio calculations are more critical for SOI
technology relative to bulk technology. Figure 8.63 shows a sketch of differential
antenna ratios connected to an SOI transistor (Martin, 2021). A differential calculation is needed since the floating body can be charged from both the source or drain
and gate in this case. The areas (A1 and A2) of the two antennas connected to the
source and drain are added or subtracted and divided by the area of the floating body
region or the gate area (Yamamoto et al., 2019). Charging from both the gate and
drain antennas can increase the stress on the BOX layer, damaging the BOX

Fig. 8.63 Sketch showing differential antennas with a larger antenna on the drain and a smaller
antenna on the gate

dielectric for the additive case shown in Chap. 5. The worst-case conditions occur
when the polarities are opposite, creating higher stress than similar polarity charging (Martin, 2021):

Chapter 9

Common Sources of Process Damage

9.1 Introduction
Many process steps have the propensity to create damage by different means.
Plasma damage through charge accumulation on the wafer surface is one of the
most common problems. However, there are many other cases where damage occurs
through various means other than plasma damage. For example, damage can occur
through triboelectric effects caused by friction through simple processes such as
deionized water rinses (Sect. 9.6). Processing may contaminate the wafer with unintended materials that degrade and destroy the electrical function. Structural damage
can occur by processing damage physically, destroying the intended structures on
the wafer surface. This chapter reviews the most commonly known sources of process damage and the mechanisms involved.

9.2 Accelerated Degradation Under Processing Conditions
CMOS wafers are subject to extreme conditions during processing in semiconductor manufacturing equipment. These conditions are much more extreme than the
chip’s operating conditions during its lifetime in the field. Processing conditions can
expose a wafer to temperatures of hundreds of degrees centigrade, while chip operating temperatures are usually limited to a maximum temperature of 125 °C for
high-temperature applications such as automotive. Many processing steps have a
maximum temperature of around 400 °C to prevent excessive heat from damaging
the metallization after the transistor is formed, which can degrade the chip’s electrical characteristics. Exposure to plasma damage at elevated temperatures can degrade
or destroy dielectrics rapidly, far faster than under normal operating conditions. The
acceleration of degradation under process conditions can occur a billion times faster
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_9

205

206

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

than during normal chip operation (Cheung, 2001). As a result, materials that could
survive for the chips’ required lifetime can be degraded to the point of failure during
a few minutes of processing.
Figure 9.1 shows the gate dielectric breakdown failure acceleration for a thick
dielectric with temperature and voltage acceleration. The lifetime of the dielectric at
a processing condition of 400 °C is several orders of magnitude shorter than at a
maximum operating temperature of 125 °C (Strong et al., 2009, pg. 272). The lifetime at 400 °C can be very short in the range of seconds, which is a similar time as
many process recipe conditions. The charging conditions determine the acceleration
due to the voltage stress.
As an example, Fig. 9.2 shows the charge to breakdown (QBD) degradation as a
function of temperature for a plasma ashing process (Ma et al., 1995). The QBD at
340 °C is severely degraded for an antenna ratio of 104, with the capacitors breaking
down at zero charge, indicating dielectric breakdown during plasma damage before
the QBD test. The lower temperature ashing process is significantly better.
Acceleration of damage by temperature, voltage, time, and chemical action occurs
in many CMOS manufacturing processes.
High-k dielectric degradation is similarly accelerated with temperature. High-k
dielectrics are typically easier to damage compared to SiO2 because the bond
strength in the material is weaker (McPherson et al., 2002). Figure 9.3 shows a plot
of charge to breakdown (QBD) for a high-K dielectric with a SiO2 interfacial layer.
The QBD is degraded by about 6 orders of magnitude between 125 °C and 290 °C,
indicating that damage during processing may be a severe risk (Wu & Sune, 2013).

different electrical field strengths
