9.3 Common Sources of Process Damage
Many commonly used semiconductor processes can cause process damage in a
CMOS manufacturing process. This section reviews some of these sources of damage. Almost all processes have some potential for damage. A list of common process damage sources covered here is shown in Table 9.1.

9.4 Ion Implant Damage
Ion implantation is used multiple times in a CMOS manufacturing process to provide a broad spectrum of dopant atoms at different energies and doses into the wafer
to tailor the CMOS device properties (Rimini, 1995). Ion implantation exposes the
wafers to a beam of positively charged ions that collect on the wafer, creating a situation where damage to devices and structures can occur as charge accumulates in
the wafer. The ion beam is scanned across the wafer, providing a uniform dose
across the wafer. The structures on the wafer surface are intermittently exposed to
the ion beam, charging the structures. Ion implantation is a good case study for
process damage due to the simplicity of the physics involved.

9.4.1 Ion Implantation Charging Damage
Charge collects on structures on the wafer surface, such as transistor gates, as the
wafer is exposed to an ion beam, as shown in Fig. 9.4. The ion current from the
implanter beam strikes the wafer surface, and the ion charge collects on conducting
and nonconducting structures on the wafer surface. The implanter ion beam current
projects a current Ii (usually mA) onto a region of area Ab determined by the beam

9.4

Ion Implant Damage

209

Fig. 9.4 Sketch showing an ion implant beam scanning across a wafer

dimensions as it scans across the wafer. A structure on the wafer with area A that
receives charge Q during exposure time t is given by Eq. (9.1) (Mack et al., 1985).
Q = A I i t / Ab

(9.1)

The total charge the structure receives is proportional to its area and the total
dose D (ions/cm2), as shown in Eq. (9.2).
Q = DA

(9.2)

If the structure is a capacitor, the capacitor is formed by the gate metallization
over the isolation oxide and the silicon substrate. The voltage V the structure reaches
is given by Eq. (9.3), where q is the charge on an electron (Coulombs):
V=

qD
C

(9.3)

As an example of a worse-case condition, a gate metal structure on top of an
isolation (STI) oxide 200 nm thick can reach a voltage of nearly 100,000 volts for a
high implant dose of 1016 ions/cm2. A 1000 V across a 200 nm dielectric can create
a 50 MV/cm electric field, easily destroying the capacitor. This worse-case condition assumes that the capacitor collects all the implant charge and that no charge
leaks away by secondary emission or surface leakage.
Charging damage can be significantly worse for structures that collect substantial amounts of ion beam charge and discharge it into sensitive structures. Figure 9.5
shows a sketch of an example sensitive structure. This type of structure is called an
antenna structure. A large-area metal gate structure on top of the STI isolation is

210

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.5 Sketch showing a gate antenna on top of shallow trench isolation exposed to an ion
implantation beam

connected to a small gate metal structure on active active with a gate dielectric
underneath. The gate metal antenna structure on the dielectric isolation collects ions
in proportion to its area from the implanter beam, building up charge with increasing voltage. The accumulated charge on the metal antenna attempts to find a leakage
path to exit the antenna as the ion charges repel each other. The charge leaks from
the antenna through the lowest resistance path. The gate dielectric is typically the
lowest resistance path, conducting current by quantum mechanical tunneling. The
gate dielectric leakage increases as the voltage on the gate metal increases through
Fowler–Nordheim or direct tunneling. The antenna’s voltage increases until the gate
leakage from the antenna matches the ion current onto the antenna, reaching a
quasi-steady-state condition where the currents are matched. The gate dielectric
undergoes damaging stress under these conditions, causing degradation in electrical
characteristics.
The worst-case example described previously is extremely rare under real-world
conditions. The charge building up on the antenna may leak across the surface of the
wafer in addition to the gate dielectric leakage, reducing the maximum voltage
level. In addition, secondary emission of ions and electrons may be ejected from the
antenna due to the impacting ion beam. The secondary emission changes the voltage
and amount of charge on the antenna. The sketch in Fig. 9.5 shows these current
paths. The beam current accumulates on the wafer surface and leaks across the
surface.

9.4

Ion Implant Damage

211

9.4.2 Flood Gun
Ion implantation has a high propensity for process damage as it injects a beam of
charged ions onto the structures on the wafer’s surface. The fundamental mechanism for ion implant operation results in a likely occurrence of process damage. The
ion beam current is a parameter that can be easily adjusted to modulate the charge
flowing onto the wafer. Therefore, there is typically a direct correlation between
process damage and beam current. This correlation can be used to prove that the
cause of a problem is process damage. Due to the intrinsic process damage nature
of an ion implanter, implanters are equipped with an electron source called a
flood gun.
The flood gun injects negatively charged electrons into the positively charged ion
beam in the implanter to neutralize the ion charge. The system is optimized to neutralize the beam charge by compensating the ions with an equal number of electrons, mitigating the plasma damage. Figure 9.6 shows a plot of the voltage induced
on a MOS capacitor with varying flood gun current (Wu & Kolondra, 1991). The
voltage across the capacitor decreases until the electron charge matches the ion
beam and neutralizes it, compensating for the implant beam current. A negative
voltage is induced on the capacitor as the flood current increases beyond the neutral
point where the ions are precisely compensated. An excessive flood gun current
stresses the capacitor negatively, causing process damage. The implant and flood
gun conditions must be carefully optimized to mitigate plasma damage.

Fig. 9.6 Plot showing the charging voltage on a capacitor as a function of the flood gun current
for a 100 keV Ar ion implant with a 1 mA beam current

212

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.7 Cross-section sketch showing implant related process damage mechanisms that can
affect a CMOS imager cell. The sketch also applies generally to CMOS. The text highlights
implant related mechanisms

9.4.3 Implant Damage Excluding Charging
In addition to ion charging damage, ion implantation can degrade CMOS chips
through several other mechanisms (Romano et al., 2015). Figure 9.7 shows a CMOS
wafer cross-section sketch showing potential mechanisms of implant-related damage (Ito, 2012).
Ion implanters can knock-on unintended atoms into the substrate, causing junction and transistor leakage. These knocked-on atoms can be from the films, photoresist, etc., on the wafer surface, or from contamination such as metal atoms on the
wafer surface. In addition, the knocked-on atoms can become electrically active.
For example, a Shockley–Read–Hall (SRH) site can cause leakage, resulting in
single-atom defects.

9.4.4 Implant Structural Damage
Ion implantation imparts a large amount of kinetic energy into the implanted wafer,
which can damage the crystalline semiconductor lattice and materials deposited on
the wafer surface. The damage is dependent on multiple parameters in the implant
recipe. Figure 9.8 shows a diagram of ion implant recipe parameters that can impact
the severity of lattice damage (Rao, 2014). Implantation of heavy ions causes more
damage than implanting light ions. Higher doses and higher energy cause more
damage. Additional parameters are shown in the figure.
Ion implantation can cause structural damage to the semiconductor substrate
for higher doses, creating electrical leakage. High doses greater than about

9.4

Ion Implant Damage

213

Fig. 9.8 Diagram showing important ion implant parameters related to crystalline damage

Fig. 9.9 Cross-section sketch showing location in an MOS transistor where implant related
defects may occur

1014 ­ions/­cm2 cause enough damage to the semiconductor lattice to destroy the crystal structure, amorphizing it completely. The crystal structure is regrown after
implantation with thermal annealing in a process called solid-phase epitaxial
regrowth (SPEG). The silicon crystal regrows slowly along the crystal planes of the
wafer substrate until the original crystal structure of the implant region is regrown.
Defects may form during this regrowth process, specifically in regions where the
regrowth regions contact a feature on the surface of the wafer, such as a gate edge
or isolation (STI) field edge. A dislocation or stacking fault may form where the
regrowth meets the surface feature, causing leakage or device failure. Careful optimization of the processing conditions during implantation and annealing may be
needed to eliminate the dislocations. Figure 9.9 shows a cartoon of locations in a
MOS transistor where dislocations tend to form, including the gate edge, spacer
edge, and STI edge (Chang, 2011). The dislocations tend to form at nearly a 45°

214

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.10 Example TEM photographs of ion implant related defects. (a) Mask edge defect (MED)
at the edge of a nitride spacer and salicide region. (b) Peak range defect (PRD), mask edge defect
(MED), and end of range defect (ERD). (c) MED at a nitride edge. (d) MED at a nitride edge. (e)
MED at the edge of a nitride spacer and salicide region with end of range defects below the salicide
region. (f) MED at a nitride spacer, salicide region, and contact. (g) Pinned amorphous region at a
STI edge. (h) End of range defects. (i) End of range defects

angle (for <100> substrates) caused by epitaxial growth from both the side and bottom of the amorphous region.
Figure 9.10 shows TEM examples of implant-related defects (Current, 2014,
2019; Lee, 2020; Rudawski et al., 2008; Rudawski et al., 2009). Figure 9.10a shows
a mask edge defect (MED) TEM of a dislocation at the edge of the spacer in a
CMOS transistor (Tung et al., 2003, pg. 165). Excessive stress at the bottom spacer
corner results in a fracture of the silicon lattice or the SPEG regrowth from the bottom and side of the junction. This type of defect is commonly caused by ion implantation or the salicide metallization process. Figure 9.10c and d shows mask edge
defects formed at the edge of two implanted nitride edges. Figure 9.10e shows
another mask edge defect at the edge of a nitride spacer and salicide region.
Figure 9.10g shows an amorphous region at the STI edge that is not recrystallized
as the SPEG is pinned at the STI edge.
In addition to implant defects formed at feature edges, implant-related defects
can form in regions unrelated to the surface features. For example, Fig. 9.10b shows
three different defects, including a mask edge defect. There are two possible defects
that can be formed by ion implantation without a feature edge. One defect is the end
of range defects (EOR) formed at the maximum depth of the amorphous region
where large supersaturations of silicon interstitial atoms exist that increase in size
during annealing through an Ostwald ripening mechanism (Koffel et al., 2009). The
third defect is the peak range defect (PRD) located at the peak dopant concentration
in the implant doping profile, where the heavy concentration of dopants interferes
with recrystallization. Figure 9.10h and i shows examples of end-of-range defects.
Ion implant defects are a complicated function of the wafer structures, implant
conditions, and post-implant processing conditions. Process steps such as contact
formation, salicide formation, and stressor formation interact with the implant and

play critical roles in defect formation. The annealing processes are critical for the
elimination of implantation related defects.
The amorphous region created by a heavy-dose ion implant can be challenging
to recrystallize in small amorphous features, such as finfets, as the amorphous–crystal boundary is very small and quasi-one-dimensional. Figure 9.11 shows example
transmission electron microscope pictures of a finfet channel after implantation and
different annealing steps (Fletch, 2014; Pipes et al., 2014; Wood et al., 2013; Ye
et al., 2021). Figure 9.11a shows the fin as implanted where the arsenic implant has
amorphized the fin’s top and side. Figure 9.11b shows an attempt to recrystallize the
fin with a rapid thermal process spike anneal. However, dislocations and stacking
faults remain in the center of the fin. Figure 9.11c shows that hot ion implantation
results eliminate the dislocations. Figure 9.11d shows an amorphous region formed
by an implant. Figure 9.11e shows slight recrystallization after a 650 °C anneal, and
Fig. 9.11f shows partial recrystallization after a 1050 °C rapid thermal processing
(RTP). Figure 9.11g shows an amorphous region, and Fig. 9.11h shows recrystallization after a spike anneal. Figure 9.11i shows dislocations across the entire fin,
shorting the drain and source of the transistor.

9.4.5 Ion Implantation Through the Gate
Ion implantation through the gate dielectric and metallization is sometimes performed to adjust the CMOS electrical parameters. The ions can break bonds in the
dielectric and semiconductor, creating traps and interface states and degrading the
electrical characteristics. In addition, the ions impacting the gate metallization or
gate dielectric can knock on ions into the gate dielectric and substrate, degrading the
electrical characteristics. Ion implantation at higher energies has a higher propensity for dielectric damage. Figure 9.12 shows a plot of dielectric breakdown yield as

216

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.12 Plot showing time-zero dielectric breakdown yield (TZDB) as a function of As ion
implant energy at a dose of 1.5 × 1012 ions through the gate. The yield criteria is defined as greater
than 8 MV/cm

a function of ion implant energy through the gate for a 7 nm dielectric (Shiraki,
2008). The criterion for failure is a breakdown voltage of less than 8 MV/cm.
Degradation due to ion implantation through the gate dielectric is also a function
of the implant dose. Figure 9.13 shows an example plot of the charge to breakdown
(QBD) as a function of the implant dose for different gate dielectric thicknesses
(Jank et al., 2000). Thinner oxide layers are less likely to be damaged because the
ions have a higher probability of passing through the dielectric without interacting
with the fewer number of atoms in the dielectric.

9.5 Triboelectric Charging in Wet Processes
Some wet chemical processes can create process damage through electrostatic damage by triboelectric charging, where friction generates a charge on the wafer surface
(Mack et al., 1985). Wafer scrubbing with a rotating noncontacting brush is commonly used in CMOS wafer processing for enhanced wafer cleaning to remove
particles from chemical mechanical processing and other processes. Spin dryers are
used to dry wafers by centrifugal force after wet cleaning steps. Process steps that
spin the wafers, such as single wafer cleans, resist coatings, etc., can generate a triboelectric charge (Alba et al., 1997). Marangoni drying extracts moisture from the
wafer surface with alcohol vapor. Processes like this can generate a triboelectric

9.6

Damage Due to Spin and Deionized Water Processes

217

Fig. 9.13 Charge to breakdown for various oxide thicknesses for different implant (100 KeV
boron) doses through gate dielectrics. The left most data points are unimplanted samples. One
group added a furnace anneal of 950 °C for 30 min in an inert ambient after the implant

charge on the wafer surface, causing process damage as the charge is dissipated
(Hagimoto et al., 2009). Additionally, visible electrostatic discharge defects can be
created on the wafer, like the defects shown in Chap. 5.
Additionally, nonvisible damage discussed in the previous sections may occur
but cannot be detected inline during processing. High-resistance deionized (DI)
water is used in CMOS processing to avoid contamination. Water may not create a
sufficient leakage path for charge dissipation from the wafer, leaving a charge on the
wafer. In addition to process damage, triboelectric charge can attract particles onto
a wafer, creating yield problems.

9.6 Damage Due to Spin and Deionized Water Processes
CMOS wafers commonly use spin processes where the wafer is held by a vacuum
chuck and rotated at high speed while deionized water and chemicals are dispensed
on the wafer surface. Process damage and electrostatic discharge can damage sensitive CMOS devices due to the friction of the high-resistance water across the wafer
surface.
Figure 9.14a shows a cross-section sketch of an oxidized wafer on a spin chuck
with deionized (DI) water flowing across the surface covered with SiO2 (Istsumi
et al., 2002, pg. 245,261). High-speed rotation of the wafer of thousands of

218

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.14 (a) Cross-section sketch of a spin chuck with deionized water flowing on the surface of
a wafer. (b) Showing the voltage induced on the surface of the dielectric versus the rotation speed
during a deionized water spin rinse. The voltage was measured by an electrostatic meter

revolutions per minute causes friction on the wafer surface. The friction across the
wafer surface generates heat and electrostatic charge that can break apart the water
atoms into H+ and OH- ions. The difference in electronegativity causes the OHatoms to become trapped in the silicon dioxide and the H+ to remain in the deionized water. The rotating ions generate a magnetic flux perpendicular to the wafer
surface. The flux causes a nonuniform charge distribution across the wafer surface,
pushing H+ ions to the wafer center where they can diffuse through the silicon dioxide layer, reaching the silicon–oxide interface and passivating defects like interface
states in a similar manner as a hydrogen alloy.
Like a thermal hydrogen alloy, the passivating process can anneal plasma damage from previous process steps. Additionally, large voltages can be generated on
the wafer surface with a DI water spin, as shown in Fig. 9.14b, where higher spin
speeds generate higher surface voltages.
The charge induced in the dielectric has been shown to improve plasma damage
problems through hydrogen alloying of defects and possibly compensation of
charge remaining from damage at a previous step (Istsumi et al., 2002, pg. 253,257).
Also, the induced voltage can damage the dielectric.
The magnetic field induced by the spinning ions results in nonuniform damage
across the wafer, as shown in Fig. 9.15 as shown by measurements of minority carrier lifetime and flatband voltage shifts in the C–V characteristics.

9.7 Resist and Masking-Related Damage
Resists and other masking materials enable semiconductor processes to modify one
region of the wafer while protecting other wafer regions from modification. Masking
materials are generally insulators and can absorb charge on their surface. The

9.7

Resist and Masking-Related Damage

219

Fig. 9.15 Plot showing across-wafer nonuniformity caused by a DI spin rinse. (a) Across-wafer
variation in minority carrier lifetime. (b) Across-wafer variation in C-V flatband voltage and corresponding negative charge trapped on the wafer surface (Sze & Lee, 2010, pg. 253,257)

Fig. 9.16 Sketch showing resist charging modifying the plasma charge behavior near an antenna

collected charge can affect the processing conditions on the wafer surface. The collected charge can flow or dissipate across the wafer surface due to the mask pattern
and resistivity. The masking materials can be impacted by the processing and change
as the recipe progresses. High temperatures can cook the mask material, modifying
it with chemical changes such as outgassing. In addition, the chemicals in the processing chamber may react with the masking material, changing its composition.
The collected charge may build up in the masking material and gain the capability to deflect ions and electrons, modifying the processing conditions at the wafer
surface and deviating from the intended process conditions (Dixon et al., 1997).
Figure 9.16 is a sketch showing a resist pattern near an antenna collecting charge,
modifying the electric field at the antenna edge, changing the local process conditions, and process damage. Additionally, the charge on the wafer surface may bend
the electron or ion path, changing the impact angle with the wafer and affecting
local process conditions. This effect may occur in multiple types of CMOS processing, including ion implantation, plasma etching, sputtering, etc.

220

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.17 SEM photograph of an example electrostatic discharge arc showing tendrils

The collected charge on the masking layer can build up to the point where a catastrophic electrical discharge can occur, damaging the wafer surface. Figure 9.17
shows a microscope photograph of a defect caused by an electrical arc (Wavelength
Electronics, 2015). Note the distinctive arc pattern with tendrils of material migrated
from one conductor to another. This kind of defect can correlate with specific resist
patterns, design rules, and wafer structures that collect the most charge.

9.7.1 Resist and Masking Overlap-Related Damage
A resist or masking pattern overlapping a conducting antenna may collect and conduct charge, increasing the effective antenna area and forming a combined antenna
consisting of the antenna and the additional antenna formed by the conducting resist
(Dirnecker et al., 2002). A masking layer can also have the opposite effect, protecting an antenna by insulating it, providing that the masking layer is nonconductive.
Figure 9.18 shows a plot of antenna yield for different values of resist overlap on a
gate antenna during an ion implant. The figure also shows the effects of different
resist patterns. This plot shows that increasing resist coverage reduces process damage and increases antenna yield. The worst-case conditions in this example are for
structures with an extensive overlapping resist feature or a nearby resist pattern. The
best-case conditions are for a structure where the resist completely covers the feature. The damage in this example is caused by an ion implantation step with the
flood gun turned off as a worst-case condition.

9.7

Resist and Masking-Related Damage

221

Fig. 9.18 Plot showing the yield of a polysilicon gate antenna as a function of the resist coverage
for an ion implant into a gate antenna. Data is shown for no nearby resist and a resist pattern 1 μm
and 5 μm away from the antenna, along with the resist overlapping the gate

9.7.2 Plasma Ashing
Photoresists and numerous types of masking materials block unwanted processing
from selected regions of the wafer to form patterns essential to the formation of
CMOS chips. Common process elements, such as etching, implantation, etc., use
masking materials to achieve the required finished wafer structures. The masking
materials become damaged through the bombardment of ions, chemical reactions,
densification, etc., making them difficult to remove from the wafer and requiring
complex stripping processes to remove the materials from the wafer. In addition, the
wafer surface must be thoroughly cleaned to eliminate the possibility of contamination. Masking material removal by plasma processing is commonly used because of
its aggressive ability to remove the difficult-to-remove damaged materials. Plasma
stripping processes have the propensity to cause process damage.
Figure 9.19 shows a probability plot demonstrating a wide threshold voltage
distribution caused by a plasma resist strip process for a 90 nm 50 Å CMOS imager
process (Sacchettini et al., 2019). The structures evaluated only have a bond pad
serving as an antenna. The group receiving a wafer strip process has a wide distribution with a defect tail. A 400 °C anneal after the plasma strip nearly recovers the
distribution to its previous state.
Figure 9.20 shows an increased extrinsic defect density at low capacitor breakdown voltages for metal antennas during a resist strip process (Shin & Hu, 1993).

222

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.19 Probability plot of threshold voltage shift of an nmos transistor connected to a bond pad
antenna subject to an O2 plasma resist strip. The wide Vt distribution is the same for different strip
times. A 400 °C anneal partially heals the wide distribution (left side of the plot)

Fig. 9.20 Probability plot showing dielectric breakdown caused by resist strip after a metal
etch process

9.8 Electron and Ion Beam Damage

223

The control group also has an extrinsic defect tail with different characteristics at
higher breakdown voltages.

9.8 Electron and Ion Beam Damage
CMOS wafers are sometimes exposed to other types of electron or ion beams in
addition to the most commonly used ion implants. Wafers are often inspected with
electron beams used in critical dimension measurements, alignment measurements,
or defect inspections. The entire wafer or small wafer regions are measured to collect data for process monitoring and control at multiple points in the process flow.
An electron beam can cause process damage by several mechanisms, including
charging the wafer surface, high-energy photon exposure, sputtering, wafer heating,
or electrostatic discharge (Egerton et al., 2004). Electron beams are sometimes used
for wafer curing, also creating the potential for damage (Pyo & Kim, 2013). The
failure pattern on the wafer usually matches the electron beam pattern across the
wafer, giving a specific signature to isolate the cause of the failure. Table 9.2 shows
a chart depicting the different types of beam damage from electron and ion beams,
including charging, electrostatic discharge, heating, structural damage, etc. (Egerton
et al., 2004).
Ion beams are used in CMOS failure analysis to cut holes in the wafer and expose
underlying features for examination (Fleetwood, 2000). The beam can cause process damage to structures and devices near the location where the beam touches the
wafer. Figure 9.21 shows a plot of an NMOS transistor damaged by exposure to an
ion beam (Cambell et al., 1997). The subthreshold current is shifted to the left,
showing evidence of the creation of a large number of fixed oxide charges by the
damage. Accidental transistor damage during failure analysis can confound failure
analysis, leading to false conclusions.
Ion and electron beam processing are sometimes used in CMOS processing for
purposes other than ion implantation, such as curing films (Pyo & Kim, 2013).
Figure 9.22 shows an example of electron beam degradation causing a threshold
voltage shift, with little or no dependence on the antenna ratio, indicating a mechanism other than charging.

Table 9.2 Damage effects from electron and ion beams
Electron irradiation damage
Elastic scattering (electron–nucleus)
Atomic displacement (knock on)
Sputtering

Electrostatic charging
Inelastic scattering (electron–electron)
Impact heating

Mass loss

225

9.9 Bond Pad Damage
Bond pads naturally form an antenna structure due to the large area required to connect the chip to the outside world. Therefore, the bond pad must be a relatively large
minimum size as required by assembly processes and probe card requirements. A
typical bond pad size is around 50–100 μm in length and width. Devices and structures connected to a bond pad at the chip to the external world interface are more
likely to be damaged than regions internal to the chip. A bond pad on a product chip
is connected to electrostatic discharge (ESD) protection circuits that protect the chip
from damage originating from outside of the chip. The ESD protection circuits also
tend to partially protect against process damage after the circuit is completed during
processing, making product bond pads more resistant to process damage.
Bond pads also connect to parametric test structures placed in the scribe line or
other wafer regions. However, ESD protection circuits are not generally added to
parametric bond pads because they interfere with some parametric measurements.
In addition, the lack of ESD protection circuits makes parametric structures more
likely to be damaged. As a result, parametric structures can show severe electrical
shifts or variations while the product chip is unaffected, creating a potential misdiagnosis of chip failure mechanisms (Lee et al., 2009).
As a result of this requirement, processes performed during or after bond pad
formation must be carefully optimized to minimize process damage. The process
steps related to the bond pad formation that are likely to create process damage
include bond pad metal etch, passivation deposition, passivation removal from the
pad, etc.
Additionally, pads much smaller than bond pads with dimensions of a few
microns are used for microprobing to characterize the electrical behavior of the
product and parametric devices. These pads are less likely to cause process damage.
Comparison of identical structures with microprobe pads to regular bond pads can
provide insights into pad-related process damage.

9.10 General Considerations for Plasma Processing
Plasma processes are used ubiquitously throughout a CMOS process flow for many
different purposes, including film deposition, etching, cleaning, etc. The processing
tools used for different purposes have some common elements and common behaviors affecting process damage. This section explores several common elements of
plasma processing that affect process damage.

226

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

9.10.1 Equivalent Circuit for a Plasma System
A wafer placed in a plasma is electrically coupled to the plasma, creating an electrical interaction between the chamber, plasma, wafer, and structures on the surface of
the wafer. Figure 9.23 shows a simple circuit schematic for the convolution of these
elements (Cheung, 1999, pg. 63). The plasma in the chamber is an electrical conductor due to the large concentration of electrons, ions, and radicals. The plasma is
surrounded by a sheath or dark space where a high electric field exists containing
few charge carriers. The sheath electrically behaves as a capacitor labeled Csheath on
either side of the plasma. There is a circuit element consisting of a blocking capacitor connected to the radio frequency (rf) supply, used to ensure that high voltages
are dropped across the sheath. This element is labeled Cblock in the figure. The CMOS
element subject to process damage forms a dielectric labeled Cox in the figure. A
direct current (DC) voltage bias is commonly applied across the plasma in the system. The voltage can be high, sometimes reaching approximately a thousand volts.
This voltage level can quickly destroy a CMOS wafer. However, most of the voltage
drops across the plasma sheath, and only a small voltage reaches the wafer, rarely
causing damage. Process tools are optimized to couple appropriate voltages to the
wafer backside. The backside condition can affect the equivalent circuit by adding
a backside capacitor if there is a dielectric on the backside.
Plasma damage is caused by a combination of the plasma processing system and
the CMOS wafer. The complexity of the phenomenon is partly due to the interaction
of the wafer controlled by the CMOS manufacturing company and the processing
tool controlled by the tool vendor. Combining the two factors in modeling or simulation is difficult and not often achieved. Cox represents the gate dielectric in
Fig. 9.23, as the CMOS manufacturer controls this component, and the equipment
manufacturers and the recipe control the rest of the capacitors in the figure.

Fig. 9.23 Equivalent circuit for a wafer in a plasma system

9.10

General Considerations for Plasma Processing

227

9.10.2 Alternating Current Bias
A plasma system commonly uses a radio frequency (RF) signal that couples to the
floating wafer. For example, a frequency of 13.56 MHz is commonly used. The bias
across the wafer varies positively and negatively following the RF signal. The stress
and process damage to the CMOS structures vary depending on the AC cycle.
Figure 9.24 shows a sketch of AC waveforms creating process damage following
the applied RF signal (Cheung, 1999, pg. 62). The floating wafer capacitively coupled to the RF signal follows the signal. Electron current spikes occur over a portion
of the RF cycling, creating dynamic time-varying process damage. The combination
of direct current (DC) bias and the RF signal must be carefully optimized to minimize process damage.

9.10.3 Across-Wafer Uniformity
Modern processing tools are highly optimized to achieve a uniform plasma across
the wafer. Variations in the plasma across the wafer can cause a wide variation in
process damage. Plasma variation can cause charge to flow into the wafer in some
regions and out of the wafer in other regions. This nonuniformity can cause considerable variation in the electrical damage and symptoms across the wafer. Current
flows in the bulk of the silicon wafer as charge flows in and out of the wafer in different regions. Figure 9.25 shows a circuit schematic showing the current flow under
nonuniform plasma conditions (Cheung, 1999). Due to plasma damage, the

Fig. 9.24 Sketch showing AC electron current process damage following the RF waveform offset
by the DC bias. Vp is the plasma potential, Vd is the DC bias, and Vf is the floating potential

228

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.25 Circuit model for current flow in a wafer exposed to a nonuniform plasma showing current going into the wafer at the center and out of the wafer at the wafer edge. Cant represents the
antenna capacitance, and Cgate represents the gate dielectric capacitance. Arrows show the direction
of current flow in this example

threshold voltage may increase or decrease on the wafer edge due to electron injection into the gate dielectric, while the center of the wafer shifts in the opposite
direction.
The across-wafer signature in electrical parameters for a nonuniform plasma
typically varies widely. Figure 9.26 shows a probability plot of the Vt shift across the
wafer for a floating gate plasma damage finfet test structure (Wu et al., 2015). Vt
shifts in both positive and negative directions across the wafer due to the change in
the direction of the charging current.
Modern semiconductor fabrication tools have been heavily optimized to expose
the wafer to a uniform plasma over the entire surface to mitigate the cross-wafer
process damage variation. The plasma must be uniform across the wafer for all
process conditions, including starting and stopping the plasma. Starting or stopping
the plasma can create a transient nonuniformity, causing process damage at the
beginning or end of the plasma recipe. However, variation in process damage across
the wafer is quite common, as shown in Fig. 9.27. The Vt variation across the wafer
is recorded by a finfet based floating gate structure with connected metal antennas
(Tsai et al., 2018). There is a difference in the across-wafer signatures depending on
the metal layer.

9.10

General Considerations for Plasma Processing

229

Fig. 9.26 Plot showing the nonuniform back end of line plasma damage induced Vt shift for a
floating gate nonvolatile memory cell across the wafer in a finfet technology

Fig. 9.27 Plot comparing plasma damage Vt variation across a wafer recorded by a nonvolatile
memory detection test structure for two different layers of metal (M1 and M5)

230

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

9.10.4 Electrostatic Chuck
Semiconductor fabrication tools must hold the wafer securely inside the tool for
processing. Electrostatic chucks hold the wafer in place with electrostatic force.
This technique is superior to other methods, such as clamping the wafer for several
reasons, including reduced particle generation, reduced wafer breakage, improved
edge of wafer processing, backside cooling, etc. Electric charge is injected into the
wafer chuck to clamp the wafer firmly. The operation of the electrostatic chuck creates the potential for process damage in addition to the plasma used in the processing. Lift pins raise the wafer from the chuck and can cause process damage by
changing wafer potentials.
Figure 9.28 shows sketches of an electrostatic chuck operation (Nojiri, 2012).
Figure 9.28a shows a unipolar electrostatic chuck. A voltage is applied to the chuck,
inducing a sheet of charge on the back of the wafer that clamps the wafer to the
chuck. Figure 9.28b shows a sketch of a bipolar electrostatic chuck. The bipolar
chuck operates by applying a voltage potential difference between two wafer
regions, inducing a positive charge in one region of the wafer and a negative charge
in another region, and clamping the wafer to the chuck. Figure 9.28b shows the
charge induced by a bipolar clamp with a positive charge on one portion of the
wafer and a negative charge on another. The bipolar clamp can create a voltage
potential variation across the wafer, affecting the electrical stress on process
damage-­sensitive structures.
High voltages operate an electrostatic chuck by inducing a charge in the wafer,
creating an electrostatic force that holds the wafer on the chuck. For a bipolar chuck,
opposite polarities are applied to the two electrodes, inducing an opposite polarity
charge across the wafer. Figure 9.29 shows an example waveform (Nojiri, 2012). At
the end of the process, the chuck polarities are reversed to flip the electrostatic force
to release the wafer. Process damage can occur if the high voltages in the electrostatic chuck affect devices on the wafer surface.

Fig. 9.28 Sketch of the operation of an electrostatic chuck. (a) Sketch and schematic of a unipolar
electrostatic chuck. (b) Sketch and schematic of a bipolar electrostatic chuck

9.10

General Considerations for Plasma Processing

231

Fig. 9.29 Electrostatic chuck voltage waveform for an etch process using a bipolar chuck. The
blue line is the voltage applied to the positive electrode, and the green line is the waveform applied
to the negative electrode

The wafer can experience a voltage and current spike when electrostatically
clamped or declamped, creating a process damage event. Therefore, the recipe must
be optimized to prevent process damage. The waveform typically requires a slow,
gentle voltage ramp to prevent process damage and optimization of the plasma start
conditions. Figure 9.30 shows a histogram of voltages measured with a special dedicated nonvolatile memory cell test wafer (Siu et al., 2002). A nonoptimized recipe
has a significant threshold voltage of around 15 volts, indicating severe plasma
damage and an optimized recipe has a threshold voltage of around 2 volts, indicating significantly reduced process damage. The 2 V shift of the NVM structure cannot predict the actual threshold shift on a CMOS transistor. The plasma start
conditions played a critical role in the damage, in this case, causing yield loss. The
lift pins inadvertently touched the wafer during processing, worsening the problem.
The chucking process may change the wafer map of plasma damage failures by
creating variation in potential across the back of the wafer and inducing a current in
the wafer. Figure 9.31 shows a wafer map of a unipolar chuck recipe tunnel dielectric breakdown (Lee et al., 2012). The breakdown voltages are degraded on the edge
of the wafers in the unipolar chuck group, while a bipolar chuck recipe had no edge
of wafer breakdown problems. However, in this example, the chuck configuration is
confounded with other process parameters changed by the chucking method, such
as power.

9.10.5 Plasma Ignition and Extinction
Starting and stopping the plasma in a process tool is a critical step that can cause
process damage. The ignition and extinction of the plasma are problematic steps in
the recipe that control the plasma uniformity and avoid process damage. This transient step can cause process damage. Therefore, optimization of the recipe is
required to minimize process damage. Figure 9.32 shows examples of voltage
waveforms measured in situ for various plasma start-up conditions (Cheung, 1999,
pg. 218).

9.10.6 Recipe Transients
Plasma transients can occur between recipe steps, in addition to the plasma ignition
and termination described in Sect. 9.10.5. The plasma conditions, gas flow, etc.,
may change as the etch recipe transitions between recipe steps, creating a spike in
the current and voltage across a stressed structure such as the gate dielectric.
Additionally, the plasma conditions may undergo an abrupt transition when the
etched surface conditions change. For example, near the endpoint, the etched pattern may transition from a solid conductor to a patterned line and space pattern
where the only exposed conductor is the line edges. The pattern transition may
induce a transient in the plasma conditions, inducing a spike in the current and
voltage across the antenna. Figure 9.33 shows a simulation of the current flowing
through a gate dielectric as the wafer transitions from a solid pattern to an etched

Fig. 9.32 Examples of waveforms for various plasma ignition and extinction conditions. (a)
Differences in the ignition waveform with unstable chamber pressure and stable chamber pressure.
(b) Differences in the plasma extinction waveform with different chamber pressures (mTorr) showing a voltage spike with decreased pressure

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.33 Simulated current (Jtn) through a gate dielectric shows a transient spike at the point
where the pattern transitions from a solid conductor to a patterned conductor. (a) With a floating
substrate. (b) With a grounded substrate

pattern as the endpoint occurs (Shul & Pearton, 2000, pg. 294). Minimal current
flows through the dielectric when there is a solid conductor before the endpoint.
After the endpoint, the current can only flow through the pattern edges, creating a
current spike. As shown in the figure, the current spike is much worse with a
grounded substrate than with a floating substrate.

General Considerations for Plasma Processing

9.10.7 Magnetic Fields
Magnetic fields are often used in CMOS processing tools to increase the plasma
density in some types of semiconductor fabrication equipment. The motion of individual particles in a plasma is controlled by the Lorentz force consisting of a magnetic field and an electric field (Shul & Pearton, 2000). The low-mass electron
follows the magnetic field lines in a helical path, as shown in Fig. 9.34a (Nojiri,
2012). Some semiconductor tools contain a permanent magnet or temporary electrically controlled magnetic field. Figure 9.34b shows a sketch of an etch chamber
with a permanent magnet as an example. The magnetic field causes the electrons to
flow across the wafer, as shown in Fig. 9.34c. A magnetic field-driven chamber
configuration can sometimes cause plasma density nonuniformity, playing a role in
plasma damage.
Figure 9.35 shows an example of plasma damage caused by a magnetic field in a
magnetron-reactive ion etch system (Nojiri, 2012). There is a considerable improvement in the flatband voltage shift and gate dielectric breakdown voltage without a

Fig. 9.34 Electron trajectory in a magnetic field. (a) Helical electron path in a magnetic field. (b)
Sketch of a Tokyo Electron (TEL) dipole ring magnet (DRM) etch chamber showing the magnetic
field lines across a wafer. (c) Electron path across the wafer surface in the TEL DRM

Fig. 9.35 Example of magnetic field-dependent plasma damage in a magnetron reactive ion etching system. (a) Chamber sketch and a plot of flat band voltage shift with and without a magnetic
field as a position across the wafer. (b) Gate electric breakdown with and without a magnetic field
as a function of wafer position

236

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

magnetic field. This type of chamber configuration is mostly obsolete partially due
to plasma damage problems.

9.10.8 Plasma Damage in Exposed Silicon Regions
The layout pattern can also cause variation across the die and wafer. For example,
an open region where the silicon surface is exposed can provide a direct path
between the plasma and substrate (Shul & Pearton, 2000, pg 259). An example is
shown in Fig. 9.36, where current flowing through the gate dielectric can follow a
path through the substrate to an exposed silicon region. This local conduction can
cause a process damage pattern that can follow the layout or distance to the exposed
silicon regions. A nonuniform plasma between the gate and the open substrate can
create conditions where the ion and electron fluxes between the two locations are
different, aggravating the plasma damage.
Scribe line structures with exposed silicon placed between the chips on the wafer
can result in processing differences affecting process damage. These types of structures are commonly used in scribe lines for alignment marks, metrology marks, etc.
Sometimes, the underlying silicon can be directly exposed on the surface of the
wafer, which is different from the chip patterning covering the wafer.

9.10.9 Wafer Bevel and Backside Substrate Effects
The wafer substrate can play a significant role in process damage (Carrere et al.,
2003). The wafer can serve as a current conduction path between features on the
wafer surface or between the CMOS wafer and the processing chamber. Figure 9.37
shows a wafer sketch showing the current flowing through the wafer between different metal lines. Current flow can also occur between the structures being damaged

Fig. 9.36 Process cross-section sketch showing exposed silicon creating a conduction path from
the gate antenna to an exposed substrate region. The plasma-induced wafer current flows from the
gate dielectric to the exposed silicon region or in the reverse direction

9.10

General Considerations for Plasma Processing

237

Fig. 9.37 Wafer sketch showing current flow through the substrate during plasma damage

and the processing chamber. The wafer’s resistivity can be a crucial factor in process damage caused by across-wafer nonuniformity, with high-resistivity wafers
having a significant voltage difference between the injection and collection regions.
Low-resistivity substrates have the propensity for a large damaging current flow
between locations.
The edge of the wafer and any exposed region where bare silicon is exposed can
play a significant role in process damage. An exposed substrate region allows a
direct path for the plasma to reach the wafer, allowing current to flow in the wafer
that interacts with the CMOS structures, impacting process damage.
The edge of a wafer is manufactured with a bevel where the edge angles are
optimized to avoid abrupt angles, reducing defects and fractures of the wafer edge.
In addition, processing on the bevel is optimized to eliminate yield-killing defects
peeling from this region of the wafer, where the wafer edge shape creates processing
conditions much different from those of the flat region of the wafer. The difference
in wafer processing can result in the wafer edge structures having exposed silicon,
which affects process damage.
The backside of the wafer has different films deposited on it as the front side is
processed. These films can electrically isolate the wafer backside from the wafer
chuck holding it during processing. However, dielectric films on the backside of the
wafer can create a capacitor between the wafer and the chuck. This inadvertent
capacitor can modify the plasma processing equivalent circuit shown in Fig. 9.23,
adding a capacitor in series to the circuit. The additional capacitor can adversely or
positively affect process damage depending on the impact on the processing conditions. The presence of a dielectric on the backside of the wafer can change the
plasma damage characteristics, improving or degrading the damage. The absence of
films on the backside may increase the voltage and stress across the CMOS devices.
Films on the backside may mitigate the damage by decreasing the current stress or
increase the damage by shifting the plasma conditions. Figure 9.38 compares the
damage caused by a via etch with different backside conditions by comparing the
threshold voltage shift difference between an antenna structure and a reference

238

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.38 Plot showing an increased Vt shift and variation for a conductive backside compared to
an insulating backside during a via etch

Fig. 9.39 Cross-section sketch showing a focus ring at the edge of the wafer used to modify the
plasma for improved uniformity

structure (Cheung, 1999). The threshold voltage variation and shift may increase
with a conductive backside compared to an insulating backside.
Modern processing tools are heavily optimized to enable a uniform process
across the entire wafer. As a result, the wafer edge physically and electrically disrupts the plasma processes. Modifications to the processing chamber are performed
to mitigate the wafer edge disruption. These modifications can improve process
damage by making the plasma uniform but can cause process damage, specifically
at the wafer edge. A chamber design may contain a focus ring or other techniques
used to modify the plasma at the wafer edge to improve process uniformity. The
difference in the plasma at the edge of the wafer can modify the plasma damage
(Yang et al., 2017). Figure 9.39 shows a sketch of a plasma chamber with a focus
ring at the edge of the wafer that improves uniformity by modifying the electric and
magnetic behavior. The focus ring must minimize process damage while improving
uniformity.

9.10

General Considerations for Plasma Processing

239

Fig. 9.40 Cross-section sketch of a wafer edge and bevel. The arrow points to the chip region
attacked at the wafer edge

The wafer bevel at the edge of the wafer accumulates and loses films during
processing in a somewhat erratic and uncontrolled manner as the process is optimized for processing the chip and not the wafer edge. As a result, the wafer bevel is
sometimes electrically isolated from the plasma and tool when it is covered with
dielectrics. Other times, the bevel is directly exposed to the plasma and chuck. As
the wafer transitions between exposed and insulated edges, the wafer’s electrical
characteristics in the plasma are changed, potentially increasing plasma damage. In
addition, electrical currents may intermittently flow through the wafer when this
transition occurs. As a result, the bevel conditions during processing may significantly impact process damage and potentially create a worst-case arcing condition.
Figure 9.40 shows a sketch of the bevel on the edge of a wafer (Ma, 2003). The
metal structures on the right side of the sketch can be intermittently covered and
uncovered, creating a transient damage condition where current flows through
the wafer.
Lithography tools use a scanner or stepper job (program) to control the location
on the wafer where the chips are placed. The stepper job controls the placement of
each reticle field on the wafer. The job is typically optimized to avoid printing partial chips on the wafer and reduce the number of locations where a partial chip exists
at the bevel. This optimization improves particle problems and reduces the occurrence of problem areas like the one shown in Fig. 9.40.

9.10.10 Chamber Cleaning
The chamber in a plasma processing system can inadvertently deposit materials and
byproducts inside the chamber as many wafers are processed. The unintended deposition increases as each wafer is processed, increasing the cumulative build-up

240

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.41 Plot showing a pmos threshold voltage shift as a function of the number of wafers
processed

inside the chamber. Control of the deposits in a chamber requires periodic maintenance to clean the processing chamber to remove the deposited materials. The clean
can be performed manually by opening the chamber or in situ by cleaning the chamber with a plasma step. Depending on the cleaning requirements, the in situ clean
can be performed with a product wafer, dummy wafer, or no wafer in the chamber.
The processing conditions may change as material builds up in the chamber, affecting process damage. Figure 9.41 shows an example of processing damage increasing with the number of wafers processed between cleans in a metal etch chamber,
showing a threshold voltage shift and a large increase in variability (Krishnan
et al., 1996).

9.10.11 Topology Effects
Plasma damage interacts with structures on the wafer surface. Three-dimensional
structures can have significantly different plasma damage from structures with no
topology. Some CMOS technologies, such as finfets, DRAM, and three-­dimensional
NAND, extensively use three-dimensional structures that tend to aggravate plasma
damage conditions. For example, Fig. 9.42 compares the plasma damage during a
plasma nitridation process for a planar transistor and a three-dimensional recessed-­
gate transistor. The gate dielectric of the 3D transistor is heavily degraded by a low-­
pressure process, resulting in a low charge to breakdown (Cho et al., 2007).

9.11

Plasma Film Deposition

241

Fig. 9.42 Comparison of plasma damage between a planar structure and a three-dimensional
recessed gate structures during the gate dielectric plasma nitridation (used in DRAM). (a) Sketch
of a recessed gate structure. (b) Probability plot of charge to breakdown (QBD) comparing a planar
and a 3D transistor for two different pressure plasma nitridation processes

Plasma processing in 3D structures can differ substantially from that of planar
structures, with ions and electrons reflecting off surfaces, different plasma densities
inside 3D features, etc. Processing finfets (see Fig. 9.58), DRAMs, 3D NAND, etc.,
can be challenging with the added propensity for damage because of the tall 3D
structure (see Fig. 9.62 and Chap. 3).

9.11 Plasma Film Deposition
Plasma processing is commonly used for film deposition because of its high
throughput and other capabilities. The surface of the wafer is exposed to the plasma
during the deposition. Process damage can occur due to high-energy light or wafer
charging (Lee et al., 2004). There is a significant difference in process damage
between the deposition of conductors and that of insulators. Plasma film deposition
is ubiquitous throughout a CMOS process, and is commonly used for the deposition
of dielectrics and less frequently for conductors. Table 9.3 shows a partial list of
films known to cause process damage when deposited with a plasma process.

9.11.1 Plasma Dielectric Deposition
For dielectrics, damage is more likely to occur when processing begins and structures on the wafer surface are exposed. After some of the insulating film is deposited, the film protects the structure from direct charge as the insulator prevents
current flow onto the underlying structures. As the dielectric film deposition

progresses, the underlying conductors are covered with the increasing dielectric
film, and the plasma no longer directly contacts the metal layer (Cheung, 1999). The
plasma capacitively couples to the metal layer through the deposited dielectric
(Cdiel), creating the potential for plasma damage even after the conductor is covered,
as charge is coupled into the metal layer and associated antenna, driving current into
the gate dielectric. Figure 9.43 shows a sketch of a metal line with a dielectric
deposited on it and an equivalent circuit with the additional capacitive coupling.
Coupling through the deposited dielectric can generate plasma damage throughout the deposition process, continuing to cause damage with increasing thickness.
Figure 9.44 shows a plot of a hot carrier reliability test plotted against the deposited
dielectric thickness. The reliability is degraded with continued deposition. Several
potential causes for the degradation include capacitive coupling, high-energy photons, increased photoconduction, or barrier lowering (Cheung, 2001).
A large spectrum of plasma deposition process options are used in CMOS processing. Consequently, there can be a considerable variation in process damage
across the potential processes for similar applications. Figure 9.45 shows an

example of two different dielectric deposition processes with a significant difference in plasma damage results (Cheung, 1999, pg. 174). Optimization of the process, tool, etc., is commonly required to correct plasma damage during processing.

9.11.2 Plasma Conductor Deposition
There are very few publications on plasma damage during plasma conductor deposition. During deposition, a conducting film covers the wafer throughout the deposition process, starting with the initial few atomic layers. The conductors are in
electrical contact with the underlying structure throughout the deposition process
and can prevent charge accumulation, which can cause plasma damage. In addition,
plasma processing typically involves less energy at the wafer surface than does
physical vapor deposition (PVD) or sputtering. These factors make plasma damage
less likely for plasma conductor deposition. Conductors typically block light, making high-energy light damage less likely for plasma conductor deposition.

9.11.3 High-Density Plasma Deposition
Some plasma film deposition processes are optimized for good film step coverage
and gap-fill. For example, high-density plasma (HDP) film deposition is used in
some process modules such as shallow trench isolation (STI) or subtractive metallization, where good gap-fill characteristics are required (Wolf, 2002). An HDP process used for gap-fill applications includes a sputtering component that facets the
sharp corners of the dielectric to expand the opening, enabling better gap-fill. The
HDP process typically oscillates between deposition and faceting, potentially
exposing the metal corners many times. Additionally, coupling through the dielectric during processing can worsen the damage. Process damage for an HDP process
can occur at the beginning of the deposition, where metallization is directly exposed
to the plasma, or at any time during the process, where the corners of the feature are
exposed, creating a path for the charge to flow into the corners of the conductor
(Ackaert et al., 2000). Figure 9.46 shows a sketch of an HDP gap-filling progression
where the corners of the metal may become exposed.

9.12 Sputter Deposition
Physical deposition techniques known as sputter or physical vapor deposition
(PVD) are used in specific CMOS process applications like the deposition of metals. The sputtering process is a physical process using kinetic energy imparted into
noble gas ions to knock ions off of a target and onto the wafer surface, depositing a

9.12 Sputter Deposition

245

Fig. 9.46 Sketch showing the progression of dielectric deposition in a high-density plasma dielectric deposition using a sputtering component for gap fill between metal lines

Fig. 9.47 Sketch showing the operation of a sputter deposition system

film on the wafer surface. Figure 9.47 shows a sketch of sputter deposition. The
metal and noble gas ions impact the wafer surface, transferring charge to the wafer
and may cause process damage.
Energetic ions impacting the wafer surface can create defects, including traps
and interface states that degrade oxide quality. Figure 9.48 compares PVD gate
metal deposition with a low-pressure chemical vapor deposition (LPCVD) polysilicon deposition (Ushiki et al., 1998). The LPCVD process uses a high-temperature
thermal process to deposit films without plasma components. The charge to breakdown, which is a measure of dielectric quality, is degraded with PVD. Changing the

246

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.48 Probability plot of gate dielectric charge to breakdown (QBD) showing a comparison of
a sputtered gate metallization with a low-pressure chemical vapor deposition (LPCVD) polysilicon

sputtering ion from argon to xenon has improved QBD considerably. The heavier
xenon atom can provide a similar performance to the lighter argon atom using less
accelerating energy, damaging the substrate less and reducing the damage.
Sputter deposition can also generate high-energy photons, including secondary
X-rays capable of damaging the transistors and dielectrics (Janesick, 2001).

9.13 Plasma Etch-Induced Process Damage
9.13.1 Introduction
Plasma etching is a widespread cause of process damage. The wafer is bombarded
with ions, electrons, and radicals, which can charge structures on the wafer as
described in Sect. 9.10. Other sources of process damage from plasma etching
include high-energy photon damage, kinetic damage, and the knock-on of atoms
into the wafer from the plasma or materials on the wafer surface. Figure 9.49 shows
a sketch highlighting the different ways plasma etching can damage a wafer. Modern
CMOS processes require high-energy plasmas to achieve straight profiles in the
high-aspect ratio features etched into the wafer. High-energy processes can achieve
high throughput, resulting in a lower wafer cost.
Plasma etching is performed many times throughout a CMOS manufacturing
flow, increasing the likelihood of damage (Shul & Pearton, 2000). Many etch steps
routinely cause plasma and process damage, including isolation-related etches,
gate-related etches, contact and via etches, metal etches, bond pad etches, etc. Most
electrical damage mechanisms described previously can be caused by plasma
etching.

9.13 Plasma Etch-Induced Process Damage

247

Fig. 9.49 Sketch showing possible sources of process and plasma damage during plasma etch

The damage mechanisms described in Fig. 9.49 include charging damage from
ion and electron charging of the wafer surface and high-energy photon damage (Ma,
2004). Residuals from the masking materials and polymer deposition from the etch
byproducts can contaminate the wafer surface. The masking materials may contain
contaminants like mobile ions. The ion kinetic energy can sputter atoms from the
surface of the wafer, contaminating nearby structures. Additionally, surface atoms
can be knocked onto locations deeper into the substrate.

9.13.2 Overetch
Overetch is an additional etch time added to the etch recipe, which etches for an
additional amount of time beyond the point where the etch nominally clears the
material from the wafer (Wang, 2004). The additional time provides a margin to
ensure that the complete pattern is etched into the entire wafer, accounting for variation across patterns, time, etchers, etc. Finally, etch recipes terminate by commonly
using a fixed time or an endpoint that detects the changing conditions of the plasma
as the wafer nears completion.
If a conductor is etched, the entire wafer is covered with a conductor at the start
of the etch step. During the initial segment of the etching process, the entire wafer
serves as a giant connected antenna, limiting damage. The charging current can flow
through all dielectrics capable of conducting current under the charging conditions.
The charge may flow through all conducting regions of the wafer in a distributed
manner, or if a low resistance defective path exists, the current can flow through the
defect, discharging the entire wafer charge energy locally with a high likelihood of
damage. In addition, any defects with weak structures may discharge the entire
wafer’s charge, creating a locally damaged region.

248

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Near the end of the etch, close to the endpoint, the conductor is completely
etched in some wafer regions, while other regions contain remaining material still
to be etched. The antennas in the etched regions are entirely formed and can amplify
the plasma charging. The fully formed antennas can collect charge only on the edges
as the top of the antenna is covered with nonconducting resist or other masking
material preventing charging from the top. The edges may not be vertical before the
etch completion, increasing the collection area of the conductor sidewall.
The antennas etched into a conductor vary dynamically with time, changing area,
or perimeter. As a result, the dynamic antenna can differ from the final pattern. This
variation can cause damage that differs from the straightforward antenna ratio calculation in nonobvious ways.
The process damage during the overetch can also be a function of the design
rules, as wide-open areas tend to overetch faster, increasing the degree of process
damage. Once the entire wafer is etched, all antennas are formed, and process damage from the patterned antenna occurs over the entire wafer on all the structures.
A simple pattern of repeating lines and spaces may have significant etch variation between lines due to these effects, as shown in Fig. 9.50 based on computer
simulations (Shul & Pearton, 2000). The line and space patterns are shown with

decays to a steady state value.

9.13.3 Layout and Topology Dependent Process Damage
Plasma etch processes have a natural variation in the local etch rate which is dependent on the space between features and the pattern density. The difference in behavior between ions and electrons at the wafer surface contributes to this phenomenon.
The electrons impact the wafer over an extensive range of angles due to its low
mass, repulsion by the sheath, and negative charge trapped in the masking layer. The

250

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

ions tend to be highly collimated, impacting the wafer over a narrow range of angles.
The electron and ion difference causes a difference in the etch performance in narrow regions.
Figure 9.52 is a sketch comparing the impact angle difference between electrons
and ions (Shul & Pearton, 2000). The low-mass, high-energy electrons with a large
mean free path can impact the surface of the wafer at different angles. These etch
differences cause a difference in the etch performance, and plasma damage creates
a difference in process damage. Figure 9.52d compares the angular distributions of
electrons and ions based on a simulation.
The ions impact the wafer over a narrow range, while electrons impact the wafer
at all angles. The ions impact the wafer unidirectionally for an open region of the
wafer, and the electrons impact the wafer isotropically, as shown in Fig. 9.52a.
Figure 9.52b shows the ion and electron impact on the edge of a patterned feature.
The electrons impact the etched regions over a 90-degree angle, while the ions
impact vertically. Figure 9.52c shows the ion and electron impact in a small space
between features, with electrons and ions both impacting the wafer vertically. The
electrons tend to be trapped in the resist layer, while the ions become trapped on the
wafer surface. These differences in impact angles create a difference in etch performance and damage. The ions and electrons trapped in the features bend the ion
beam and cause reflections, modifying the charging and patterning. Figure 9.52d
shows the angular distributions of electrons and ions in a plasma system.

Fig. 9.52 Sketch showing the difference in electron (Je) and ion angle (Ji). (a) Angles of ions and
electrons impacting a flat wafer. (b) Angles of ions and electrons on the side of a feature. (c) Angles
of ions and electrons in a small space. Dashed-line arrows show the electron flux and solid-line
arrows depict the ion flux. The decreasing length of the dashed lines indicates a smaller electron
flux. The symbols + and − depict where electrons and ions become trapped on the wafer surface
and in the resist. (d) Plot showing the angle distribution and ions and electrons

9.13 Plasma Etch-Induced Process Damage

251

This effect results in a difference in antenna effects with variation in layout.
Figure 9.53 shows the etch time required to clear different spaces (Cheung, 1999).
Smaller spaces require longer etch times as fewer charged species impact the wafer
over a narrow range of angles. Conversely, wider spaces allow for an extensive
range of angles, etching faster. As a result, the structures with larger spaces undergo
longer over-etches and increased plasma damage. Figure 9.54 shows an example
plot of the etch time required as a function of the space between lines, showing the
longer etch needed for smaller spaces (Simon et al., 2000). Micro-trenching

Fig. 9.53 Sketch showing etch differences between a large space and s small space due to electron
and ion angle differences

Fig. 9.54 Example plot showing the increase in etch time required to clear a space. Smaller spaces
require increased etch time

252

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

sometimes occurs in small features as ions and electrons are reflected off the sidewall of the feature. This effect can change the pattern dependence on plasma damage.
This effect can vary widely as a function of the antenna layout, changing with
antenna pitch, connections between lines, and variations in topology. As described
in Sect. 9.13.3, the antenna geometry can vary from the start of the damaging
process step to the end, particularly during a subtractive conductor etch step. For
example, on a patterned conductor structure, the etch time for a subtractive metal
etch may be a function of the metal space, as shown in Fig. 9.54. The metal
etched in wide spaces is removed entirely first, while the metal in small spaces is
removed entirely later. Therefore, the antenna area and edge change as different
features complete the etch step with the removal of the conductor being etched.
The worst-­case antenna layout may not occur at a minimum pitch, and multiple
test structures with different pitches are needed to determine the worstcase layout.
Additionally, the conducting lines do not have to be connected to the final antenna
to impact the process damage, as shown in Fig. 9.55 (Simon et al., 2000). Figure 9.55
shows the drive current shift for a layout with the metal lines connected to the
antenna in different configurations. Figure 9.55 shows that damage still occurs as a
function of space even though the metal lines are not connected to the final antenna
pattern. The isolated unconnected patterns form temporary island antennas during
the etch process, as shown in Fig. 9.56 (Simon et al., 2000).

etched. (b) Only a single line antenna is formed in a larger pitch

9.13.4 Etch Rate Process Damage
CMOS process recipes are generally optimized for maximum efficiency and
throughput. The etch rate of the material is increased for efficiency. Unfortunately,
high throughput creates the worst-case conditions for process damage, as the power,
bias, etc., are increased to achieve high efficiency, also increasing the current flow
of the damaging charge onto the wafer. Additionally, fast processing leaves less time
for the surface charge to leak away, increasing the damage probability. The etch rate
can often be correlated with process damage, giving a strong clue pointing to the
root cause of related problems. Figure 9.57 plots yield loss for a capacitor with a
large via antenna increasing with increasing etch rate (Cheung, 1999).

9.13.5 Spacer Etch Damage
CMOS processes commonly use dielectric spacers to offset source-drain implants
from the gate to improve electrical characteristics and reliability. The spacer offsets
edge damage from the gate edge during the spacer etch. The spacers used to optimize the CMOS transistor electrical performance require optimum etch processing.
The gate, source, and drain may be directly exposed to the plasma during the spacer
etch, creating a high probability of plasma damage. The spacer etch may directly
attack the semiconductor substrate, which can cause physical damage to the source
and drain regions of the transistor (see Sect. 9.10.8). The spacer etch process is

254

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.57 Plot showing decreasing capacitor yield with increasing via etch rates for antenna structures with a large number of vias

critical as it must remove the dielectric spacer anisotropically with minimal silicon
loss and damage. The overetch at the end of the spacer etch recipe is a critical step
that must be optimized to minimize plasma damage.
The metal gate is commonly covered with a dielectric, which may protect it from
process damage. Process damage in this type of structure commonly occurs in the
source-drain regions. Damage to structures without a dielectric on top of the gate
can also suffer damage in the gate region. Figure 9.58 shows sketches of a planar
CMOS structure and a finfet CMOS structure showing the spacers after the gate is
processed and formed (in green). The transistor in this section of the process flow is
sensitive to damage as the transistor is open to damage.
For CMOS finfet processes, a replacement gate flow (gate-last) is typically used
to be compatible with high-k dielectrics, optimize the gate metallurgy, etc. (Lai
et al., 2009). First, a dummy gate is formed, and the spacer is deposited and formed
on the dummy gate. For finfets, the spacer etch must be longer to remove the material along the fin sidewall. The dummy gate is subsequently removed, and a gate
dielectric and metal gate are deposited in the open crevice left by the dummy gate
removal. Figure 9.59 shows a cartoon of a finfet gate formed with a replacement
gate process flow (Gulshan, 2019). A replacement gate flow has multiple steps
where damage can occur, including forming the spacer on the dummy gate, removing the dummy gate, replacing the gate with metal, etc.

9.13 Plasma Etch-Induced Process Damage

255

Fig. 9.58 Sketch showing a planar transistor and a finfet

Fig. 9.59 Sketch showing a replacement gate finfet

Figure 9.60 shows the gate leakage failure rate at a specific voltage and current
as a function of the spacer over etch (Gabriel & Weling, 1994). A dielectric is deposited on top of the gate metal before gate patterning, and spacer etch. The purpose of
this deposited layer is to protect the gate metal from plasma charging during the
spacer etch. Typically, a thicker layer on the gate protects better against
plasma damage.

9.13.6 Surface Etch Damage
The silicon surface is inevitably directly exposed during CMOS wafer fabrication.
Plasma etch steps directly touching the silicon include gate etch, spacer etch, contact etch, etc. Modern plasma etches tend to be high energy, creating highly accelerated electrons and ions. The high-energy plasma components can damage the silicon
surface through various mechanisms, including amorphizing the crystalline lattice,
knocking on atoms like oxygen from the native surface into the wafer, and implanting foreign atoms like etch ions species such as argon, halogens, etc. Figure 9.61
shows a molecular dynamic simulation with these effects (Nakakubo, 2015, pg. 33).
The wafer silicon surface becomes structurally damaged and contaminated by
the etch process, impacting the CMOS electrical parameters. CMOS processes are
optimized to minimize or eliminate surface damage. The etch recipe can operate
with reduced power as the etch intersects the surface to minimize damage. Wet or
dry cleans are added to gently remove the damaged layers.

9.13.7 Contact and Via Etches
The dielectric etch for contacts and vias connecting metal layers can also cause
plasma damage. A dielectric typically has good isolation properties, protecting any
underlying metal or semiconductor layer until all the dielectric is removed from the

9.13 Plasma Etch-Induced Process Damage

257

hole being etched. At this point, the ions in the etch can cause process damage,
beginning as the ions from the plasma contact the conductor at the bottom of the
contact hole. The amount of damage is strongly dependent on the overetch time.
Additionally, there can be a capacitive coupling component of the damage similar
to the one described in Sect. 9.11.1 that can cause a voltage to be coupled onto the
underlying conductor before the plasma contacts the conductor. This effect is typically worse for structures with many contacts or vias.
The process damage increases with increased numbers of vias or contacts connected to a common conductor. Each via or contact collects charge and transfers it
to the underlying metal, eventually reaching the transistor after being transmitted
through the underlying metal layers. Therefore, design rules sometimes limit the
maximum number of contacts and vias connected to a common metal line to minimize damage.
The conductors in a CMOS process can be at different distances from the semiconductor surface, requiring substantial amounts of overetch to remove the dielectric for the deepest contact. For example, the same contact level may be used to
contact both the gate conductor and the substrate. However, the gate contacts are
usually located farther from the substrate than the substrate contacts. As a result, the

258

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.62 Cross-section of a DRAM process flow showing a contact used to connect both the
bitline (BL) and the cell plate

gate contact undergoes plasma damage while the etch continues to remove the additional material necessary to clear the substrate contact, impacting the health of the
critical gate dielectric.
CMOS technologies with three-dimensional structures can have extremely high
aspect ratio contacts and vias. For 3D technologies such as NAND or DRAM processes require high aspect ratio contacts to reach the CMOS devices below the array
structures. Figure 9.62 shows a cross-section cartoon of a CMOS DRAM with the
interconnects (Xiao, 2012). The same contact connects the DRAM capacitor cell
plate and the bitline that runs beneath the capacitor to reduce manufacturing costs.
The cell plate capacitor contact is much farther from the silicon than the bitline
contact. Therefore, the cell plate completes etching and is exposed to the plasma
longer than the bitline contact. The cell plate is exposed to the plasma during the
time required to etch the sizeable additional amount of dielectric to expose the bitline contact, increasing the propensity for plasma damage. NAND technology, especially 3D NAND, requires different depth contacts, as shown in Chap. 8, creating a
propensity for damage.
Figure 9.63 shows some examples of process damage caused by plasma damage
during the contact etch (Tsui, 2000). Figure 9.63a shows a transistor’s gate leakage
with different contact numbers connected to the gate. The transistor with the larger
number of contacts shows increased variability in gate dielectric leakage and variation. The reduction in the gate current for about 40% of the distribution is likely due

Fig. 9.63 Plots showing increasing process damage with an increasing number of contacts connected to the gate. (a) Degraded gate current and variation with increasing number of contacts. (b)
