to trapped charge reducing the gate current. The damage can shift time-zero electrical results and create latent reliability problems that can not be detected at time zero
and can only be detected with reliability stress. Figure 9.63b shows that a hot electron reliability test can observe latent damage undetectable at time zero with worsening reliability with increasing numbers of contacts connected to the gate.
For example, Fig. 9.64 shows a degradation in dielectric breakdown for a gate
antenna structure for a directional in situ resist strip in the same chamber as the
contact etch (Ma et al., 2000). The dielectric breakdown is degraded with an increasing number of contacts, indicating increased charge flow onto the gate through more
contacts. The degradation increases with increased power during the resist strip.
Process damage in contact to silicon surfaces can occur through other mechanisms in addition to antenna charging. Contact etches remove the dielectrics over
the wafer surface and exposes the surface directly to the plasma as the etch completes the dielectric removal at the bottom of the contact hole. Exposing the wafer
surface at the bottom of the contact creates the damaged region described in Chap.
8. Surface damage in a contact hole typically degrades the contact resistance, as the
critical silicide formation is disrupted.

9.13.8 Surface Charge Creation
The dielectric films deposited on the wafer surface can adversely impact the CMOS
devices by trapping charge that electrically modify the surface properties (Li et al.,
2007). Dielectric films are used as stressors to increase channel mobility in CMOS

technology. The stress induced by the dielectric film can change the electrical properties of the film, including trapping and hydrogen sensitivity. Plasma etch removal
of surface films can cause surface charge by creating charged defects. Figure 9.65
shows an example of this phenomenon. Different transistor layouts have a different

dielectric used to block salicide formation. An experiment replacing the plasma etch

9.14 Through-Silicon Vias, Backside Power, and Advanced
Packaging Damage
Modern CMOS devices often use advanced packaging techniques to improve
CMOS chips’ packing density, speed, power dissipation, etc. Unfortunately,
advanced packaging techniques have the propensity for process damage because the
large features required for the interface to the external world can form antennas to
the tiny transistors in the CMOS chip. In addition, the trend toward backside power
distribution may increase the likelihood of damage. For example, through-silicon
vias (TSVs) enable bonding to the backside of a wafer by forming a conductor
through the entire wafer (Lee et al., 2014). Wafers are thinned before or after the
formation of the TSV to minimize processing time. A large hole is etched through
the wafer, connecting the front side of the wafer to the backside. The hole is typically filled with copper to form a low-resistance electrical connection. A connection
from the CMOS circuitry to the TSV is formed. An assembly process completes the
packaging by connecting the backside through-silicon vias to the package, forming
an interface with the external world through the package. Figure 9.66 shows a cross-­
section sketch of a wafer with a TSV connecting to the backside (Li et al., 2018).
The TSV process flow is typically lengthy and complex, consisting of multiple steps
with process damage risk. Common steps that can cause damage include TSV etch,
TSV dielectric liner deposition, TSV dielectric etch to clean theliner, Cu barrier
metal, TSV sputter clean, etc. Process steps after the TSVs are formed can also
cause damage as the chip completes the assembly process.
Figure 9.67 shows an example of process degradation from a TSV process to a
40 nm dielectric thickness MOS capacitor (Li et al., 2018). The No TSV group has

reduce the probability of process damage. Commonly, similar electrostatic discharge (ESD) protection circuits to bond pads can be placed between sensitive circuits and the TSV to partially protect the chip from damage during TSV processing,
with a layout area penalty. Figure 9.68 shows a plot comparing gate dielectric leakage, showing the benefits of TSV ESD protection (Premachandran et al., 2020).
Most TSV and backside processes occur after a wafer is completely processed,
making ESD structures effective.

9.15 Exposure to High-Energy Light
Exposure to light can cause process damage if the photon energy is sufficient to
break atomic bonds, eject electrons, etc. The damage increases with increasing photon energy. Figure 9.69 shows a sketch of some of the damage mechanisms for
photon damage (Li et al., 2004). High-energy light exposure is a common intentional component of CMOS processing used in process steps such as plasma processing, laser annealing, etc.
The temporary or permanent films on the wafer interact with the photons, absorbing or blocking the light. Therefore, a detailed analysis of the materials on the wafer
is needed to determine the propensity for damage at a particular step in the process
flow for a particular wavelength of light.
High-energy radiation, such as ultraviolet light, has sufficient energy to break the
atomic bonds in the materials present on the wafer, such as dielectrics. Defects can
be created in dielectrics, causing fixed charge, traps, etc. Transient tracks of created
electron–hole pairs can be formed in the silicon regions, creating temporary low-­
resistance conduction paths. These effects can interact with ongoing plasma damage
or create defects with light alone.
Semiconductor dielectrics are sensitive to light exposure at higher energies.
Electrons can be ejected from their bonded positions and be accelerated in any electric field. The free electrons have both the propensity to cause damage and drain
accumulated charge from antennas. Figure 9.70a shows the quantum electron yield
where free electrons are generated as a function of photon energy (Cheung, 1999,
pg. 179). Free electrons can be generated in SiO2 with photon energies greater than
2.5 eV, where a bond is occasionally broken when a photon is absorbed.

Fig. 9.69 Sketch showing different types of damage to dielectrics and semiconductors caused by
exposure to high-energy photons

9.15

Exposure to High-Energy Light

265

Fig. 9.70 (a) Photoconductivity quantum yield of silicon dioxide as a function of photon energy.
(b) Photon energy as a function of wavelenth for common atoms used in plasma processing

In addition to the increased diode leakage when the wafer is exposed to light in
a plasma, dielectric conduction is increased when the wafer is exposed to high-­
energy photons during processing. Figure 9.70a shows a plot of the photoconduction electron generation quantum yield of silicon dioxide as a function of the
wavelength. The conduction of SiO2 dramatically increases for photon energies
greater than about 9.0 eV, which is the bandgap of SiO2. Photoconduction differs for
high-k dielectrics with lower bandgaps of about 6 eV (Huff & Gilmer, 2005). Many
common gases used in plasma processing emit high-energy photons capable of
dielectric conduction and damage. Figure 9.70b shows a plot of photon energy as a
function of wavelength for common ions used in plasma processing (Cheung, 1999,
pg. 176–177). Energies greater than the gate dielectric bandgap are frequently
encountered in CMOS processing. The photoinduced dielectric leakage may mitigate process damage during plasma processing. The combination of high-­
temperature plasma processing and light exposure further increases the dielectric
leakage by more than one factor alone.
All dielectrics exposed to high-energy light from plasma exposure can be expected
to have high leakage relative to ambient conditions. Measurements have shown a
resistance level of 50-200 kΩ/cm2 for oxides in a plasma (Cismaru et al., 1999). For
comparison, an oxide dielectric resistance level under dark ambient conditions was
greater than 1012 Ω/cm2. The six orders of magnitude decrease in the dielectric resistivity increases charge loss from the antennas, potentially mitigating damage.
The higher energy photons shown in Fig. 9.70b can cause substantial damage to
dielectrics. Figure 9.71 shows a plot of C–V threshold voltage shifts caused by
8–10 eV photons in the range of common plasma ions (Fleetwood, 2013). Substantial
shifts in the order of tens of volts can occur if the gate is biased during exposure.
These conditions can occur if a dielectric is charged by damage and exposed to UV
light simultaneously, creating a worst-case damage situation without the light being
blocked by films on the wafer. The thickness of the 200 nm dielectric is similar to
that of shallow trench isolation dielectric thicknesses.

266

9

Common Sources of Process Damage

Fig. 9.71 High-frequency capacitance voltage curves for a 200 nm silicon dioxide dielectric
exposed to various UV energies while under gate bias. The gate electrode is transparent at UV
wavelengths. The undamaged device is labeled No UV

Fig. 9.72 Example of high energy photon damage to a silicon oxide nitride oxide silicon (SONOS)
nonvolatile memory. Vt vs. cycling performance for a control cell not exposed to extreme ultraviolet radiation (No EUV) is shown in red. Vt vs. cycling performance for a cell exposed to extreme
ultraviolet (EUV) radiation of 13.5 nm (91.85 eV) is shown. Partial recovery with an anneal after
EUV exposure is shown

High-energy photon damage can be observed in diverse types of structures. For
example, Fig. 9.72 shows an example of a nonvolatile memory cell cycling degradation caused by 13.5 nm wavelength photons (Tsui et al., 2011). The entire Vt window is shifted upward by the EUV exposure. The window position partially recovers
with a 600 °C anneal.

9.16 Damage Recovery by Exposure to High-Energy Light

267

9.16 Damage Recovery by Exposure to High-Energy Light
Exposure to high-energy light, such as UV radiation, can also cause healing or
recovery from process damage in addition to damage, confounding the electrical
behavior. This opposite result from UV damage can confound the understanding
of plasma damage. Exposure to UV light can reduce process damage by ejecting
trapped electrons or holes from locations where they are trapped. Energy damage
in a CMOS process can also occur from X-rays created during processing by secondary emission during processes such as sputtering (Pearton et al., 1992).
Figure 9.73 shows the dark current recovery in a charge-coupled device after
X-ray damage with exposure to UV light (Janesick, 2001). The UV light was
turned off at 100 s, and the change in slope shows that UV light played a role in
the recovery.

Fig. 9.73 Dark current recovery by exposure to UV in a charge coupled device (CCD) after x-ray
radiation exposure. The CCD is exposed to x-rays and then exposed to UV radiation at 5 eV. The
UV radiation is later turned off at the indicated point on the plot

268

Chapter 10

Inline Process Damage Measurements

10.1 Introduction
The capability to collect in-process electrical and damage-related data is advantageous. It allows rapid detection of process damage information, enabling troubleshooting and improvement, avoiding waiting months for full CMOS flow wafer
processing completion. The same parametric structures on product wafers used to
monitor process damage at the final parametric test step at the end of the process can
be modified to allow inline parametric testing with simple inline bond pads, as
described in Chap. 11.
Additionally, other inline methods can provide an indirect method of detecting
process damage. These methods do not provide direct quantitative measurements of
process damage but can provide valuable qualitative information.

10.2 Surface Potential Measurements
Plasma, ion implantation, and similar processes can inject charge into the surface of
a wafer. This charge can be retained on the wafer surface after processing for some
time if the wafer surface has suitable material conditions. The wafer surface must be
highly insulating with high-resistivity materials to prevent charge from migrating
across the surface of the wafer. After processing, the wafer must be maintained in an
environment with little moisture, low light, and over a specific temperature range to
keep charge in place on the wafer surface until the measurement is performed.
Measurements typically have to be performed quickly after exposure to plasma to
avoid charge migration or loss. Deionized water rinses (see Chap. 9) and thermal
alloys can neutralize the wafer surface charge, allowing the test wafer to be reused.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_10

273

274

10

Inline Process Damage Measurements

Fig. 10.1 Sketch of a wafer potential measurement system and a sketch showing the instrument
configuration for surface charge measurements on a wafer

Charge on the surface of a wafer can be detected electrically by placing an electrical probe close to the wafer surface connected to an electrostatic voltmeter (Shibata
et al., 2011). The voltmeter is sensitive enough to detect the potential induced on the
surface of the wafer by the charge due to processing. Figure 10.1 shows a sketch of
a measurement system. The probe tip is placed near the wafer surface. The expected
voltage can be calculated using a simple area capacitor formula.
As an example, the potential of a patterned resist layer with a 1013 ions/cm2
40 keV phosphorous implant is shown in Fig. 10.2 (Shibata et al., 2011). Charge
from the ion implant remains embedded in the resist and can be detected by electrostatic potential measurements. The amount of charge is dependent on the type of
resist. The resist is optimized for different lithography wavelengths and performance, with little or no consideration for the film’s resistivity. A smaller amount of
charge remains in the i-line resist, while the krF resist retains a significant amount
of charge. Excessive charge build-up in the resist can discharge in a destructive arc
to silicon or other structures, as shown in Chap. 5.
The charge embedded in the resist can be a function of the design rules and the
underlying layers, potentially resulting in process damage in specific patterns.
Figure 10.3 shows the surface potential across a resist and isolation pattern, with
some locations reaching 200 volts. No charge is observed on the gate oxide silicon
surface, as it would have been discharged into the wafer or implanted through the
gate dielectric (Shibata et al., 2011). This effect is the mechanism causing resist
pattern sensitivity described in Chap. 9.

10.3 Surface Parametric Measurements
Sophisticated instrumentation can enable measurement of many parameters inline
between process steps as the wafer is manufactured using very simple test wafers
(KLA-Tencor, 2002). An example of advanced inline electrical capability is the

10.3 Surface Parametric Measurements

275

Fig. 10.2 Surface potential measurement of implanted resist charge for different resist types and
implant doses measured across a region with no resist

Quantox™ system shown in Fig. 10.4 (Wu et al., 2007). This system contains three
methods to enable inline measurements (Schroder, 2001). A corona bias is used to
bias the surface of the wafer. The corona bias uses a high-voltage probe to inject
low-energy ions onto the surface of the wafer in a room atmosphere. The low-energy
ions stick to the wafer surface and tend to be temporarily immobile. The combination of the corona, dielectric, and substrate results in a structure that behaves as a
metal oxide semiconductor (MOS) capacitor. The surface potential can be driven to
specific bias conditions, allowing characterization of the capacitor. A light source in
the system enables the generation of minority carriers in the substrate. A vibrating
Kelvin probe creates an AC signal, allowing a current measurement where the current is given by Eq. (10.1), where I is the current calculated from the measurement,
Vc is the forced voltage, C is the capacitance generated by the measurement, and t
is time.
I = VC

dt

(10.1)

Surface photovoltage (SPV) measurements enable the capability to measure
minority carrier lifetime.

Surface parametric measurement capability can enable detailed rapid electrical
characterization of simple test wafers (Gupta & Horner, 2000). Table 10.1 shows a
flowchart for a simple process flow for inline measurements. Almost all of the measurements that can be performed on a MOS capacitor can be performed on a test
wafer using these techniques, albeit slower. The short loop flow does not create an
antenna but can detect process damage. These measurements include trapped

charge, surface voltage, surface barrier height, flat band voltage, dielectric thickness, dielectric leakage, interface trap density, mobile ions, dielectric integrity,
minority carrier lifetime, doping density, etc. This capability provides a valuable
tool for process damage diagnostics (Schroder, 2001).
Simple inline process tests can be used to optimize processes to minimize process damage by allowing the testing of recipes and isolation of individual components of the recipe. Figure 10.5 shows an example of recipe component isolation
(Gupta & Horner, 2000). The surface photovoltage technique is used in this example. At the end of the process, a recipe step is required to turn off the plasma, remove
the static charge holding the wafer to the chuck, and remove the wafer from the
chuck. The figure shows SPV measurements for two different methods of

278

10

Inline Process Damage Measurements

terminating the process. The larger negative value indicates that the “Powerlift”
recipe results in more process damage.
Simple test wafers can be used to determine the fundamental process and equipment differences. For example, Fig. 10.6 compared different etchers where the test
wafer was exposed to a 30 s etch step. This technique provides a valuable tool for
process optimization. In this example, a more negative SPV voltage indicates more
injected charge in the dielectric, which is symptomatic of increased process damage. The control (E) has a typical capacitance–voltage characteristic, while the
experimental groups have distorted characteristics (Gupta & Horner, 2000).
Another example of the usefulness of measurements on simple test wafers is
shown in Fig. 10.7, which were performed on elements of a CMOS imager process
(Sacchettini, 2019). The dielectric on the pixel is critical for CMOS imagers. Three
different dielectric films are compared in this experiment by exposing test wafers
and product-level wafers to plasma damage. The plasma damage injects charge into
the dielectric, changing the electrical surface properties. The test wafer is measured
using the corona technique, which detects a change in the surface voltage, as shown
in Fig. 10.7. The dark current is measured on CMOS imager pixels, as described in
Chap. 8, comparing the performance of the three films. There is a good correlation
between the test wafer results and the product-level dark current, highlighting the
inline technique’s advantages. The oxide–nitride–oxide (ONO) film has positive
charge trapped in the as-deposited film, likely due to the charge trapping described
in Chap. 8.

10.4 Short Loop Flows
Simple short loop process flows can be used to construct capacitors, transistors, etc.,
to allow quick and easy experimentation of some sources of process damage. For
example, a short loop capacitor flow with a gate antenna can be used to investigate
etch damage during the gate etch process. Figure 10.8 shows a cross-section cartoon
of a short loop structure used to test process damage on a flash memory tunnel oxide
(Lee et al., 2009). The short loop flow starts with a thick oxide. A hole is cut in the
thick oxide, where the tunnel dielectric is formed. A gate material is deposited and
patterned, forming a capacitor structure. Different antenna structures, including
area and edge antennas, are formed over the thick oxide. Limited process damage
experiments can be performed on wafers formed by this short loop process flow. For

280

10

Inline Process Damage Measurements

Fig. 10.9 Electrical results from the capacitor short loop flow. (a) 10.1% uniformity gate etch with
a 30% O2/(C2F6 + O2) gas ratio. (b) 6% uniformity gate etch with a 20% O2/(C2F6 + O2) gas ratio.
(c) 4.9% uniformity gate etch with a 10% O2/(C2F6 + O2) gas ratio

example, gate etch, resist strip, etc., can be performed on the short loop, allowing
experimental optimization of process damage without consuming expensive full-­
flow CMOS wafers.
The results of an experiment performed with this short loop are shown in
Fig. 10.9 (Lee et al., 2009). Three different gate etch recipes are compared, revealing a significant difference in damage with variation in the recipe gases. Figure 10.9a
shows the breakdown characteristics of the tunnel dielectric as a function of the etch
recipe. Figure 10.9b shows a low-frequency C–V plot of the capacitor. The abnormal shape of the C–V plot, at the minimum near the flat-band voltage, is associated
with excessive interface generation caused by process damage. The damage correlates with the nonuniformity of the etch across the wafer, indicating possible current
flow through the wafer.

Chapter 11

Process Damage Test Structures

11.1 Introduction
Parametric test structures added to a CMOS product or test wafers are necessary to
characterize and understand plasma and process damage. This chapter summarizes
the structures necessary to cover the mechanisms described in this book. The structures need to be optimized for each CMOS technology. A comprehensive set of test
structures can require a great deal of effort. The test structure design should be
consistent with the relevant design rules and have structures with larger antennas,
etc., above the design rule requirements to allow margin verification of the design
rules. All the structures should have a control structure without antennas for reference. Structures should be duplicated with and without diodes to enable definitive
data to prove that plasma damage is occurring. The layout of structures should
exactly match the product layout, including dummy gates, proximity correction patterns, etc. Table 11.1 lists potential parametric test structures for plasma and process
damage organized by the process module discussed in this chapter.

11.2 Bond and Probe Pads
Bond or probe pads form an antenna on every structure probed through the connection to the pad. Therefore, every structure connected to a pad has the possibility of
additional plasma damage during the pad and passivation opening process modules.
The addition of duplicate test structures with different bond pad sizes gives the
capability of separating the damage from these steps from damage caused by the
rest of the process. A mixture of different pad sizes and passivation opening sizes
can be used to isolate damage during the passivation process module. The structures
can be measured with and without passivation.

Ring oscillators with and without antennas, with and without diodes
Well or triple-well antennas connected to transistor terminals to detect
well-related damage

range of allowed metal spaces

11.3 Inline Probe Pads
Parametric test structures allow for optimizing a CMOS process to minimize the
detrimental effects of process damage. The test structures should be designed to
allow inline measurements after completing specific process steps. This method
allows the process damage to be quickly quantified as processing progresses,
enabling the detection of the most destructive process steps. Metal, contact, and via
layers can serve as inline probe pads, enabling inline measurements. The design

11.3

Inline Probe Pads

285

rules generally forbid large area bond pads on intermediate layers to avoid excess
topology interfering with chemical mechanical polishing (CMP), deposition, and
lithography processes.
CMP dishing can occur for a metal pad formed with a damascene process, creating a low spot in a metal feature in the center of the pad. The subsurface dishing
feature can interfere with subsequent processing, creating defects and uneven topology that may kill chips. Modification of the pad layout can avoid this problem. For
example, Fig. 11.1a shows an inline pad layout formed with metal stripes (Kahng &
Samadi, 2008). If the metal is formed by a damascene process, the dielectric between

Fig. 11.1 Sketch showing the layout of bond pads to allow inline testing during CMOS processing. (a) Bond pad formed with alternating lines and spaces of metal lines. (b) Bond pad formed
with a sea of vias on top of a solid metal pad. (c) Bond pad formed with alternating metal lines and
spaces with a sea of vias on top

286

11

Process Damage Test Structures

the lines prevents or minimizes the dishing. This style of bond pad can be formed in
the copper, tungsten, and cobalt layers used in intermediate metal layers. The probe
tip contacting the pad is much larger than the metallization pitch. The probe tip
shorts many metal lines together, connecting the pad to the underlying structures
and forming a low-resistance connection.
The striped pads shown in Fig. 11.1a can also be used in subtractive metallization processes, where metal is formed on the wafer surface and then patterned.
However, a subtractive solid metallization probe pad can form the opposite problem
of damascene metallization, where subsequent process steps like dielectric deposition can form a high spot or doming, interfering with subsequent processing.
An inline pad can be formed in contact and via layers, allowing measurement
between the metal routing layers and further partitioning the process flow to detect
problem process steps. Figure 11.1b shows an example probe pad formed by placing a sea of contacts or vias on top of a solid metal pad. The probe tip is much larger
than the via or contact dimension, shorting many contacts or vias and creating an
electrical connection to the underlying devices. Figure 11.1c combines the previous
structures.
Different types of pads can be stacked on top of each other, allowing the same
device to be probed multiple times during CMOS wafer processing. This technique
allows monitoring of the progression of process damage throughout the process.
However, the probe pads can also create antennas with the propensity for process
damage, which must be accounted for in the test structure design.
The probe tips contacting the wafer inline may leave a mark on the probe pad
surface due to mechanical damage. As a result, particles may be generated by the
damaged region, degrading yield and requiring caution when performing inline
probing.

11.4 Gate Area and Edge Antennas
As described in the previous chapters, process damage occurs with increasing
antenna area or perimeter. Simple test structures with appropriate antennas can be
used to detect process damage. Figure 11.2 shows sketches of different antenna
structures. Figure 11.2a shows a transistor layout with no antenna used as a control
test structure to compare with the antenna structures. Figure 11.2b shows a transistor connected to an area antenna in the gate metal layer of the transistor. Figure 11.2c
shows a perimeter antenna structure.
Typically, a minimum area transistor allowed by the design rules is used in
antenna structures to increase the antenna ratio, as described in Chap. 2. Structures
with different antenna areas and perimeters are used to vary the degree of process
damage and determine the design rule capability for a process. For example, antenna
ratios of 100, 1000, 10,000, etc., are used to explore orders of magnitude changes in
the antenna ratio. Structures similar to the sketch in Fig. 11.2 can be repeated in any
conducting layer in a CMOS process and connected to a small area transistor.

11.6

Source-Drain Antennas

287

Fig. 11.2 Layout sketch showing gate antenna test structures with (a) no antenna, (b) area antenna,
and (c) edge antenna. The antennas are not drawn to scale

11.5 Well Antenna Structures
Plasma damage due to coupling through CMOS wells, as described in Chap. 2, can
be characterized by the addition of transistors connected to well antennas. Structures
such as well antennas (Chap. 2) can be configured to match the design rules varying
from the minimum antenna dimensions and beyond.

11.6 Source-Drain Antennas
Damage from source-drain antennas is described in Chap. 2. Structures can be
designed to create source-drain antennas with different metal antennas, as shown in
this section. Differential antennas have different metal-to-gate antenna ratios and

288

11

Process Damage Test Structures

different source-drain antenna ratios. These structures can generate the results
shown in Chap. 2.

11.7 Matching Transistor Structures
Matching transistors are crucial devices for critical circuits. Matching transistors are
typically constructed using the best-known methods, and many design rules are
optimized for minimum transistor variation (Hastings, 2005). Matching test structures can include plasma damage experiments that are consistent with the design
rules to analyze potential problems. Specifically, matching metal patterns and coverage of the transistor with metal play critical roles in matching transistor variation.

11.8 Light Damage Structures
High-energy light can damage devices, as described in Chap. 9. Light damage test
structures consisting of structures covered and not covered by large metal features
can be used to detect light damage. The light damage detection capability of these
structures can be confounded with hydrogen blocking or absorption, as described in
Chap. 14.

11.9 Ring Oscillator Structures
Ring oscillators are commonly used to characterize and monitor circuit speeds.
Ring oscillators are susceptible to plasma and process damage, as described in
Chap. 5. Ring oscillators with antenna structures are not very practical as the
antenna-related capacitive loads slow down the oscillator. However, structures with
and without gate diodes can detect degradation in performance because of the small
diode capacitance. The difference in speed with the addition of the diode can be
predicted with design simulations enabling the detection of performance
degradation.

11.10 Multiple Metal Layer Detection
The area available on a wafer for parametric test structures is often limited, requiring optimization of the structures for maximum learning and minimum area.
Antennas in multiple process steps can be combined into one structure. The

11.11

Contact and Via Structures

289

Fig. 11.3 Cross-section sketch showing parametric antenna structures. (a) Multilevel antenna for
detection of plasma damage on many levels. (b) Single level antenna for the detection of process
damage on metal3 M3, via4 V4, and metal4 M4

composite structure enables the detection of plasma damage on as many levels as
possible with fewer test structures but creates difficulty in isolating the exact damaging step. Figure 11.3 shows an example of combined and isolated structures
(Martin, 2020). Figure 11.3a shows a combined test structure cross-section sketch.
Each of the metal and via layers is replicated in the antenna layout and is stacked on
top of each other. Parametric measurements on these structures at the completion of
the process reveal the cumulative effects of process damage on the stacked layers.
Figure 11.3b shows an isolated structure where only process damage on metal 3
M3, via 3 V3, and metal 4 M4 is measured. Connections in the layers not measured
use the minimum areas to avoid creating small antennas. This type of structure
allows quantitative measurements of process damage on these specific levels.

11.11 Contact and Via Structures
Specialized contact and via antenna structures can electrically isolate process damage that occurs during the fabrication of these process modules. Since all contacts
and vias are usually designed with the same exact sizes of contacts and vias in a
CMOS process, the process damage increases with increasing numbers of connections to a single node. Instead of using area or perimeter to increase process damage, via and contact antennas use different numbers of contacts to accelerate
damage. Figure 11.4 shows a layout sketch of contact and via antennas. Figure 11.4a
and b show contact antennas connected to the gate metal of a transistor. One structure has a single contact, and the second structure has an extensive array of contacts,
allowing comparison of the transistor characteristics. A similar structure for vias is
shown in Fig. 11.4c and d, where one structure has a single via and the second structure has an array of vias. Thus, the extent of the damage caused by contact and via
processes can be determined by an electrical comparison of the two structures. The

290

11

Process Damage Test Structures

Fig. 11.4 Layout sketch showing contact and via antennas. Not to scale. (a) single contact used as
a control structure for comparison. (b) array of contacts creating an antenna on the contact layer.
(c) single via used as a control structure for comparison. (d) array of vias creating antennas on the
via layer. Additionally, the resist can completely cover the antenna

numbers of contacts and vias in this type of structure typically vary by orders of
magnitude to span the requirements for a CMOS chip.

11.12 Resist Antenna Structures
Resist structures can play a role in process damage, as described in Chap. 9. Resist
or other masking materials can modify the behavior of any antenna with a resist
patterning layer over it. Figure 11.5 shows some example layout sketches of resist-­
related structures (Dirnecker et al., 2002). Figure 11.5a shows a gate area antenna
structure used as a control. Figure 11.5b shows a gate antenna with a large resist
area overlapping it. This structure determines if the resist conducts charge, increasing the effective antenna area. Figure 11.5c shows a gate antenna with a nearby
resist antenna with a gap between the resist antenna and the gate antenna. These
types of structures typically are used in implant layers with a preference for the
implantation conditions more likely to cause damage, as described in Chap. 9. This
type of structure is used to measure the damage caused by charge flow or arcing
between the resist and the gate.

11.13 Diode Protection

291

Fig. 11.5 Layout sketch showing resist antenna test structures. (a) Control antenna with no resist.
(b) Resist antenna connected to a gate antenna. (c) Resist antenna with a gap to the gate antenna

Fig. 11.6 Layout sketch of a metal antenna protected by a diode

11.13 Diode Protection
Diodes can be used to protect structures from process damage, as described in Chap.
3. Diodes protect sensitive process elements across multiple processing steps, starting from the process step, where the diode is connected in the metal to the end of the
process. Diodes can be selectively added to process damage test structures. Identical
test structures with and without diodes connected at various points in the process
can provide critical data to isolate the specific process steps causing process damage. The diodes can be easily added or removed from a structure by adding or
removing a contact or modification of a metal layer. The comparison of structures
with and without diodes allows careful experimentation to understand the source of
process damage. Figure 11.6 shows an example sketch of an antenna protected by
a diode.

292

11

Process Damage Test Structures

11.14 Comb Structures
Process damage on an edge or comb structure can be a function of the pitch or space
in addition to the perimeter of the comb (see Chap. 9). Differences in etch rates or
plasma composition as a function of pitch or space are responsible for this variation.
Therefore, test structures with varying spaces or pitches are necessary to determine
the worst-case design rules for process damage.
The fingers of perimeter antennas do not necessarily have to be electrically connected to affect process damage (see Chap. 9). The unconnected fingers add to the
antenna perimeter or area until they are disconnected, as all the conductor material
is removed from the space during the etch. Figure 11.7 shows a layout sketch of
connected and unconnected perimeter antennas (Simon et al., 2000).

11.15 Alternative Transistor Layout
11.15.1 Introduction
An alternative transistor layout for bulk CMOS can provide some improvement in
process damage resistance by reducing the probability of failure along the shallow
trench isolation edge described in Chap. 5. These types of structures are also used
in radiation-hardened applications. However, these types of layout are not practical
for finfets. The key feature of these alternative transistor layouts is preventing leakage along the isolation edge by eliminating the leakage path or increasing the leakage path distance to reduce the parasitic current. These techniques are valuable in
circuits such as critical analog modules such as sense amplifiers. These types of
layout may also improve transistor parameters such as breakdown. In addition, the
layout penalty for these types of layout is minor, and the layout can be used
selectively.

Fig. 11.7 Layout sketch showing two different comb antennas. (a) Connected metal comb. (b)
Unconnected comb

11.15

Alternative Transistor Layout

293

11.15.2 Dog Bone Field Edge Layout
One layout option is to place a small rectangle at the gate edge to increase the gate
length overlapping the field edge, as shown in Fig. 11.8, resulting in a dog bone-­
shaped gate layout (Esqueda et al., 2005). The field edge leakage path is increased,
and the leakage path is placed under gate control. The plot shows improved leakage
with increasing dog bone size.

11.15.3 Donut Devices to Suppress Field Edge Leakage
Donut devices can be used to suppress field or STI edge leakage by eliminating the
parallel field conduction path between the drain and source (Bezenova et al., 2018;
Lee & Lee, 2013; McLain et al., 2009; Wang et al., 2009). These types of devices
are often used in radiation-hardened CMOS to prevent field edge leakage after radiation damage. Figure 11.9 shows several options for field edge leakage prevention.
Figure 11.9a shows the gate placed entirely inside the active area, with the drain in
the center of the donut and the source outside of the donut. This donut device uses
a gate and active area layout to place all the field edge regions on the source side of
the transistor to prevent any possibility of field edge leakage. All field edges are on
the source side, which is often connected to the ground. The octagon shape of the
donut is used to reduce the peak electric fields by eliminating the gate corners shown
in the other devices. Figure 11.9b–d shows alternative layouts where the length of
the conduction path along the field edge is increased but not eliminated. These
devices do not completely eliminate the field edge leakage path but may increase the
path length and resistance to the point where the STI edge leakage is negligible.

Fig. 11.8 Dog bone layout to improve field edge leakage and plot showing drain to source leakage
as a function of dog bone length

294

11

Process Damage Test Structures

Fig. 11.9 Layout examples for a donut gate transistor

Design rules may or may not allow a contact to the gate over the active area.
Traditionally, gate contacts over active areas were forbidden because metallization,
such as aluminum, would diffuse into the polysilicon gate and damage the gate
dielectric. Modern CMOS processes are generally capable of forming gate contacts
over active areas as the metallization prevents any interaction of the contact and gate
materials. The structure in Fig. 11.9a requires a gate contact over the active area,
and the rest of the structures can be contacted over the isolation. The device in
Fig. 11.9a is the best solution for STI edge leakage if the contact to the gate over the
active area is allowed in the design rules.

11.16 Through-Silicon Via Test Structures
Through-silicon vias (TSVs) are a potential source of plasma damage due to the
complex structure and the large amount of plasma exposure during the processing
of the very deep structure, as described in Chap. 9. Device structures connected to
TSVs can suffer process and plasma damage. The TSV serves as an antenna, with
the number of TSVs connected to a gate serving as the antenna ratio. Different
antenna ratios can be created by varying the TSV numbers connected to varying
gate sizes. Figure 11.10 shows an example layout for a TSV antenna structure (Li
et al., 2018). TSVs are connected to a small area transistor or capacitor. The metallization used in the chip to connect the TSVs is used for the test structure.

11.17 Structures for Metal Insulator Metal Capacitors
Plasma damage of metal–insulator–metal capacitors is described in Chap. 8. As
shown in Chap. 8 figures, several options are needed to fully characterize the capacitor, including a lower plate connected to the ground, a metal line, or a gate. These
three devices need to be configured with different metal sizes and via antennas for

11.19

Structures for Metallization Damage

295

Fig. 11.10 Layout sketch of TSV plasma damage test structure

the different metal layers or a multilevel antenna, as shown in Chap. 8. The antenna
ratio between the metallization and the MIM capacitor can be explored with different MIM capacitors and antenna sizes consistent with the design rules.

11.18 Memory Array Wordline Structures
The wordlines of memory arrays such as DRAM or flash are susceptible to plasma
damage, as described in Chap. 8. Parametric structures can be configured to place
various antennas connected to wordlines, along with skipping or adding layers, as
shown in Chap. 3. Structures with and without diodes can be used if the layout is
possible. Parametric structures are helpful for the observation of more catastrophic
problems such as dielectric leakage, breakdown, or fundamental cell failure. The
complete characterization of the reliability problems described in the memory section typically requires the layout of wordline damage structures which are placed
inside the CMOS memory product chip so that full product-level functional and
reliability testing can be performed.

11.19 Structures for Metallization Damage
Plasma damage can occur to the interconnect metallization in a CMOS chip, degrading the intermetal dielectrics, causing leakage, and other degraded electrical properties. Low-k dielectrics can be more susceptible to damage because of the weak
bonds relative to silicon dioxide. The metallization reliability may be degraded,
affecting electromigration and stress migration and creating field reliability hazards.
Plasma damage in metals is commonly measured using the simple comb and serpentine test structures shown in Fig. 11.11. Comb structures are commonly placed
on each metal layer and stacked, allowing multiple metal layers to be tested with

296

11

Process Damage Test Structures

Fig. 11.11 Sketch showing common metallization structures. (a) Interdigitated comb structure.
(b) Interdigitated comb structure with a serpentine continuity structure between the combs

one structure and emulating an actual chip layout (Baklanov et al., 2012, pg. 380).
A worst-case layout can be implemented by connecting one terminal of the comb to
the substrate through contacts and vias. The connection to the substrate creates a
high electric field during plasma damage compared to a structure with both terminals of the comb floating, creating the test structure shown in Chap. 6. The comb
and serpentine structures can also be connected to transistors to form an antenna.
Additionally, damage can be detected with contact and via chains (Baklanov
et al., 2012; Tsai et al., 2018). These types of structures may fail more frequently
due to the interfaces between different metals and process-related interfaces.

Chapter 12

Design Rules Related to Process Damage

12.1 Introduction
Design rules and common sense naturally minimize some worst-case process damage layout configurations. For example, it makes very little sense to connect an
extensive array of contacts to the gate of a transistor. It wastes chip area with no
added benefit. Designers usually avoid nonsense situations like this. However,
design rules must be carefully modified with consideration for process damage to
reduce the probability of process damage. There is a trade-off between mitigating
process damage and complex, burdensome design requirements. Design practices
cannot eliminate process damage. For example, plasma damage can vary as a function of the metal pitch. It is likely too costly from a design perspective to attempt to
manage this issue. The fundamental responsibility for mitigating process damage
lies with the process technology, with design practices assisting in mitigation.

12.2 Process Module-Specific Design Rules
Antenna-specific design rules are commonly added to CMOS design rules to prevent the formation of the worst-case circuits and layout. Table 12.1 shows example
design rules for the prevention of charging. The design rules typically mitigate
charging by limiting the size of the antennas by setting an upper limit on the antenna
ratio based on the antenna area or perimeter. Design rules also attempt to minimize
the worst-case conditions. The management of process damage risk by design practices can be challenging to implement in design rule checkers, increasing the complexity of the design methodology. The suggested rules in Table 12.1 are provided
to create a list for consideration. The actual rules are very specific to the underlying

302

12 Design Rules Related to Process Damage

12.2.1 Well Design Rules
Well design rules are needed for bulk CMOS to limit the well coupling-related damage described in Chap. 2. Wells are usually configured in banks containing many
transistors to conserve the chip area, as well-related rules tend to be large. Therefore,
any limitation to the well size limits the maximum number of transistors in a single
well. Rules for all types of wells in a given CMOS technology must be considered.
Special attention needs to be paid to triple-wells as they tend to have less leakage
and are more susceptible to plasma damage. Large wells are more conducive to
practically using diode protection, as the design penalty can be small, with one
diode for a large well. Therefore, diode protection, such as the ones described in
Chap. 3, can often be implemented without penalty. Rules W1 and W2 in Table 12.1
are suggested to protect against well-related damage. Design rules should prevent
an unnecessarily large well from being used, with the minimum well necessary to
enclose the transistors being implemented.

12.2.2 Gate-Related Design Rules
Gate-related plasma protection design rules are commonly used to prevent a large
gate antenna from being formed. Gate protection design rules typically do not cause
any design burden because there is no benefit to running large gate runs on top of
the isolation in modern CMOS. Maximum gate antenna rules typically prevent the
inadvertent layout of antennas. The gate layer is not often used as a routing layer as
any design edits require mask revisions early in the process, degrading the cycle
time for design revisions. The gate protection rules can limit the maximum gate
routing on the field isolation (rules G1 and G2) or control the maximum antenna
ratios (rules G3 and G4). Direct gate connection to a power supply or ground should
be discouraged as power supply buses tend to form large antennas (rule G5). Gates
connected to power supplies or ground should be connected through the drain and
source of a transistor, avoiding a direct connection.

12.2.3 Source-Drain-Related Design Rules
Similar to well-related plasma damage protection, the maximum source-drain active
areas should be limited to avoid the formation of an unnecessarily sizeable active
area region. There are typically no good design reasons for an abnormally large
active area. Historically, the active area was sometimes used as a routing layer, but
modern performance requirements prohibit this use. The active area should be
restricted to the minimum necessary for the source-drain junction formation and the
related contacts. Rule S1 shows an example of this type of rule. Also, the minimum

12.2

Process Module-Specific Design Rules

303

source-drain area can be enforced by rules such as a maximum contact to gate distance or a maximum active area surround of the contact. Advanced CMOS processes have aggressive design rules to achieve high gate density, making it unlikely
that large source-drain areas are used.

12.2.4 Metal-Related Design Rules
Metallization fundamentally creates antennas due to the dense routing requirements
of modern CMOS technology. In addition, advanced logic devices commonly have
more than ten metal layers to achieve the wiring density necessary to connect high-­
density transistor arrays. As a result, large metal antennas can be naturally created
while achieving the necessary wiring density.
Process damage design rules limit the magnitude of metal antennas, typically
achieved by limiting the maximum antenna area, perimeter, or the antenna ratio of
the metal area or perimeter to the gate, source, drain area, or perimeter. This limitation limits the maximum length of a metal line on an individual layer, requiring a
given signal routed in metal to jump between metal layers through via connections
to spread the antenna among multiple metal layers. Figure 12.1 shows a layout
sketch of this modification (Sicard & Bendhia, 2007, pg. 175). Figure 12.1a shows
a metal line running across the chip in a single layer without plasma damage design
rules, and Fig. 12.1b shows the impact of the implementation of antenna design
rules. The metal antenna limitations by design rules have to be sufficiently large to
be practical, allowing the chip design to achieve the necessary wiring density without choking the routing by requiring many signals to be routed in many metal layers.
The maximum metal area and perimeter are limited by design rules to prevent
plasma damage. These rules can significantly impact the layout because metal routing can run large distances across the chip. The metal routing for long runs must
jump from one metal layer to another to break up the antenna into smaller

Fig. 12.1 Top down layout sketch showing an example of (a) worst case layout creating a large
antenna and (b) reduction in the antenna ratio by adding a metal jumper, routing the signal to
another metal layer to break up the large antenna

304

12 Design Rules Related to Process Damage

independent sections to avoid violating the design rules. An isolated metal line not
connected to the gate at the process step where charging damage occurs mitigates
the charging problem due to the reduction in the antenna size (rules M1 and M2).
The metal is connected to the circuit at subsequent process steps, with each interconnect mask layer antenna size being below the design rule limits.
Connections to power and ground tend to form massive antennas that are practically difficult to limit. Large antennas connected to gates are easier to manage by
preventing a direct connection to power or ground (rule G5). On the other hand,
large antennas to sources and drains are more challenging to manage, as a significant fraction of the sources and drains of the transistors must be connected to the
power supply and ground. Typically, antenna rules can be more strict for gate connections than for sources or drains.
It would be helpful to reduce the metal antenna sizes for antennas far from the
transistor to reduce remote antenna plasma damage problems described in Chap. 2.
Usually, circuit blocks are placed in the chip layout, with the metal being routed
later by computer-aided design tools such as place and route. As a result, design
rules to limit remote antennas (rule M3) are challenging to implement as they would
require shifting the placement of transistor blocks to avoid remote antennas.
The differential antennas described in Chap. 2 are problematic to mitigate with
design rules (rules M4 and M5). Attempting to force similar antenna sizes on both
the source and drain requires increasing the amount of metallization on one diffusion antenna while reducing the amount of metallization on the other diffusion.
Forcing more wiring than is needed for design reasons may result in an area and
performance penalty.

12.2.5 Contact and Via-Related Design Rules
Contacts or vias can also create charging problems, usually during the etch or metallization steps, which form the contact or via. Therefore, the design rules limit the
maximum number of contacts or vias to reduce the antenna area (rules C1, C2, V1,
and V2). Practically, only one or two contacts are needed for a good electrical connection to the gates, so the design rules can forbid any large contact arrays connected to the gate. The number of practical contacts to the drain is limited by the
width of the transistor for planar CMOS technology, so design rules can be written
to require as many contacts in a line that can fit in the active area parallel to the gates
as possible. Additional contacts can be forbidden by the design rules.
For finfet transistors, the very small width of the fins allows only a maximum of
a single drain or source contact per fin, limiting the need for multiple contacts.
Normally, a single rectangular drain or source contact shorts several fins together,
forming a single structure. Finfet gate contacts are usually limited because of the
limited area.

12.2

Process Module-Specific Design Rules

305

Limiting via antennas with design rules is more complicated than contacts. The
number of vias placed between two perpendicular crossing metal lines is a function
of the width of the metal lines. Usually, as many vias as possible are placed between
the two lines in an orthogonal regular array. This practice achieves the lowest resistance interconnect and the best reliability for a given metallization technology.
Limiting the size of via antennas requires a reduced number of vias between large
metal busses, impacting resistance, performance, and reliability. Via antenna rules
(rules V1 and V2) are generally specified to restrict the maximum upper numbers of
vias to avoid interfering with low-resistance bus requirements by eliminating the
worst-case conditions.

12.2.6 Metal-Insulator-Metal Capacitor-Related Design Rules
Metal-insulator-metal (MIM) capacitors also need process damage-related design
rules to prevent the problems described in Chap. 8. The design rules can prevent a
direct connection between a MIM capacitor terminal and a transistor gate by forbidding the connection and avoiding the worst-case situation (rule MIM1). Optionally,
design rules can limit the maximum antenna ratio of the MIM capacitor to the gates
(rule MIM2). However, limitations in MIM antenna ratios to source-drain regions
are problematic because of the heavy use of MIM capacitors connecting power supplies and ground to improve noise and supply stability.

12.2.7 Bond Pad-Related Design Rules
Bond pads can be particularly problematic because the large area of the bond pad is
a natural antenna. As a result, charging can occur during the bond pad formation and
the passivation process modules. The electrical probing and assembly requirements
set minimum bond pad areas, setting an upper limit on the pad size and antenna
ratio. Pad sizes larger than the dimensions necessary for testing and assembly are
usually unnecessary and can be restricted by the design rules (rule P1). CMOS
product chip pads are usually heavily protected by electrostatic discharge protection
circuits that also protect against process damage. Parametric test structures are susceptible because the bond pad commonly has no electrostatic discharge circuits to
protect it like the pads in the product chip. Special pad rules and configurations for
parametric structures are usually necessary. For example, a duplicate test structure
layout with tiny pads can be used to experimentally determine damage from pad
antennas.

306

12 Design Rules Related to Process Damage

12.2.8 Through-Silicon Via-Related Design Rules
The use of through-silicon vias (TSVs) described in Chap. 9 can cause process damage that can be mitigated through design rules. The design rules can limit the maximum number of TSVs connected to a single sensitive node. The design rules may
forbid TSV connections to sensitive nodes such as gates or MIM capacitors without
a diode or an electrostatic discharge protection circuit.

12.2.9 Technology-Specific Design Rules
Plasma damage-related design rules for technology-specific structures such as
DRAM and NVM rowlines should be considered to mitigate potential damage
problems. The primary area of concern is often damage on the wordlines or gate
connections for technologies like DRAMs, nonvolatile memories, and CMOS imagers. Typically, there is little or no layout space available to add diodes, as described
in Chap. 8. Damage mitigation usually involves careful optimization and minimization of metallization-related antennas and placing a transistor as close as possible to
the worldline or gate connection with metal antennas on the opposite side of the
transistor from the wordline connections.

Chapter 13

Parametric Damage Testing Strategy
and Procedures

13.1 Introduction
Process and plasma damage effects are complicated and deserve careful attention to
the definition of the parametric testing strategy. Data collection is needed for many
parameters, followed by reliability testing to determine the reliability degradation.
Different types of test structures need to be measured to isolate process damage
from other parametric shifts.

13.2 Plasma Damage Detection Strategy
The widely varying damage caused by process and plasma damage requires a comprehensive and well-thought-out testing strategy to detect time zero and reliability
degradation. Figure 13.1 shows an example flowchart for a process damage parametric test flow. The suite of process damage-related parametric tests is typically
a subset of the standard tests performed during wafer acceptance testing at the end
of the process flow. The test results are compared with the process specifications
established for the CMOS technology. Each parametric test is usually controlled
with process control charts, enabling the statistical detection of process damage. A
diagnostic analysis of failing parameters is performed to determine the cause of
the deviation and if process damage is a possible cause of the problem
(Martin, 2020).

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_13

309

310

13 Parametric Damage Testing Strategy and Procedures

Fig. 13.1 Example flow chart showing process and plasma damage testing strategy

13.3 Parametric Testing
The basic function and integrity of the measured devices and structures need to be
checked before extensive, time-consuming measurements are performed. Integrity
checks are designed to detect fundamentally defective structures and devices that do
not function properly to shorten test times and avoid contaminating the test database
with erroneous results. In addition, defective devices need to be screened and analyzed to determine if the failure is associated with process damage or some other
cause. This section describes simple checks to ensure that the measured devices are
functioning by using extreme limits that would not be expected and failing devices
that exceed the extreme limits. The values of the limits set are typically orders of
magnitude higher than the expected values during regular device operation. These
tests also eliminate catastrophically misprocessed wafers. Table 13.1 shows a list of
commonly used integrity checks.
Shorts tests need to be performed to ensure that the different terminals are electrically isolated. Typical test conditions force a small voltage, which is applied
between two terminals, and the resulting current is measured. The test results are
compared with the expectation of continuity or isolation, and defective devices are
screened. For example, the gate should not leak to the drain and source at a high
level, and the drain should not leak to the source at a high level.
Continuity tests can be performed to ensure that proper connections exist between
the probe tips and the devices to be measured. For example, probe tips connected to
the source and drain should conduct current when the junction is forward-biased,
and a small current should flow when the junctions are reverse-biased. The well
continuity should also be checked.

Parametric testing is widely used in CMOS technology to develop CMOS technology and monitor the production line health. Parametric testing for process and
plasma damage can reuse the same measurement methods used for regular parametric testing with little or no modifications. The regular methods used in parametric
testing need to be applied to the specialized process damage structures described
previously. Table 13.2 shows a list of the more common tests performed on process
damage-related structures.
The parameters shown in Table 13.2 are transistor-centric and related to the process damage of the CMOS transistors. As described in Chap. 6, the backend of line
(BEOL) metallization used to construct the interconnect may also be susceptible to
plasma damage. Therefore, a different suite of parametric tests is needed to analyze
BEOL damage-related parameters. Table 13.3 shows a list of metallization parameters related to process damage. Most of the parameters shown are commonly measured during parametric testing. The electromigration and stress migration reliability
tests tend to be very time-consuming, requiring specific test flows and high-­
temperature testing or stress. Fast tests such as the breakdown energy of metal
(BEM) can be used for routine monitoring (Hong & Crook, 1985).
Structures with diodes protecting the gates need to be tested differently from
devices without diodes, as the diodes may overwhelm other unrelated leakages. For
example, gate leakage measurements can be overwhelmed by diode leakage, etc. In

addition, the diode may limit the gate voltages through breakdown or forward bias
current.
Inline parametric testing can be performed during processing, as described in
Chap. 11, while the wafer is partially completed using specialized bond pads with a
pattern similar to a waffle. Inline probing can be very useful in isolating the exact
process step responsible for process damage.
The history of parametric testing performed on a given structure and location on
the wafer must be considered when reprobing structures. Some tests can damage or
destroy the structure, causing damage unrelated to processing. These tests include
dielectric tests such as breakdown, leakage, etc. Higher voltage tests, such as transistor or junction breakdown tests, can degrade the devices. Tests that can cause the
transistor to operate in a negative resistance region, causing snap-back, can damage
the transistor.
The parametric health and capability of a CMOS process are heavily characterized and optimized during the technology development. There is a need for ongoing
monitoring during manufacturing to ensure that damage to the production process
remains under control. This monitoring is necessary to detect and remedy excursions such as those described in Chap. 4. Wafers are typically sampled periodically
from the outgoing production volume and tested using a process damage-specific
parametric test flow. Statistical methods are used to optimize the sampling with cost
and capability constraints.

13.4 Diagnostic Revealing Stress
After the initial parametric test is performed, reliability tests are performed to determine if latent process damage has occurred. These tests can be very time-­consuming
and are impractical for large-scale routine parametric testing. A revealing or diagnostic stress can be performed on transistors as a proxy for dielectric-related transistor damage. The advantage of this type of stress is that it can be done very quickly,
consumes a small amount of test capacity, and allows for increased process damage
testing. This stress is a valuable technique that cannot replace process damagerelated reliability testing. A revealing stress cannot be used for reliability lifetime
prediction. It only shows the presence of process damage.

13.4

Diagnostic Revealing Stress

313

Fig. 13.2 Probability plot showing the threshold voltage shift after a 1 s constant current revealing
stress for a pmos transistor with and without a bond pad compared with the results of hot carrier
stress. The large change in threshold with the revealing stress correlates with a large degradation
in hot carrier reliability. This indicates sucessful detection of the reliability problem with the reavealing stress

The revealing stress is performed by forcing a constant current into the gate of
the transistor for a short time. The constant current stress ensures that current flows
through the dielectric, filling neutral electron and hole traps and revealing the presence of hidden damage. On the other hand, a constant voltage stress does not ensure
that current flows through the dielectric. The forced current level must inject carriers into the dielectric with a sufficient bias to place the capacitor into direct or
Fowler-Nordheim tunneling leakage. Additionally, the forced current level must be
well below the amount that causes dielectric breakdown. Therefore, the exact current level needs to be determined empirically. Various levels are used in the literature (Cheung, 1996; Martin, 2009). The revealing stress may shift all transistors,
including undamaged devices without antennas. The key metric is the difference
between structures with and without antennas.
As an example of the usefulness of revealing stress, Fig. 13.2 shows an example
of a revealing stress performed on two different structures (Beckmeier & Martin,
2016). The standard parametric or reliability tests are performed before and after the
revealing stress. One structure has a large bond pad, and the other structure has a
minimal bond pad. The shift in threshold voltage from before and after the revealing
stress is plotted on a probability plot. The minimal bond pad structure has a nearly
normal distribution with a slight Vt shift. The structure with a large bond pad has a
much larger Vt shift with a wide variation. Figure 13.2 shows the result of hot carrier
stress for the two pad experiments performed on identically processed wafers without a revealing stress. The hot carrier stress results show that the revealing stress
correctly and qualitatively predicted that the large pad structure would have degraded
reliability relative to the control small pad structure. The results indicate that the

314

13 Parametric Damage Testing Strategy and Procedures

Fig. 13.3 Example of a 2 s revealing stress for transistors with 5.2 nm gate oxide. The probability
plot shows the initial threshold prestress voltage distribution of transistors with various antenna
experiments on the left side of the plot, which are indistuinguishable for the various antenna sizes.
The right side of the plot shows the shifted distribution with the different antenna experiments
separated with a 2 s stress

revealing stress successfully detected the damage that degraded the hot-electron
lifetime.
Figure 13.3 shows an example of revealing or diagnostic stress (Martin, 2009).
An antenna experiment shows no difference at time zero with and without plasma
damage for various antenna sizes. However, after a 2 s stress, the distribution of all
transistors is shifted, and the curves are separated, with the control distribution
showing the least amount of shift, while transistors with antennas shift by varying
amounts. For this type of measurement, the time between the stress and the measurement needs to be tightly controlled as a recovery process occurs, shifting the
distributions back toward the original threshold, as described in Chap. 6.
The revealing stress can usually correctly predict that a wafer may have reliability problems (Martin, 2022). However, a revealing stress can have false positives,
showing a sizeable revealing shift when minimal reliability problems exist.
Figure 13.4 shows a diagram comparing the experimental results of hot carrier and
NBTI reliability (y-axis) with the reveling stress results (x-axis). Points along the
diagonal represent experiments with a good qualitative correlation between the reliability tests and the revealing stress results. Points next to the x-axis represent experiments with a strong revealing stress response and minimal reliability stress
response, indicating a false-positive result for the revealing stress.
Revealing stress is a valuable technique for detecting the presence of traps unoccupied with charge carriers and other damage, such as interface states. The impact
of traps is a strong function of the dielectric thickness, as shown in Chap. 5.

13.6

Diagnostic Analysis

315

Fig. 13.4 Plot showing a qualitative correlation between a revealing stress with hot carrier and
NBTI tests

13.5 Reliability Testing
Process and plasma damage complicates CMOS reliability testing by confounding
the inherent reliability with the associated reliability degradation due to process
damage. A suite of different transistor-level parametric reliability tests are normally
performed on CMOS technologies to guarantee a reliable process. These tests
include time-dependent dielectric breakdown, hot carrier lifetime, off-state lifetime,
bias instability testing, electromigration, stress migration, etc. Some of these reliability tests have industry-standard procedures defined by the Joint Electron Device
Engineering Council (JEDEC).
Reliability test parametric structures need to be duplicated with and without
antennas to allow the qualification and verification of the technology reliability with
and without process damage. The worst-case damage likely occurs with antennas
present, requiring duplication of testing to ensure reliability in worse-case situations.

13.6 Diagnostic Analysis
An investigation of the failure characteristics can provide a positive conclusion that
process or plasma damage occurred, requiring extensive data analysis. Plasma damage has a set of unique symptoms that allows the diagnosis of a plasma damage
problem, separating it from a plethora of other potential problems. The unique

symptoms are described in Chap. 4 and are shown in Table 13.4. Typically, a plasma
damage problem occurs with several, but not all, of the symptoms shown in the
table. A high-confidence plasma damage conclusion can be reached by understanding how all of the symptoms match the failure mode in a coherent manner.
Once a problem is determined not to be plasma damage, reaching a root cause
conclusion may be more difficult, requiring exhaustive investigation, failure analysis, and experimentation to select among the potential causes of failure described in
Chap. 4.

Chapter 14

Role of Hydrogen

14.1 Introduction
Hydrogen plays a critical role in CMOS technology, passivating and neutralizing
defects. CMOS technology would be slower, less capable, and less reliable without
the hydrogen mechanisms that passivate defects. In addition, hydrogen plays a significant role in process and plasma damage, confounding the data analysis and
problem-­
solving. Typically, a large fraction of process damage problems are
observed interacting with hydrogen processing, shifting electrical parameters, and
changing the impact of process damage. Exposure to hydrogen can partially recover
the impact of plasma damage, hiding the damage and creating the potential for
latent damage to be detected as a reliability problem.
Hydrogen is ubiquitous in CMOS processing; preventing the exposure of the
wafer during manufacturing to hydrogen is nearly impossible. Most plasma processes crack gases, often containing hydrogen components, creating atomic hydrogen that rapidly diffuses into the wafer. Hydrogen containing acids, resists, and
other films expose the wafer to hydrogen. Even water used in wafer processing
exposes the wafer to a hydrogen source (see Chap. 9) (Pankove & Johnson, 1991).
Most CMOS processes intentionally expose the wafer to a 400–500 °C hydrogen
alloy in a furnace tube at or near the final process steps to reduce interface states by
hydrogen passivation. The alloy furnace is partially filled with hydrogen molecules
that penetrate the wafer, interacting with it through multiple mechanisms. The
molecular hydrogen breaks apart into more reactive atomic hydrogen through interaction with defects in the wafer. In some cases, the wafer is directly exposed to
atomic hydrogen through a remote plasma process to provide a high-concentration
source of atomic hydrogen.
There are many complex hydrogen mechanisms in silicon and its dielectrics that
occur independently or interact. These hydrogen effects are very complicated and
not completely understood. Table 14.1 shows a list of important CMOS-related
