14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.1 Plot showing activation energies and temperatures for hydrogen-related action in CMOS
technology for silicon nitride, silicon dioxide, p-type silicon, and n-type silicon

hydrogen mechanisms along with the related chemical equation. In addition, the
activation energies and temperature ranges of interest are shown for some of the
mechanisms. Unfortunately, there are conflicting activation energies in the literature. Some older activation energies have been excluded as they conflict with modern values (Baumvol, 1999). This chapter provides a concise description of hydrogen
mechanisms in CMOS technology.
Temperature plays an enormous role in hydrogen action in CMOS technology,
driving the mechanisms shown in the table. The temperatures of interest in CMOS
manufacturing range from near room temperature to around 1200 °C. The temperatures of interest during packaging and reliability testing range from −25 °C to
around 200 °C. The temperatures shown are from large area structures involving
large numbers of hydrogen reactions. Single atom effects covered in this book can
be expected to be observed at lower temperatures at a part per million or billion
level. Figure 14.1 shows a chart of the hydrogen-related effects in the silicon substrate, gate dielectric, and silicon nitride as a function of temperature and activation
energy discussed in this chapter (Fleetwood et al., 2024).

14.2 Hydrogen Diffusion and Drift
The smallest atom, hydrogen, is highly mobile in silicon and most dielectrics, moving interstitially or hopping between sites, quickly moving in the temperature range
of CMOS operation, causing electrical changes in CMOS devices. In addition,
hydrogen can drift due to the electric field present in operating transistors and other

14.2 Hydrogen Diffusion and Drift

325

Fig. 14.2 Hydrogen diffusion coefficient in silicon plotted against temperature compiled from
various sources

devices. Hydrogen can drift and diffuse in several forms, including molecular
hydrogen (H2), neutral atomic hydrogen (H0), positively ionized atomic hydrogen
(H+), and negatively ionized atomic hydrogen (H−). Hydrogen easily changes
between these forms as it drifts and diffuses. Atomic hydrogen can combine to form
molecular hydrogen and dissolve later, reforming atomic hydrogen, transitioning
back and forth with time and position in the semiconductor and dielectrics.
Hydrogen migrates in two distinct methods. At high temperatures, hydrogen
migrates predominately as an atomic species by interstitial diffusion. At low temperatures, hydrogen diffuses by a hopping mechanism between stable or metastable
defects (Fleetwood et al., 2009). Figure 14.2 shows a diffusion coefficient plot versus temperature for hydrogen in silicon (Hallam et al., 2018).
Free unbound hydrogen is very mobile under electric fields and can be expected
to drift in CMOS devices under typical operating conditions, changing the electrical
characteristics of the devices. Hydrogen drift or diffusion can be expected during
the operation of CMOS devices. The hydrogen mechanisms described in this chapter can impact the functionality of the CMOS chip, performance, and reliability.
Figure 14.3 shows the results of a drift experiment (Herring et al., 2001). A Schottky
diode is reverse-biased, and a laser light pulse is applied to the back of the diode.
The light pulse supplies holes to the hydrogen-phosphorous complex where the
hydrogen is located, reducing the energy for the proton required to escape the
phosphorous-­hydrogen complex where it is trapped. The free hydrogen drifts under
the electric field. The plot shows the change in diode capacitance after the laser
pulse for a sample with and without hydrogenation. Without hydrogenation, the plot
shows no hydrogen drift, only a transient caused by the laser light. For a diode that

326

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.3 Change in reverse-biased Schottky diode capacitance with a hole injection laser pulse
with and without a hydrogen plasma treatment. The hydrogenation was performed with a remote
hydrogen plasma for 20 min at 120 °C

has been hydrogenated by a remote hydrogen plasma at 120 °C for 20 min, the plot
shows a transient decay over seconds as the hydrogen drifts through the diode’s
depletion region. Similar effects can be expected during CMOS operation.

14.3 Passivation and Depassivation of Interface States
Interface state passivation of hydrogen is a critical process enabling successful
CMOS technology. The interface quality where the crystalline silicon semiconductor meets the amorphous or nanocrystalline gate dielectric controls the transistor
performance and reliability. Unfortunately, there are a large number of defects at
this interface. Therefore, a CMOS process flow usually contains one or more hydrogen passivation steps near the last steps of the flow just before the wafer processing
is completed. For example, a hydrogenation step may consist of a thermal treatment
or alloy in a partial hydrogen ambient consisting of molecular hydrogen. Also, the
hydrogen treatment may involve exposing the wafer to an atomic hydrogen plasma.
Additionally, a hydrogen treatment may consist of a thermal treatment, driving
hydrogen from a hydrogen-containing film such as silicon nitride into the wafer.
Hydrogen can also be driven from the wafer. The hydrogenation is usually performed at temperatures of around 400–500 °C, which is an optimal temperature for

14.3

Passivation and Depassivation of Interface States

327

Fig. 14.4 (a) Sketch of ball and stick pattern of SiO2-Si with a hydrogen atom passivating a missing bond with the following colors: Si—red, O—white, and H—blue. (b) Cross-section TEM of
silicon-silicon dioxide interface

passivation of interface states (See Fig. 14.7). For temperatures greater than 500 °C,
the metallization on the wafer may be damaged, so the maximum temperature is
limited. The optimum alloy temperature is shown in Fig. 14.11.
The most common interface state defect is a dangling bond where one or more of
the unpaired silicon valence bonds create an electrically active defect known as a Pb
center. Hydrogen can bond with the Pb center, forming an electrically inactive center. Figure 14.4a shows a ball and stick diagram of the Si-SiO2 interface where a
hydrogen atom has passivated a dangling bond (Gos, 2011). Figure 14.4b shows a
transmission electron microscope (TEM) image of the Si-SiO2 interface where the
crystalline silicon meets the amorphous silicon dioxide (Tao et al., 2024). The rough
and nonideal interface can be observed, showing the complex structure involved in
interface states.
Hydrogen passivates interface states by chemically reacting with the dangling
silicon bond at the Pb center, with the following reactions forming an electrically
neutral silicon hydrogen bond. The electrically neutralized defect does not affect the
electrical function of the CMOS devices.
H 2 + Pb + → Si − H + H +
or
H + Pb + → Si − H
Hydrogen can also depassivate passivated interface states through the reverse
processes.

328

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.5 Computer simulation of the silicon surface mobility with hydrogen termination for different levels of hydrogen passivation

Si − H + H → Pb + + H 2
or
Si − H → Pb + + H
Hydrogen passivation of interface states improves CMOS devices. Interface
states degrade the movement of the electrical channel carriers, reflecting or temporarily trapping the electrical carriers. This effect degrades the mobility of the transistor, limiting its speed and drive capabilities. Hydrogen passivation neutralizes the
electrical activity of the interface states. Figure 14.5 shows a computer simulation
of the channel mobility with 90% and 100% of the interface states passivated
(Ishihara et al., 2020). The transistor mobility is severely degraded with unpassivated interface state levels of 1011 cm−2. This level is larger than that achieved in
CMOS technology (~1010 cm−2).
Hydrogen-passivated interface states can become depassivated through exposure
to atomic hydrogen. The depassivation increases with increased temperatures. A
catalytic reaction can occur where a single proton (hydrogen ion) can depassivate
multiple passivated interface states, as shown in Fig. 14.6. A proton can diffuse to
the passivated interface state, combining with the passivating hydrogen atom forming H2, which diffuses away, depassivating the interface state. The hydrogen molecule diffuses through the dielectric and can be cracked into two atoms by dielectric

14.4

Mobile Hydrogen

329

Fig. 14.6 Sketch of silicon dioxide-silicon interface showing a catalytic reaction passivating and
depassivating interface states. (1) Energy from a photon or electric field creates an electron-hole
pair. (2) The hole drifts toward the gate and reacts with a neutral hydrogen atom, creating a positively charged hydrogen ion. (3) The hydrogen reacts with a Si-H bond at the silicon interface,
depassivating an interface state and creating a hydrogen molecule. (4) The hydrogen molecule is
captured by a defect such as an E′ center cracking the molecule, creating hydrogen ions. Steps 3
and 4 create a catalytic reaction where a single hydrogen ion can depassivate multiple interface states

defects such as a fixed oxide charge E’ center. The cracked hydrogen atom can diffuse to another interface state and repeat the process (Rashkeev et al., 2001).
Hydrogen treatment of CMOS wafers is heavily optimized to obtain the best possible overall results. The final hydrogen treatments in the process flow control the
overall hydrogen passivation of defects. The temperature of the treatment is important, as shown in Fig. 14.7 (Ishihara et al., 2020). The two types of Pb centers responsible for interface states are measured using an electron spin resonance method
(ESR). The Pb center concentration is measured as a function of the anneal and alloy
temperature. Thermally treating the wafer in an inactive neutral nitrogen ambient
reduces the interface state density by reducing the Pb center density. The reduction
in interface states is further improved by adding hydrogen to the alloy. The lowest
interface state density occurs near 425 °C in this experiment. For lower temperatures, the passivation is limited by the hydrogen-silicon reaction rate. For temperatures above 450 °C, the interface state density increases as the hydrogen is thermally
ejected from the Pb center (Ishihara et al., 2020).

14.4 Mobile Hydrogen
In addition to the poor interface state passivation performance, hydrogen alloys at
higher temperatures can leave unreacted ionized hydrogen (protons) in the dielectrics. The protons are mobile in the dielectrics, behaving as a mobile ion, affecting
the MOS device stability. Figure 14.8a shows a current-voltage measurement on a
silicon on insulator structure with a 400 nm silicon dioxide layer, changing the
threshold voltage. The voltage is swept in opposite directions while measuring the
current through the dielectric. The hysteresis is caused by protons drifting back and

330

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.7 Interface defect related Pb0 and Pb1 density plotted as a function of temperature for a
hydrogen alloy or nitrogen anneal process for a 2.5 nm oxide grown at 850 °C. The interface state
density is plotted as a ratio from the initial low temperature unannealed interface state
concentration

forth between the top and bottom interfaces. Figure 14.8b shows the width of the
hysteresis voltage as a function of the alloying temperature with and without hydrogen. The maximum hysteresis occurs around 600 °C. Hydrogen exposure at these
temperatures is generally avoided.
Hydrogen can be repeatably mobile under certain conditions. A hydrogen-based
proton memory has been demonstrated, showing protons behaving as mobile ions in
a SiO2 dielectric dielectric (Fleetwood et al., 1998). The proton is trapped in the
dielectric between the silicon substrate and a polysilicon or aluminum gate. The
positively charged protons are attracted or repelled by the gate voltage, transiting
between the top and bottom of the dielectric and modifying the threshold voltage.
As shown in Fig. 14.9, the threshold voltage modification is reflected as a hysteresis
in the C-V curve (Vanheusden, 2011). The hysteresis could be observed through
millions of cycles of the C-V plot, indicating that the hydrogen was not trapped and
remained mobile. This hydrogen behavior is abnormal and not generally observed
in CMOS technology, indicating that special processing conditions are needed to
generate this behavior, such as a dielectric with high concentrations of oxygen
vacancies (Warren et al., 1997).

14.4

Mobile Hydrogen

331

Fig. 14.8 Proton motion observed in a silicon on insulator (SOI) mos capacitor structure. (a)
current-voltage curve of the SOI capacitor showing hysteresis. Sweep 1 is a positive to negative
voltage sweep on the gate. Sweep 2 is a subsequent negative to positive sweep. Sweep 3 follows
curve 2 with a positive to negative sweep. (b) plot showing the width of the hysteresis versus
annealing temperature with and without hydrogen

14.5 Dopant Passivation and Depassivation
14.5.1 Acceptor Passivation and Depassivation by Hydrogen
Hydrogen can passivate the dangling silicon bonds created by introducing dopants
into the silicon lattice (Pankove & Johnson, 1991). Acceptors such as boron are
trivalent atoms that substitute for silicon in the lattice, creating a hole by capturing
an electron in the dangling bond. As a result, one of the silicon bonds is left vacant.
Hydrogen can be attracted to the acceptor site and be captured by the silicon lattice
at the location of the missing bond, as shown in Fig. 14.10. The A-H pair is neutral
and does not capture an electron from the valence bond, eliminating the electrical
activity of the acceptor dopants.
Hydrogen can heavily passivate acceptors at low temperatures around 100 °C
(Pankove & Johnson, 1991). At temperatures higher than 100 °C, the hydrogen has
enough thermal energy to escape the dangling bond site. Above 100 °C, very few
acceptors are neutralized. However, at temperatures lower than 100 °C, the hydrogen does not have enough thermal energy to penetrate to the acceptor site. These
competing mechanisms create a maximum acceptor neutralization temperature of
around 100 °C, as shown in Fig. 14.11. The higher resistivity near 100 °C is direct
evidence of boron neutralization by hydrogen.
A large fraction of the acceptors in p-type silicon can be neutralized by hydrogen. The fraction neutralized can exceed 90% with heavy plasma hydrogenation,
changing the resistivity orders of magnitude. The passivation can be reversed by
heating the wafer at temperatures significantly higher than the peak at 100 °C, as
shown in Fig. 14.12 (Pearton et al., 1992). The ratio plotted tracks the recovered
dopant density (NR) as a fraction of the initial acceptor density (N0). There is a difference in the recovery temperature with different acceptor dopants.
The acceptor passivation by hydrogen tracks the hydrogen diffusion into the silicon ((Pankove & Johnson, 1991) pg. 93). Figure 14.13 shows a plot of the resistivity

Fig. 14.10 Sketch showing hydrogen passivation of acceptor dopants. (a) the acceptor dopant
(boron) is bonded into silicon lattice with missing bond. (b) passivated acceptor with hydrogen

Dopant Passivation and Depassivation

hydrogen ambient for B, Ga, and Al in silicon

Fig. 14.13 Spreading resistance measurements as a function of depth for various uniformly boron
doped substrates exposed to a hydrogen plasma. The top 3 curves were plasma hydrogenated for
1 h at 122 °C. The most heavily doped bottom sample was plasma hydrogenated for 4 h at 122 °C

measured by spreading resistance for several uniformly boron-doped silicon samples. The resistivity is high near the silicon surface, where hydrogen has diffused
from the surface. The hydrogen also tracks the acceptor dopant distribution created
by ion implantation as the hydrogen is trapped at the acceptor location.

14.5.2 Donor Passivation and Depassivation by Hydrogen
Donor dopants can also be passivated by hydrogen. The hydrogen passivation of
donors follows similar physics to the acceptor passivation previously described. The
fraction of donors passivated is typically less than the fraction of acceptors passivated. However, in certain cases, such as highly doped n-type silicon, more than 90%
of donors can be neutralized by hydrogen (Pearton et al., 1992). Figure 14.14 shows
the electrically active implanted donor dopant profile measured by spreading resistance. The electrically active dopant concentration is reduced by an order of magnitude with plasma hydrogenation.
The depassivation of donor-hydrogen complexes can occur similarly to the
depassivation of acceptors. Depassivation of donor-hydrogen can occur at temperature ranges of 24–110 °C that are in the range of operation and testing of chips
(Seager & Anderson, 1990).

14.7 Hydrogen Interaction with Thermal Donors

335

Fig. 14.14 Plot of electrically active implanted Sb donor concentration in silicon measured by
spreading resistance with and without a hydrogen plasma treatment

14.6 Hydrogen Interaction with Silicon
and Oxygen Vacancies
Hydrogen can react with both silicon and oxygen vacancies in the wafer, creating
electrically active defects in the silicon bandgap. These defects can create shallow
energy levels in the bandgap below the conduction band, causing changes in device
behavior.

14.7 Hydrogen Interaction with Thermal Donors
Thermal donors are oxygen clusters formed in the silicon lattice. Thermal donors are
electrically active defects primarily created in silicon by exposing a wafer to thermal
processing steps in the temperature range of 300–550 °C. About 25 different thermal
donor species have been detected in silicon (Singh & Nagabalasubraminian, 2019).
Thermal donors are created when oxygen from the wafer substrate manufacturing
process and other chip manufacturing processes are in the silicon lattice. Thermal
donor formation requires the presence of oxygen in the wafer. Thermal donors can
affect the chip operation when formed in the electrically active wafers near the surface of the wafer. Modern wafer manufacturing uses various techniques to mitigate

336

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.15 Free electron concentration as a function of depth in the silicon derived from
capacitance-­voltage measurements on schottky barrier diodes. The thermal donors are formed during the 30 min. 450 °C treatment in air. Three hydrogenation experiments are performed before the
thermal donor anneal. The bottom curve has no hydrogen exposure. The middle curve had a backside plasma hydrogen treatment. The top curve had a frontside hydrogen exposure

the existence of oxygen at the wafer surface. These techniques include hydrogen and
argon annealing of the wafer before chip manufacturing begins.
Hydrogen can enhance the migration of oxygen clusters. In addition, hydrogen
can bind to oxygen clusters, enhancing thermal donor formation (Fleetwood et al.,
2009). Figure 14.15 shows a plot demonstrating accelerated thermal donor formation by plasma hydrogen exposure (Simoen et al., 2006). Schottky diodes are formed
on the treated wafers and measured using a capacitance-voltage technique. The free
electron concentration is calculated and plotted as a function of depth into the silicon. The electron concentration due to the created thermal donors is strongly
enhanced by plasma hydrogen exposure before thermal annealing. N-type wafers
are used to avoid hydrogen acceptor passivation.

14.8 Hydrogen Interaction with Contaminants
14.8.1 Metals, Chalcogenides, Etc.
Impurities in a CMOS wafer, such as transition metals, chalcogenides, etc., create
deep-level traps in the center of the bandgap that can degrade device performance,
as described in Chap. 15 (Claeys & Simoen, 2018). Hydrogen can passivate these

14.8

Hydrogen Interaction with Contaminants

337

Fig. 14.16 Deep level transient spectroscopy (DLTS) plot showing transition metal contamination
in a silicon wafer. Samples are shown for an uncontaminated silicon sample, after contamination
in a contaminated furnace (2 h 900 °C in Ar), after passivation with hydrogen (2 h 700 °C H2), and
after passivation with a hydrogen plasma (2 h 700 °C). The metal contaminants signal peaks are
identified

deep-level traps, eliminating their electrical activity. Figure 14.16 shows a plot of
the results of hydrogen treatment of samples contaminated with metal impurities
(Pearton et al., 1984). An electrical technique called deep-level transient spectroscopy (DLTS) is used to measure the electrically active metal impurities in the wafer.
A silicon wafer was annealed in a contaminated furnace, contaminating the wafer
with Ni, Fe, and Au. The wafer was then passivated with hydrogen by annealing in
a clean furnace or exposing the wafer to a hydrogen plasma in a clean tool. The
treatment partially electrically deactivates the metal defects, as shown in the figure.
Unfortunately, the hydrogen passivation of contaminants is not thermally stable
and can be depassivated at low temperatures in the range of 200–300 °C, making
hydrogen passivation an impractical solution to metal contamination (Fleetwood
et al., 2009; Pearton et al., 1984).

14.8.2 Carbon
Carbon is a common silicon contaminant introduced into the wafer through SiC
quartzware used during the crystalline silicon growth in the prevalent Czochralski
wafer fabrication method. Also, carbon can be introduced into the wafer through
common semiconductor processes such as plasma etching, etc. Hydrogen can interact with carbon in the wafer, forming hydrogen-carbon complexes and creating
electrically active donor and acceptor defects in the silicon bandgap (Fleetwood
et al., 2009; Pearton et al., 1992).

338

14 Role of Hydrogen

14.9 Bias Temperature Instability
Bias temperature instability is one of the most severe reliability risks in CMOS
technology, particularly negative bias temperature instability (NBTI) for PMOS
devices (Strong et al., 2009). A detailed discussion of NBTI is outside the scope of
this book. However, a short description of the hydrogen behavior affecting NBTI
follows. Figure 14.17 shows a sketch of hydrogen mechanisms affecting NBTI
(Tsetseris et al., 2005). During negative voltage stress, thermal energy and the electric field break a donor-hydrogen complex in the silicon (1a,1b). As a result, the
hydrogen diffuses to the interface, where it can depassivate the hydrogen-passivated
interface state (2). In addition, the hydrogen can diffuse into the dielectric, where it
can become trapped, forming a positive fixed oxide charge and shifting the threshold voltage (3). Finally, the hydrogen in the dielectric can form a hydrogen molecule
that drifts back to the interface and repassivates the interface state (4,5), completing
the cyclic process. The dynamic change in fixed oxide charge and interface states in
the NBTI degradation produces shifts in the transistor parameters capable of causing circuit failure.

Fig. 14.17 Sketch showing the role of hydrogen in NBTI. (negative bias on n-type Si): (1a)–(1b)
release of H from donor-H complexes in the silicon, (2) depassivation of interface states, (3) entry
of H into silicon dioxide, (4) formation of H2 molecule, (5) repassivation of interface state

14.10

Hydrogen Degrades Noise in CMOS

339

14.10 Hydrogen Degrades Noise in CMOS
The hydrogen behavior in CMOS is very dynamic, constantly moving, interacting
with defects, forming and breaking bonds at the temperatures where CMOS chips
operate. This behavior, as shown in this chapter, can be observed as transistor noise.
For example, Figs. 14.6 and 14.17 show the dynamic interaction of hydrogen with
the interface states that create random telegraph noise (RTS) and 1/f noise as the
interface states are passivated and depassivated by the mobile hydrogen. RTS noise
is quantized transitioning between discrete observable states as single atomic level
defects, as described in Chap. 7. The 1/f noise occurs when many defects are
involved in the noise (Fleetwood et al., 2024). Figure 14.18 shows increasing noise
as a function of the change in threshold voltage caused by interface states after
radiation damage and annealing. Careful experimentation attributed the noise to
hydrogen action (Aigier et al., 1992; Fleetwood et al., 2024).
Figure 14.19 shows an example of hydrogen-related noise in CMOS. A noise
metric is plotted as a function of temperature before and after radiation. The three
peaks in the after-radiation curve correspond to hydrogen action in the dielectric of
lateral hydrogen motion in the dielectric, H2 diffusion or disassociation, and H2
drift. The corresponding activation energies are approximately 0.3, 0.45. and
0.75 eV. The advantage of studying radiation-damaged transistors is that large

Fig. 14.18 Noise power plotted as a function of the shift in threshold voltage caused by interface
states for power transistors irradiated and annealed after irradiation. The anneal is performed at
room temperature with the transistor biased. The threshold voltage shift is attributed to hydrogen
activity

340

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.19 Plot showing hydrogen related noise behavior at room and low temperatures before
and after radiation damage. Peak (1) is associated with lateral hydrogen movement in the gate
dielectric. Peak (2) is associated with H2 diffusion or dissociation in the gate dielectric. Peak (3) is
associated with H2 drift in the dielectric

numbers of dielectric defects are created, significantly reducing the measurement
difficulty. Plasma-damaged structures should behave similarly.

14.11 Hydrogen-Based Donors and Acceptors
Isolated hydrogen in the silicon lattice can act as a donor or acceptor depending on
the charge state of the proton. Hydrogen can behave both as a donor or acceptor,
varying with the charge on the atom. Figure 14.20 shows a plot of the energy level
of hydrogen in the silicon bandgap varying with the hydrogen location in the lattice
(Johnson et al., 1994). The plot shows the energy levels for hydrogen as a function
of hydrogen position in the lattice. As the hydrogen is trapped interstitially, the stability of the hydrogen-based donor and acceptors can be expected to be weak with
the energy level unstable with temperature and electric field.

14.12 Physical Defects Caused by Hydrogen
Hydrogen can cause structural and electrical defects in a CMOS wafer by itself
without interacting with other atoms (Fleetwood et al., 2009; Pearton et al., 1992).
Hydrogen can form clusters and platelets where high hydrogen concentrations

14.13 Hydrogen Barriers

341

Fig. 14.20 Calculated hydrogen energy levels in the bandgap as a function of the hydrogen position in the silicon lattice for hydrogen in phosphorus doped silicon. BC is bond center and Td is the
tetrahedral location

create an electrically active defect at low temperatures. These concentrated defects
can form or dissolve depending on the temperature.
Hydrogen can play other roles in the silicon structure, including silicon embrittlement and modifying ductility (Pankove & Johnson, 1991; Pearton et al., 1992). In
addition, hydrogen can impact structural defects, such as aiding in the growth of
dislocations (Pankove & Johnson, 1991).
Hydrogen is an essential component of plasma etch processes. Also, hydrogen
can thermally etch silicon at high temperatures without using plasmas. For example,
silicon exposure to hydrogen at 670 °C can etch silicon (Lee et al., 2006).

14.13 Hydrogen Barriers
14.13.1 Introduction
Some materials commonly used in semiconductor processing can effectively block
hydrogen from diffusing through them. This effect can be advantageous or disadvantageous depending on the engineering strategy for controlling the hydrogen
reaching the semiconductor surface where the active devices exist. This section
describes some examples of the common hydrogen barrier films used in CMOS
fabrication. Unfortunately, an exhaustive survey of hydrogen barriers was not found

342

14 Role of Hydrogen

during the preparation of this book. Literature from tritium barrier experiments and
hydrogen metal protection experiments can provide insight into hydrogen barriers
(Nemanic, 2019).
The hydrogen barrier capability of a film is strongly dependent on its material
properties, including crystallinity, density, grain structure, porosity, solubility, diffusivity, etc. (Nemanic, 2019). An effective hydrogen barrier should be capable of
preventing hydrogen penetration with a film thickness compatible with CMOS processing. Generally, the thinner the film, the easier it is to add to a CMOS process flow.
Many films used in CMOS processing impact the hydrogen behavior in a CMOS
chip by design or inadvertently. For example, common metals such as copper or
aluminum used in metallization can block hydrogen, preventing the passivation of
the interface states (Fukamoto et al., 2004). Other metals, such as titanium, can
absorb hydrogen, preventing it from reaching the semiconductor surface (Popegen
et al., 2013). Hydrogen diffuses through dielectric films depending on the properties
of the materials. Hydrogen can be trapped in the grain boundaries of films such as
polysilicon (Pearton et al., 1992).

14.13.2 Alumina
Aluminum oxide (Al2O3) is a high dielectric constant material that is one of the
more effective hydrogen barriers. It is commonly used in applications where the
prevention of hydrogen encroachment is needed. One such application is for the
manufacturing of ferroelectrics. Ferroelectrics such as lead zirconate titanate (PZT)
or strontium bismuth tantalate (SBT) and their associated electrodes are extremely
sensitive to hydrogen. Exposing these materials to hydrogen can lead to catastrophic
electrical failure (Chowdhury et al., 2007). Hence, ferroelectric memories have
heavily optimized hydrogen barriers, with alumina commonly used as a barrier.
Alumina films as thin as 10 nm have been used as hydrogen barriers. Figure 14.21
shows the results of hydrogen barrier experiments on alumina (Helmich et al.,
2020). Various thicknesses of alumina films are placed on a silicon wafer with a
hydrogen emitting silicon nitride layer on top of the alumina. The wafers are
exposed to a rapid thermal anneal (RTA), which drives hydrogen out of the nitride
film into the wafer and atmosphere. The hydrogen concentration in the wafer was
measured by monitoring the change in the p-type wafer resistivity caused by the
boron neutralization by hydrogen described in Sect. 14.5.1. The plot shows increasing hydrogen concentration with increasing RTA temperature and decreasing hydrogen concentration with alumina thickness, demonstrating alumina’s effectiveness as
a hydrogen barrier.

14.13 Hydrogen Barriers

343

Fig. 14.21 Hydrogen concentration in silicon after a rapid thermal process heat treatment at 680,
770, 800 °C. The wafer is protected by an alumina layer on the silicon. A hydrogen rich silicon
nitride layer above the alumina is used as a hydrogen source. The diffusion length L is fit to the
data and shown in the legend. The Only Al2O3 point shows the background hydrogen concentration
in the film

14.13.3 Silicon Nitride
Silicon nitride is a common hydrogen barrier. It can perform as a hydrogen barrier
or as a hydrogen-emitting film, depending on the properties of the film and post-­
deposition processing conditions. There are many different methods of silicon
nitride deposition, including thermal chemical vapor deposition (CVD), plasma-­
enhanced chemical vapor deposition (PEVCD), atomic layer deposition (ALD),
physical vapor deposition (PVD), etc. (Kaloyeros et al., 2020).
The silicon nitride properties vary widely depending on the deposition method
and process conditions. For example, some silicon nitride films can be optimized
through processing conditions to be a hydrogen-emitting film or a hydrogen barrier.
PECVD silicon nitride is one of the most common deposition methods due to its low
cost and film properties. This section describes the performance of PECVD nitride
as an example.
Figure 14.22 shows a plot of the hydrogen barrier properties for selected nitride
films with the properties shown in Table 14.2 (Benoit et al., 2007). After the nitride
films are deposited, the films are exposed to a deuterium alloy (400 °C 100% D2) to
measure the barrier properties. The use of deuterium alloy as a tracer element allows

Fig. 14.23 Example of hydrogen related degradation at a shallow trench isolation edge due to

14.13.4 Hydrogen and Water Trapped Beneath Barriers
The electrical behavior of hydrogen depends on the materials present on the wafer,
which affect the drift and diffusion properties. Hydrogen barriers and gettering
materials can change the severity of mobile hydrogen problems (Park et al., 2000).
For example, a silicon nitride hydrogen barrier can trap moisture in a wafer, causing
isolation degradation along the shallow trench isolation edge, as shown in Fig. 14.23.
The subthreshold conduction is degraded by creating a positive charge, as described
in Chap. 5. The problem can be resolved in several ways, such as vacuum annealing
immediately before the nitride deposition. The proposed mechanism in this example
is oxygen-enhanced diffusion of the boron dopants, lowering the field threshold
voltage at the edge of the channel. Additionally, a fixed oxide charge may be created
by the water-based oxidation of the wafer from the trapped moisture.

14.14 Hydrogen Emission from Silicon Nitride Films
Silicon Nitride is commonly deposited using silane (SiH4) as the silicon source for
the film and ammonia (NH3) as the nitrogen source. These hydrogen-based precursors result in films with a large hydrogen concentration, up to 30% (Braun, 2021;
He, 1995). The atomic or molecular hydrogen can be emitted from the silicon nitride
films at film deposition temperatures and higher temperatures. The emitted hydrogen can diffuse through films on the wafer’s surface, reaching the silicon surface
and causing changes in the electrical characteristics of the CMOS devices. These
electrical changes can be beneficial or detrimental to the transistors. Examples of
beneficial changes include passivating interface states, improving DRAM refresh,

346

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.24 Plot showing the amount of hydrogen emitted from a silicon nitride layer as a function
of temperature for four different films. The label Si ~ N is an intermediate silicon-nitride ratio film.
Si-rich is a silicon rich silicon nitride film. N-rich is a nitrogen rich nitride film. Stack is a three
layer film consisting of the previous three films. Peak 1 is the surface desorption of H2. Peak 2 is
H2 effusion from Si-H bonds. Peak 3 is hydrogen effusion from Si-N bonds. Note that there is no
high temperature effusion from the silicon rich film, indicating a lack of Si-N bonds

etc. (Lee et al., 2012). Examples of detrimental changes include degradation of hot
electron lifetime and negative bias temperature instability (Gasner, 1990).
Hydrogen effusion from different silicon nitride films is shown in Fig. 14.24
(Jarafi et al., 2019). The nitride film is exposed to a temperature ramp in a vacuum,
causing outgassing, and the emitted gases are analyzed with a mass spectrometer.
This technique is called thermal desorption spectroscopy (TDS). The amount of
hydrogen at each temperature step is measured, producing the plot. There is a significant difference in the hydrogen emission as a function of film properties.
The film with the silicon and nitrogen concentrations nearly equal (Si~N) has a
prominent peak below 400 °C where unreacted unbonded H is emitted from the
films. The films have two emission peaks near 500–550 °C and 750–800 °C. The
lower temperature peak is caused by thermal energy breaking Si-H bonds, resulting
in H emission from the film. The higher temperature peak is caused by thermal
energy breaking N-H bonds, resulting in H emission from the film. The higher temperature hydrogen emission for the N-H bond is due to the higher bond strength of
the N-H bond. The disassociation energy for the H-Si bond is 293.3 Kj/mol, and the
disassociation energy for the H-N bond is 358.8 Kj/mol, indicating the H-N bond
breaking occurs at higher temperatures (Haynes, 2016–2017, pg. 9–76).
Figure 14.25 shows a sketch explaining the hydrogen emission dependence on
the silicon nitride deposition recipe conditions (Wang, 2001). Higher deposition

14.14 Hydrogen Emission from Silicon Nitride Films

347

Fig. 14.25 Sketch showing emitted hydrogen as a function of silicon nitride process deposition
conditions. There are two recipe conditions that can achieve a hydrogen target level as shown by
the pink line

temperatures result in less hydrogen emission due to less hydrogen incorporation
into the film. The minimum hydrogen incorporation occurs when the deposited film
is stoichiometric with a nitrogen-to-silicon ratio of 1.33 for the Si3N4 film. More
hydrogen is trapped in the film for silicon or nitrogen-rich films as the hydrogen
bonds with the excess silicon or nitrogen atoms.
The film can be optimized for low or high hydrogen emission with recipe modifications. Higher hydrogen emission at low temperatures can be obtained with a
silicon-rich film by increased silane flow or lower power. Higher emission at high
temperatures can be obtained with a nitrogen-rich film. The minimum emission can
be obtained with a stoichiometric film deposited at high temperatures.
Figure 14.26 shows some important properties of silicon nitride as a function of
the refractive index and silicon-to-nitrogen ratio (Bredemeier et al., 2019). The ratio
of silicon-hydrogen and nitride-hydrogen bonds in the film determines important
hydrogen-related properties. The film density increases with increasing nitride concentration, limiting hydrogen diffusion and limiting hydrogen emission.
The hydrogen concentration as a function of the refractive index is shown in
Fig. 14.27, peaking near a refractive index of 2.4 (Bredemeier et al., 2019). The
hydrogen emission from a silicon nitride film exposed to a 900 °C rapid thermal
anneal as a function of the refractive index is also shown in the figure. Increasing the
refractive index and increasing the silicon-to-nitrogen ratio leads to more hydrogen
loss from the film. Low silicon concentration films contain low concentrations of

Fig. 14.28 Plot showing CMOS imager dark current improvement as a function of passivation density

hydrogen and emit lower amounts of hydrogen. Therefore, this type of film performs better as a hydrogen barrier.
Nitride films can significantly impact sensitive CMOS devices, improving or
degrading performance depending on process condition details. Figure 14.28 shows
a plot of CMOS imager dark current as a function of the passivation for silicon
nitride and phosphorous silicate glass films (Regolini et al., 2007). There is a strong
improvement in the imager dark current with increasing film density. The improvement with increasing density is attributed to the higher density passivation, which
prevents the out-diffusion of hydrogen from the wafer as well as hydrogen emission
from the film.
The different silicon nitride barrier deposition conditions described previously
also affect the crystallinity of the film. The effectiveness of the barrier is improved
with a more amorphous film and degraded with a more crystalline film, as shown
for sodium in Chap. 16, where silicon nitride is demonstrated as a mobile ion
barrier.

14.15 Hydrogen Interaction with Metals
Hydrogen can react with the metals commonly used in CMOS wafers, changing its
behavior. For example, hydrogen is commonly gettered with titanium, cobalt, or
nickel silicides, becoming trapped in the metal temporarily or permanently

350

14 Role of Hydrogen

Fig. 14.29 Secondary Ion Mass Spectroscopy (SIMS) of a titanium or cobalt silicide layer
exposed to hydrogen on top of a silicon dioxide layer showing hydrogen trapped in the silicide layers

depending on the subsequent temperatures on the wafer. Figure 14.29 shows the
hydrogen profile in a cobalt or titanium silicide layer on top of a silicon dioxide
layer (Wang, 2001). The titanium silicide layer traps more hydrogen than the cobalt
silicide layer.
Other metals, such as aluminum, can function as a catalyst, cracking a water
molecule and creating H+ and OH- products that change the hydrogenation of the
wafer (Liou et al., 2001). The use of aluminum in processing equipment can increase
hydrogen exposure.
Other common materials, such as the plastic used in wafer boxes and front-­
opening universal pod (FOUP) used to transport wafers between process steps, can
absorb hydrogen and other gases, remitting them on the wafer. FOUPs are commonly purged with nitrogen to limit wafer exposure.

14.16 Deuterium
The rare hydrogen isotope deuterium is commonly used as a tracer for hydrogen-­
related experiments because its natural rarity makes detection easy. Deuterium can
be used as a process gas, and the location of the isotope in the wafer can be tracked
with common analytical techniques such as secondary ion mass spectroscopy
(SIMS), providing insight into hydrogen behavior during semiconductor processing. The use of deuterium allows the isolation of the hydrogen from specific sources

14.17 Enhanced Low-Dose Rate Radiation Damage

351

from the ubiquitous general background hydrogen. Deuterium, with a higher mass,
diffuses slightly slower but otherwise matches the behavior of hydrogen.
Deuterium can also improve CMOS reliability under certain conditions, improving CMOS reliability for problems such as hot-carrier injection, stress-induced
leakage current, and bias temperature instability (Jung et al., 2022). The additional
mass due to the extra neutron is believed to make the Si-D bond more robust,
enhancing reliability.

14.17 Enhanced Low-Dose Rate Radiation Damage
Hydrogen can play an important role in radiation damage for CMOS devices. The
radiation damage can be increased for lower radiation exposure rates when compared to high dose rate exposure. This phenomenon is called enhanced low dose rate
sensitivity (ELDRS). The hydrogen-related physics behind ELDRS is believed to be
very closely related to the physics of bias temperature instability (Tsetseris
et al., 2005).
Figure 14.30 shows a plot demonstrating ELDRS (Zimin et al., 2018). The shift
in threshold voltage for a NMOS transistor as a function of total radiation dose is
plotted for a high and low dose rate. The low dose rate threshold voltage shift is

Fig. 14.30 Plot showing enhanced low dose rate sensitivity for radiation damage. The threshold
voltage of nmos devices is plotted as a function of total dose. Two dose rates of 1 rad/s and
100 rad/s are compared with the lower dose rate showing more damage and a larger shift in threshold voltage

352

14 Role of Hydrogen

substantially worse. This phenomenon is attributed to the formation of hydrogen
molecules from diffusing hydrogen atoms (Tsetseris et al., 2005). This phenomenon
can lead to an underestimation of the radiation sensitivity of CMOS technologies
measured at high dose rates.

Chapter 15

Metallic Defects

15.1 Introduction
Metal impurities in CMOS wafers can cause many electrical and physical defects
detrimental to the yield and operation of a CMOS chip. A single metal atom can
destroy sensitive devices such as a DRAM cell or a CMOS imager pixel, resulting
in yield loss or degraded quality. Advanced logic chips can fail to yield due to a
single atomic metal defect in one finfet, killing the entire chip. Higher amounts of
metal contamination can degrade all types of CMOS chips. The mitigation and
elimination of metal defects during CMOS processing have been heavily studied
and are essential to successful CMOS manufacturing. CMOS manufacturers have
minimized metal contamination through rigid control of the contamination levels of
incoming consumable materials, optimization of the processing tools to prevent the
wafer from coming into contact with harmful contaminants, etc.
Figure 15.1 shows a periodic table highlighting the risk of device degradation or
damage for each element (Shamazaki et al., 2008). The elements with the highest
propensity for damage are on the right side of the transition metals (in Red) in the
periodic table and include gold, which is probably the worst element for killing the
minority carrier lifetime. Copper is highly mobile and is a significant risk because
of its wide use in chips for metallization. Cobalt and nickel are widely used in silicides to reduce the resistance of semiconductor regions. Finally, iron is a common
contaminant due to its ubiquitous use in metals used in modern industry.
The orange-colored elements denote elements with medium risk. Titanium and
tungsten are widely used in CMOS metallization. Yellow highlights the elements
associated with a lower risk for device degradation. Zirconia and hafnia are widely
used in gate dielectrics where limited thermal processes prevent damage to the
underlying silicon. The alkali metals lithium, sodium, and potassium cause the
mobile ion problems described in Chap. 16.

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_15

355

356

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.1 Periodic table showing the propensity of an element for causing damage or electrical
degradation to CMOS chips. Red color indicates a large propensity for damage or degradation.
Orange color indicates a medium propensity for damage or degradation. Yellow color indicates a
smaller propensity for damage or degradation. Green color indicates damaging elements that typically cause mobile ion problems

An excellent book on this subject is highly recommended (Claeys &
Simoen, 2018).
The probability of an element contaminating a wafer is related to the likelihood
of the wafer being exposed to the element somewhere along the manufacturing flow.
For example, the use of stainless steel is minimized in semiconductor equipment to
avoid wafer contamination by the steel components (Fe, Cr, Ni, Mo, Ti, Al, Cu,
etc.). Gold is a severe contaminant but rarely causes problems because its use in
wafer manufacturing is nonexistent in CMOS processes (excluding wire bonding).
Commonly used metals in CMOS metallization, such as Ti, Co, Ni, W, Cu, Hf, etc.,
have increased risk because they are used in many CMOS processes. The consumables used in manufacturing are heavily optimized to minimize metal contamination
from the incoming wafer, gases, chemicals, etc.
Metal contamination can be detected through electrical testing of the affected
silicon devices, as described in the following sections. However, chemical analytical techniques have substantial advantages in troubleshooting metal ion contamination in a wafer fab. Chemical techniques allow exact, unambiguous determination of
the exact contaminating atoms. Additionally, chemical techniques are very useful
for inline troubleshooting, allowing equipment, chemicals, etc., to be isolated and
tested as the sources of the contamination. Chemical techniques use a simple test
wafer instead of the complex devices needed for electrical testing.
The semiconductor industry generally uses a few analytical techniques for metal
contamination detection. The most common method is vapor phase decomposition
(VPD), in which vapor hydrogen fluoride is used to remove the surface native oxide,
forming water droplets, followed by the collection of the water droplets for chemical techniques. The droplets can be analyzed by total reflection X-ray fluorescence
(TXRF) and other techniques, resulting in detection levels of around 108 to

15.3

Common Effects of Metal Contamination

357

1010 atoms/cm3 (Claeys & Simoen, 2018). Other common analytical techniques
include time-of-flight secondary ion mass spectrometry (TOF-SIMS), which has a
sensitivity of approximately 6 × 108 atoms/cm3. Energy-dispersive X-ray (EDX)
analysis is useful for analyzing particles visible in a cross section with a sensitivity
of 0.1–1.0 atomic percent (see Chap. 16 for a discussion of analytical chemistry).

15.2 Common CMOS Contamination Sources
Virtually any process step and equipment can introduce metallic impurities into the
wafer. Therefore, semiconductor consumable materials, such as wafers, resists,
chemicals, gases, water, etc., are heavily filtered and purified to eliminate metallic
and other impurities. Wafer manufacturers grow the silicon crystal and fabricate the
starting material wafers using methods to minimize metal contamination. Fabrication
tools are designed not to introduce metallic materials into the wafer by using specific alloys, ceramics, etc., in the chamber exposed to the wafer and external to the
chamber. Any component of the fabrication tools that comes into direct contact with
a wafer is a potential source of contamination.
The metallization steps necessary to create a CMOS chip may add metal impurities in unintended locations, including the wafer substrate, gate dielectrics, etc. The
copper metallization used in CMOS processes has a high propensity for metallic
contamination. The copper back end of line processes use metal and dielectric barriers to minimize copper migration. Gate metallization, such as tungsten, can contaminate the gate dielectric and substrate. Salicide formation using titanium, cobalt,
nickel, etc., in direct contact with the silicon substrate can contaminate the wafer.
Assembly-related processes such as through-silicon vias (TSVs) can also contaminate the wafer. High-k gate transition-metal oxides are a potential source of metal
contamination. Table 15.1 shows a partial list of common sources of metallic contamination in wafer fabrication. However, virtually, all process steps have the propensity to contaminate the wafer with metals (Claeys & Simoen, 2018).

15.3 Common Effects of Metal Contamination
Metal contamination in a CMOS wafer can cause many distinct types of physical
and electrical defects. A partial list of first-order effects is shown in Table 15.2 in
rough order of the contamination level necessary to observe the effects, decreasing
from top to bottom. Heavy contamination can cause physical structural defects, and
low contamination levels can destroy DRAM or imager chips. Multiple observable
types of physical defects can be caused by metal contamination. Metal contamination can induce wafer haze and roughness on the surface where metal precipitates
on the wafer surface, modifying the reflectivity. Haze is typically associated with
for high levels of metal contamination. Metals that induce haze include iron, cobalt,

358
Table 15.1 Common sources
of metal contamination in
CMOS wafers

15 Metallic Defects
Crystal growth and wafer manufacturing
Wafer cleaning, deionized water
Resist and masking material deposition, strip, and
removal
Wafer handling and backside contact with chucks, etc.
Ion implantation
Gate dielectric (high-k)
Rapid thermal processing and furnace processing
Silicidation or salicide processing
Gate metallization
Contact metallization
Metallization
3D integration, through-silicon vias, etc.

nickel, copper, rhenium, and palladium (Graff, 1995). Sometimes, special etchants
are used to highlight haze, making it more visible.
Metal contamination on the wafer surface can prevent normal oxide growth, creating dielectric defects. In addition, metal defects can induce crystalline defects like
stacking faults in silicon. Metal atoms can precipitate into clusters of metals in the
wafer bulk or on the surface. Metal atoms can create silicon defects such as dislocations or become trapped at defect locations, possibly enlarging them. The propensity for cluster formation is a strong function of the thermal history and ramp-down
conditions during processing (Graff, 1995).
Metal defects can severely degrade semiconductor junction diodes and the source
and drain junctions of CMOS transistors, resulting in excessive leakage, reduced
breakdown, shorted junctions, etc. In addition, metal defects can have a subtle effect
on the device parameters of CMOS devices, shifting threshold voltages, creating Vt
hysteresis, mobility reduction, degraded subthreshold slopes, short channel effects,
and increased noise (Claeys & Simoen, 2018).
Single-atom metal defects can cause failure in sensitive devices when one of the
first-order effects becomes large enough to cause the device to fail. For example, a
DRAM cell fails when the junction or dielectric leakage becomes sufficient to drain
the stored charge from the capacitor node, causing the cell to lose the digital state
before the required refresh interval. Another example is that excessive leakage in a
CMOS imager photodiode may cause the pixel to lose the stored image information.
Details on these types of failures may be described in the technology-specific chapter (Chap. 8).

15.4 Electrical Activity of Metal Ion Contaminants
Metal impurities in a silicon wafer create generation-recombination (G-R) centers
where electrons and holes can be trapped, created, recombine, and transition
between the bands. The defect centers are commonly called Shockley-Read-Hall

(SRH) centers. Figure 15.2 is a band diagram sketch of the electrical processes that
occur at an SRH defect, showing the before and after conditions for each transition
(Sze & Lee, 2010). An electron in the conduction band can become trapped in the
center (Fig. 15.2a), remain in the center for a while, and be re-emitted back into the
conduction band (Fig. 15.2b). A hole in the valence band can become trapped in the
center (Fig. 15.2c), remain in the center for a while, and be re-emitted back into the
valence band (Fig. 15.2d). An electron and hole can also recombine in the metal
defect, annihilating both carriers (Fig. 15.2e).

360

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.2 Generation-recombination mechanisms that occur at a metal SRH defect: (a) electron
capture, (b) electron emission, (c) hole capture, (d) hole emission, and (e) electron-hole
recombination

The SRH mechanisms created by metal and other defects degrade the electrical
device characteristics of contaminated wafers, as summarized in Table 15.2.
Increased leakage in junction diodes or mos capacitors is one of the primary symptoms of SRH metal-related leakage. SRH-related leakage typically dominates near
room temperature, while other mechanisms, such as carrier thermal generation,
dominate at elevated temperatures. Other mechanisms, such as radiative and Auger,
are neglected here as they are less important in silicon. Radiative effects can be
neglected in indirect bandgap silicon. Auger mechanisms only become significant at
high doping or current density.
Figure 15.3 shows some of the G-R effects in a one-sided n+/p− reverse-biased
diode with the depletion region (dashed line) extending into the silicon. Nearly
identical effects occur in a mos capacitor in depletion. SRH centers are shown at the
wafer surface or inside the n+ region (Fig. 15.3a), inside the depletion region
(Fig. 15.3b), at the STI edge (Fig. 15.3c), inside the wafer bulk (Fig. 15.3d), and at
the backside wafer surface (Fig. 15.3e). Carriers generated by the thermal processes
create an electron-hole pair at the G-R centers. The generated carriers respond to the
electric field with the negative electrons drifting toward the positive biased n + region
and the positively charged holes drifting toward the grounded backside contact. The
current created by the carrier flow in the electric field creates a leakage current in the
diode with electrons collected by the positive supply and the holes collected by the
substrate ground.
Figure 15.4 shows a plot of diode leakage as a function of the areal surface metal
concentration for a device intentionally contaminated with a wet chemical treatment
(Claeys & Simoen, 2018, pg. 307). The metal contaminants diffuse into the wafer

with further processing after the contaminating step. The contaminants form SRH
defects in the active region of the diodes. The surface metal concentration cannot
directly be converted to a volume concentration because the metal diffusion profile
is unknown.
Figure 15.5 shows a similar diagram for a positively biased mos capacitor biased
in depletion containing identical defects as the diode shown above in Fig. 15.3
(Claeys & Simoen, 2018, pg. 108). Again, carriers generated in the bulk of the semiconductor (4) and the backside drift (5) and diffuse toward the surface, where they
are captured by the MOS capacitor depletion region, increasing the minority carrier
population and affecting the device characteristics. Carriers generated in the depletion region (1) separate with the electrons drifting to the surface, and the positive
gate field pushes the holes away from the surface. Defects at the surface generate
electron-hole pairs inside the gate region (3) or outside the gate region (2), and the
electric field also separates the electron-hole pairs. The metal defect-induced generation recombination mechanisms can affect many device characteristics, as shown
in Table 15.2.
The structure of a MOS transistor combines the mechanisms of the junction
diode and MOS capacitor. During normal operating conditions, the electrical biases
inside a CMOS transistor can affect how metals drift, diffuse, and accumulate inside
the transistor junctions. Figure 15.6 shows an example sketch explaining how positive copper ions behave inside the transistor. (Claeys & Simoen, 2018; Yeon et al.,

15.5

Relevant Important Properties of Metals

363

Fig. 15.6 Cross-section sketch of a nmos transistor showing the location of metal contamination
in a mos transistor, accumulating in the source of the device

2012, pg. 308). The NMOS transistor is biased with the drain and gate biased positively. The source is grounded. This biasing condition tends to push and pull the
positively charged metal ions into the source, concentrating the metal defect density
into the source region.
A transistor operated in an unipolar mode with the source and drain relative
polarities always biased in the same direction tends to collect metals in the source
region (3,4) and drive metals from the drain region (1,2). An example of this biasing
type is a pulldown NMOS transistor in a CMOS inverter. On the other hand, a transistor operated in a bipolar mode with the relative source and drain reversed during
operation tends to push the metal ions back and forth between the source and drain
during operation, resulting in metal distribution throughout the device. An example
of this biasing type is a bidirectional CMOS pass gate passing a varying voltage.
Metal ions in a PMOS transistor behave differently from the NMOS transistor
behavior described above. The polarities in Fig. 15.6 are reversed for a PMOS
device with the gate and drain biased negatively with the source grounded. This
biasing scheme attracts the positively charged metal ions toward the drain in a
PMOS device instead of the source in a NMOS device.

15.5 Relevant Important Properties of Metals
15.5.1 Introduction
The atomic properties of metals and other contaminants determine the likelihood of
chip damage and degradation in addition to the probability of exposure. This section
describes the material properties relevant to semiconductor contamination.
Table 15.3 lists the properties important in metal contamination, some of which are

364

properties related to electrical

semiconductors

discussed in the following sections (Claeys & Simoen, 2018). The likelihood of the
wafer and chip being exposed to metal contaminants plays a significant role, as
some potentially damaging metals are not used in chip manufacturing.
The propensity for harm to a CMOS chip cannot be directly predicted from a
simple examination of the periodic table and is determined empirically. However,
studying the individual fundamental properties can provide some limited insights.
This section reviews some of the material properties shown in Table 15.3. The
energy levels in the bandgap, diffusivity, capture cross section, lifetime, diffusion
length, and solid solubility are reviewed in detail. Other parameters are discussed in
the references (Claeys & Simoen, 2018; Graff, 1995).

15.5.2 Trap Energy Location in Bandgap
The bandgap energy barrier is a crucial parameter for a semiconductor. The bandgap
barrier height must be large enough to prevent electrons and holes from significant
direct tunneling from band to band. Silicon is an indirect semiconductor where it is
usually difficult for carriers to tunnel between bands because the bands are not
aligned in space and energy. As a result, traps are normally required to allow transitions between bands. Figure 15.7 shows a sketch of the bandgap of a semiconductor
(Rubin, 2024). Figure 15.7a shows the energy transition required without a trap.
The energy gap for silicon is 1.1 eV.
The energy level of the metal contamination trap defect in the bandgap is an
important metal defect property. The worst-case trap energy level occurs when the
trap is located near the center of the bandgap, as shown in Fig. 15.7c. The tunneling
or emission probability is higher when the defect is located in the center of the
bandgap, causing more generation, recombination, and a higher trapping probability. The center of bandgap traps significantly impacts leakage and other forms of
device degradation. Traps located away from the center of the bandgap, as shown in
Fig. 15.7b, have a lower tunneling or emission probability and have less impact on
device degradation.

15.5

Relevant Important Properties of Metals

365

Fig. 15.7 Sketch showing trap levels in the silicon bandgap. (a) No trap present. (b) Trap T1
located away from the center of the bandgap. (c) Trap T2 located at the center of the band gap

Fig. 15.8 Plot showing metal defect position in the band gap for some of the worst contaminants
in silicon. The dashed line marks the center of the band gap. Two sources of data are compared
with (S) denoting and old source (Chen et al., 1987) and (C) denoting a modern source (Claeys &
Simoen, 2018)

The technical literature commonly includes graphs and tables of the defect
energy of metal contaminants that are useful in determining the severity of a contaminant by its location in the bandgap. For example, Fig. 15.8 shows a chart showing the position of energy levels for common silicon metal contaminants in the
bandgap (Sze & Lee, 2010). The bandgap center at 0.56 eV is marked with a dashed
line in the plot. Elements such as copper, iron, etc., have levels close to the center of
the bandgap, indicating that they create a strong SRH center.
Figure 15.8 compares older and recent data on trap levels for some of the worst
contaminants in silicon. Older publications typically show a few levels in the bandgap for each element, labeled (S) in Fig. 15.8 (Sze & Lee, 2010). Newer

366

15 Metallic Defects

publications using more advanced techniques tend to show many levels in the bandgap, as shown in the figure labeled (C) (Claeys & Simoen, 2018). The results in the
literature can be inconsistent with many different values published by different
authors (Rein, 2005). The trap levels are not very useful for identifying unknown
contaminants but may provide some insight into the electrical severity of a metal
contaminant.
An individual element can create multiple electrical energy levels in the bandgap, each with different electrical characteristics. Additionally, some defect states
are capable of trapping more than one charge carrier. Each energy level has an individual defect density, which determines the number of defects per unit volume in
the semiconductor. For example, the energy levels for hafnium contamination in the
bandgap are shown in Table 15.4 (Claeys & Simoen, 2018; Sachdeva et al., 2006).
Hafnium has 20 unique defect levels in the silicon bandgap. The first column in the
table denotes the energy level name. The defect energy level shows where the defect
exists in the silicon bandgap relative to the valence (Ev) and conduction bands (Ec)
for fast and slow diffusing levels. The capture cross section and the measured defect
density are shown. Despite hafnium having a large number of levels in the bandgap
and a few levels near the center of the bandgap, it is not an important SRH defect
center due to its low diffusivity at CMOS processing temperatures after gate formation. The number of levels for each element can vary widely.

15.5.3 Diffusivity
A contaminant’s ability to move through silicon and other materials affects its ability to reach device structures that can be degraded and damaged. Slow diffusing
elements are more likely to stay at the location where they were introduced into the
wafer and not migrate to the critical device regions. Conversely, elements that diffuse rapidly are highly likely to reach sensitive device regions and degrade them.
Elements diffuse through different mechanisms (Bracht, 2000). Fast diffusing
elements diffuse through interstitial diffusion, migrating rapidly through the spaces
between atoms in the crystal lattice. Slow diffusing elements diffuse substitutionally, replacing the silicon atom in the lattice and then repeating the replacement
process for the next atom as it diffuses. Additionally, some contaminants diffuse by
both mechanisms with diffusion rates between pure interstitial and substitutional
diffusion rates. Figure 15.9 shows a plot of the diffusion rates versus inverse temperature for several common metal contaminants. The three different types of diffusion are shown in the figure. Note the difference in the temperature slope between
the three different types of diffusion.
The slope of the diffusion rate is an essential factor in determining the severity of
a contaminant. The diffusion coefficient temperature slope is the lowest for the
interstitial diffusers labeled (a) in Fig. 15.9. The shallow slope means that these
contaminants can diffuse at temperatures inside the operating temperature range of
CMOS chips. Low-temperature diffusion can cause chip operation and reliability
degradation during packaging, testing, burn-in, reliability tests, etc., used in chip
manufacturing flows.
Most historical diffusion data was taken at high temperatures. Extrapolation to
low temperatures is not necessarily accurate, requiring data from low-temperature
diffusion measurements.

15.5.4 Carrier Capture Cross Section
The carrier capture cross section is a property that measures the propensity for a
contaminant to capture an electron or hole as it nears the defect. A large cross section indicates that the SRH defect is more likely to capture the carrier, which impacts
the device characteristics. The capture cross sections are different for electrons and
holes, sometimes by many orders of magnitude, creating a defect that traps one
dominant carrier. Table 15.5 shows a table of example data for some common silicon contaminants (Aissa et al., 2015). The location of the defect in the bandgap is
shown. The cross section of the electrons (σn) and holes (σp) are shown, along with
the ratio of the n-type to the p-type cross section (σn/σp). High ratios indicate that the
defect is less active as an SRH center, as the smallest cross section limits the generation rate. The energy level in the bandgap is shown to affect the defect behavior, as
described in Table 15.5. The difference between elements is significant, and the role

368

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.9 Diffusivity (D) of some elements in silicon as a function of inverse temperature. (a)
Elements with solid lines diffuse mainly through interstitial diffusion. (b) Gold with large dotted
lines diffuses through both interstitial and substitutional diffusion. (c) Elements with dashed lines
diffuse mainly through substitutional diffusion

of the cross section in CMOS degradation is not always obvious. For example, copper, a severe contaminant, has a small cross section for both electrons and holes.
Other elements, such as iron, chrome, vanadium, titanium, gold, and zinc, have
larger cross sections, indicating that they should be electrically active in silicon.
