15.5.6 Minority Carrier Lifetime and Diffusion Length
The minority carrier lifetime and diffusion length are important related metrics for
a semiconductor. The minority carrier lifetime and diffusion length play essential
roles in CMOS technology, affecting device leakage, DRAM refresh, CMOS imager
defects, etc.
Minority carriers are carriers of the opposite type relative to the local silicon doping (Meroli). A minority carrier is created by the SRH generation process, moves
through the device through diffusion and drift, and it is eliminated through SRH
recombination. The lifetime is the average time the minority carrier exists between
its creation through generation and annihilation by recombination, existing in the

370

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.10 Solid solubility of common metal contaminants in silicon as a function of inverse
temperature

conduction band for electrons and the valence band for holes. The lifetime τ and
diffusion length Ldiff are related by Eq. (15.1), where D is the diffusivity for electrons or holes. The equation applies separately for electrons and holes.
Ldiff = Dτ

(15.1)

The diffusion length specifies the distance the minority carriers travel before
recombining. These two parameters are closely related as they are different methods
of measuring the same phenomenon. A carrier with a long lifetime and diffusion
length has more time to travel through diffusion for a longer distance. Figure 15.11
shows the relationship between lifetime and diffusion length as a function of dopant
concentration for p-type and n-type semiconductors.
Generation is the process of creating mobile electrons in the conduction band
and holes in the valence band. The generated charge carriers allow the semiconductor to conduct charge, changing its resistivity. Generation can occur thermally or

15.5

Relevant Important Properties of Metals

371

Fig. 15.11 Plot showing the relationship between lifetime and diffusion length as a function of
dopant density for both n-type and p-type wafers

Fig. 15.12 Plot showing the generation and recombination lifetime as a function of the metal
defects position in the bandgap as a function of the defect energy level location in the silicon bandgap

through electrical methods such as drift and diffusion. Recombination is the process
of annihilating an electron and a hole, causing them to cease to exist as charge carriers removing them from the conduction and valence bands. Lifetime can refer to
either the generation or recombination process. There is a significant difference
between the generation and recombination lifetimes as a function of the defect location in the bandgap.
Figure 15.12 shows a sketch of the generation and recombination lifetimes as a
function of the trap energy level in the bandgap (Rein, 2005; Schroder, 1997). The

372

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.13 Recombination lifetime as a function of metal concentration in silicon for common
metal contaminants. The points labeled ref in the top left corner show the results for an uncontaminated silicon sample. The worst lifetime degradation is on the bottom right of the plot

left side of the plot with a trap energy level of zero denotes the edge of the valence
band (Ev). The right side of the plot label Ec denotes the edge of the conduction
band. The shape of the curve differs for generation and recombination. Note that the
generation lifetime is a stronger function of the trap bandgap location, while the
recombination lifetime is a weaker function of the trap location.
Metal contamination degrades minority carrier lifetime and diffusion length by
creating SRH centers where the charge carriers become trapped and recombine,
annihilating the carriers. More metal-related SRH centers can degrade the lifetime
and diffusion length. Figure 15.13 shows a plot of the recombination lifetime versus
the contaminant concentration for some metals and other elements as a function of
the concentration of the contaminants (Shamazaki et al., 2008). The lifetime of
some elements, such as copper and iron, is a strong function of the metal concentration. The concentration of other elements, such as cerium or hafnium, has little or
no impact on the lifetime.

15.6 The Worst Contaminants
15.6.1 Copper
Copper is one of the worst silicon contaminants because of its common usage in
semiconductor processes where it is used as an interconnect in metal lines and packaging processes such as through-silicon vias. The properties of copper, such as its

15.6

The Worst Contaminants

373

Fig. 15.14 Sketch showing different types of copper defects in silicon. (a) Point defects, including
individual atoms and point defects. (b) Formation of copper silicide precipitates in the silicon bulk.
(c) Migration to existing defects, including dislocations or grain boundraries. (d) Out diffusion to
the surface. (e) Segregation in p+ acceptor regions

location in the bandgap, high interstitial diffusivity, etc., create a severe risk for
CMOS chips contaminated with copper. Copper is one of the fastest diffusing metals, which makes copper particularly dangerous to CMOS chips (Claeys &
Simoen, 2018).
Copper precipitates at high concentrations typically greater than 1016 atoms/cm3
into several different forms and locations, including copper silicide precipitates,
precipitation into dislocations, oxygen clusters, and other existing defects. Copper
is attracted to boron or acceptor-doped regions and tends to precipitate there.
Figure 15.14 shows a sketch showing some of the copper mechanisms in silicon.
The behavior of copper in silicon is strongly dependent on the temperature ramp
down of thermal steps during wafer fabrication. Slow cooling creates copper silicide
precipitates and dislocation loops. At moderate ramp-down rates (~500 °C/s), copper can remain interstitial until it combines with boron (and other acceptors), forming copper-boron pairs. Rapid quenching at 2000 °C/s can form plate-shaped copper
silicide defects (Claeys & Simoen, 2018, pg. 152). Copper forms point defects at
lower concentrations, resulting in nonvisible and single-atom defects.
CMOS processes have many low-temperature steps throughout the backend of
the process flow where the metallization layers are formed, exposing the wafer to
long times at around 400 °C. At this temperature range, copper has a propensity to
migrate from throughout the bulk of the wafer to the wafer surface, collecting in
active transistor regions such as the channel and isolation oxide interface. As a
result, the surface copper can degrade device performance by reducing the transistor
drive through degradation in mobility through an increase in surface recombination
velocity. In addition, transistor and junction leakage can be degraded by the presence of surface copper. Figure 15.15 shows a plot of the lifetime and surface recombination velocity for high and low-temperature anneals. Higher surface
recombination values indicate degraded performance. Minority carrier recombination at a semiconductor surface tends to be much higher than recombination in the
semiconductor bulk (Claeys & Simoen, 2018, pg. 168). The surface recombination

374

15 Metallic Defects

locally depletes the minority carriers. The surface recombination velocity is a metric measuring the minority carrier loss at the surface. Note that the diffusion length
and surface recombination velocity behave in opposite directions with the annealing
process, as shown in Fig. 15.15. This result indicates a change in surface and bulk
behavior.
Copper is one of the worst elements for diffusing at near-room temperatures.
This property of copper can cause degradation of CMOS chips during operation in
the field and during packaging, testing, and burn-in at the final stages of chip manufacturing. Figure 15.16 shows a plot of minority carrier lifetime measured by an
optical technique for chemically copper-contaminated wafers. First, the wafers
were coated with a copper-containing chemical, and the copper was diffused with
an 850 °C oxidation. Then, a thermal anneal was performed on the wafers at lower
temperatures at various times. The lifetime degraded when the wafers were annealed
at around 100 °C for 45 min, indicating that the copper’s electrical behavior changed
with the low-temperature anneal. The control wafers with no copper contamination
showed no lifetime degradation. The low-temperature anneal disassociates a Cu-Cu

Fig. 15.15 Comparison of diffusion length and surface recombination velocity for copper contaminated silicon annealed at high and low temperatures. (a) Diffusion length is degraded less by
only low temperature annealing. (b) Surface recombination velocity is degraded less with low
temperature annealing

15.6

The Worst Contaminants

375

pair and creates an electrically active substitutional defect, forming an SRH center
and degrading the lifetime (Ramappa et al., 2001).
Copper also migrates easily through many types of materials, including dielectrics. Copper metallization in CMOS chips must be surrounded by metal or dielectric copper barriers to prevent copper migration, which can cause chip failure. The
barriers for copper metallization must be optimized to prevent copper migration in
addition to other properties such as adhesion. Failure can be caused by copper drifting between two metal lines, migrating through the dielectric during electrical bias
where two adjacent copper lines are at different potentials. This degradation can
occur under bias and temperature conditions during normal chip operation (Ciofi
et al., 2006). The copper migrates into the dielectric until a sufficient number of
copper atoms is accumulated in the dielectric. The copper can then form a filament,
causing dielectric breakdown (Wu et al., 2018).
Copper in a dielectric is usually a positive ion and may behave like a mobile ion,
as described in Chap. 16 (Ciofi et al., 2006; Miyazaki et al., 2008). Therefore, the
migration of copper can be detected using the same techniques used to detect mobile
ions described in Chap. 16. Figure 15.17 shows the detection of copper migration
using the triangular voltage sweep technique (TVS) (Ciofi et al., 2006). The current
during a voltage ramp is measured across the capacitor created by the intermetal
capacitance. The copper migration is detected by the current in the circuit displacing the current from the copper migration.

Fig. 15.16 Diffusion length measured by surface photo voltage diffusion length as a function of
annealing temperature for copper-contaminated p-type wafer samples annealed at various times.
An uncontaminated sample is shown as a reference

376

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.17 Detection of copper drift and migration using the triangular voltage sweep (TVS)
technique. The voltage across a dielectric between two copper lines is swept between opposite
polarity voltages, and the ion movement is detected by measuring the current flowing into the
structure. The small spike labeled Cu shows the copper movement detected by measuring the displacement current in the circuit as copper ions move from one metal line to the adjacent line

Copper metallization requires a barrier metal or dielectric surrounding the copper interconnect on all sides. Otherwise, the copper can readily migrate through the
intermetal dielectrics. As an example, Fig. 15.18 shows a plot of the drift rate of
copper ions in various common intermetal dielectrics with no intentional dielectric
or metal barriers (Loke et al., 1999). The copper drift rate is plotted as a function of
the inverse temperature. There is a significant difference between materials, with
some showing high drift near room temperature. The material properties that make
low-k materials useful, such as low density and porosity, may make the materials
more susceptible to copper migration.
Figure 15.19 shows a triangular voltage sweep plot comparing the copper barrier
performance of several metal barriers (Wojick et al., 2011). The poorer performing
barrier metals, such as physical vapor deposition (PVD) ruthenium, show a significant spike in capacitance near zero volts where the electrical field changes direction,
indicating a large amount of copper migration. PVD tantalum nitride (TaN) shows a
tiny spike, indicating low copper migration. A control experiment using only aluminum metalization shows a similar small spike, indicating the signal was from mobile
ions and not copper.

15.6.2 Iron
Iron is an ubiquitous contaminant for CMOS technology. It is commonly used in
many metals used in processing technology, such as stainless steel. Iron is one of the
metals that produce SRH defects with defect states near the center of the bandgap,
as shown in Fig. 15.8. Iron contamination has one of the worst impacts on the lifetime and electrical behavior of CMOS devices, as shown in Fig. 15.13. Iron has a
high vapor pressure and can evaporate from surfaces during thermal processing,
contaminating nearby wafers and equipment.
Iron can form more than many different levels in the bandgap, as shown in
Table 15.6. The defect level can be formed in several different configurations,
including interstitial iron (Fei), substitutional iron (Fes), iron-silicon vacancies (V),
iron-oxygen interstitials (Oi), and iron-hydrogen (H). The trap level in the silicon
bandgap is shown, along with the structural details of the defect. e and h denote if
the level is an electron or hole trap (Claeys & Simoen, 2018).
Iron can interact with the dopant boron in silicon in an unstable manner, alternating between two chemical and electrical states (Rein, 2005). Figure 15.20 shows a
diagram of the behavior of the two states. The two alternating states are a single iron
atom in an interstitial site and an iron-boron pair. The positively charged iron atom
is attracted to the negatively charged accepter ion that has captured an electron. As
a result, the iron and boron combine and are bound together by colombic attraction,
forming a bonded pair. The iron-boron pair is metastable and can be dissociated by
a small amount of energy added to the pair. The energy can be added thermally, by
exposure to light, or by injection of electrical current. The added energy causes the
iron-boron pair to disassociate, creating an electrically active acceptor and an electrically active interstitial iron atom. The two states are unstable, and oscillation

between the states can occur over the typical operating temperature range of a
CMOS chip, including room temperature.
The metastable iron-boron pair forms in low-energy environments near room
temperature. Annealing wafers at near room temperature (in the dark) can induce
the formation of iron-boron pairs.
The minority carrier lifetime for silicon containing iron and boron states is
dependent on the current level of injection, with the longest lifetime depending on
the injection level and the presence of the two states.
The minority carrier lifetime is a function of the fraction of metastable iron-­
boron and interstitial states (Rein, 2005, pg. 347). A plot of the lifetime as a function
of inverse temperature is shown in Fig. 15.21. Below 100 °C, the iron-born pair
formation dominates, as shown in Fig. 15.21b. From 100–150 °C, iron-boron pair
dissociation dominates, and beyond 150 °C, interstitial iron dominates. The fraction
of iron-boron pairs and interstitial iron as a function of temperature is shown in
Fig. 15.21a. Over the temperature range of CMOS device operation, transitions can
be expected between the two states, impacting the electrical behavior of devices.
The iron-boron metastable behavior may be a source of metastable electrical behavior in CMOS, such as random telegraph signal (RTS) noise.

15.6.3 Distinguishing Between Copper
and Iron Contamination
As copper and iron are common silicon contaminants, it is helpful to be able to
determine if a particular problem is caused by copper or iron. The metastable behavior of iron related to the iron-boron pair can be used to identify iron contamination,
as this behavior does not occur in copper-contaminated samples.
Figure 15.22 shows a technique to distinguish copper and iron contamination
(Ramappa et al., 2001). Two wafers with copper or iron contamination are measured

380

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.21 Plots showing the behavior of the low level injection lifetime for iron-boron pairs and
interstitial iron as a function of inverse temperature. The top plot (b) shows the dominant mechanism as a function of temperature for iron-boron combination (filled circles), Iron-boron pair dissociation (stars), and iron interstitials (triangles). The bottom plot (a) shows the fraction of iron
interstitials and iron-boron pairs as a function of inverse temperature

to determine the initial minority carrier lifetime. Next, they are exposed to light,
which causes the disassociation of iron-boron pairs and the dissociation of Cu-Cu
pairs in the iron and copper-contaminated samples, respectively. Both disassociation processes degrade the lifetime with light exposure, with a larger degradation
shown for the copper wafer. Then, the lifetimes of the wafers are measured as a
function of time at room temperature. The lifetime of the iron-contaminated wafer
recovers through the iron-boron combination process, as described in Fig. 15.20.
The copper-contaminated wafer does not recover as there is no recombination process. The light exposure severely degrades the diffusion length semi-permanently at
near room temperatures.

and iron contaminated samples to halogen light. The lifetime is measured near the surface.

15.7 Case Studies for Metal Contamination
15.7.1 DRAM Refresh
DRAM memory chips are particularly sensitive to metal contamination as the
refresh time is sensitive to leakage in the single transistor and single capacitor cell.
Metal-related leakage in the cell junction, access transistor, and isolation between
cells can degrade the refresh performance and other cell metrics such as variable
retention time, row hammer, etc. (see Chap. 8).
The following is a back-of-the-envelope calculation highlighting the impact of
low levels of leakage on the DRAM cell performance.
The capacitor in a DRAM cell, as shown in Fig. 15.23, typically has a capacitance of about 8fF in a modern DRAM cell. The voltage Vc used to store the charge
in the cell is about 0.5 V (difference between the bitline and cell plate). The charge
Qs stored in the cell is given by Eq. (15.2) and is estimated to be 4 fC. The number
of electrons Ne stored in the cell is given by Eq. (15.3), where q is the charge per
electron. The calculation shows a small number of electrons stored in the cell
(about 2500).
QS = CS Vc = 8 × 10 −15 F × 0.5V = 4 × 10 −15 C

A typical DRAM cell can retain data for a few seconds, and the DRAM datasheet
minimum refresh specification is around 64 ms. Assuming that the DRAM cell can
lose 50% of its charge before the cell fails, the DRAM cell can lose 1250 electrons
before failing (Schmid et al., 1994). Therefore, the maximum leakage current (IL) to
meet a 1 s refresh is estimated by Eq. (15.4), resulting in a leakage level of about a
femptoamp. Most leakage mechanisms are worse at high temperatures, so the
refresh specification is more difficult to meet at the upper datasheet temperature
specification.

There are multiple leakage mechanisms in a DRAM cell that can cause a refresh
failure, as shown in Fig. 15.24. These mechanisms include transistor-related leakage properties described in Chap. 8, along with the capacitor leakage. Some of these
mechanisms can be degraded by metal-related defects, including junction leakage,
diffusion currents from the bulk and backside, and gate-induced leakage. The sum
of all the leakages from the different mechanisms has to be less than the maximum
leakage current, requiring each mechanism to be well below roughly an order of
magnitude less than the maximum leakage current for an estimated 1 × 10−16 Amps
per mechanism.
A single metal contamination point defect can cause leakage in the range of
0.1 fA at 70 °C. For a defect in the depletion region, the SRH leakage can be estimated from Eq. (15.5), where q is the electron charge, ni is the intrinsic carrier
concentration, CR is the capture rate of the SRH trap, ET is the trap level in the bandgap, and Ei is half the bandgap (Vanhellemont & Simeon, 2007). The capture rate
ranges around 10−10 to 10−8 cm3 s−1.

384

15 Metallic Defects

Fig. 15.26 DRAM Refresh degradation due to copper contamination in assembly processes. The
right blue curve show the refresh characteristics post anneal without copper contamination. The
left most curve shows refresh degradation with backside copper sputter deposition, intentionally

Low-temperature processing conditions (200–400 °C) are routinely used in chip
assembly processes, such as wafer thinning, silicon vias, etc., which can induce
problems due to metal migration. Figure 15.9 shows that copper, nickel, and iron are
fast diffusers at low temperatures. Copper is notorious for migrating rapidly at low
temperatures, even migrating at room temperature (Istratov & Weber, 2001). Copper
contamination is a common cause of refresh degradation during assembly-related
processes. As an example, Fig. 15.26 shows the degradation of DRAM refresh with
copper contamination during the final assembly process (Lee et al., 2012; Yeon
et al., 2012). The DRAM refresh time was degraded when the wafer was intentionally contaminated. The refresh degradation was an experiment that intentionally
contaminated the wafer with copper by the deposition of copper on the wafer backside, followed by a 300 °C anneal for 30 min.

15.7.2 Tungsten Contamination in CMOS Imagers
CMOS imagers and charge-coupled devices are another technology that is very sensitive to metal contamination with single-atom defects (Toren & Bisschop, 1995).
The sensitivity of a pixel to metal contamination is much higher than that of any
other technology, reaching a level as low as 105 SRH defects/cm3. This sensitivity is
well below the detection capabilities of most other technologies and measurement
techniques.
One imager parameter sensitive to metal contamination is the dark current. The
dark current measures the signal coming from the pixel when the pixel is in the dark.

15.7

Case Studies for Metal Contamination

385

Fig. 15.27 Dark current probability distribution of a CMOS imager for intentionally contaminated and accidentally contaminated imager pixels with an uncontaminated control. One sample
was intentionally contaminated by ion implantation of tungsten (2.7E12 ions/cm2). The x-axis is a
digital code that measures the dark current from an analog to digital converter

The dark current signal has several components, including noise, thermal generation, and SRH center generation. The pixel signal is transferred to an analog-to-­
digital converter that converts the dark current to a binary number or code. The
distribution of dark current across many pixels can be used to detect metal
contamination.
Figure 15.27 shows the dark current distribution for a CMOS imager where an
imager was accidentally contaminated with tungsten, along with samples that were
intentionally contaminated with tungsten ion implantation (Domengie et al., 2010).
An uncontaminated reference imager is also shown. The majority of the pixels have
a low digital code number near zero, indicating that the dark current is low.
Conversely, defective pixels have a high code number, indicating a high dark current level.
The accidentally contaminated sample has a peak of defective bits near the
binary code of 50. The analysis in this study concluded that the accidental sample
was contaminated with tungsten at a level of fewer than 1.6 × 1010 atoms/cm3. The
study concluded that a single metal atom SRH center contributed a level of 1.64
dark current codes per defect. The number of SRH centers ranged from 16 to 45 per
defective pixel with a dark current code of 50. The large number of centers per
defective pixel was attributed to tungsten defect clustering, possibly caused by a
cathode gun in a process tool.

386

15 Metallic Defects

The intentionally contaminated ion implanted samples had a peak dark current
code of around 6, indicating less leakage per defective pixel. This result was attributed to a lack of tungsten atom clusters. This study highlights the sensitivity of
CMOS imagers.

Chapter 16

Mobile Ion Contamination

16.1 Introduction
Mobile ions can be created in a CMOS wafer by contamination with elements that
have the propensity to easily lose one or more electrons, forming a positively
charged free ion that can move through materials on the wafer. The mobile ions tend
to drift and diffuse toward critical circuit elements, damaging and destroying the
electrical circuit function.
Mobile ion problems are almost nonexistent in modern CMOS technologies. The
rare, infrequent mobile ion problems that do occur are usually catastrophic, taking
down a manufacturing plant, contaminating equipment, and contaminating the
inline wafers, making them unsellable. The electrical symptoms of mobile ion contamination are strange and nonobvious. Knowledge of mobile ion problems belongs
in an engineer’s knowledge base, even if there is a low probability of occurrence.
Mobile ions were a severe problem in the early days of metal-oxide-­semiconductor
(MOS) transistor development, delaying the introduction of the technology by many
years (Snow et al., 1965). Early MOS chips could only use PMOS transistors
because PMOS devices were somewhat immune to mobile ion problems. Early
PMOS chips were slow due to the low mobility, causing a performance penalty to
mitigate mobile ion problems. PMOS technology required negative supply voltage.
Solutions to the mobile ion problem allowed the manufacture of higher performance
NMOS devices, eventually leading to CMOS technology, which combined both
types of transistors.
Mobile ions cause time-dependent chip instability where a functioning chip fails
and possibly recovers during operation and testing. The failure was commonly
caused by threshold voltages of the transistor shifting dramatically with electrical
stress at higher temperatures, causing a loss of circuit function. After mobile ions
were determined to cause the instability, an intense, focused effort to eliminate the

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_16

389

390

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.1 Handprint image formed on a wafer showing the presence of mobile ions measured by
a corona discharge system

problem resulted in viable manufacturing solutions enabling the manufacture of
NMOS devices, followed by CMOS devices.
The source of the mobile ions was heavily investigated and was determined to be
the contamination of the wafers by alkali metals from the first column of the periodic table. Mobile ions can inadvertently be introduced in a CMOS production line
through contaminated consumables such as gases, chemicals, slurries, etc.
Contaminated manufacturing equipment is not easily repaired as mobile ions can
diffuse into glass, quartz, silicon carbide, and other materials, requiring disassembly
and replacement of tool components. The recovery of a manufacturing line from
mobile ion contamination can be expensive and time-consuming, causing economic
pain to the affected company.
The human body was determined to be a source, contaminating the wafers and
equipment with primarily sodium and potassium. Figure 16.1 shows a corona measurement of a silicon dioxide coated wafer with a handprint on it (Wilson et al.,
2006). The corona measurement can detect the electrical activity of the fingerprint
where positively charged mobile ions move under electrical bias at elevated temperatures. Modern fabrication lines protect the wafers from contamination by
humans by using extensive protective gear such as bunny suits, gloves, masks, etc.

16.2

Physics of Mobile Ion Contamination

391

The author has observed a few mobile ion contamination problems, including
contamination caused by workers tracking snow-melting salt into a fab (Ohring,
2015). Nowadays, rigid control of contamination sources protects fabs and keeps
them mobile ion free. However, chip manufacturers do not publicly disclose most
mobile ion problems as they are highly embarrassing.

16.2 Physics of Mobile Ion Contamination
Many different atoms and some molecules behave as mobile ions, drifting and diffusing in a CMOS chip. The term mobile ion describes elements with a set of properties that are particularly damaging to CMOS chips. Table 16.1 lists these properties.
Mobile ions can migrate through the wafer at the typical operating temperatures of
CMOS chips and the temperatures used for burn-in and reliability testing. Mobile
ions drift during electrical operation and diffuse with thermal energy, reaching critical device locations in the wafer where they cause loss of function and reliability
failures.
There are several ways for mobile ions to harm CMOS chips. First, mobile ions
can cause threshold voltage shifts, resulting in circuit failures. Mobile ions can
cause increased junction leakage and kill minority carrier lifetime. Severe contamination levels can cause dielectric breakdown.
Mobile ions interfere with the operation of CMOS devices by being semi-­
permanently charged. The mobile ion charges offset the voltage applied to the
CMOS transistors’ gate, shifting the operating voltages and causing circuit failure.
In addition, mobile ions are mobile at low temperatures in the range of CMOS operating temperatures and can move around the wafer, reaching sensitive devices.
Table 16.1 Key properties of mobile ions in semiconductors

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

16.2.1 Common Mobile Ions
Most of the mobile ions are positive ions, having lost an electron. The alkali metals
in the first column of the periodic have one valence electron, which can easily be
lost due to the low electronegativity of the alkali metals. The ions lose the single
valence electron and become semi-permanently positively charged. The ions are
mobile and move through the wafer through drift and diffusion mechanisms. The
ions are strongly affected by electrical fields due to the biases applied to the
transistors.
The alkali metals sodium and potassium are the most common mobile ion contaminants due to their universal environmental presence. However, lithium is an
unusual mobile ion contaminant as it is seldom present in CMOS manufacturing
processes and is occasionally used in lithium grease. Hydrogen can also be a mobile
ion, but it is quickly neutralized by interacting with the materials in the wafer.
Finally, due to their rarity in CMOS manufacturing, rubidium and cesium are seldom seen as mobile ions. The radioactive element francium is rare, has a short half-­
life (~22 min), and is not observed in CMOS wafers.
Elements besides alkali metals can be mobile ions. For example, the alkali earth
elements in the second column, magnesium, and calcium, are mobile ions in CMOS
chips (Williams et al., 1995). Numerous elements can be ionized and behave as a
mobile ion in a CMOS wafer. Several atoms, such as those shown in Table 16.2,
have been reported in the literature acting as mobile ions.
Transition metals can also be mobile ions. The use of copper is ubiquitous in the
semiconductor industry as it is the primary element used for metallization because
of its low resistance and other properties (see Chap. 15). Like alkali metals, copper
has a single electron in its outer shell, making it highly mobile. Copper behaves as
a mobile ion in CMOS chips. CMOS metallization processes are designed to prevent copper migration by using barrier metals and dielectrics to prevent migration.
Silver has been reported as a mobile ion in the literature (Snow et al., 1965), and
gold is known to be highly mobile in silicon (Coffa et al., 1987). Neither gold nor
silver is present in a CMOS process, although gold has historically rarely been used
as a minority carrier killer in a few non-CMOS technologies. Historically, gold has
sometimes been used in wire bonding.

16.2

Physics of Mobile Ion Contamination

393

Hydrogen can also be a mobile ion, but it is quickly neutralized by interacting
with the materials in the wafer and can occur with positive, negative, and neutral
charges in atomic and molecular configurations. Hydrogen drifts and diffuses very
rapidly due to its low mass (a single proton). The behavior of hydrogen in a CMOS
wafer is very complex, as described in Chap. 14.
Water can play a role in mobile ions as it is commonly observed to be absorbed
in films used in CMOS chips. It can diffuse from films and decompose into hydrogen, becoming mobile (Ciofi et al., 2006b). Water-related signals in triangular voltage sweep measurements are typically observed as mobile protons caused by
breaking the water molecules in CMOS wafers, as shown in Fig. 16.23. The prevalent theory is that water can exist as bonded or unbonded forms in films. For example, water can be bonded to hydrophilic silanol. The two different water forms
behave differently. Water that diffuses to the dielectric can be fractured, forming
atomic hydrogen that reacts with the interface, eliminating the water molecule.
The mobile ions in a CMOS wafer diffuse at relatively low temperatures and drift
under electric fields similarly to electrons and holes, albeit at different voltages and
temperatures. The element radius determines the mobility of the mobile ion in a
CMOS wafer with decreasing mobility down the alkali metal column of the periodic
table. Table 16.2 shows the activation energy and mobility for common mobile ion
contaminants in SiO2.

16.2.2 Drift and Diffusion of Mobile Ions
Figure 16.2 shows a plot of potassium and sodium mobility in the presence of an
electric field versus inverse temperature in silicon dioxide (Barbottin & Vapaille,
1986, pg. 427). The diffusion mobility is approximately described by a simple
Arrhenius equation as shown in Eq. (16.1), where μ0 is a material constant, Ed is an
energy barrier to diffusion, K is the Boltzmann constant, and T is the temperature
(Schroder, 2006). The energy barrier is the energy required to emit the mobile ion
from traps in the dielectric. The plot shows that sodium is easily mobile inside the
operating temperatures of CMOS chips. On the other hand, potassium is much less
mobile and requires a slightly longer time to affect CMOS chip operation

An important parameter for mobile ion contamination is the drift time required

at 1 MV/cm across various temperatures. The mobile ions Li and Na can transit the
dielectric in a few seconds in the operating temperature range of a CMOS chip. The
CMOS chip operating temperature ranges vary from 0 to 70 °C for commercial

16.2.3 Dielectric Properties Affecting Mobile Ion Migration
The material properties of the dielectric film containing the mobile ions strongly
affect the mobile ion drift and diffusion properties. The film density is important.
Low-density low-k dielectric films are often porous, providing less resistance to
mobile ion migration. Dielectrics may contain different characteristics and levels of
mobile ion traps that can temporarily delay or permanently stop ion migration.
Figure 16.4 shows a plot comparing the copper drift characteristics of different
dielectrics as a function of inverse temperature (He & Lu, 2012, pg. 67). The activation energy of drift varies between dielectrics. Copper generally diffuses faster
through low-k dielectrics, often orders of magnitude faster than diffusion through
silicon dioxide. Crystallinity is important in dielectric barriers, as shown in Sect.
16.14.3.
The drift and diffusion of mobile ions are affected by the local concentration of
the ions. Some materials can cause an increased local concentration as mobile ions
become concentrated at an interface, increasing the concentration-dependent diffusion component of the migration. Mobile ions may diffuse abnormally fast laterally
between and along interfaces of various films.

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

as a function of inverse temperature. The activation energies are shown for each dielectric.

16.3 Electrical Symptoms of Mobile Ion Contamination
Mobile ion problems cause several different electrical problems in CMOS devices,
as shown in Table 16.3. The top of the table shows the exact device failure mechanism caused by mobile ions. The second half of the table shows electrical symptoms
observed parametrically and at the chip level during electrical tests that contain
biased and unbiased temperature stresses.
Different mobile ions can damage the chip in different ways. Alkali metals and
alkali earth elements typically cause threshold voltage shifts of active and isolation
transistors. Copper tends to cause dielectric leakage and dielectric breakdown. For
a given mobile ion problem, one or many of the signatures shown are observed.
Detailed experimentation often reveals multiple signatures that tend to prove a given
problem is caused by mobile ions.

16.4 Threshold Voltage Shifts
16.4.1 Introduction
The transistor threshold voltage (Vt) shift described in this section is a primary
mechanism for chip failure caused by mobile ions, affecting both active and isolation devices. The threshold voltage shifts with time, temperature, and bias. The
threshold voltage shift can cause circuit failure, performance degradation, power
dissipation degradation, and other failure modes.
Figure 16.5 shows a sketch of the mobile ion impact on threshold voltages for
contaminated NMOS and PMOS transistors after positive and negative gate voltage
stress performed at elevated temperatures. Initially, as the wafers exit the fab, the
distribution of the mobile ions in the gate dielectric is unknown and usually randomized by the thermal energy of the final heat treatments in the process flow. If a positive bias is applied to the gates, the electric field forces the mobile ions to the gate
dielectric-silicon interface, where the NMOS Vt is decreased, and the PMOS is
increased. If a negative bias is applied to the gates, the mobile ions are forced by the
electric field to the gate dielectric-gate interface, where the NMOS Vt is increased,
and the PMOS is decreased. After the ions are driven on one interface, an unbiased
thermal bake rearranges the ion distribution, diffusing the ions away from the interface. The resultant threshold voltage resides somewhere between the positive and
negative threshold voltage points.

398

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.5 Sketch showing the threshold voltage behavior of contaminated NMOS and PMOS
transistors with voltage stress. (a) Initial Vt of an NMOS transistor in an unknown state. (b) NMOS
transistor after a positive gate voltage stress with a reduced Vt . (c) NMOS transistor after a negative gate voltage stress with an increased Vt . (d) Initial Vt of a pmos transitor in an unknown state.
(e) PMOS transistor after a positive gate voltage stress with an increased Vt . (f) PMOS transistor
after a negative gate voltage stress with a decreased Vt

Initially, as the wafers exit the wafer fab, the mobile ions are randomly distributed and widely diffused throughout the wafer by the last thermal steps, such as the
final alloy. This condition is shown in Fig. 16.5a and d. If the wafers are stressed
with a positive bias on the gate in (Fig. 16.5b and e), the positive mobile ions are
repelled by the positive gate potential and are forced to the gate dielectric-­
semiconductor surface. The positive mobile ions at the semiconductor surface
attract electrons that lower the Vt for an NMOS transistor and raise the absolute
value of the Vt for a PMOS transistor. If the wafers are stressed with a negative gate
bias, the positive mobile ions are attracted to the gate by the negative potential and
away from the gate dielectric-semiconductor interface, shifting the threshold voltages in the opposite direction. Mobile ions away from the semiconductor-dielectric
interface have a reduced impact on the threshold voltage compared to mobile ions
close to the semiconductor-dielectric interface. As a result of the negative gate voltage attracting the ions, the threshold voltage of an NMOS transistor decreases, and
the threshold voltage of a PMOS transistor decreases, as shown in Fig. 16.5c and f.
Finally, if an unbiased bake is performed on the transistors after the positive or
negative stress is completed, the mobile ions diffuse away from the interfaces,
returning closer to the initial state where they are randomly distributed, moving the
Vt closer to the initial Vt.
PMOS transistors are normally biased with the gate negative respective to the
other transistor terminals. On the other hand, NMOS transistors are normally biased
with the gate positive respective to the other transistor terminals. This difference in
the polarity of operation for the two devices impacts the device’s susceptibility to
mobile ions. The PMOS transistor is less susceptible to the positive mobile ions as
the negative gate bias attracts the ions to the gate where they have the least impact
on the transistor channel. Conversely, the NMOS transistor with the positive gate
bias forces the mobile ions close to the channel where they have the most impact on
the transistor channel. This phenomenon explains why early MOS chips worked
better using PMOS circuits.

16.4 Threshold Voltage Shifts

399

16.4.2 Isolation Failure
In addition to the active CMOS transistors, mobile ion problems can cause the isolation that separates transistors electrically to fail, causing shorts and leakage between
transistors. The isolation forms a parasitic MOS transistor where the isolation
dielectric serves as the gate dielectric. The silicon semiconductor forms the channel
like an active transistor, and any gate metal running across the isolation forms the
gate of the parasitic device.
The isolation commonly fails first during mobile ion contamination for several
reasons, as described in Chap. 5. Additionally, many isolation areas are unprotected
by the gate metal, which can block mobile ions. In contrast, the active transistor
always has the gate metal protecting it. The isolation has a very thick dielectric relative to the gate dielectric. As a result, the isolation is more sensitive to mobile ions
than a CMOS thin transistor dielectric and has a larger threshold voltage shift for an
equal amount of charge caused by mobile ions (Chap. 5). For example, the threshold
voltage shift for a 2000 A isolation dielectric is 100 times larger than that for a 20 A
gate dielectric using the same dielectric constant. However, a much larger threshold
voltage shift is needed to cause an isolation device failure relative to the active transistors. Figure 16.6 shows a plot of isolation failure with mobile ion contamination
tested with a biased bake (Gutai, 1997). First, the initial Vt before stress is shown.

Fig. 16.6 Plot of the drain current versus gate voltage for an nmos parasitic isolation transistor
stressed at +/− 20 V at 200 °C 20 s. For a 3000 A isolation dielectric thicknesses. The curves are
measured at room temperature. (a) After positive stress. (b) Initial curve before stress. (c) After
negative stress

400

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Next, the NMOS isolation device is stressed with +20 V, and the Vt shifts lower.
Next, the device is stressed with −20 V, and the Vt shifts higher. Repeating the positive and negative stress results in curves similar to the initial measurements as the
mobile ions move back and forth in the isolation dielectric between the gate interface and the semiconductor surface.
As described in Chap. 5, the large isolation threshold voltages are necessary to
prevent the isolation devices from leaking at normal CMOS operating voltages.
However, mobile ion contamination can cause the threshold voltages to drop to the
point where leakage between the active transistors causes the circuits and chip
to fail.

16.4.3 CMOS Transistor Degradation
In addition to the isolation field devices, the active transistors can degrade with
mobile ion contamination, showing a similar Vt shift, as shown in Fig. 16.6. The
CMOS transistor’s threshold voltage shifts cause the transistors to operate in a
region outside the design space where the circuit was designed to operate, leading
to circuit and chip failure. Figure 16.7 shows a sketch of the mobile ion shifts for an
NMOS capacitor and transistor, showing the results of mobile ion contamination

Fig. 16.7 Sketch showing a C-V shift for an nmos capacitor and an I-V Vt shift for an nmos transistor after a positive gate voltage stress at elevated temperatures. The threshold voltage for both
the capacitor and the transistor is shifted to the left (more negative)

16.4 Threshold Voltage Shifts

401

(Hofstein, 1966). For a positive gate voltage stress at an elevated temperature, the
threshold voltage for both devices shifts to the left. Conversely, the threshold voltages shift to the right for a negative gate voltage stress.
Gate dielectrics contaminated by mobile ions tend to have a smaller threshold
voltage shift than isolation devices. The gate metallization and gate-related dielectrics (spacers, etc.) can partially block mobile ions from reaching the sensitive gate
dielectric. In addition, the orders of magnitude thinner gate dielectric makes lateral
diffusion in the gate dielectric more difficult. Migration of mobile ions from the
dielectric-gate interface to the dielectric-silicon interface has a lesser effect on the
threshold voltage due to the shorter distance traveled. There is some evidence that
mobile ions are less mobile in high-k gate dielectrics (Parent et al., 2010). Figure 16.8
shows an example of mobile ion contamination in a 32 nm SOI technology
(Kothandaraman & Cohen, 2016).
Through-silicon vias are used to connect the chip to the outside world in advanced
packaging applications, as described in Chap. 9. The complex TSV process can create mobile ion contamination problems. Figure 16.8 shows a mobile ion problem
caused by a TSV process. A nearby TSV contaminated a PMOS transistor, causing
a 100 mV mobile ion Vt shift. The 100 mV shift is sufficient to cause circuit failures
in sensitive circuits. Transistors without TSVs are unaffected by mobile
contamination.

Fig. 16.8 Subthreshold voltage plot showing a 100 mV Vt shift of a 32 nm SOI pmos device contaminated by mobile ions from a nearby through silicon via (TSV)

402

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

16.5 MOS Capacitor Degradation
Mobile ion contamination can modify a MOS capacitor’s capacitance-voltage
(C-V) characteristics, as shown in Fig. 16.7. C-V characterization and analysis is
a common method for detecting mobile ion contamination. C-V characterization
for mobile ion detection uses a series of biased bakes and electrical
measurements.
The shift in MOS capacitor C–V characteristics can also affect the product, causing changes in circuit function. MOS capacitors are often used for circuit design
elements, and a change in characteristics may cause chip failure. Mobile ions have
been observed to cause additional C-V degradation beyond the lateral shift along
the voltage axis shown in Fig. 16.7. Figure 16.9 shows various C-V plots observed
on mobile ion-contaminated capacitors for a thick 200 nm dielectric (Snow et al.,
1965). The C-V curves are distorted due to various effects, including nonuniform
mobile ion contamination across the capacitor, effectively causing multiple different capacitors in parallel with multiple threshold voltages. Also, dielectric leakage
degradation may occur for heavily contaminated samples.

Fig. 16.9 Capacitance-voltage (CV) plots showing anomalous behavior caused by mobile ions.
(a) uncontaminated C-V curve. (b) Shift in Vt and change in inversion region behavior. (c)(d) C-V
curves due to dielectric leakage or multiple threshold voltages. (e) Shift in Vt beyond the measurement voltage range

16.7 Gate Dielectric Degradation with Mobile Ions

403

16.6 Diode Degradation
Mobile ions collecting in the dielectrics near a diode junction can affect the diode
current-voltage properties by changing the electric field near the junction, similar to
a gated diode (Schroen et al., 1965). The positive electric ions change the electric
field lines in this region, changing the diode forward and reverse bias currents. The
diode characteristics that can be modified by mobile ion contamination include
junction breakdown, leakage, and capacitance.

16.7 Gate Dielectric Degradation with Mobile Ions
Gate dielectric properties can be degraded by contamination with many elements,
including mobile ions, correlating with increasing degradation with increasing contamination ion density. The affected dielectric properties include leakage, breakdown voltage, and reliability measured by time-dependent dielectric breakdown.
Figure 16.10 shows a plot of time-dependent dielectric breakdown (TDDB) as a
function of mobile ion contamination concentration (Huang et al., 2014).

Fig. 16.10 Plot showing degradation in time-dependent breakdown with increasing copper
contamination

404

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

16.8 Intermetal Dielectrics Degraded by Mobile Ions
Copper is one of the mobile ions listed in Table 16.2, and its mobile ion behavior is
described in Chap. 15. Due to low resistance, it is the primary metallization used in
most metal layers in modern CMOS technology. Copper is highly mobile in dielectrics (see Chap. 15), migrating by drift and diffusion out of metal lines into the surrounding dielectrics, where it can degrade the dielectrics. Copper dielectric
contamination may cause dielectric leakage, reduced breakdown, and degraded
reliability.
Much optimization is required to develop a successful copper metallization process, including mitigation of copper migration. A copper line is typically surrounded
by a refractory metal barrier metal in a damascene trench, which helps mitigate
copper mobile ion problems (Chen, 2012). The metal barrier prevents copper from
diffusing and drifting under bias into the surrounding dielectrics. Figure 16.11
shows a current-voltage plot of the intermetal high-k dielectric breakdown for Cu
metal lines with and without a barrier metal. The group without the barrier liner
shows a decreased breakdown by about 10 V, indicating a degraded dielectric
caused by copper. The reference shows both a degradation in breakdown and
reliability.

Fig. 16.11 Breakdown of I-V curve of low-k dielectric between copper lines with and without a
metal liner

16.9

Product-Level Reliability Failures

405

16.9 Product-Level Reliability Failures
The electrical effects described previously in this section can be observed in individual parametric test structures. Additionally, mobile ion-related electrical reliability problems can be observed during product life tests and as reliability failures
in the field. CMOS products undergo extensive quality and reliability testing at
accelerating voltages and temperatures for extended periods to ensure that products do not fail during their life in the field. The mobile ion-related failure time is
governed by the amount of contamination, distance from the contamination location to the sensitive failing circuit, circuit sensitivity to mobile ion contamination,
voltage, temperature, and time. Table 16.4 shows an example of foundry qualification data showing a single failure at 500 h in high-temperature operating life reliability testing due to mobile ion contamination. A single chip failed for sodium
mobile ion contamination out of hundreds tested. All other qualification tests had
no failures.
Product-level failure analysis of mobile ion-related problems uses tests that
examine some of the unique electrical properties of mobile ions, including full or
partial failure recovery in an unbiased bake and an ability to reverse the failure with
a reverse polarity biased bake (See Table 16.3). Additionally, analytical measurements, such as secondary ion emission spectroscopy (SIMS) etc., can be used to
locate mobile ions in a CMOS chip.
One possible product mobile ion failure mode is a shift in the power consumption current of the chip (Sabin et al., 1997). This shift may be observed in standby
current measurements, where there is maximum sensitivity to isolation or threshold
voltages. Few circuits are active in standby mode, resulting in a low current level,
enabling the detection of small current shifts. Also, a chip in standby is largely inactive with steady-state biases on circuits, creating a constant field to attract or repel
mobile ions. Figure 16.12 shows a plot of the standby current drifting through time
measured at 175 °C with and without passivation. The reference concluded that
mobile hydrogen protons were the cause of the drift.

operating life test at 500 h of testing (Shideler, 1996)

16.10 Electrical Detection of Mobile Ions
16.10.1 Electric Measurement Introduction
Mobile ion contamination is often first detected electrically on failing production
wafers that have escaped all the standard prevention measures. Once isolated,
mobile ion problems can be detected chemically on simple test wafers or short loop
reduced flow wafers where MOS capacitors are formed.
Electrical detection is an essential diagnostic tool for mobile ions, providing a
high level of proof of contamination. Table 16.5 lists some common ways mobile
ions can be detected using electrical techniques on parametric test structures. Some
methods, such as triangular voltage sweep (TVS) and thermally stimulated ionic
current (TSIC), directly measure the current due to ionic motion through dielectrics.
Other methods, like threshold voltage device stresses, measure the results of ion
motion, such as threshold voltage shifts. This section describes the more common
techniques in detail.

16.10 Electrical Detection of Mobile Ions

407

Table 16.5 Common electrical methods for the detection of mobile ions
Electrical method
Triangular voltage sweep (TVS)
Capacitance-voltage analysis (CV)
Active device and field device stress
Thermally stimulated ionic current (TSIC)

y-axis shows volume density sensitivity. This plot is derived from Eq. (16.5)

16.10.2 Measurement Sensitivity and Capability
The minimum detection level is an important capability of each measurement technique. The lower limit of TVS measurement sensitivity has been reported to be
around 109 ions/cm2 (Schroder, 2006). TVS measurement capability is limited by
the low current capability of the instrumentation, hot chuck, and probe station in the
femtoampere to picoampre range. TVS sensitivity can be increased by increasing
the size of the test structure, creating a larger chip area for detection, and raising the
measurable mobile ion current. The sensitivity is a function of the ramp rate, and the
optimum rate must be determined empirically.
C-V analysis, active transistors, and field devices have a sensitivity limited by the
instrumentation’s capability to resolve a slight shift in the threshold voltage or flatband. C-V analysis has a reported sensitivity of around 1010 ions/cm2 (Schroder,
2006). However, the threshold voltage shift (ΔVt) due to mobile ions is a strong

408

sensitivity assuming 5 mV Vt resolution capability for mobile ion detection as a

about 1011 ions/cm2. For isolation, a typical dielectric thickness of around 200 nm

has a sensitivity of around 109 ions/cm2. The increased sensitivity for isolation is
one of the reasons the isolation tends to fail first in mobile ion contamination
situations.

16.10.3 Detection of Mobile Ion Contamination Using
a CMOS Transistor
A CMOS transistor or an isolation transistor can be used to detect mobile ions. The
initial detection of mobile ion contamination in a failing chip circuitry often occurs
in a CMOS transistor or an isolation device. These measurements take advantage of
the amplification properties of a CMOS transistor that can magnify a slight change
in gate charge into an easily measurable current.
Figure 16.14 shows an example of the behavior of an NMOS device with mobile
ion contamination. The device’s threshold voltage is measured by plotting drain
current versus gate voltage, typically measured at a low drain voltage (0.05–0.1 V).
The threshold voltage is extracted by extrapolating the curve to the x-axis. The
threshold voltage can be measured at either room or the high temperature where the
device is stressed. First, the initial threshold voltage is measured (Fig. 16.14a),
where the threshold voltage is affected by the initial location of the mobile ions.
Then, after high-temperature voltage stress with positive gate bias, the positive
mobile ions shift to the dielectric-silicon interface, lowering Vt (Fig. 16.14b). After
high-temperature voltage stress with negative gate bias, the positive mobile ions
shift to the gate dielectric-silicon interface, increasing Vt (Fig. 16.14c). The mobile
ion concentration can be calculated by the simple formula Q = C ΔVt/q, where C is
the gate capacitance of the capacitor in accumulation, ΔVt is the difference in threshold voltage after the positive and negative stress, and q is the electronic charge
(1.602 × 10−19 coulombs).
Figure 16.5 shows a sketch of the mobile ion impact on threshold voltages for
contaminated NMOS and PMOS transistors after positive and negative gate voltage
stress performed at elevated temperatures. Initially, as the wafers exit the fab, the
distribution of the mobile ions in the gate dielectric is unknown and usually randomized by the thermal energy of the final heat treatments in the process flow. If a positive bias is applied to the gates, the electric field forces the mobile ions to the gate
dielectric-silicon interface, where the NMOS Vt is decreased, and the PMOS is
increased. If a negative bias is applied to the gates, the mobile ions are forced by the
electric field to the gate dielectric-gate interface, where the NMOS Vt is increased,
and the PMOS is decreased. After the ions are driven to one interface, an unbiased
thermal bake rearranges the ion distribution, diffusing the ions away from the interface. The resultant threshold voltage resides somewhere between the positive and
negative threshold voltage points.

410

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

16.10.4 Triangular Voltage Sweep
Because of its unique properties, the triangular voltage sweep (TVS) technique is
the most common electrical technique for mobile ion detection. TVS measurements
can be performed on several different types of structures, including MOS capacitors, simple parallel plate capacitors, comb, and serpentine capacitors. C-V measurements require an MOS capacitor, while TVS can be performed on any capacitor
type. Mobile ion motion can be detected in these structures as the ions transition
from one terminal of the capacitor to the other. In addition, TVS has some capability
of identifying the exact problem element causing mobile ions through the temperature and voltage dependence of the mobile ion behavior.
The measurement system used for triangular voltage sweep is shown in Fig. 16.15
and consists of a voltage waveform generator, femtoampere meter, and hot chuck in
a wafer measurement station (Barbottin & Vapaille, 1986). The voltage waveform
generator ramps the voltage applied to the capacitor at a constant programable ramp
rate to apply an electric field to the capacitor that causes the mobile ions to drift
through the dielectrics. A highly sensitive femtoampere (or picoampere) meter measures the displacement current flow between the two plates of the capacitor that
compensates for the mobile ion motion. The voltage source is typically connected
to the chuck, and the femtoampere meter is connected to the probe tips to minimize
noise. The wafer sits on a low-noise hot chuck that can control and ramp the wafer
temperature. The entire system needs to be electrically isolated and shielded to provide a low-noise environment to enable current measurements in the femtoampere
to picoampere range. The capacitor size, amount of mobile ion contamination, and
mobility of the ions in the dielectric determine the current measurement capability
requirements.

Fig. 16.15 Sketch showing TVS measurement system

16.10 Electrical Detection of Mobile Ions

411

Initially, the wafer temperature is ramped to a selected temperature with the
capacitor unbiased. The temperature is selected empirically by testing a range of
temperatures with each measurement on a virgin capacitor. Typically, ranges from
100 °C to 350 °C are used. An alternative measurement technique prestresses the
dielectric at a higher temperature, applying a constant voltage and driving the
mobile ions to one interface. This stress maximizes the TVS signal, capturing the
maximum measurement current from one polarity to the other.
The voltage is swept at a defined ramp rate in one polarity while the current is
continuously measured as the mobile ions drift to the negative electrode. Typically,
a maximum electric field of a few MV/cm stress is used to drive the ions while
avoiding higher electric fields where direct or Fowler-Nordhiem tunneling currents
can interfere with the measurements. Then, the measurement is repeated, ramping
the voltage in the opposite direction, and moving the ions to the opposite plate.
Figure 16.16 shows an example TVS measurement measured at 350 °C (Kuhn &
Silversmith, 1971). The current spikes shown in the plot are the mobile ion signal,
while the flat regions of the measurement indicate no mobile ion movement. An
uncontaminated TVS measurement is flat and has no peak. Two different mobile
ions are detected in the measurement. The more prominent peak is sodium, and the
smaller peak is potassium. The area under the mobile ion peaks should be equal for
both polarity sweeps if all of the mobile ions are swept back and forth between the
plates. If the areas of both polarity sweeps do not match, it indicates that ions are
being trapped at one of the interfaces.

Fig. 16.16 Example triangular voltage sweep detecting the presence of mobile ions at 350 °C. (a)
The mobile ion current is measured as the voltage is swept in both directions. The arrows show the
directions of the voltage sweeps

412

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.17 Example plots showing the detection of mobile ions. (a) Increased mobile ion concentrations detected at low frequency compared to a high frequency measurement at 250 °C

The mobility of the mobile ion in the dielectric determines the peak location on
the voltage axis. In this example, the potassium peak occurs at a higher positive or
more negative voltage due to the lower mobility of the larger mobile ion, requiring
a higher field to drive it through the dielectrics.
Figure 16.17 shows some additional examples of TVS signals on a MOS capacitor ((Schroder, 2006) pg. 341). High-frequency and low-frequency plots are compared, highlighting the importance of a low ramp rate in the TVS measurement to
obtain the maximum signal. The high-frequency plot does not detect the presence of
mobile ions as the ions as the ions do not move with the rapidly alternating voltage.
The MOS C-V inversion charge characteristics are still observable as the downward
dip in the trace.
Figure 16.18 compares TVS signals for different temperatures (Stauffer et al.,
1995). No mobile ion signal is observed at lower temperatures (10 and 40 °C)
because the mobile ions are either immobile at these temperatures or not observable
with the measurement conditions and instrument capability. The ions may be mobile
over time periods longer than the measurement time. The quasistatic C-V characteristics with the dip in capacitance due to the depletion region can be observed as the
dip in the plot at lower temperatures. At higher temperatures, the quasistatic C-V
disappears as the thermal generation of carriers inverts the channel for all voltages.
At higher temperatures, the mobile ion peak increases with increasing temperatures.
At some higher temperatures, all ions are mobile, and the peak will no longer
increase. The mobile ion charge density can be calculated by integrating the charge
observed in the measurements.
The TVS signal peak’s position and width provides information on the mobile
ions. Figure 16.19 compares two TVS measurements for a thermal oxide and a
plasma-enhanced tetraethyl orthosilicate glass (PE-TEOS) that were intentionally
contaminated with sodium (Lifshitz & Smolinsky, 1989). The higher-quality

16.10 Electrical Detection of Mobile Ions

413

Fig. 16.18 Comparison of TVS measurements at different temperatures

Fig. 16.19 TVS measurements comparing two different sodium contaminated dielectrics measured at 300 °C. (a) Measurement on a thermal oxide. (b) Measurement on a plamsa-enhanced
tetraethyl orthosilicate (PE-TEOS) glass

thermal oxide has a narrow peak, indicating that the mobile ion movement occurred
at similar electric fields for all ions. The narrow peak indicates that dielectric consists of uniform ion trap depths, suggesting a homogenous material. The broader

414

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.20 Plots showing a comparison of different dielectrics uncontaminated by alkali metals.
(a) PE-TEOS deposited at 400 °C. (b) PE-TEOS deposited at 300 °C. (c) SOX polysilicate glass
deposited at 400 °C. (d) Accuglass™ 211 polysilicate glass deposited at 400 °C

peak for the PE-TEOS dielectric indicates a large spread of trap energies, indicating
a more inhomogeneous film.
The peak’s distance from the zero-volt origin indicates the difficulty in moving
mobile ions in the film. The thermal oxide peak near zero volts indicates that the
ions move easily in the thermal oxide. The large distance from the origin of the
PE-TEOS dielectric indicates that the ions are more difficult to move relative to the
thermal dielectric ions. The difference in the two traces from the x-axis origin in
Fig. 16.19 indicates that one interface has slightly deeper traps that slow the ion
migration.
The TVS signal can also show the behavior of mobile ions at the interface. If the
TVS peak disappears after a sweep in one direction, it indicates that the mobile ions
are trapped at that interface and cannot escape as the polarity is reversed. If hydrogen is the mobile ion under study, the mobile hydrogen may become immobile, as
described in Chap. 14. Figure 16.20 compares four different dielectrics free of alkali
metal mobile ion contamination (Lifshitz & Smolinsky, 1989). Figure 16.20a shows
a PE-TEOS film deposited at 400 °C with no discernable mobile ion contamination.
Figure 16.20b shows a PE-TEOS film deposited at 300 °C with a mobile ion peak
in one sweep direction. Figure 16.20c and d show TVS peaks in one direction for
two low-temperature deposition polysilicate glass films.
The single peak TVS signal in three plots observed in only one direction in
Fig. 16.20 is attributed to the motion of hydrogen protons created from water
trapped in the films. The TVS signal outside the peak has a slope and is not flat in
these films, indicating dielectric leakage or a small percentage of the protons

16.10 Electrical Detection of Mobile Ions

415

moving at electric fields unrelated to the peak motion. In this example, the protons
are trapped at the aluminum gate electrode. The shape of the TVS curve for proton
contamination is strongly dependent on the voltage ramp conditions due to the high
mobility of the protons.
The less-dense low-temperature films have a high propensity to trap water, creating a film with mobile hydrogen ions. The film’s water concentration increases as
wafers are exposed to the atmosphere, increasing the TVS signal with time. The
water concentration can be decreased by baking or alloying the wafer, reducing the
TVS proton signal. In this example, the TVS signal disappears in the SOX dielectric
when the film is annealed at 900 °C.
The negative hydroxyl (OH-) molecules remaining after the proton is emitted are
not mobile and are usually not observed in the TVS sweep as negative ion motion
but can rebond with protons, eliminating the mobile protons.

16.10.5 Detection of Mobile Ions in Intermetal Dielectrics
Triangular voltage sweep (TVS) is a valuable technique for measuring mobile ions
in MOS capacitors on the wafer’s surface. Additionally, TVS is useful for measuring mobile ions in other structures that do not involve the semiconductor surface. It
is commonly used for measuring back-end-of-line contamination in intermetal
dielectrics. This feature is a key advantage of the TVS technique over threshold
voltage and capacitance-voltage techniques.
Common metallization test structures used for metallization characterization can
be used for TVS measurements if they are sufficiently large to generate a detectable
mobile ion current. The necessary size of the test structure for mobile ion detectability depends on the severity of the mobile ion contamination, resulting in a more
extensive test structure requirement for lower amounts of contamination.
Figure 16.21 shows a metal comb and a metal comb with interleaved serpentine (He
& Lu, 2012). These structures are commonly used in parametric and yield characterization for metallization process modules. These structures can be used to measure contamination between metal lines and between metal layers. The metal
structures are simply connected as a two-terminal device, as shown in Fig. 16.15,
and the same measurement methods are used.
Simple flat parallel plate capacitors formed from two different metal layers can
also be used for metallization TVS test structures. However, most metals can block
or impede mobile ion migration, making the comb and serpentine a more sensitive
structure as the mobile ions can move between the metal lines in the surrounding
dielectrics. Typically, a comb or serpentine yields a much larger TVS peak compared to a plate capacitor (Chonko et al., 1994).
An example of a TVS measurement using a comb structure is shown in Fig. 16.22
(Huang et al., 2014). Figure 16.22a shows a plot of TVS current versus voltage with
different voltage ramp rates, showing that a faster ramp rate gives a more significant
peak. Figure 16.22b shows a similar plot showing the TVS signal with different
temperatures. No TVS peak is observed below 200 °C in this example. The mobile

416

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.21 Sketch showing common metallization test structures that can be used for TVS measurements. (a) Two terminal metal comb. (b) Metal comb with intertwined serpentine structure
(four terminals)

Fig. 16.22 TVS plot of mobile ion contamination measured on a metal comb. (a) TVS plot showing increasing TVS signal with increasing ramp rate shown as volts per second at 300 °C. (b) TVS
plot showing increasing TVS signal with increasing temperature

ion in this reference is assumed to be copper, requiring a higher temperature relative
to other mobile ions.
TVS is extremely useful for the characterization of copper metallization, enabling
the optimization of barrier metallization and other techniques to prevent copper drift
and diffusion, improving the reliability of the metallization, and increasing the
probability of meeting the chip’s lifetime requirements. Figure 16.23 shows the

16.10 Electrical Detection of Mobile Ions

417

Fig. 16.23 TVS plot showing the simultaneous detection of hydrogen and copper in a low-k
dielectric

simultaneous TVS detection of both copper and hydrogen protons (Ciofi
et al., 2006a).
TVS can be useful during technology development as an experimental tool for
process optimization experimentation. For example, Fig. 16.24 compares different
copper damascene barrier metals by TVS (Wojick et al., 2011). Different barrier
metals and deposition methods are compared, generating a metric to compare copper metallization. The ruthenium physical vapor deposition group has the worst
performance for copper migration, generating a prominent TVS peak. The tantalum
nitride physical vapor deposition performs best with a small TVS signal. An aluminum control sample is used as a reference, detecting only a small amount of sodium
and no copper. Figure 16.24b shows a current versus time plot for a time-dependent
dielectric breakdown (TDDB) test measured at 5 MV/cm at 350 °C for the same
barrier metals. The TDDB results correlate well with the TVS results, showing the
same experimental group dependence and demonstrating the value of TVS reliability prediction (Wojick et al., 2011).

16.10.6 Thermally Stimulated Ionic Current Measurements
Thermally stimulated ionic current measurements (TSIC) are a technique that is
closely related to TVS. However, instead of ramping the voltage, the temperature is
ramped. A constant voltage is applied to a MOS, and the temperature is ramped

418

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.24 TVS results of a copper barrier experiment comparing different barrier metal materials
surrounding a copper damascene line. (a) different processes are compared, including ruthenium

while measuring the current. This measurement setup for TSIC is identical to the
one for TVS shown in Fig. 16.15. TVS is generally preferred over TSIC because the
temperature ramps for the TSIC measurement tend to be more time-consuming than
TVS measurements at a constant temperature. In addition, it is often challenging to
keep probe tips on the bondpads during a TSIC measurement as the probe tips
expand as the temperature increases.
Figure 16.25 shows an example of a TSIC measurement for samples intentionally contaminated with sodium and potassium (Nauta & Hillen, 1978). The shape of
the TSIC peak varies in the plots because of a contamination concentration difference throughout the dielectric. If the capacitor is prestressed at a constant voltage at
a high temperature and then ramped down to room temperature under bias, the
mobile ions can be driven to one interface, giving a clearer signal during the measurement. The TSIC current peaks at a specific temperature for each contaminant,
with the potassium peak at a higher temperature than the sodium peak due to the
larger atomic radius. The width of the peak indicates the spread in activation energies for the mobile ion contaminant. The activation energy can be extracted directly
from the TSIC curve, determining the activation energy at each temperature of the
temperature ramp (Mori et al., 1994).

and negative bias at 2.5 V for a 150 nm SiO2 dielectric

16.10.7 Mobile Ion Detection Using
Capacitance-Voltage Techniques
Simple MOS capacitor measurements are a valuable technique for measuring
mobile ion contamination using the capacitance-voltage characteristics of an MOS
capacitor by detecting shifts in the high to low capacitance transition (flatband)
region of the C-V curve. The shift in the position of the capacitance along the voltage axis indicates the presence of mobile contamination. Gate metallization may
block mobile ions introduced after the gate is formed from reaching the gate dielectric, preventing the detection of mobile ions.
A specific measurement algorithm shown in Table 16.6 is used to detect mobile
ions. The electrical measurements typically need to be measured at room temperature because the semiconductor thermal generation can overwhelm the carrier concentration, hiding the depletion and inversion regions, and eliminating the ability to
detect the threshold voltages in the measurements (See Fig. 16.17). This effect
requires that the capacitor be measured at room temperature, with the bias across
the capacitor maintained as the temperature decreases from the high-stress temperature to hold the mobile ions at either the semiconductor or gate interfaces, enabling
detection. The capacitor is stressed at a high temperature under alternating constant
field biases to drive the mobile ions to one interface.

while holding voltage stress.

with opposite polarity for several minutes.
Ramp temperature back down to room temperature
