Fig. 16.26 Example C-V measurement for the detection of mobile ions for a 200 nm SiO2 dielectric. (a) Initial curve before stress measurement. (b) After 5 min. at −10 V, 150 °C. (c) After 5 min.
at +10 V, 150 °C. (d) After 5 min. at 150 °C with the terminals shorted (no bias). (e) A predicted
theoretical curve is shown on the right side of the plot

Figure 16.26 shows an example of mobile ion detection using the C-V technique
for a MOS capacitor (Snow et al., 1965). First, an initial curve (a) is generated, followed by a negative −10 V gate stress, shifting the curve (b) a negligible amount.
Next, a positive +10 V gate stress is performed, generating a significant C-V curve
shift (c) to the left. The large asymmetry in the positive and negative shifts indicates
that the mobile ions were initially concentrated near the gate dielectric- semiconductor interface. Finally, an unbiased bake with the capacitor electrodes shorted
diffuses the mobile concentration to a distribution close to its initial distribution (d).
The mobile ion concentration can be directly determined from the C-V plot using

16.11 Mobile Ion Degradation of Nonvolatile Memories

421

the simple calculation Q = C ΔV/q, where C is the capacitance measured midway
on the y-axis, ΔV is the shift in gate voltage on the x-axis, and q is the electronic
charge (1.602 × 10−19 C).

16.11 Mobile Ion Degradation of Nonvolatile Memories
For several reasons, nonvolatile memories (NVM), including 3D NAND, NOR,
Flash, etc., are highly susceptible to mobile ions. First, very few electrons are stored
as data in an NVM cell, as low as a few electrons per cell up to a few hundred electrons per cell (Prall, 2007). A similar number of mobile ions is sufficient to destroy
the data stored in the cell. Second, multilevel cells are commonly used, dividing the
small electron count into fractions for each data state (Aritome, 2016). Third, the
charge is stored semi-permanently in an NVM cell where it can attract mobile ions
even in an unbiased state, unlike other types of CMOS chips where there is no
attractive force for mobile ions in an unpowered state. Mobile ions thermally diffusing through the dielectrics in an NVM CMOS chip are attracted to the charge storing the data state, and they can be captured by the charge trapping layer or floating
gate. The trapped positive mobile ions near the floating gate compensate and neutralize electrons stored in the NVM cell, shifting the cell’s threshold voltage and
causing the data state to be corrupted. Additionally, NVM chips often use NVM
cells to generate a reference current for data sensing. Mobile ion driven Vt shifts of
the reference cells can cause all the data stored in the chip to be corrupted (Sim
et al., 2005). Metadata containing data for many critical chip operations may also be
stored in the NVM array.
The accidental charge stored on NVM cells due to plasma damage, as described
in Chap. 8, can create the conditions for mobile ions to be attracted during wafer
processing, affecting the NVM cell’s behavior after fabrication. In addition, thermal
processing steps can thermally erase the cells, leaving the NVM chip with the cells
in a neutral, uncharged Vt state with the mobile ions embedded in the array, where
they can quickly degrade the chip later while it is in operation.
Mobile ion contamination in nonvolatile memories has a unique and specific
characteristic, making identification of mobile ion contamination straightforward
(Liou et al., 2003). The interaction of the positive mobile ions with the negative
electrons stored in the trapping layer or floating gate causes a recognizable behavior
in the cell with a program, erase, and bake sequence applied to the cell.
Figure 16.27 shows typical sequences used in an NVM cell to detect mobile ions.
During processing, the NVM cell often undergoes plasma damage unintentionally
programming the cell, raising the Vt, by storing electrons in the trapping or floating
gate layer, as shown in Fig. 16.27a. During subsequent thermal processing after the
plasma damage step, thermal energy drives mobile ions through the dielectrics. As
a result, positive mobile ions near the NVM cell are attracted to the negative charge
in the cell and migrate onto the dielectrics surrounding the floating gate or trapping
layer, as shown in Fig. 16.27b. The wafer may complete processing with both

422

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.27 Sketch of a NVM cell showing the interaction of electrons on the floating gate interacting with mobile ions. The UV steps (ultraviolet light exposure) shown in the sketch are typically
performed with an electrical erase to remove electrons from the cell

16.11 Mobile Ion Degradation of Nonvolatile Memories

423

electrons and mobile ions trapped in the cell, as shown in Fig. 16.27b. Typically, the
first electrical operation on the cell erases the cell, removing electrons to put the cell
in a low Vt state to achieve a known cell condition. Optionally, exposure to UV light
can achieve the same result. The erase or UV step removes the electrons from the
trapping or floating gate layer, leaving the mobile ions. As a result, the mobile ions
are no longer attracted to the negative electrons and are more mobile (Fig. 16.27c).
Next, in the sequence shown in Fig. 16.27d, the cells are programmed and baked
(Fig. 16.27e).
Finally, in the sequence, some cells are programmed (Fig. 16.27h), and some
cells are erased (Fig. 16.27f) in the same array in close proximity. Both the programmed and erased cells are then baked (Fig. 16.27g–i). The mobile ions are
driven away from the erased cell by diffusion as they are no longer held by the negative electrons, resulting in an increased threshold voltage. The programmed cells
lose electrons or mobile ions (or both), resulting in a decreased Vt. This unique
behavior through the sequence shown is typically considered proof of mobile ion
contamination.
Back-end-of-line processing temperatures (typically 400–500 °C) can provide
enough thermal energy to cause mobile ion drift and diffusion. These temperatures
may partially or entirely thermally erase the cells, thermally ejecting electrons from
the region where they are trapped. Higher temperatures (~1000 °C) can be expected
to completely erase the cells, removing all electrons from the trapping region.
The cell bake conditions used in this example were 24 h at 250 °C (Liou et al.,
2003). The bake conditions need to be determined empirically for a given technology. Chip package thermal constraints may limit the maximum temperature.
The sequence of the threshold voltage increases and decreases through this testing procedure provides strong evidence of a mobile ion problem in an NVM process. The critical piece of evidence is a reduced threshold voltage when the NVM
cell is baked in the programmed state and an increased threshold voltage when the
NVM cell is baked in the erased state, indicating mobile ion drift toward the cell in
the programmed state and mobile ion diffusion away from the cell in the erased
state. Figure 16.28 shows an example of the test sequence shown in Fig. 16.27,
demonstrating positive results of the NVM mobile ion test sequence where both
charge gain and loss are observed for the final bake (Liou et al., 2003).
An additional signature of mobile ion contamination in NVM is that the data
pattern or topology in an array can control the motion of mobile ions. For example,
a data pattern in an array consisting of stripes of programmed and erased cells can
shift the mobile ion distribution. The program stripes attract mobile ions, while
erased stripes do not attract mobile ions, concentrating the mobile ions in the programmed stripes of the array. Figure 16.29 shows an x-y map of an intentionally
contaminated NVM array generated by time-of-flight secondary ion mass spectroscopy (TOF-SIMS). The mobile sodium ions are concentrated in the striped regions
containing programmed cells with electrons on the floating gate that attract the ions.
The erased stripes of cells have a minimal number of electrons or net positive charge
that does not attract mobile ions. As a result, the sodium atoms have migrated from
the erased regions, increasing the mobile ion concentration in the programmed
regions.

424

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

consisting of the following steps: Erase (electrical or UV), Program, Bake (250 °C 24 h), Program
or Erase, Bake (250 °C 24 h). The letters show the sequence from Fig. 16.27

16.12 Die Seal Techniques to Prevent Mobile
Ion Contamination
A CMOS chip has structural features designed to prevent mobile ion contamination
from the outside world. The surface of the chip is covered with a mobile ion barrier
passivation layer to keep ions, water, etc., out of the chip. Holes are etched into the
passivation over the bond pads to allow electrical connection to the pads from the

16.13

Analytical Chemical Detection of Mobile Ions

425

Fig. 16.30 Scanning electron microscope (SEM) image of scribe line damage between two
CMOS chips

outside world. A significant overlap of the bond pads (usually several microns) by
the passivation limits mobile ion penetration around the pads.
The passivation at the edge of the chip is often damaged, where individual chips
are singulated from the wafer for packaging. Lasers, saws, etc., are commonly used
to singulate or dice the chips mechanically, creating structural damage in the scribe
line adjacent to the chip edge. Figure 16.30 shows an SEM image of laser damage
in a scribe line between chips (Marks et al., 2020). This kind of damage creates a
significant amount of damage, generating a path for mobile ion ingress.
A continuous die-seal structure is added to the perimeter edge of the chip, creating a ring of protection surrounding the chip (Chen & Ker, 2005). A die seal typically attempts to create a solid wall of metal at the chip edge from the silicon surface
to the final passivation, taking advantage of the fact that most metals provide a
mobile ion barrier. Figure 16.31 shows an example sketch of a CMOS die-seal. As
shown in the figure, continuous metal lines for all metal layers are routed around the
chip perimeter. In addition, the contacts and vias are patterned as a continuous
trench between the metal layers instead of the usual single square contact pattern
(Hastings, 2005).
In this example, the die seal is connected to the substrate through a p+ diffusion.
The die seal can be connected to ground, preventing the attraction of mobile ions
toward the die seal. Alternatively, the die seal can be connected to an n + diffusion
with the die seal biased at the positive Vcc, repelling mobile ions away from the chip.

16.13 Analytical Chemical Detection of Mobile Ions
Analytical chemical analysis (ACA) of mobile ion contamination problems is often
used to detect mobile ion contamination in CMOS wafers for several reasons. ACA
can be performed on simple test wafers without the more complicated process necessary to create electrical test structures, allowing the testing of individual tools and
materials and isolating problem solving to a few process steps or tools. A vast spectrum of potential materials characterization techniques is available for detecting

used for mobile ion detection follows.

16.13.1 Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry
However, a couple of methods are commonly used for many applications in CMOS
chips, along with the detection of mobile ions. The various flavors of secondary ion
mass spectrometry (SIMS) are one of the most commonly used techniques (van der
Heide, 2014). It is a destructive technique that drills a hole in the wafer with an ion
beam and analyzes the atoms ejected from the wafer. The exact atom and isotope
can be determined and isolated, giving an accurate concentration. The beam or spot
size can be relatively small in the range of 1–100 μm, allowing for good spatial

16.13

Analytical Chemical Detection of Mobile Ions

427

Fig. 16.32 SIMS plots showing mobile ion contamination in a wafer. (a) Sodium and potassium
contamination in a resist layer on silicon. (b) Sodium and potassium contamination in several
dielectric films deposited on the wafer after a 5 min. 400 °C anneal. The films were phosphorous
silicate glass (PSG) on top, with three layers of oxide on top of a silicon nitride barrier. Samples
were contaminated by resist processing and chemical mechanical polishing

resolution across the wafer, enabling an x-y plot across the wafer such as that shown
in Fig. 16.29. In addition, SIMS can provide a depth profile into the wafer by analyzing the atoms ejected from the wafer as a function of the sputtering time. This
advantage allows the determination of which films in a wafer are contaminated and
the level of contamination versus the depth into the wafer. SIMS can provide an
accuracy of sodium detection of about 5 × 1012 atoms/cm2, which is comparable to
the electrical sensitivity shown in Fig. 16.13 (van der Heide, 2014).
As an example of SIMS analysis, Fig. 16.32 shows SIMS mobile ion measurements in a CMOS wafer (Gambino et al., 1999). The concentration is plotted as a
function of depth into the wafer. Figure 16.32a shows a dielectric contaminated by
a resist layer on a wafer after the resist layer was removed, indicating that the resist
layer is heavily contaminated. Figure 16.32b shows the mobile concentration in five
layers of films deposited on a wafer with a 400 °C 5 min heat step measured from
the front side of the wafer. The sodium and potassium contamination is distributed
throughout the five films, but the left-most phosphorous silicate glass (PSG) film
partially getters the mobile ions, trapping them into the film. The right-most silicon
nitride film functions as a mobile ion barrier, blocking the penetration of mobile
ions. The substrate’s low contamination levels on the right side of the plot are likely
the mobile ions’ forward knock-on during the SIMS sputtering process. An additional SIMS measurement from the wafer backside is needed to determine if any
mobile ions reached the silicon substrate (van der Heide, 2014).

428

16 Mobile Ion Contamination

Fig. 16.33 Sketch showing the use of vapor phase decomposition to remove materials from the
surface of a wafer for analysis

16.13.2 Vapor Phase Decomposition
Vapor phase decomposition (VPD) and inductively coupled mass spectroscopy
(ICP-MS) used together are powerful techniques used to detect low-level contamination in the materials on a CMOS wafer. It is used widely in the semiconductor
industry for routine monitoring of fabrication lines, qualification of process equipment, etc. It has parts per trillion sensitivity and more than nine orders of magnitude
capability. It can detect almost everything on the periodic table (Boukhayma, 2018).
Vapor phase decomposition (VPD) is a technique used to remove materials from
the surface of a wafer for analysis, as shown in Fig. 16.33 (Yonezawa et al., 2018).
One method is to expose the wafer to a vapor such as hydrogen fluoride (HF) that
etches the materials, such as native oxide, from the surface of the wafer into the
vapor, followed by a transfer into an analysis system. A second method scans a
chemical droplet across the wafer that etches the surface material. The droplet is
transferred into the analysis system.
Inductively coupled plasma mass spectroscopy (ICP-MS) exposes the aerosolized sample to an argon plasma. The plasma ionizes all of the elements in the sample. The ions are analyzed by a mass spectrometer system that separates the ions by
mass using a spectrometer. As a result, the concentration of each contaminant ion
can be determined, yielding a sensitive measurement capable of detecting very low
levels. Table 16.8 shows an example of the areal detection limits for an ICP-MS tool
by element. Detection of sodium and potassium can be as low as 106/cm2 (Yonezawa
et al., 2018).

eliminating the root cause of the contamination by removing mobile ions from the
offending consumable or tool. Table 16.9 lists some common historical techniques
to mitigate mobile ions by gettering the mobile ions or preventing ingress to the

16.14.2 Modern Mitigation
Mobile ion mitigation is an essential function of a semiconductor organization that
is ingrained in all aspects of the operation, including incoming materials and equipment, monitoring and operating procedures, testing, qualification, etc. All consumables are screened and tested for contamination by the supplier and the
semiconductor manufacturer using industry-wide standards such as those published by Semiconductor Equipment and Materials International (SEMI) (Reinhardt
& Kern, 2018). These contamination control procedures are highly successful,
resulting in the total elimination of the problem except for the very rare failure of
these systems.

16.14.3 Mobile Ion Barriers
Hydrogen barriers are discussed in Chap. 14. Any barrier capable of preventing the
ingress of single protons likely blocks the much bigger atoms such as copper,
sodium, or potassium (see Chap. 14). The most common barrier is silicon nitride,
which is often used for the final passivation in a CMOS chip, capable of blocking

16.14

Mobile Ion Mitigation

431

Fig. 16.34 Sodium diffusion through different silicon nitride barrier layers with different crystallinity shows increasing sodium penetration for more crystalline films. The sodium penetration into
the silicon nitride film is measured by radioactive tracer profiling

mobile ions, as shown in Fig. 16.32. However, as Chap. 14 describes, silicon nitride
may also cause detrimental effects by blocking or trapping hydrogen or water-­
related molecules. In addition, the crystallinity of the film affects the barrier properties, as shown in Fig. 16.34, where the amorphous film is the most effective mobile
ion barrier, and the most crystalline film is the least effective barrier. This result
indicates that mobile ion diffusion along grain boundaries dominates mobile ion
migration.
Doped glasses such as boron phosphorus-doped silicate glass (BPSG) and phosphorous silicate glass (PSG) can getter mobile ions, trapping them inside the film
(Liou et al., 2001). Figure 16.35 shows a SIMS plot of the sodium concentration
versus depth for various films in a flash memory process. The sodium is trapped in
the PSG film and mobile ion diffusion into the films is limited. Also, the sodium is
blocked in the nitride layer, although the exact location is not clear.

Chapter 17

Fixed Charge

17.1 Introduction
Fixed charge is the term used to describe a charge semi-permanently trapped in
dielectrics. This type of charge storage is described as an electret, where the charge
is stored in the bulk of the dielectric instead of on the conducting plates in a capacitor.
Fixed charge problems are not common in CMOS technology, but they occur
occasionally, and understanding the subject matter is an important topic for an engineer’s tool kit. Fixed charge problems tend to occur in thicker oxides, such as shallow trench isolation (STI), due to the larger volume of the dielectric. Thinner
dielectrics, such as gate dielectrics, are more immune to fixed charge problems,
usually containing a small amount of charge, which typically causes a slight threshold voltage shift. Some of the accumulated fixed oxide charge know-how in the literature is obsolete as it no longer applies to advanced CMOS technology. However,
it is difficult to predict what will be important in the future, and this topic is included
in this book.
Fixed oxide charge tends to be very stable in CMOS wafers and is unaffected by
electrical stress. It tends to be unaffected by thermal stress below 400–500 °C. Fixed
oxide charge is not mobile like mobile ions; mobile ion tests can detect no charge
motion in the case of fixed charge. Fixed charge does not recover with time after
exposure (Grove, 1967). Charge injected into dielectrics by electrical stress tends to
recover, unlike fixed oxide charge.
Fixed charge often correlates with the interface state density. The interface state
density may dominate the electrical results (see Chap. 5). Optimization of thermal
anneals and hydrogen alloys is often required to minimize the interface state density
to the point where fixed oxide charge can be studied (Nicollian & Brews, 1982, pg.
778). As an example, Fig. 17.1 shows a plot highlighting the correlation between
interface state density and fixed charge density, indicating common mechanisms
(Hori, 1997, pg. 157).
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature
Switzerland AG 2025
K. Prall, CMOS Plasma and Process Damage,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-89029-1_17

17.1.1 Electrical Symptoms of Fixed Oxide Charge
Fixed charge occasionally causes electrical problems in CMOS chips, generally
through threshold voltage shifts, affecting first isolation devices, as described in
Chap. 5. Large amounts of fixed charge can shift active transistors, affecting circuits
through threshold voltage shifts. Figure 17.2 shows a sketch of a capacitance-­
voltage plot showing a shift to the left with a positive fixed charge (Qtotal) on an
n-type wafer (Boogaard, 2001. pg. 99). Fixed charge can be detected on short-loop
capacitor wafers, full-flow CMOS wafers, or test wafers with a dielectric on the
surface. In addition, fixed charge can be detected with the inline measurement techniques shown in Chap. 10.
The fixed charge can be positive or negative depending on the materials used and
the dielectric processing conditions. Optimized processes create a minimal amount
of fixed charge with a negligible impact on the CMOS chip.
Specific processes and materials or combinations of process steps typically cause
fixed charge problems. For example, the final thermal steps in a CMOS process flow
can cause problems as the charge is created and cannot be removed at lower
temperatures.
The origin of fixed oxide charge is attributed to multiple mechanisms, including
excess silicon in the film, impurities, oxygen vacancies, dipoles, etc. (Barbottin &
Vapaille, 1989, pg. 241–242).

17.1 Introduction

437

Fig. 17.2 Sketch of a high-frequency capacitance-voltage (C-V) plot showing the shift due to
fixed oxide charge. The curve shifts to the left for a positive fixed charge for an n-type wafer. The
curve is normalized to accumulation capacitance. The effect of positively and negatively charged
interface states is shown

The shallow trench isolation (STI) in a CMOS chip commonly fails first when a
fixed charge problem occurs, shorting all devices together and resulting in excessive
leakage at the chip level. The isolation leakage can occur at the field edge, as
described in Chap. 5, or the entire field region can be inverted without an edge signature. At the product level, isolation failure may cause standby current problems.
Failing isolation can cause anomalous behavior of device structures in the chip
as the parametric measurements detect the isolation leakage instead of the expected
behavior. Diode breakdown and leakage can increase or decrease depending on the
polarity of the fixed charge, as the STI leakage dominates the junction properties.
Contact string measurements may show an unexpected reduction in contact resistance as current flows through the isolation, reducing the resistance over the normal
current path through the contact string. These are just a few examples of abnormal
parametric results caused by isolation inversion and failure. Many other possibilities exist.
Typically, the threshold voltages of the NMOS and PMOS isolation devices shift
in opposite directions depending on the polarity of the fixed charge. A positive fixed
charge increases the absolute value of the PMOS field Vt and decreases the NMOS
field Vt, while a negative fixed charge increases the NMOS field Vt and decreases
the absolute value of the PMOS field Vt.
An important signature of fixed charge isolation failure is that field devices covered with a polysilicon or metal gate often do not shift and fail, while ungated field

438

17 Fixed Charge

devices and field devices covered with metal (not gate metal) shift and fail. The
explanation is that the gate protects the field device from the electric field generated
by the fixed charge above the gate or blocks the diffusion of the atoms responsible
for the fixed oxide charge so that they do not reach the substrate. This symptom also
indicates that the source of the fixed charge originates from the process steps after
the gate metal is formed. If all isolation devices fail (gate metal, no gate, interconnected metal-gated), it indicates that the source of the fixed oxide charge originates
from process steps before the gate metallization is formed.
Shifts in the electrical behavior of the active transistors often are not observed
while the isolation fails. This result is because the gate metal also blocks the electrical field or blocks diffusion in the same manner as the isolation devices. The thin
dielectrics used for the active transistors are much less susceptible to fixed charge
than the thick isolation dielectrics, as shown in Chap. 16 for mobile ions. Fixed
charge has an identical dielectric thickness sensitivity as that of mobile ions
described in Chap. 16.

17.2 Wafer Crystal Orientation
The wafer crystal orientation is a significant factor in fixed charge generation (Deal,
1967). The fixed charge as a function of orientation for common silicon crystal orientations follows in decreasing amount of fixed charge as the crystal orientation
changes from <111> to <110> to <100>. The <111> orientation has the highest
amount of fixed charge. The <110> orientation has an intermediate amount of fixed
charge. The lowest amount of fixed charge occurs for the <100> crystal orientation.
The ratios of fixed oxide charge for <111>, <110>, and <100> are approximately 3:2:1.
CMOS wafers universally use starting material wafers with a <100> orientation
because of the reduced amount of fixed charge, among other reasons such as mobility. For example, Fig. 17.3 shows the fixed charge generated by the oxidation of silicon for two different substrate orientations (Deal, 1967). Wafers are oxidized in two
different conditions (920 °C and 1200 °C). For the <111> crystallization orientation, the fixed oxide charge increases substantially for thicker oxides. However, for
a <100> crystal orientation, the fixed oxide charge is constant as a function of oxide
thickness.
The fixed charge in the dielectric as a function of crystal orientation correlates
with the interface state density, as shown in Fig. 17.4. Both defects are related to the
bond density difference between crystal planes in the silicon lattice (Hori, 1997, pg.
158). The plot shows the advantages of using <100> wafers in CMOS technology to
minimize the fixed oxide charge. Interface states are covered in Chap. 5.

17.3 Thermal Silicon Dioxide Fixed Charge

439

oxide thickness (tox) for <111> and <100> wafer crystal orientations for different oxidation

17.3 Thermal Silicon Dioxide Fixed Charge
Historically, silicon dioxide has been widely used in wafer processing, and some of
the first fixed charge problems were discovered in SiO2. Thermally grown silicon
dioxide often contains fixed charge that is usually positive. The oxidation conditions
for the growth of silicon dioxide on a wafer play a substantial role in the generation
of fixed charge. There is a significant difference in the fixed charge as a function of
the oxidation temperature, as shown in Fig. 17.3, where 1200 °C growth conditions
have less fixed oxide charge than 920 °C. The fixed charge as a function of

440

17 Fixed Charge

temperature is shown in Fig. 17.5 for thermal oxidation (Grove, 1967, pg. 343). The
fixed charge for dry oxidation is a strong function of temperature. The fixed charge
for wet oxidation is a weaker function of temperature. Lower temperatures tend to
be worse for fixed oxide charge. Also, there is a weak dependence on p- and n-type
wafers, with p-type wafers having slightly more fixed oxide charge.
Post-oxidation annealing can affect the fixed oxide charge concentration.
Annealing in a nitrogen or noble gas ambient can reduce the fixed oxide charge to
lower levels, as shown in Fig. 17.6. The fixed oxide charge concentration is reversible. Wet or dry oxidation, followed by an anneal has a low fixed oxide charge.
Subsequent oxidation again increases the fixed oxide charge (Deal, 1967). This
effect can be repeated with cycles of oxidation and annealing.
The final thermal steps in a CMOS process flow determine the fixed oxide for the
completed wafers. Therefore, optimizing the final steps to minimize the fixed oxide
charge is essential to achieve optimum device performance. In addition, anneals
often follow dielectric deposition and growth steps to improve the film quality, and
the anneals often change the fixed charge density of the films. For example, Fig. 17.6
shows the fixed charge density for a 35 nm thermal oxide grown and annealed at
975 °C in N2. Initially, the fixed charge density begins to decrease as the oxide is
annealed, reaching a minimum at around 20 minutes. For times beyond 20 minutes,
the fixed oxide charge increases. The increased charge with increased anneal time is
attributed to the nitridation of the dielectric and silicon surface in the N2 ambient.

Fig. 17.5 Plot showing fixed oxide charge density as a function of temperature for dry (O2 only)
and steam oxidation (H2O) for a silicon dioxide dielectric grown on a <111> substrate. The fixed
charge is reduced with a post-oxidation nitrogen anneal

17.4 Fixed Charge in Deposited Dielectrics

441

Fig. 17.6 Fixed oxide charge as a function of the post-oxidation anneal time in nitrogen for a
35 nm thermal oxide grown at 975 °C and annealed at 975 °C. The zero time point is an unannealed control

Anneals with noble gases such as argon do not show an increased charge with
increased time (Vyas et al., 1982).
Historically, excursions during wafer manufacturing where the wafers are accidentally exposed to oxygen or room air during high-temperature processing can
cause isolation failure by shifting the threshold voltage to the point where the isolation leakage becomes catastrophic. Leaks in processing tools can occasionally cause
fixed charge problems.
Fixed oxide charge in thermally oxidized films is a function of the stress in the
film, as shown in Fig. 17.7, where higher temperature growth conditions yield a film
with increased stress and a reduced fixed oxide charge (Barbottin & Vapaille, 1989,
pg. 242).

17.4 Fixed Charge in Deposited Dielectrics
17.4.1 Introduction
In addition to thermally grown silicon dioxide, silicon dioxide is commonly deposited using several different techniques. There are multiple advantages to using
deposited oxides. Low-temperature processes have a reduced impact on the CMOS
transistors. Low-temperature processes are used for intermetal dielectrics where

high temperatures cannot be used without damaging the metal on the wafer. Higher
deposition rates can be obtained with deposited dielectrics such as plasma-enhanced
chemical vapor deposition (PEVCD), resulting in low costs. Deposited dielectrics
can have high step coverage properties, allowing gap fill between features.
Deposited dielectrics often differ substantially from thermal oxide with different
bonding, composition, contamination, etc., which can change the fixed oxide charge
in the films. Table 17.1 shows some common silicon dioxide-related processes
reported to have fixed oxide charge problems (Enigmatic, 2023). Fixed charge problems in deposited dielectrics occur under specific process conditions and do not
always occur.

17.4 Fixed Charge in Deposited Dielectrics

443

Fixed charge incorporation into dielectrics is often accidental. Sometimes, fixed
charge is introduced intentionally into a film to influence the electrical properties of
CMOS devices. For example, fixed charge optimization is used to optimize the electrical performance of through-silicon vias (TSVs), as described in Sec. 17.8. It is
crucial to determine if the fixed charge is stable and nonvariant through time, temperature, and electric field to eliminate reliability risks.

17.4.2 Fixed Charge Caused by Carbon Incorporation
Some deposited oxides use organic precursors containing carbon. Examples of an
organic precursor include tetraethyl orthosilicate glass (TEOS) using the precursor
tetraethoxysilane (Si(OC2H5)4), which is commonly deposited using plasma-­
enhanced chemical vapor deposition (PECVD). Its uses include dielectrics between
metal layers, passivation, etc. Another example is spin-on-glass processes (SOG).
SOG is a common deposition method used for gap fill to planarize wafers over surface structures such as shallow trench isolation, gate metal patterns, and metallization. Many different types of dielectric materials can be deposited by SOG processes,
including low-dielectric constant films (Baklanov et al., 2007, pg. 33). SOG chemicals commonly contain carbon-based or organic precursors. Non-organic films are
carbon-free but can also have fixed charge problems.
Carbon can be a source of fixed charge in CMOS wafers. Carbon can diffuse
through silicon (Chap. 15) and silicon dioxide, particularly at higher temperatures.
Carbon has been shown to pile up at the silicon interface, where it can generate a
fixed charge (Mori et al., 1994). For example, Fig. 17.8 shows a secondary ion mass
spectroscopy (SIMS) profile of carbon diffusing from a deposited PECVD TEOS

Fig. 17.8 Secondary ion mass spectroscopy (SIMS) of carbon migration from a PECVD TEOS
Boron phosphorous silicate glass (BPSG) film into a silicon dioxide film after an anneal. Carbon
pileup at the SiO2-Si interface is shown after a 1000 °C anneal (highlighted by the red circle)

444

17 Fixed Charge

Fig. 17.9 (a) Plot showing the flatband voltage shift measured on a MOS capacitor for various
deposited dielectrics on top of a silicon dioxide layer under a silicon nitride layer comparing TEOS
BPSG, silane BPSG, phosphorous doped silicate glass (PSG), boron doped silicate glass (BSG),
and silane-based silicon dioxide. (b) Cross-section of the structure used in this experiment

BPSG film into an underlying silicon dioxide layer. The carbon piles up at the silicon dioxide silicon interface after a 1000 °C anneal, forming a peak of heavy concentration (circled in red). The carbon concentration at the silicon surface in the
annealed sample shifted the flatband voltage by around 35 volts relative to that of
the unannealed sample. This level of fixed charge would be catastrophic to a CMOS
chip, causing a total loss of isolation.
As an example of fixed charge problems in films deposited with organic precursors, Fig. 17.9 compares the fixed oxide charge Vt shift for organic and inorganic
films (Mori et al., 1994). The TEOS BPSG-based organic film has a sizeable flatband voltage shift of over 20 volts. All the other films deposited in the experiment
used inorganic silane (SiH4) as the precursor and had a negligible flatband voltage
shift, indicating a minimal fixed charge.

17.4.3 Interaction with Hydrogen and Diffusion Barriers
Fixed charge problems such as the one shown in Fig. 17.8 are heavily influenced by
the presence of a diffusion barrier like silicon nitride which can block the diffusion
of carbon, hydrogen, etc. The presence of a diffusion barrier can trap the carbon in
the wafer, increasing the concentration of carbon at the surface and, subsequently,
the fixed charge, as shown in Fig. 17.10b (Mori et al., 1994). The presence of the
barrier prevents carbon from outgassing from the wafer during thermal steps, trapping it in the wafer. Figure 17.10a shows the nitride layer’s impact on the isolation
failure. The addition of the nitride layer causes the isolation layer to electrically fail
at a larger dimension than when the nitride layer is absent. The threshold voltage of
the isolation device drops toward zero voltage with decreasing dimensions.

17.4 Fixed Charge in Deposited Dielectrics

445

Fig. 17.10 Sketch and plot showing the interaction of carbon diffusion with a silicon nitride layer.
(a) Plot of threshold voltage plotted against isolation length with and without a silicon nitride capping layer. (b) Cross-section sketch showing the presence of silicon nitride layer prevents the carbon from out diffusing from the wafer allowing carbon to be trapped at the dielectric silicon
interface

In addition to trapping carbon in the wafer, silicon nitride may emit hydrogen
into the underlying films or trap water and hydrogen, as described in Chap. 14. This
may impact the electrical isolation performance.

17.4.4 Fixed Charge in Spin-On Dielectrics
Fixed charge has also been reported in spin-on dielectrics (SOD) with similar results
as the previous sections show. Both organic and inorganic (SOD) dielectrics have
been reported to have fixed oxide charge problems (Hsu et al., 1993; Pramanik
et al., 1989). Interactions between SOD films and TEOS-deposited dielectrics and
interactions with nitride passivation and hydrogen have been reported.

17.4.5 Fixed Charge in Silane-Based Dielectrics
Fixed charge problems in silane-based dielectrics have also been reported. This data
indicates that the problem is not only caused by the presence of carbon (Butler
et al., 1990).

446

17 Fixed Charge

17.5 Fixed Charge in Nitrided Dielectrics
Nitridization of a dielectric is used to harden dielectrics for applications such as
nonvolatile memories where improved reliability can be obtained (Hori, 1997, pg.
209). It is also used as a gate dielectric for older CMOS processes, where it is used
to block boron diffusion and increase the dielectric capacitance in addition to reliability improvements (Wolf, 2002, pg. 112). The presence of nitrogen in a dielectric
can generate fixed charge. Nitridation is performed by exposing a silicon dioxide
layer to a nitrogen-containing gas such as NO, NH3, or N2O in a thermal or plasma
system. The nitrogen diffuses through the SiO2 layer to the interface, where it bonds
in the dielectric.
Figure 17.11 shows an example of fixed charge generation during a silicon dioxide gate dielectric nitridation. Figure 17.11a shows the results of nitridation processes in a rapid thermal processing system (RTP) and a furnace system. The fixed
charge initially increases, reaches a maximum, and then decreases through time.
The initial increase is attributed to the fixed charge generated by nitrogen, and the
reduction is attributed to an annealing effect when the dielectric is saturated with
nitrogen (Hori, 1997, pg. 238).
A post-nitridation annealing step is commonly used to reduce the fixed charge
and interface state density of a nitrided SiO2 layer (Hori, 1997). Figure 17.11b
shows an example of the fixed charge behavior with such an anneal for lightly
nitrided films (NOS and NO) and heavily nitrided films. The fixed charge density
with annealing is reduced in the lightly nitrided films and increased in the heavily
nitrided films. This effect sets an upper limit on nitridation for a viable process.

Fig. 17.11 Plot showing fixed charge as a function of the nitridation process and post-nitridation
annealing. (a) Fixed charge density for a SiO2 dielectric as a function of nitridation time for a rapid
thermal process (RTP) and a furnace process (25 nm). (b) Fixed charge density as a function of
post-nitridation anneal time for a lightly nitrided film (NO and ONO) and heavily nitrided films
(NOS). The fixed charge for an unnitrided SiO2 film is shown as a reference on the y-axis
(red arrow)

17.6 Fixed Charge Polarity

447

17.6 Fixed Charge Polarity
Fixed oxide charge can be positive or negative depending on the atomic composition of the dielectric. The polarity is strongly dependent on the deposition method
and processing conditions. Both positively and negatively charged films can be
deposited with variations in the recipe conditions. Some dielectrics tend to have a
specific fixed charge polarity. For example, thermal silicon dioxide usually has a
positive fixed oxide charge, and aluminum oxide usually has a negative fixed oxide
charge. Recipes can be optimized to favor one polarity, or different dielectrics can
be combined to obtain a specific polarity. As an example, Fig. 17.12 compares the
fixed oxide charge as a function of the dielectric thickness (Boogaard, 2001, pg.
123). Thermal silicon dioxide has a positive fixed oxide charge. A PECVD silane-­
based silicon dioxide, followed by a 900 °C anneal has near-zero fixed charge below
10 nm and a positive fixed charge for thicker films. A 7 nm thermal oxide, followed
by PECVD silane oxide has a slightly negative fixed charge that is a slight function
of thickness. A PECVD silane oxide has a negative fixed oxide charge reaching a
level of −1.0 × 1012 cm−2. The film with the most negative fixed oxide charge is
obtained by oxidizing silicon with plasma oxidation, exposing the wafer to an
Ar-N2O plasma without silane, followed by PECVD with silane. The plasma oxidation and silane PECVD have a high propensity to create negative fixed charge. The
results of this experiment indicate that there are two competing mechanisms for a
silane PECVD process. The mechanisms are oxidation of the silicon substrate and
dielectric deposition. Increased fixed charge at the lower thicknesses indicates that
plasma oxidation of the silicon surface is the dominant mechanism and is likely due
to an excess of negatively charged oxygen atoms near the silicon surface.

Fig. 17.12 Plot showing the fixed oxide charge density as a function of oxide thickness comparing
several different SiO2 deposition methods including thermal silicon dioxide, PECVD oxide,
plasma oxide followed by PECVD oxide, thermal oxide followed by PECVD oxide, PECVD oxide
followed by a post-oxidation anneal

448

17 Fixed Charge

17.7 Negative Fixed Charge in Alumina
Alumina (Al2O3) films are well known to have a significant amount of negative
charge. Alumia is an interesting example of a film that can be used to engineer the
fixed charge polarity and concentration in addition to its barrier properties described
in Chap. 14 (Kotipalli et al., 2013). For the intentional design of a fixed charge layer,
a single or multiple-layer dielectric can be selected for its fixed charge properties.
For example, Fig. 17.13 compares the capacitance-voltage plots of several different
dielectrics, highlighting the differences in fixed charge. The curves shifted to the left
(Si3N4 and SiO2) contained a positive fixed charge, and the alumina film plots shifted
to the right and contained a negative fixed charge.
The alumina deposition and post-deposition thermal conditions can be optimized
to target a specific charge density (Batra et al., 2015). Figure 17.14a shows the
results of varying the deposition temperature for an alumina thermal atomic layer
deposition (ALD) process. The chapter’s authors state that the fixed oxide charge is
caused by the excess negatively charged oxygen atoms. Annealing the films, as
shown in Fig. 17.14b, indicates that thermal annealing neutralizes the fixed
oxide charge.
The preclean before alumina is deposited can significantly impact the fixed
charge density, as shown in Table 17.2, shifting the threshold voltage by more than
1 volt. The cleans are a hydrofluoric acid last clean (HF last), HF clean with hot
deionized water with hydrochloric acid (HCl), and a Radio Corporation of America
(RCA) clean, followed by hot deionized water with hydrochloric acid (HCl) clean
(Tofflinger, 2014, pg. 94). The initial fixed charge Qox,eff is measured on the first C-V

Fig. 17.13 Normalized capacitance-voltage (C-V) plot comparing the fixed charge of several
dielectrics, including silicon dioxide, silicon nitride, and alumina. The experiment is performed on
p-type wafers

17.8 Fixed Oxide Charge in Through-Silicon Vias
The recent development of through-silicon vias (TSVs) to enable chip stacking and
wafer backside contacts provides an interesting study of fixed charge. TSVs have
several advantages for CMOS chips. Multiple chips can be stacked into a small
volume package. Signal transmission times can be reduced by taking a shorter path
through the wafer instead of across the surface of the chip. Backside power distribution (BPD) is a recent technology that routes power and ground on the back of a
wafer in addition to the metal routing on the front of the wafer. BPD can be expected

450

17 Fixed Charge

Fig. 17.15 (a) sketch of a MOS capacitor capacitance-voltage (C-V) plot showing the variation in
the TSV capacitance over the supply voltage range (min to max). The variation is reduced by shifting the C-V curve so that the variation is minimized. (b) sketch of the cylindrical MOS capacitor
formed by a TSV passing through a silicon wafer. The minimum and maximum supply voltage
show the variation in capacitance as the supply voltage varies with the TSV operating without fixed
charge engineering

to have similar plasma damage problems as frontside metallization and TSV
technology.
TSVs connect the metallization on the front of the wafer through the wafer to the
back of the wafer. A copper cylinder is formed in a hole etched in the wafer. The
copper cylinder must be surrounded by a dielectric to separate it electrically from
the silicon substrate. A barrier metal is placed between the dielectric and copper for
adhesion and copper migration prevention. The metal cylinder surrounded by a
dielectric, as shown in Fig. 17.15b, forms the gate of a MOS capacitor, and the silicon wafer functions as the semiconductor (Rovitto, 2016, pg. 11). This cylindrical
structure behaves identically as a planar MOS capacitor.
TSVs penetrate the wafer well below the ordinarily active surface CMOS devices
into possibly lightly doped regions doped during the crystal growth of the silicon
substrate. The capacitor’s flatband and threshold voltages are controlled by the carrier concentration induced by the added dopants during the crystal or epitaxial
growth. CMOS wafers are usually lightly doped and p-type. The natural C-V behavior of this device is sketched in Fig. 17.15a (Katti et al., 2010). The MOS capacitor
naturally transitions between the accumulation, depletion, and inversion operating
regions over the voltage ranges used in a CMOS chip. The TSV signal voltages
range from ground to the supply voltage (Vdd), as shown in the figure. Operating the
TSV in this voltage range with the capacitance changing is problematic as the TSV
capacitance varies with voltage, frequency, and temperature (Zhang, 2014).
The solution to this problem is to shift the C-V curve left or right so that the
CMOS operating voltages only operate in the accumulation or inversion regions.

17.9

Fixed Charge Mitigation Techniques

451

Fig. 17.16 Capacitance-voltage plot showing fixed charge optimization to enable TSV operation
in the accumulation with the addition of an aluminum oxide layer

Typically, the higher capacitance accumulation region is chosen because it provides
the most stable capacitance. The typical methods of adjusting the flatband and
threshold voltages in CMOS devices are challenging to implement in a TSV. Ion
implantation is problematic, as it is difficult to implant ions in the TSV sidewalls
with an angled implant due to the height and diameter of the TSV. Additionally, the
ability to use thermal anneals required to activate the implants is severely limited by
the presence of metal on the wafers when the TSV is formed.
Optimization of fixed oxide charge is commonly used to set the flatband voltage
of the TSV MOS capacitor (Zhang, 2014, pg. 108). It is a practical, low-cost solution to the problem. For example, Fig. 17.16 shows a capacitance-voltage plot of a
TSV where the dielectric between the TSV Cu metal and the silicon substrate has
been optimized to set the flatband and threshold voltages. The plot compares a
PECVD TEOS silicon dioxide dielectric with a two-layer dielectric consisting of a
PECVD TEOS silicon dielectric and an aluminum oxide (Al2O3) dielectric deposited with atomic layer deposition. Adding the Al2O3 layer used the propensity of
alumina to contain a negative fixed oxide charge to shift the C-V curve (see Sec.
17.7) to the point where the TSV MOS capacitor is always in accumulation.

17.9 Fixed Charge Mitigation Techniques
Numerous known factors affect fixed charge problems. A partial list of factors is
shown in Table 17.3, along with a reference. Chip manufacturers typically avoid
process conditions that create fixed charge to avoid fixed oxide charge problems.
